Of late years an abundant shower of curates has fallen upon the north of England; they lie thick as summer gnats above the hills, and each parish seems to breed them as readily as it breeds thistles. But not of late years are we about to speak. We will go back to the beginning of this century—back before the air was scorched with certain modern heats, before the day grew brassy and the ear grew weary. We will slip, as one slips from a harshly clattering street into a cool pantry, out of the noon and into an earlier hour; evade the dust, and dream of dawn.

And if, from this prelude, you suppose a romance is being warmed for you—something sugared, spiced, and sent up in a fine froth—be undeceived. You look for sentiment, poetry, reverie; you demand passion, stimulus, and melodrama. Lower your appetite; take it down to a plainer board. Something real, cool, and solid is set before you, as unromantic as Monday morning, when the first sound in the house is the scrape of a shovel at the hearth and the mutter of one who knows work waits. It is not positively affirmed you shall never taste excitement—perhaps, towards the middle and close of the meal, the dish may quicken; but it is resolved the first course shall be such as even a strict churchman might swallow on Good Friday: cold lentils with vinegar, no oil; unleavened bread with bitter herbs, and no roast lamb.

In eighteen-hundred-eleven and twelve that affluent rain of curates had not descended. Young Levites were scarce then: there was no Pastoral Aid, no Additional Curates’ Society stretching a purse to tired rectors, and bidding them hire fresh vigour from Oxford or Cambridge. The present successors of apostles—disciples of Dr. Pusey, as some call them, and tools of the Propaganda, as others name them—were, at that date, still under cradle-blankets, or undergoing regeneration by nursery-baptism in wash-hand basins. A stranger, looking on the Italian-ironed double frills of a net-cap, could not have guessed it framed the brows of a preordained and specially sanctified successor of St. Paul; nor, in the folds of a long night-gown, have foreseen the white surplice which, in after-years, would be lifted to cruel effect over simple souls, and waved—shirt-like, conspicuous—higher than any reading-desk old-fashioned vicars had ever allowed.

Yet even then, in those lean days, curates existed: the plant was rare, but it might be found. A favoured district of the West Riding of Yorkshire could boast three rods of Aaron blossoming within a circuit of twenty miles. You shall see them. Step into a neat garden-house on the skirts of Whinbury; pass through the little passage where the draught smells faintly of damp woollen, clean soap, and the day’s cooking; enter the parlour. There they sit at dinner. Allow me to introduce them: Mr. Donne, curate of Whinbury; Mr. Malone, curate of Briarfield; Mr. Sweeting, curate of Nunnely. These are Mr. Donne’s lodgings, in the house of John Gale, a small clothier. Mr. Donne has invited his brethren to regale with him. You and I will join the party, observe what is to be observed, and listen to what is to be heard. At present they are only eating; while they eat, we will speak aside.

They are in the bloom of youth, and youth brings activity as naturally as a quick fire brings a hiss from wet logs. Their moping old vicars would gladly have seen that activity poured into the channel of pastoral duty—spent in superintending schools, visiting the sick, watching over the poor. But the youthful Levites find this dull. They prefer lavishing their energies on a proceeding which, to other eyes, looks heavy with ennui, cursed with monotony—more like a weaver’s endless treadle than like any pleasure. It is a rushing backwards and forwards amongst themselves: not a round, but a triangle of visits, kept up through winter, spring, summer, and autumn. Season and weather make no difference; with an unintelligible zeal they dare snow and hail, wind and rain, mire and dust, to go and dine, or drink tea, or sup with each other.

What draws them, it would be difficult to say. It is not friendship, for whenever they meet they quarrel. It is not religion—the thing is never named among them; theology may be touched on now and then, but piety never. It is not the love of eating and drinking: each might have as good a joint and pudding at home, tea as potent, toast as succulent, as any his brother can offer. Mrs. Gale, Mrs. Hogg, and Mrs. Whipp—their landladies—affirm “it’s just for naught else but to give folk trouble.” By “folk,” of course, the good women mean themselves; for indeed they are kept in a continual fry by this system of mutual invasion.

Mr. Donne and his guests are at dinner. Mrs. Gale waits on them; she carries the plates steadily, but there is a spark from the hot kitchen fire in her eye. The privilege of inviting a friend to a meal occasionally, without additional charge—a privilege included in the terms on which she lets her lodgings—has, in her judgement, been more than sufficiently exercised. The present week is but at Thursday, and on Monday Mr. Malone, curate of Briarfield, came to breakfast and stayed dinner; on Tuesday Mr. Malone and Mr. Sweeting of Nunnely came to tea, remained to supper, occupied the spare bed, and favoured her with their company to breakfast on Wednesday morning; now, on Thursday, they are both here at dinner, and she is almost certain they will stay all night. C’en est trop, she would have said, if French had lain ready on her tongue; but Yorkshire, sharper and plainer, served her as well.

The room is warm with the smell of roast beef just cut, of suet and browned batter from the Yorkshire pudding, of boiled greens and carrots breathing a sweet wet earthiness from their tureens. Knives rasp upon plates; chairs shift; the beer, poured, gives no brisk song of bubbles, only a dull glug. Mr. Sweeting minces his slice of beef with delicate vexation and complains it is tough. Mr. Donne says the beer is flat. That is the worst: if they were only civil, Mrs. Gale would bear the throng; if they seemed but satisfied, she would not mind the labour. But “these young parsons is so high and so scornful, they set everybody beneath their fit.” They treat her with less than civility because she keeps no servant and does her house-work herself, as her mother did before her; and they are always speaking against Yorkshire ways and Yorkshire folk. By that very token Mrs. Gale does not believe one of them is a real gentleman, or of gentle kin. “The old parsons is worth the whole lump of college lads; they know what belongs to manners, and is kind to high and low.”

“More bread!” cries Mr. Malone, with the prolonged tone that proclaims, in two syllables, the land of shamrocks and potatoes.

Mrs. Gale hates Mr. Malone more than either of the other two; but she fears him also. He is tall, strongly built, with Irish legs and arms that look made for a spade or a cudgel, and a face as genuinely national—not the frank Milesian type, not Daniel O’Connell’s style, but that high-featured, North-American-Indian cast which belongs to a certain class of Irish gentry, carrying a petrified and proud look fitter for the owner of slaves than the landlord of free peasantry. His father called himself a gentleman—poor, in debt, and besottedly arrogant; his son was like him.

Mrs. Gale offered the loaf.

“Cut it, woman,” said her guest.

And the “woman” cut it accordingly. Had she followed her inclination, she would have cut the parson too; her Yorkshire soul revolted from his manner of command.

The curates have good appetites; and though the beef is “tough,” they eat a great deal of it, sawing through the fibres, forcing each mouthful down with tolerable allowances of “flat beer.” The Yorkshire pudding vanishes, the vegetables follow, as leaves before locusts. The cheese receives distinguished marks of attention. Then a spice-cake comes by way of dessert—dark, clove-scented, moist, with currants studding the slice; it vanishes like a vision, and is no more found.

Its elegy is chanted, not in the parlour, but in the kitchen. Abraham, Mrs. Gale’s son and heir, a youth of six summers, had reckoned upon the reversion of that cake; and when his mother brings down the empty platter, he lifts up his voice and weeps sore—as if some ancient custom had been broken and some little household language of treats and promises had died in an instant.

Upstairs, meanwhile, the curates sip their wine, a liquor of unpretending vintage and moderately enjoyed. Mr. Malone would much rather have had whisky; but Mr. Donne, being an Englishman, does not keep that beverage. As they sip, they argue—not on politics, philosophy, literature (topics which are now, as ever, totally without interest for them), not even on theology practical or doctrinal, but on minute points of ecclesiastical discipline: bubbles of talk that seem empty to all save themselves. Mr. Malone contrives to secure two glasses where his brethren content themselves with one, and grows by degrees hilarious after his fashion—that is, a little insolent, rudely brilliant in his own esteem, and clamorously amused with his own sayings.

Each companion becomes, in turn, his butt. He has a stock of jokes which he serves out regularly on convivial occasions, seldom varying his wit; he never considers himself monotonous, and cares not what others think. Mr. Donne he favours with hints about his extreme meagreness, allusions to his turned-up nose, cutting sarcasms on a threadbare chocolate surtout the curate affects whenever it rains or seems likely to rain, and criticisms on a choice set of cockney phrases and pronunciations which certainly deserve remark for the finish they lend his style.

Mr. Sweeting is bantered about his stature—he is a little man, a mere boy in height and breadth beside the athletic Malone; rallied on his musical accomplishments—he plays the flute and sings hymns “like a seraph,” some young ladies think; sneered at as “the ladies’ pet;” teased about his mamma and sisters, for whom poor Mr. Sweeting retains a lingering regard, and of whom he is foolish enough to speak in the presence of the priestly Paddy, from whose anatomy the bowels of natural affection seem to have been omitted.

The victims meet these attacks each in his own way: Mr. Donne with a stilted self-complacency and half-sullen phlegm, the sole props of a somewhat rickety dignity; Mr. Sweeting with the easy indifference of a light disposition which never professed to have dignity to maintain.

When Malone’s raillery grows too offensive—which it soon does—Donne and Sweeting join in an attempt to turn the tables. They ask how many boys shouted “Irish Peter!” after him along the road (his name is Peter—the Rev. Peter Augustus Malone); whether it is the mode in Ireland for clergymen to carry loaded pistols in their pockets and a shillelah in their hands when they make pastoral visits; what is the signification of such words as vele, firrum, hellum, storrum (so he pronounces veil, firm, helm, storm); and employ such other methods of retaliation as the innate refinement of their minds suggests.

This will not do. Malone, being neither good-natured nor phlegmatic, is presently in a towering passion. He vociferates; he gesticulates. Donne and Sweeting laugh. He reviles them as Saxons and snobs at the top pitch of his high Celtic voice; they taunt him with being the native of a conquered land. He menaces rebellion in the name of his “counthry,” vents bitter hatred against English rule; they speak of rags, beggary, pestilence. The little parlour becomes an uproar. Above, the sound is not merely speech, but a brutal percussion: Malone’s fist meeting the mahogany table with repeated and sonorous contact; decanters and glasses starting and jingling at each assault; the allied English disputants mocking with sharp laughter; the isolated Hibernian stuttering out declamation.

Below, in the kitchen, Mr. and Mrs. Gale sit by their fire, listening as people listen to a storm they know will pass without flooding the house. In such a household, sound has its own weather. The quarrel above is loud, but familiar; it ruffles the air, yet promises no lasting damage. They know these clerical tempests: no curate dines or takes tea with another without some exercise of the sort; and on whatever terms they part to-night, they will meet to-morrow as the best friends in the world.

As they sit thus, a foot is heard on the outer door-step, distinct through the general din; then the knocker quivers to a sharp appeal—clean, peremptory, like a note struck on iron.

Mr. Gale goes and opens.

“Whom have you upstairs in the parlour?” asks a voice—a rather remarkable voice, nasal in tone, abrupt in utterance.

“O Mr. Helstone, is it you, sir? I could hardly see you for the darkness; it is so soon dark now. Will you walk in, sir?”

“I want to know first whether it is worth my while walking in. Whom have you upstairs?”

“The curates, sir.”

“What! all of them?”

“Yes, sir.”

“Been dining here?”

“Yes, sir.”

“That will do.”

With these words, a person enters—a middle-aged man in black. He walks straight across the kitchen to the inner door, opens it, inclines his head forward, and stands listening. There is much to hear; the noise above is just then louder than ever.

“Hey!” he ejaculates to himself; then turns to Mr. Gale. “Have you often this sort of work?”

Mr. Gale, having been churchwarden, is indulgent to the clergy.

“They’re young, you know, sir—they’re young,” he says deprecatingly.

“Young! They want caning. Bad boys—bad boys! And if you were a Dissenter, John Gale, instead of being a good Churchman, they’d do the like—they’d expose themselves; but I’ll—”

He finishes the sentence by action. He passes through the inner door, draws it after him, and mounts the stair. The sound of his steps is steady, measured, as if he took the stairs for a parade-ground. He pauses again at the upper room, listens a few moments, then makes entrance without warning and stands before the curates.

They are silent; transfixed. The invader too, for an instant, seems to take the measure of the scene.

He is short of stature, but straight of port; upon broad shoulders he bears a hawk’s head—beak and eye—and above it a Rehoboam or shovel hat, which he does not think it necessary to lift. He folds his arms on his chest and surveys the young men—if friends they are—at leisure.

“What!” he begins, and now his voice is no longer nasal but deep—more than deep—made purposely hollow and cavernous, as if he spoke from a pulpit carved in stone. “What! has the miracle of Pentecost been renewed? Have the cloven tongues come down again? Where are they? The sound filled the whole house just now. I heard the seventeen languages in full action: Parthians and Medes and Elamites; the dwellers in Mesopotamia, and in Judea and Cappadocia, in Pontus and Asia, Phrygia and Pamphylia, in Egypt and in the parts of Libya about Cyrene; strangers of Rome, Jews and proselytes, Cretes and Arabians. Every one of these must have had its representative in this room two minutes since.”

“I beg your pardon, Mr. Helstone,” begins Mr. Donne, recovering himself with a stiff civility. “Take a seat, pray, sir. Have a glass of wine?”

His civilities receive no answer. The falcon in the black coat proceeds.

“What do I talk about the gift of tongues? Gift, indeed! I mistook the chapter, and book, and Testament—gospel for law, Acts for Genesis, Jerusalem for the plain of Shinar. It was no gift, but the confusion of tongues which has gabbled me deaf as a post. You—apostles? What! you three? Certainly not; three presumptuous Babylonish masons—neither more nor less!”

“I assure you, sir, we were only having a little chat together over a glass of wine after a friendly dinner—settling the Dissenters!” puts in Donne.

“Oh! settling the Dissenters, were you? Was Malone settling the Dissenters? It sounded to me much more like settling his co-apostles. You were quarrelling together, making almost as much noise—you three alone—as Moses Barraclough, the preaching tailor, and all his hearers are making in the Methodist chapel down yonder, where they are in the thick of a revival. I know whose fault it is. It is yours, Malone.”

“Mine, sir?” Malone asks, affronted.

“Yours, sir. Donne and Sweeting were quiet before you came, and would be quiet if you were gone. I wish when you crossed the Channel you had left your Irish habits behind you. Dublin student ways won’t do here. The proceedings which might pass unnoticed in a wild bog and mountain district in Connaught will, in a decent English parish, bring disgrace on those who indulge in them, and—what is far worse—on the sacred institution of which they are merely the humble appendages.”

There is a certain dignity in the little elderly gentleman’s rebuke, though it is not, perhaps, the tenderness most appropriate to correction. Mr. Helstone, standing straight as a ramrod, looking keen as a kite, has, despite hat and coat and gaiters, more the air of a veteran officer chiding subalterns than of a venerable priest exhorting sons in the faith. Gospel mildness, apostolic benignity, have never breathed their influence over that brown, cut visage; but firmness has fixed the features, and sagacity has carved her lines about them.

“I met Supplehough,” he continues, “plodding through the mud this wet night, going to preach at Milldean opposition shop. As I told you, I heard Barraclough bellowing in the midst of a conventicle like a possessed bull; and I find you, gentlemen, tarrying over your half-pint of muddy port wine, and scolding like angry old women. No wonder Supplehough should have dipped sixteen adult converts in a day—which he did a fortnight since; no wonder Barraclough, scamp and hypocrite as he is, should attract all the weaver-girls in their flowers and ribbons to see how much harder are his knuckles than the wooden brim of his tub; as little wonder that you, when left to yourselves, without your rectors—myself, and Hall, and Boultby—to back you, should too often perform the holy service of our Church to bare walls, and read your bit of a dry discourse to the clerk, the organist, and the beadle. But enough. I came to see Malone. I have an errand unto thee, O captain!”

“What is it?” Malone inquires, discontented. “There can be no funeral to take at this time of day.”

“Have you any arms about you?”

“Arms, sir?—yes, and legs.” And he advances the mighty members with a kind of coarse humour.

“Bah! weapons I mean.”

“I have the pistols you gave me yourself. I never part with them. I lay them ready cocked on a chair by my bedside at night. I have my blackthorn.”

“Very good. Will you go to Hollow’s Mill?”

“What is stirring at Hollow’s Mill?”

“Nothing as yet, nor perhaps will be; but Moore is alone there. He has sent all the workmen he can trust to Stilbro’; there are only two women left about the place. It would be a nice opportunity for any of his well-wishers to pay him a visit, if they knew how straight the path was made before them.”

“I am none of his well-wishers, sir. I don’t care for him.”

“Soh! Malone, you are afraid.”

“You know me better than that. If I really thought there was a chance of a row I would go; but Moore is a strange, shy man, whom I never pretend to understand; and for the sake of his sweet company only I would not stir a step.”

“But there is a chance of a row; if a positive riot does not take place—of which, indeed, I see no signs—yet it is unlikely this night will pass quite tranquilly. You know Moore has resolved to have new machinery, and he expects two wagon-loads of frames and shears from Stilbro’ this evening. Scott, the overlooker, and a few picked men are gone to fetch them.”

“They will bring them in safely and quietly enough, sir,” Malone answers, sullenly confident.

“Moore says so, and affirms he wants nobody. Some one, however, he must have, if it were only to bear evidence in case anything should happen. I call him very careless. He sits in the counting-house with the shutters unclosed; he goes out here and there after dark—wanders right up the hollow, down Fieldhead Lane, among the plantations—as if he were the darling of the neighbourhood; or—being, as he is, its detestation—bore a charmed life, as they say in tale-books. He takes no warning from the fate of Pearson, nor from that of Armitage—shot, one in his own house and the other on the moor.”

“But he should take warning, sir, and use precautions too,” interposes Mr. Sweeting, earnest, with the quick sensitivity of one whose ear catches trouble before his eye sees it. “And I think he would if he heard what I heard the other day.”

“What did you hear, Davy?”

“You know Mike Hartley, sir?”

“The Antinomian weaver? Yes.”

“When Mike has been drinking for a few weeks together, he generally winds up by a visit to Nunnely vicarage, to tell Mr. Hall a piece of his mind about his sermons—to denounce the horrible tendency of his doctrine of works, and warn him that he and all his hearers are sitting in outer darkness.”

“Well, that has nothing to do with Moore.”

“Besides being an Antinomian, he is a violent Jacobin and leveller, sir.”

“I know. When he is very drunk, his mind is always running on regicide. Mike is not unacquainted with history, and it is rich to hear him going over the list of tyrants of whom, as he says, ‘the revenger of blood has obtained satisfaction.’ The fellow exults strangely in murder done on crowned heads, or on any head for political reasons. I have already heard it hinted he seems to have a queer hankering after Moore. Is that what you allude to, Sweeting?”

“You use the proper term, sir. Mr. Hall thinks Mike has no personal hatred of Moore. Mike says he even likes to talk to him and run after him, but he has a hankering that Moore should be made an example of. He was extolling him to Mr. Hall the other day as the mill-owner with the most brains in Yorkshire, and for that reason he affirms Moore should be chosen as a sacrifice—an oblation of a sweet savour. Is Mike Hartley in his right mind, do you think, sir?” Sweeting asks simply, as one asks about a man’s health.

“Can’t tell, Davy. He may be crazed, or he may be only crafty, or perhaps a little of both.”

“He talks of seeing visions, sir.”

“Ay. He is a very Ezekiel or Daniel for visions. He came just when I was going to bed last Friday night to describe one that had been revealed to him in Nunnely Park that very afternoon.”

“Tell it, sir. What was it?” urges Sweeting, leaning forward, hungry for the tale.

“Davy, thou hast an enormous organ of wonder in thy cranium. Malone, you see, has none. Neither murders nor visions interest him. See what a big vacant Saph he looks at this moment.”

“Saph! Who was Saph, sir?” asks Sweeting, caught as much by the strange name as by the promised marvel.

“I thought you would not know. You may find it out. It is biblical. I know nothing more of him than his name and race; but from a boy upwards I have always attached a personality to Saph. Depend on’t he was honest, heavy, and luckless. He met his end at Gob by the hand of Sibbechai.”

“But the vision, sir?” Sweeting persists, the parlour’s earlier clamour now thoroughly stilled, as if even the house had held its breath to listen.

The night had a kitchen-blackness to it, as if some immense hand had turned an iron pot over the sky and left it there to steam. Rain pressed down in steady sheets; whatever stars or moon might have been abroad were smothered under cloud—cloud that, by day, would have looked only leaden, but now showed as a deep, wet soot. Mr. Malone went on through it with the stubborn gait of a man who measures weather only by how soon it soaks his cuffs. He did not pause to see what the heavens were doing; he was not one of those who can read comfort or warning in the change of air. His mind kept to landmarks as a cook keeps to the clock: Briarfield Church spire, then, beyond, the scattered lights of Redhouse.

His boots made their own music—“tramp” where flagstones saved him, “splash” where the cart-ruts held rain like basins. The soundscape of the lane was all close, dull, and sodden: the hush of saturated hedges, the suction of mud, the far, faint throb of Stilbro’ furnaces throwing a lurid, tremulous smear on the eastern rim of cloud. That industrial glow did not warm; it only hinted of heat locked away behind brick and iron, and of labour that never truly slept.

At Redhouse, the world briefly offered him comfort. Through a half-curtained window he caught the orange wink of a fire and the gleam of glasses on a round table; he could almost taste the sharp, consoling bite of whisky cut with water—almost hear the hearty clang of voices over oak and ale. The door might have opened on a bright pocket of human warmth, but those men inside were Mr. Helstone’s parishioners; they knew their curate. Malone’s thirst was real, yet his prudence was stronger. He sighed once—an Irish sigh that was half humour, half resignation—and went on.

The highroad fell away behind him. He took the short cut across fields, leaping hedge and wall with the heedlessness of a big man in a hurry. The land lay level, long, and monotonous, the kind of ground that gives no shelter and no variety, only darkness and the steady patter of rain. One building rose out of it—large, hall-like, and irregular: a high gable, then a long stretch of front, then a lower gable, and a thick, towering stack of chimneys. Trees stood behind like a black congregation. No candle showed; no voice answered the wind. Only the thin, persistent trickle of water from eaves and a low whistle round stone and bough made the place audible. It had the sealed silence of a house holding its breath.

Beyond it the ground dipped, steeply and suddenly, into a hollow where a stream could be heard running swollen and quick, a restless ribbon of sound. One light burned below—small, steady, human. Malone steered to it as to a hearth.

He reached a little house, white even in that thick darkness, and knocked. A fresh-faced servant opened, candle in hand. The flame made a narrow passage visible—clean walls, white floor, and a strip of crimson carpet climbing a cramped stair. Two doors, both faced with crimson baize, gave the place a neatness that felt almost defiant against the night outside.

“Mr. Moore is at home, I suppose?” Malone asked, shaking rain from his shoulders.

“Yes, sir; but he is not in.”

“Not in! Where is he then?”

“At the mill—i’ th’ counting-house.”

At that, one crimson door opened, and a woman’s voice spoke from within with brisk household authority. “Are the wagons come, Sarah?” A female head appeared too; curl-papers at either temple made plain she had been interrupted in the middle of some domestic campaign. She was no monster, but Malone started as if he had surprised a sentinel. Big as he was, he retreated bashfully into the wet, muttered, “I’ll go to him,” and hurried down the short lane and across the obscure yard towards the hulking black mass of the mill.

Work was over; the hands were gone. The great building sat shut and blind, its machinery stilled, its windows dark. Yet the hollow held sound: the rush of the stream, the gusts scraping over stone, and now and then a faint, metallic clink from somewhere unseen—as if iron still remembered its own motion. Malone walked round the sooty flank until he found a chink of lamplight. He beat a lively tattoo on the door with the thick end of his shillelagh.

A key turned. The door opened.

“Is it Joe Scott?” called a voice from within, sharp with expectation. “What news o’ t’ wagons, Joe?”

“No; it’s myself. Mr. Helstone would send me.”

“Oh! Mr. Malone.” The name, spoken, fell a shade flatter than hope had meant it to. After the briefest pause, the same voice, polite and a little formal, added, “I beg you will come in, Mr. Malone. I regret extremely Mr. Helstone should have thought it necessary to trouble you so far. There was no necessity—I told him so—and on such a night; but walk forwards.”

Malone followed through a dark room whose shape he could not make out, and into a brighter inner place that struck his rain-strained eyes like a sudden hearth. A good fire burned behind bars, red and frank; a lamp of elegant make shone vivid on a table. Beyond those, the room was plain almost to severity: bare boards underfoot, no carpet; three or four stiff-backed chairs painted green, with the look of old farm-kitchen furniture; a solid desk; and on the stone-coloured walls, framed sheets—plans and drawings for building, gardening, machinery—lines and measurements where other men would have hung landscapes.

Moore took Malone’s wet coat and hat and bade him hang them. Malone, with relief, drew a chair to the grate and set his knees so near the heat that it licked the damp out of his stockings.

“Comfortable quarters you have here, Mr. Moore; and all snug to yourself.”

“Yes; but my sister would be glad to see you, if you would prefer stepping into the house.”

“Oh no. The ladies are best alone. I never was a lady’s man. You don’t mistake me for my friend Sweeting, do you, Mr. Moore?”

“Sweeting? Which of them is that? The gentleman in the chocolate overcoat, or the little gentleman?”

“The little one—he of Nunnely; the cavalier of the Misses Sykes, with the whole six of whom he is in love, ha! ha!”

“Better be generally in love with all than specially with one, I should think, in that quarter.”

“But he is specially in love with one besides; for when I and Donne urged him to make a choice amongst the fair bevy, he named—which do you think?”

A queer, quiet smile went over Moore’s mouth. “Dora, of course, or Harriet.”

“Ha! ha! you’ve an excellent guess. But what made you hit on those two?”

“Because they are the tallest, the handsomest, and Dora, at least, is the stoutest; and as your friend Mr. Sweeting is but a little slight figure, I concluded that, according to a frequent rule in such cases, he preferred his contrast.”

“You are right; Dora it is. But he has no chance, has he, Moore?”

“What has Mr. Sweeting besides his curacy?”

The question delighted Malone; laughter shook him for a full three minutes, as if the mere thought of Sweeting’s possessions were a joke too rich to swallow quickly.

“What has Sweeting? Why, David has his harp, or flute, which comes to the same thing. He has a sort of pinchbeck watch; ditto, ring; ditto, eyeglass. That’s what he has.”

“How would he propose to keep Miss Sykes in gowns only?”

“Ha! ha! Excellent! I’ll ask him that next time I see him. I’ll roast him for his presumption. But no doubt he expects old Christopher Sykes would do something handsome. He is rich, is he not? They live in a large house.”

“Sykes carries on an extensive concern.”

“Therefore he must be wealthy, eh?”

“Therefore he must have plenty to do with his wealth, and in these times would be about as likely to think of drawing money from the business to give dowries to his daughters as I should be to dream of pulling down the cottage there, and constructing on its ruins a house as large as Fieldhead.”

“Do you know what I heard, Moore, the other day?”

“No. Perhaps that I was about to effect some such change. Your Briarfield gossips are capable of saying that—or sillier things.”

“That you were going to take Fieldhead on a lease (I thought it looked a dismal place, by-the-bye, to-night, as I passed it), and that it was your intention to settle a Miss Sykes there as mistress—to be married, in short, ha! ha! Now, which is it? Dora, I am sure. You said she was the handsomest.”

Moore’s face hardened—not with anger, but with weary contempt, as if the same stale dish were set before him again and again. “I wonder how often it has been settled that I was to be married since I came to Briarfield. They have assigned me every marriageable single woman by turns in the district. Now it was the two Misses Wynns—first the dark, then the light one; now the red-haired Miss Armitage; then the mature Ann Pearson. At present you throw on my shoulders all the tribe of the Misses Sykes. On what grounds this gossip rests God knows. I visit nowhere; I seek female society about as assiduously as you do, Mr. Malone. If ever I go to Whinbury, it is only to give Sykes or Pearson a call in their counting-house, where our discussions run on other topics than matrimony, and our thoughts are occupied with other things than courtships, establishments, dowries. The cloth we can’t sell, the hands we can’t employ, the mills we can’t run, the perverse course of events generally, which we cannot alter, fill our hearts, I take it, pretty well at present, to the tolerably complete exclusion of such figments as love-making, etc.”

“I go along with you completely, Moore. If there is one notion I hate more than another, it is that of marriage—I mean marriage in the vulgar weak sense, as a mere matter of sentiment—two beggarly fools agreeing to unite their indigence by some fantastic tie of feeling. Humbug! But an advantageous connection, such as can be formed in consonance with dignity of views and permanency of solid interests, is not so bad—eh?”

“No,” Moore answered, and even that syllable sounded borrowed. He sat gazing into the fire as if the coal-glow were a ledger he could read. The room’s noises were simple: the crack of wood, the soft simmer of heat in the grate, rain ticking at the panes. Beyond, the hollow kept its own watery hush. Suddenly Moore lifted his head.

“Hark!” he said. “Did you hear wheels?”

He went to the window, opened it, and listened. The rain rushed in colder and the wind rose to meet it; from the stream came a quicker, more urgent rush. He closed the sash. “It is only the sound of the wind rising,” he remarked, “and the rivulet a little swollen, rushing down the hollow. I expected those wagons at six; it is near nine now.”

“Seriously, do you suppose that the putting up of this new machinery will bring you into danger?” Malone asked. “Helstone seems to think it will.”

Moore’s eyes steadied, intent as if he had sighted something in the dark. “I only wish the machines—the frames—were safe here, and lodged within the walls of this mill. Once put up, I defy the frame-breakers. Let them only pay me a visit and take the consequences. My mill is my castle.”

“One despises such low scoundrels,” Malone observed, with weighty morality. “I almost wish a party would call upon you to-night; but the road seemed extremely quiet as I came along. I saw nothing astir.”

“You came by the Redhouse?”

“Yes.”

“There would be nothing on that road. It is in the direction of Stilbro’ the risk lies.”

“And you think there is risk?”

“What these fellows have done to others they may do to me. There is only this difference: most of the manufacturers seem paralyzed when they are attacked. Sykes, for instance, when his dressing-shop was set on fire and burned to the ground, when the cloth was torn from his tenters and left in shreds in the field, took no steps to discover or punish the miscreants: he gave up as tamely as a rabbit under the jaws of a ferret. Now I, if I know myself, should stand by my trade, my mill, and my machinery.”

“Helstone says these three are your gods; that the ‘Orders in Council’ are with you another name for the seven deadly sins; that Castlereagh is your Antichrist, and the war-party his legions.”

“Yes; I abhor all these things because they ruin me. They stand in my way. I cannot get on. I cannot execute my plans because of them. I see myself baffled at every turn by their untoward effects.”

“But you are rich and thriving, Moore?”

“I am very rich in cloth I cannot sell. You should step into my warehouse yonder, and observe how it is piled to the roof with pieces. Roakes and Pearson are in the same condition. America used to be their market, but the Orders in Council have cut that off.”

Malone, who could relish a hot drink better than a discussion of tariffs, knocked his boot-heels together and yawned. Moore, absorbed in his own vexations, did not notice.

“And then to think,” Moore went on, impatience gathering like steam under a lid, “to think that these ridiculous gossips of Whinbury and Briarfield will keep pestering one about being married! As if there was nothing to be done in life but to ‘pay attention,’ as they say, to some young lady, and then to go to church with her, and then to start on a bridal tour, and then to run through a round of visits, and then, I suppose, to be ‘having a family.’ Oh, que le diable emporte!” He broke off, then added more quietly, “I believe women talk and think only of these things, and they naturally fancy men’s minds similarly occupied.”

“Of course—of course,” Malone agreed, whistling, turning his head as if he could sniff out what the room lacked. Restlessness sat on him; the damp in his bones cried out for warmth of another kind.

This time Moore understood. “Mr. Malone,” he said, “you must require refreshment after your wet walk. I forget hospitality.”

“Not at all,” Malone replied; but his face brightened as if the right match had finally been struck.

“It is my fancy,” Moore continued, rising to a cupboard, “to have every convenience within myself, and not to be dependent on the feminity in the cottage yonder for every mouthful I eat or every drop I drink. I often spend the evening and sup here alone, and sleep with Joe Scott in the mill. Sometimes I am my own watchman. I require little sleep, and it pleases me on a fine night to wander for an hour or two with my musket about the hollow. Mr. Malone, can you cook a mutton chop?”

“Try me. I’ve done it hundreds of times at college.”

“There’s a dishful, then, and there’s the gridiron. Turn them quickly. You know the secret of keeping the juices in?”

“Never fear me; you shall see. Hand a knife and fork, please.”

Malone turned up his coat-cuffs with the seriousness of a man entering sacred office and set the chops upon the iron. At once the room filled with a livelier life: fat began to hiss and spit, sending up a smell at once animal and appetising—hot iron, searing meat, the faint sweetness of browning. He watched the surface change, coaxing colour rather than scorching it, keeping the heat brisk so the outside might seal and the inside stay moist. The rain’s chill seemed to fall back before that savoury steam.

Moore laid out supper with the method of one accustomed to serve himself. Plates went on the table; a loaf of bread; a black bottle; two tumblers. From the same well-stocked cupboard he drew a small copper kettle, filled it from a large stone jar standing in the corner, and set it to the fire beside the gridiron. Then came lemons, sugar, and a little china punch-bowl—bright domestic implements in that bare, businesslike room, as if the senses had insisted on their own rights amid ledgers and plans.

While he was brewing the punch, there came a tap at the door.

“Is it you, Sarah?”

“Yes, sir. Will you come to supper, please, sir?”

“No; I shall not be in to-night; I shall sleep in the mill. So lock the doors, and tell your mistress to go to bed.”

He returned to the table. Malone, his fine face ruddy with heat, bent over the gridiron like a man in love with his own competence. “You have your household in proper order,” he observed approvingly. “You are not under petticoat government, like poor Sweeting, a man—whew! how the fat spits! it has burnt my hand—destined to be ruled by women. Now you and I, Moore—there’s a fine brown one for you, and full of gravy—you and I will have no gray mares in our stables when we marry.”

“I don’t know; I never think about it,” Moore said, with that same distant air. “If the gray mare is handsome and tractable, why not?”

“The chops are done,” Malone declared, lifting them off at the right moment, when the edges were crisped brown and the centre promised tenderness. “Is the punch brewed?”

“There is a glassful. Taste it. When Joe Scott and his minions return they shall have a share of this, provided they bring home the frames intact.”

Malone tasted, approved, and grew exultant over the meal. His spirits rose with the heat and the liquor; he laughed too loudly at trifles, made jokes as thin as water-gruel and praised them as if they were feasts. He ate with the appetite of a man wrung out by weather—bread torn by hand, meat still giving up its juices, the punch sharp with lemon and softened by sugar, warming a track down his throat and into his chest.

Moore remained quiet, his attention often slipping away to the window and the thought of the delayed wagons. By firelight, he showed as a man made more of edge than of breadth—thin, dark, sallow, with shadowy hair streaking his forehead as carelessly as rain streaked the pane. He looked as if he spent little time at a mirror, and less at ease in himself than in his work. Only by degrees did the fineness of his face appear: a southern regularity in the lines of brow and cheek, disturbed by hollowness and an anxious cast that made beauty seem an accident under pressure. His eyes were large, grave, and grey—searching eyes, more accustomed to reckon and plan than to soften. When he smiled, it did not turn him frank or merry; it only let through a sedate charm, a suggestion—true or false—of patience and forbearing fidelity, qualities that might wear well in a home if ever he allowed himself one. He was young still, not much beyond thirty, tall and slender. Yet his voice, when he spoke, displeased a Yorkshire ear: a foreign accent sat upon his English, and though he handled diction with studied carelessness, the sound of it grated as an imported tool grates against native stone.

He was, in fact, only half British, and scarcely that. His mother’s blood ran from abroad; he himself had been born and partly reared on foreign soil. A hybrid in nature, he had a hybrid’s feeling—unapt to cleave to party or sect, unready to call any place wholly home, inclined to set Robert Gérard Moore apart from the communities among which his lot moved, and to push that man’s interests with a hard concentration that left little room for general claims. Trade was hereditary in him: the Gérards of Antwerp had been merchants for two hundred years. Once they had been wealthy; then business uncertainties had undermined credit, and for a dozen years the house had stood tottering. With the shock of the French Revolution it fell—fell utterly—and in its ruin dragged down the connected Yorkshire firm of Moore. Robert Moore the elder, resident in Antwerp, had married Hortense Gérard believing she would inherit her father Constantine Gérard’s share. She inherited, instead, his share of liabilities. Creditors were satisfied by composition; but some said her son took the old debt upon his own conscience as a legacy, and cherished an ambition—stern as it was secret—to discharge it fully one day, and rebuild Gérard and Moore on a greatness equal to what had been lost. If a childhood spent beside a saturnine mother, under the shadow of foreboded evil, can brand a mind; if a manhood drenched by the pitiless descent of actual storm can deepen the mark—then his, likely, was marked in no gold, but in iron.

Yet if he carried a great end, he could not command great means. He was forced to begin with the day of small things. Coming to Yorkshire, he—whose people had once held warehouses in seaports, factories in inland towns, a town-house and a country-seat—found no path open but to rent a cloth-mill in a remote hollow; take the cottage adjoining as his residence; and add, as pasture for his horse and space for his cloth-tenters, a few acres of steep, rugged land lining the brawling mill-stream. All this he held at a high rent—for war made everything dear—from the trustees of the Fieldhead estate, which belonged then to a minor.

He had lived in the district two years and had proved himself active. The dingy cottage had been made neat and tasteful. Of rough ground he had carved garden-space and cultivated it with singular, almost Flemish exactness. As for the mill—old, fitted with old, inefficient machinery—he had from the first held its arrangements in contempt and aimed at radical reform, pushing forward as fast as limited capital would let him. That narrowness galled him. “Forward” seemed stamped upon his soul; poverty held the reins. At times, figuratively, he foamed when checked.

In such a state it was not to be expected he would consider long whether his advance harmed others. Not being native, not long resident, he did not sufficiently care that new inventions threw old workpeople out of employ. He never asked where those whom he no longer paid found daily bread; and in that negligence he only resembled thousands, on whom the starving poor of Yorkshire seemed to have a closer claim.

The time itself was overshadowed in British history, especially in the northern provinces. War stood at its height; Europe was all involved. England, if not weary, was worn with long resistance—and half her people were weary too, crying for peace on any terms. National honour had become, to many dimmed by hunger, an empty word; for a morsel of meat they would have sold their birthright.

Cheerfulness, it seems, is kneaded as much from what a man carries in his own breast as from what the weather pours upon his shoulders. That night, the drizzle fell with a cold pertinacity, and the road off Stilbro’ Moor lay half lost in darkness and wet; yet Mr. Helstone and Mr. Moore went forward from the mill-yard gates with a kind of fierce enjoyment, as if danger were a spice they had long missed at table.

Each of the three pedestrians behind them bore a lantern; and whenever the light struck Moore’s face, it found—unusual there—animation, a quick glitter in the eye like fire caught in a black bottle, and a liveliness that made the stern line of his mouth look young. When it shone on the rector, it showed hard features set, not in prayer or pity, but in exultation: grin and gleam together, like a blade that likes its own edge. The night was raw, the expedition not without peril; any lurking hand among the men who had been at work on the moor might have taken pleasure in shooting either leader from behind a wall. Both leaders knew it. The knowledge, to two natures of steady pulse and tempered nerve, was a cordial.

It is a grave thing—so you will say—to see a parson warlike. A clergyman’s errand among men is peace; his pattern is not a captain’s. I do not deny it. Yet it is not always possible to cut a man out, as you cut a pattern for a garment, and then fit him without wrinkle to the profession hung upon him. Helstone had missed his vocation. He should have been a soldier; circumstance had made him priest. He was conscientious, brave, stern, rigid; faithful to what he called principle, and faithful also to prejudices that hardened into bone. Sympathy was scant in him, gentleness scant; but sincerity was there, and an honour that would not bend. Such men are commonly cursed by one half the people and adored by the other; he had both portions.

United, for the present, in one cause, and elated by the same risk, you might expect cordial talk between them as they rode side by side. It never came. Their temperaments—hard and bilious, each in his own fashion—met like flint and steel, not to warm but to strike. Their old subject rose as inevitably as smoke from damp turf: the war.

Helstone was high Tory; Moore, by interest and by mordant disposition, took the Whig side at least so far as opposition to war went. Of British politics beyond what touched his mill and purse he professed little; but in this one matter he delighted to fret the rector. He would talk of Bonaparte with a cool audacity, as though the man were an inevitable storm and Europe a field of ripe grain. He would taunt England, hint at her impotence, advance—quietly, obstinately—that it might be wiser to yield soon than late, since, as he believed, Napoleon must in the end crush every antagonist and reign supreme.

Helstone could not bear such sentiments. Only by remembering that Moore was half alien—born abroad, with foreign gall in his veins—could the rector refrain from the impulse to lift his cane. Yet even while disgusted he had a sort of harsh respect for Moore’s consistency: the dogged way he stood to his opinion, as a man stands to a post in a gale.

They turned into the Stilbro’ road and met what little wind the night possessed. Rain slanted into their faces like fine thrown grit; the sound of it on cloaks and hat-brims made a restless, incessant hush—nature’s own percussion, clean and steady, unlike the harsh metal clatter of machinery that had lately begun to rule men’s nerves by day. The moor itself answered with darker tones: the sucking of wheels in mire, the thin cry of some bird startled from a furze-bush, the far murmur of water swollen in a gully. It was a soundscape to bring a man either to calm or to challenge; to these two it brought challenge.

Moore, braced by the rawness and perhaps sharpened by the stinging drizzle, began again to goad.

“Does your Peninsular news please you still?” he asked.

“What do you mean?” returned Helstone, surly.

“I mean—have you still faith in that Baal of a Lord Wellington?”

“And what do you mean now?”

“Do you still believe that wooden-faced, pebble-hearted idol of England has power to send fire down from heaven to consume the French holocaust you want to offer up?”

“I believe Wellington will flog Bonaparte’s marshals into the sea the day it pleases him to lift his arm.”

“But you can’t be serious. Bonaparte’s marshals are great men, acting under the guidance of an omnipotent master-spirit. Your Wellington is the most humdrum of commonplace martinets—slow, mechanical—hampered, too, by an ignorant home government.”

“Wellington is the soul of England,” Helstone said, and his voice took that ring which in a pulpit would have been called fervour, and in a camp, spirit. “He is the right champion of a good cause—the representative of a powerful, resolute, sensible, honest nation.”

“Your good cause—so far as I understand it—is the restoration of that filthy, feeble Ferdinand to a throne he disgraced. Your representative of an honest people is a dull-witted drover acting for a duller farmer; and against these are arrayed victorious supremacy and invincible genius.”

“Against legitimacy is arrayed usurpation; against modest, single-minded, righteous, brave resistance to encroachment is arrayed boastful, double-tongued, selfish, treacherous ambition to possess. God defend the right!”

“God often defends the powerful.”

Helstone’s whole rigid little frame seemed to tighten, as if the wet air had turned to iron in his lungs.

“What! Then the handful of Israelites standing dryshod on the Asiatic side of the Red Sea was more powerful than Pharaoh’s host drawn up on the African side? Were they more numerous? better appointed? more mighty? Don’t speak, Moore, or you’ll tell a lie—and you know you will. They were poor, overwrought bondsmen. Tyrants had oppressed them four hundred years; women and children weakened their thin ranks. Their masters were pampered—armed, horsed, charioted; the wanderers were afoot. Few had better weapons than shepherds’ crooks or masons’ tools; their leader had only his rod. But right was with them. The God of battles was on their side. Crime and the lost archangel generalled Pharaoh—and which triumphed? We know. ‘The Lord saved Israel that day… the depths covered them; they sank to the bottom as a stone.’ The right hand of the Lord dashed in pieces the enemy!”

Moore, with a kind of cold enjoyment, answered, “You are all right—only you forget the true parallel. France is Israel, and Napoleon is Moses. Europe—with her old overgorged empires and rotten dynasties—is corrupt Egypt. Gallant France is the Twelve Tribes, and her fresh vigorous Usurper the Shepherd of Horeb.”

“I scorn to answer you,” said Helstone, and set his jaw as if that were an act of piety.

Moore, as though talking partly to himself now, let his voice fall. “Oh—in Italy he was as great as any Moses. Fit to head and organize measures for the regeneration of nations. It puzzles me still how the conqueror of Lodi condescended to become an emperor—a vulgar, stupid humbug; and still more how a people that once called itself republican sank again to the grade of mere slaves. I despise France! If England had marched as far on the road of civilization as France did, she would hardly have retreated so shamelessly.”

“You don’t mean to say that besotted imperial France is worse than bloody republican France?” demanded Helstone.

“I mean to say nothing,” Moore returned, with that air of keeping the knife in while smiling, “but I can think what I please—about France and England; revolutions, regicides, restorations; the divine right of kings you stickle for in sermons; the duty of non-resistance; the sanity of war—and—”

His sentence was cut clean through, not by argument but by wheels. A gig came rolling up fast, lamp glimmering; it stopped suddenly in the middle of the road. Moore and Helstone, absorbed in their quarrel, had scarcely marked its approach.

“Nah, maister; did th’ wagons hit home?” demanded a voice from the vehicle—broad, keen, with a relish in it, as if the speaker tasted every word before he let it out.

“Can that be Joe Scott?” Moore exclaimed.

“Ay, ay!” answered another voice. The gig held two persons, seen dimly by its own light. The lantern-bearers had dropped back; the mounted men had outpaced their little company.

“Ay, Mr. Moore, it’s Joe Scott,” said the first voice again; and now its owner leaned forward, showing a sharp-featured man, vigorous, alert, his expression half humorous, half combative. “I’m bringing him back to you in a bonny pickle. I fand him on top o’ th’ moor yonder—him and three others. What will you give me for restoring him to you?”

“Why, my thanks, I believe,” Moore replied; “for I could better afford to lose a better man. That is you, I suppose, Mr. Yorke, by your voice?”

“Ay, lad, it’s me. Coming home from Stilbro’ market; and just as I got to th’ middle o’ th’ moor, and was whipping on as swift as wind—(for these, they say, are not safe times, thanks to a bad government!)—I heard a groan. I pulled up. Some would ha’ whipt on faster; but I’ve naught to fear that I know on. I don’t believe there’s a lad i’ these parts would harm me—leastways, I’d give ’em as good as I got if they tried. I says, ‘Is there aught wrong anywhere?’ ‘Deed is there,’ says somebody, speaking out o’ th’ ground, like. ‘What’s to do? Be sharp and tell me,’ I ordered. ‘Nobbut four on us ligging i’ a ditch,’ says Joe, as quiet as could be. I telled ’em more shame to ’em, and bid ’em get up and move on, or I’d lend ’em a lick o’ th’ gig-whip; for my notion was they were all fresh. ‘We’d ha’ done that an hour sin’, but we’re teed wi’ a bit o’ band,’ says Joe. So i’ a while I got down and loosed ’em wi’ my penknife; and Scott would ride wi’ me, to tell me how it happened; and t’ others are coming on as fast as their feet will bring ’em.”

“Well, I am greatly obliged to you, Mr. Yorke,” Moore said.

“Are you, my lad? You know you’re not.” The man’s tone made the rebuke almost friendly. “However, here are the rest approaching. And here, by th’ Lord, is another set with lights in their pitchers, like Gideon’s army; and as we’ve th’ parson wi’ us—good-evening, Mr. Helstone—we’se do.”

Helstone returned the salutation stiffly, as though politeness were a coin he hated to part with.

Mr. Yorke ran on, warming to his own picture. “We’re eleven strong men, and there’s both horses and chariots amang us. If we could only fall in wi’ some o’ these starved ragamuffins o’ frame-breakers we could win a grand victory. We could iv’ry one be a Wellington—that would please ye, Mr. Helstone—and sich paragraphs as we could contrive for t’ papers! Briarfield suld be famous. But we’se hev a column and a half i’ th’ Stilbro’ Courier ower this job, as it is, I dare say. I’se expect no less.”

“And I’ll promise you no less, Mr. Yorke,” returned the rector, the war in him suddenly turning journalist with relish, “for I’ll write the article myself.”

“To be sure—sartainly! And mind ye recommend weel that them ’at brake t’ bits o’ frames, and teed Joe Scott’s legs wi’ band, suld be hung without benefit o’ clergy. It’s a hanging matter—or suld be. No doubt o’ that.”

“If I judged them I’d give them short shrift!” cried Moore.

“But I mean to let them quite alone this bout,” he added, cooling instantly, “to give them rope enough—certain that in the end they will hang themselves.”

“Let them alone, will ye, Moore? Do you promise that?”

“Promise? No. All I mean is, I shall give myself no particular trouble to catch them; but if one falls in my way—”

“You’ll snap him up, of course,” said Yorke. “Only you’d rather they did summat worse than merely stop a wagon afore you reckon with ’em. Well—we’ll say no more on’t at present. Here we are at my door, gentlemen, and I hope you and the men will step in. None o’ ye will be the worse o’ a little refreshment.”

Moore and Helstone objected—unnecessary, they said; they must return. But the night, with its thin, penetrating rain, pressed persuasion on the skin; and the house before which they halted shone through muslin-curtained windows with that particular domestic invitation which seems to promise not splendour but warmth, not ceremony but shelter. At last they yielded.

Mr. Yorke alighted, left his gig to a man who came out from an outbuilding, and led the way in. It was soon plain that his speech was like a cupboard with two sets of crockery: now broad Yorkshire, now pure English; his manner also shifted—polite and affable one moment, blunt and rough the next—so that you could not at once label his station by his accents.

He sent the men round by the kitchen way, saying he would “see them served wi’ summat to taste presently.” The gentlemen were admitted by the front.

They entered a matted hall lined almost to the ceiling with pictures. It had that smell old houses keep—waxed wood, woollen matting warmed, a faint tang of smoke long since settled into plaster—overlaid now by the more immediate aroma of a great fire drawing well. From the hall they were shown into a large parlour where a broad blaze in the grate made the wet clothes steam gently, and made every raindrop on hat-brims and sleeves shine before it dried. The room was cheerful in a way that went deeper than light. There was no gaudy splendour; there was taste—uncommon taste—the taste of a travelled man who had looked at beauty without buying mere show.

Italian views covered the walls: true art, not bright daubs. Even by candle-light the clear skies opened, the soft distances receded with blue air trembling between eye and hill, the lights and shadows sat in their places like well-ordered flavours in a dish—each distinct, none crude. The subjects were pastoral, sunny, as if this Yorkshire room insisted on remembering warmth. A guitar lay with some music on a sofa; cameos and delicate miniatures hinted at hands that had chosen, not snatched. On the mantelpiece stood vases of a Grecian look; and books—well arranged—in two elegant cases. There was, too, a quiet in the room that belonged to it: the fire’s low, steady roar; the soft tick of some clock not obtrusive; outside, rain still whispering at the panes. It was an acoustic refuge, a pocket of ordered sound set against the harsher world of mills and mobs—one could feel the mind ease in such a place, as the body eases when it smells bread rather than oil and iron.

Mr. Yorke offered chairs, then rang for wine. To the servant who brought it he gave hearty orders for refreshment to be carried to the men in the kitchen. Helstone remained standing; he looked as if he disliked his quarters. When the decanter was pressed towards him, he refused, and would not touch it.

“E’en as you will,” observed Yorke. “I reckon you’re thinking o’ Eastern customs, Mr. Helstone, and you’ll not eat nor drink under my roof, feared we suld be forced to be friends; but I’m not so particular nor superstitious. You might sup that decanter, and you might give me a bottle o’ th’ best in your own cellar, and I’d hold myself free to oppose you at every turn still—in every vestry-meeting and justice-meeting where we meet.”

“It is just what I should expect of you, Mr. Yorke,” Helstone returned, coldly.

“Does it agree wi’ ye, now, Mr. Helstone, to be riding out after rioters o’ a wet night, at your age?”

“It always agrees with me to be doing my duty; and in this case my duty is a thorough pleasure. To hunt down vermin is a noble occupation, fit for an archbishop.”

“Fit for ye, at ony rate.” Yorke’s eyes glinted. “But where’s t’ curate? He’s happen gone to visit some poor body i’ a sick gird—or he’s happen hunting down vermin i’ another direction.”

“He is doing garrison-duty at Hollow’s Mill.”

“You left him a sup o’ wine, I hope, Bob,” Yorke said, turning briskly on Moore, “to keep his courage up?”

He did not wait for an answer. His glance had fixed meanwhile upon the old-fashioned chair by the fireside, the one with carved arms and a three-cornered back, where Moore, pleased to be warm, had flung himself with lazy assurance.

“Move it, Robert! Get up, my lad! That place is mine. Take the sofa, or three other chairs, if you will—but not this. It belangs to me, and nob’dy else.”

Moore rose at once—he obeyed, but with a look that said he noted the command and stored it. “Why are you so particular to that chair, Mr. Yorke?”

“My father war afore me, and that’s all t’ answer I sall gie thee; and it’s as good a reason as Mr. Helstone can give for th’ main feck o’ his notions.”

Helstone, who had been watching as if he mistrusted the whole hospitality, broke in impatiently. “Moore, are you ready to go?”

“Nay; Robert’s not ready,” Yorke said, with quick possession, “or rather, I’m not ready to part wi’ him. He’s an ill lad, and wants correcting.”

“Why, sir? What have I done?” Moore asked, and there was a sharpness under the smoothness—as if the warmth of the hearth could not wholly thaw the chill he carried.

“Made thyself enemies on every hand.”

“What do I care for that? What difference does it make to me whether your Yorkshire louts hate me or like me?”

“Ay—there it is.” Yorke leaned back in his chair as if it were a pulpit of his own. “The lad is a mak’ of an alien amang us. His father would never ha’ talked i’ that way. Go back to Antwerp, where you were born and bred, mauvaise tête!”

“Mauvaise tête vous-même; je ne fais que mon devoir; quant à vos lourdauds de paysans, je m’en moque!” Moore shot back; the French came out of him like a native taste, sudden and strong, as if English were sometimes too blunt a vessel for certain bitterness.

“En revanche, mon garçon, nos lourdauds de paysans se moqueront de toi; sois-en certain,” Yorke replied, answering in French with an accent nearly as pure as Moore’s own, as though he too had kept languages stored like preserves, each with its own scent and sting.

“C’est bon! c’est bon! Et puisque cela m’est égal, que mes amis ne s’en inquiètent pas.”

“Tes amis! Où sont-ils, tes amis?”

“Je fais écho, où sont-ils? et je suis fort aise que l’écho seul y répond. Au diable les amis! Je me souviens encore du moment où mon père et mes oncles Gérard appelèrent autour d’eux leurs amis, et Dieu sait si les amis se sont empressés d’accourir à leur secours! Tenez, M. Yorke, ce mot, ami, m’irrite trop; ne m’en parlez plus.”

“Comme tu voudras,” said Yorke; and he held his peace.

He sat leaning back in his carved oak chair, his face lit by fire and candle in alternating strokes. Moore, warmed outside and dark within, stared into the blaze as if it were a thing he might one day master or be mastered by. Helstone stood rigid, as though even comfort were a compromise. And in that room—so full of chosen images and guarded quiet—the very shift of tongues seemed to show how words can belong like land, how certain syllables open the old cellars of memory while others shut them, how one man’s “friend” can be another man’s wound.

A Yorkshire gentleman he was, in the very marrow and mettle of the word: five-and-fifty by the register, but with hair gone wholly silver, as if the first frosts had taken a fancy to his head and would never leave it. His brow was broad rather than lofty; his cheeks had the wholesome ruddiness of a man used to weather and to his own will; the north showed itself in the set of his mouth and in the downright, rough-edged sound of his voice. There was no courtly curve in his countenance—nothing Norman, nothing softened for drawing-room approval; it was an English face as a crust is English: plain, browned, honest in substance, and likely to cut a delicate palate if you bit too rashly.

Fine folk might have called it vulgar. People of sense would have owned it expressive. Those who liked wit with teeth would have watched it with relish; for pith, sagacity, and a rude originality lay in every furrow, as plainly as the grain lies in seasoned oak. Yet it was an indocile face—scornful and sarcastic—the face of a man you might reason with for an hour and never once move by an inch, and whom to drive were as hopeful as trying to herd a hawk.

His figure matched it: rather tall, wiry, well-knit, and with a kind of grave self-possession in his bearing—no clown’s slouch anywhere. It was not easy to set down Mr. Yorke’s person; it was harder to set down his mind. If any one looks here for a pattern of benevolence, a soft philanthropist with hands for alms and words for everybody, they will be disappointed. He had spoken with some sound sense, even with some honest feeling, to Mr. Moore; but that was no pledge he always spoke justly, or kindly, or even consistently.

There was in him, first, a notable want of reverence—an emptiness where some men carry their kneeling-place. It did not make him an atheist; it made him intolerant. Kings and lords, priests and parliaments, dynasties and establishments—all that high scaffolding of the world—were to him, in the lump, rubbish fit for the midden. He could not taste the keen, clean pleasure other spirits find in admiring what is admirable; that fountain was dry in him, and with it went a hundred little refreshings. He believed in God and heaven; but his belief had neither the tremor of awe nor the hush of tenderness: the great words stood to him as facts stand to a hard, daylight mind, not as mysteries that move the heart.

Next, he had scant faculty for putting himself in another man’s place. He could talk, in broad doctrine, of toleration; then turn and speak with a fierce and unjust antipathy of “parsons,” and all that cleaved to parsons; of “lords,” and their appendages. He did not compare their faults with their temptations, their defects with their disadvantages; he did not imagine what he might have been, wrapped in their circumstances. Thus, while he cried for freedom and equality, there was pride at his core—pride that bowed to no one the world named superior, because he named no one so. Revolt was in his blood; he could no more endure control than a keen fire can endure water: his father had been the same; his grandfather before him; and his children after him, one might foretell, would not learn compliance.

And because his general benevolence was not abundant, he had no soft check on his sarcasm. He was impatient of dulness and weakness; when a thing grated on his strong sense, he struck at it as a man strikes a wasp. Sometimes he would wound, and wound again, not because he delighted in cruelty, but because he scarcely noticed pain when it was not his own.

As for what people call ideality—poetic imagination—he had little of it, and accounted the lack no misfortune. He could relish a good picture, and he had an ear for music that made him tolerant, even encouraging, towards painters and musicians, because their art came to him through the senses. But the quiet poet, however much fire burned under his calm, if he could not have made himself useful in a counting-house, or have shouted bids in a cloth-hall, might have lived and died despised in Hiram Yorke’s neighbourhood. Mr. Yorke did not suspect there are minds whose feast is spread in solitude, and whose bread is baked in ovens no mill-owner ever heated; that some men live, as it were, by a diet of wind and light, and are not starved by it. These were not his reflections. He dealt in what he could handle, and what he could taste.

Yet his faults were not the whole man—no more than the smoke is the whole hearth. In Yorkshire he was accounted one of the most capable and honourable; even those who disliked him were forced into respect. The poor loved him, for he was fatherly to them, and kindness sat on him without condescension. To his workpeople he was cordial and considerate; when he dismissed a man from one employment, he would strive to set him to another, or, failing that, help him remove—wife, children, and scant goods—to a place where bread might be earned. And if among his “hands” any began to show the first green spike of insubordination, Yorke—who hated being ruled, yet knew perfectly how to rule—had a rough husbandry of authority: he crushed rebellion in the germ, as he would trample a weed before it seeded.

Because his own affairs were so ordered, he took a certain fierce license in censuring those differently placed, laying their discomfort wholly to their own mismanagement; severing himself from the masters when it suited him, and speaking, with grim freedom, for the operatives.

His family was old in the district; he was not the richest, but among the most influential. His schooling had been good; in youth—before the French Revolution turned travel to a peril—he had been abroad. He had mastered French and Italian; he had spent two years in Italy and gathered paintings and rare, tasteful things which now gave his house an air of seasoned foreignness, as if sun had once lain long on its walls. When he chose, his manners were those of an old-school gentleman—finished, even courtly; his talk, when he cared to please, was singularly interesting, original, and full of sharp turns. But commonly he spoke the Yorkshire dialect, not from ignorance, but from preference: he clung to his native speech as a man clings to the bread he was raised on—coarse to a stranger, but nourishing, and sweet with memory. A Yorkshire burr, he maintained, was as much better than a cockney lisp as a bull’s bellow was than a rat’s squeak.

He knew everybody and was known for miles; yet his intimacies were few. Being thoroughly original, he had little relish for what was merely polished and ordinary. A racy, rough character—high or low—found welcome with him; a refined, vapid personage, however exalted, was his aversion. He would give an hour freely to a shrewd workman, or to some queer, sagacious old woman among his cottagers—one of those elders who keep a parish’s memory in their mouths, with old phrases salted down like winter meat—when he would grudge a moment to a commonplace fine gentleman or to the most elegant, fashionable lady if she were frivolous. In this he went too far, forgetting that amiable souls may live under smooth surfaces. Still, he made exceptions. There was a sort of plain, artless mind—unrefined, almost without intellectual ambition, incapable of valuing what was intellectual in him, yet not squeamish at his rudeness, not easily wounded by his sarcasm, not given to analyzing him—in whose company he was peculiarly at ease. With such people he was lord. They yielded to his influence without ever naming it, because they never reflected on it; and their easy insensibility was convenient to him—acceptable, in its way, as the chair he sat in or the floor he trod.

It had already been seen he was not quite uncordial with Mr. Moore. He had reasons for a faint partiality which might seem whimsical. First, Moore spoke English with a foreign accent, and French with perfect purity; and his dark, thin face, with its fine but worn lines, looked anti-British, anti-Yorkshire. The points were slight; yet they stirred old associations, like the first whiff of a spice you had not smelled since youth. They carried Yorke back to travel—to Italian streets and Parisian cafés, where voices had rung with that very cadence. He had been young then; and in listening to the alien tone, he seemed, for a moment, to taste youth again.

Secondly, he had known Moore’s father, and had had dealings with him—an old business tie not wholly agreeable, since Yorke’s firm had been in some measure implicated in that firm’s losses.

Thirdly, Robert himself he found sharp in trade. Yorke foresaw he would, by one means or another, make money; and he respected resolution and acuteness—perhaps even hardness. A fourth bond lay in circumstance: Yorke was one of the guardians of the minor on whose estate Hollow’s Mill stood; therefore Moore, in his alterations and improvements, had frequent occasion to consult him.

As to the other guest now present in Mr. Yorke’s parlour—Mr. Helstone—between host and clergyman there ran a double antipathy: of nature and of circumstance. The free-thinker hated the formalist; the lover of liberty detested the disciplinarian. Besides, it was said that in former years they had been rival suitors for the same lady.

Mr. Yorke, when young, had been known for liking sprightly, dashing women—showy carriage, lively wit, a ready tongue. He never offered himself to these brilliant belles whose company he sought; and then, all at once, he fell gravely and earnestly in love with a girl who was their opposite—a girl with the face of a Madonna; living marble; stillness made visible. It mattered not that when he spoke she answered in monosyllables; that his sighs seemed unheard; that his glances were unreturned; that she seldom smiled at his jests, and appeared to pay him no respect and no attention. For him Mary Cave was perfect, because—no doubt for a reason he could have defended with that stubborn logic of his—he loved her.

Mr. Helstone, at that time curate of Briarfield, loved Mary too—or fancied he did. Others admired her, for she was beautiful as a monumental angel; but the clergyman was preferred for his office’ sake—that office lending, perhaps, a consecrated glamour to the plain commission of matrimony, such as the young wool-staplers who courted her could not supply. Mr. Helstone neither had, nor professed to have, Yorke’s absorbing passion. He saw her more as she was than the rest did; therefore he was more master of himself, and in some measure of her. She accepted him at the first offer, and they were married.

Nature never meant Mr. Helstone to make a very good husband—least of all to a quiet wife. So long as a woman was silent, he assumed she was well and wanted nothing. If she did not complain of solitude, solitude, however prolonged, could not be irksome. If she did not talk, did not put herself forward with likes and dislikes, then she had none worth consulting. He made no pretence of comprehending women; to him they were a different order, probably inferior. A wife, in his creed, could not be a companion, much less a confidante, much less a stay. His wife, after a year or two, was of no great importance to him in any shape; and when she one day—as he thought, suddenly, for he had scarcely noticed her decline—took her leave of him and of life, leaving in the conjugal bed only a still, beautiful-featured mould of clay, cold and white, he felt his bereavement—who shall say how little? Yet perhaps more than he showed; for he was not a man whom grief could easily wring into tears.

His dry-eyed, sober mourning scandalized an old housekeeper and a woman who had waited on Mrs. Helstone in her sickness—one who had likely learned more of the dead lady’s capacity to feel and love than her husband ever guessed. They whispered together by the corpse, traded anecdotes, heightened the story of her lingering decline and its cause, until their pity turned to indignation against the austere little man in the adjoining room, sitting over papers, unconscious of the blame gathering like soot in a chimney.

Mrs. Helstone was scarcely under the sod before rumours ran through the neighbourhood that she had died of a broken heart. These swelled quickly into tales of hard usage, and at last into detailed charges of harsh treatment—grossly untrue, yet greedily received. Mr. Yorke heard them and partly believed. He had, of course, no friendly feeling for the rival who had won Mary Cave. Though now himself married, to a woman unlike Mary in every visible respect, he could not forget the great disappointment of his life; and when he heard that what would have been so precious to him had been neglected—perhaps abused—by another, he conceived against that other a rooted, bitter animosity.

Of the nature and strength of this animosity Mr. Helstone was but half aware. He did not know how much Yorke had loved Mary Cave; what he had suffered at losing her; nor was he conscious of the calumnies regarding his married life—familiar to every ear in the district but his own. He believed political and religious differences alone divided him from Mr. Yorke. Had he known the true case, no persuasion would likely have brought him across his former rival’s threshold.

In the parlour that evening, Mr. Yorke did not resume his lecture to Robert Moore. The talk, after a pause, began again in a more general strain, though still disputative. The country was unquiet; the late depredations on mill-property offered fuel enough, and the three men differed in nearly every view they held. Even the room’s quiet—its sheltered firelight, the thick walls that kept out the worst of the wind—could not wholly soften the harsh undertone of the time; through the pauses in speech, one could almost fancy the outer world listening: lanes that carried sound too far at night, distant wheels, the restless stir of a district where men lay awake with empty stomachs and full anger. It was as if the very air had learned a new, uneasy music—no birdsong, no brook’s patient talk, but the clang and grind of industry and want.

Mr. Helstone held the masters aggrieved and the workpeople unreasonable. He condemned, broadly and sternly, the spirit of disaffection against constituted authorities, and the growing indisposition to bear with patience evils he regarded as inevitable. His remedies were those of a man trained to discipline: vigorous government interference, strict magisterial vigilance; when needful, prompt military coercion.

Mr. Yorke demanded to know whether interference, vigilance, and coercion would feed the hungry and give work to those who wanted it and whom no man would hire. He scoffed at “inevitable evils.” Public patience, he said, was a camel whose back had already taken the last atom it could bear, and resistance was now a duty. The widespread disaffection against authorities he counted the most promising sign of the times. The masters, he allowed, were truly aggrieved; but their main grievances had been heaped on them by a “corrupt, base, and bloody” government. Madmen like Pitt, demons like Castlereagh, mischievous idiots like Perceval—these, in his phrase, were the tyrants and curses of the country, destroyers of trade. Their infatuated perseverance in an unjustifiable, hopeless, ruinous war had brought the nation to its pass. Their oppressive taxation, their infamous Orders in Council—whose authors, he swore, deserved impeachment and the scaffold—hung a millstone round England’s neck.
