“But where’s t’ use o’ talkin’?” he broke out. “What chance is there o’ reason bein’ heard i’ a land that’s king-ridden, priest-ridden, peer-ridden?—where a lunatic’s t’ nominal monarch, an unprincipled debauchee t’ real ruler; where such an insult to common sense as hereditary legislators is tolerated; where such a humbug as a bench o’ bishops, such an arrogant abuse as a pampered, persecutin’ established church is endured and venerated; where a standin’ army’s maintained, and a host o’ lazy parsons and their pauper families is kept on t’ fat o’ t’ land?”

Mr. Helstone rose, and set on his shovel-hat with a composure that was itself a rebuke. He replied that in the course of his life he had met two or three men who maintained such sentiments bravely so long as health, strength, and worldly prosperity were their allies; but there came a time, he said, to all men “when the keepers of the house should tremble; when they should be afraid of that which is high, and fear should be in the way.” That time was the test of the advocate of anarchy and rebellion, the enemy of religion and order. He affirmed he had been called to read the church’s prayers for the sick at the miserable dying-bed of one of her most rancorous foes; he had seen such a one stricken with remorse, seeking a place for repentance and unable to find it, though he sought it carefully with tears. He warned Mr. Yorke that blasphemy against God and the king was a deadly sin, and that there was such a thing as judgment to come.

Mr. Yorke answered that he believed fully there was such a thing as judgment to come. If it were otherwise, it would be hard to conceive how all the scoundrels triumphant in this world—men who broke innocent hearts with impunity, abused unmerited privilege, were a scandal to honourable callings, took bread out of poor mouths, browbeat the humble, and truckled to the rich and proud—were to be properly paid in the coin they had earned. “But,” he added, “whenever I get low-spirited about such-like goin’s-on, an’ their seemin’ success i’ this mucky lump o’ a planet, I just reach down t’ owd book,”—and he pointed to a great Bible in the case—“open it like at a chance, and I’m sure to light o’ a verse blazin’ wi’ a blue brimstone low that sets all straight. I know where some folk’s bound for, just as weel as if an angel wi’ great white wings had come in ower t’ door-stone an’ told me.”

“Sir,” said Mr. Helstone, gathering all his dignity, “the great knowledge of man is to know himself, and the bourne whither his own steps tend.”

“Ay, ay. You’ll recollect, Mr. Helstone, that Ignorance was carried away from the very gates o’ heaven—borne through t’ air, an’ thrust in at a door i’ t’ side o’ t’ hill which led down to hell.”

“Nor have I forgotten, Mr. Yorke, that Vain-Confidence, not seeing the way before him, fell into a deep pit which was on purpose there made by the prince of the grounds, to catch vainglorious fools withal, and was dashed to pieces with his fall.”

Mr. Moore, who had sat silent through this hard encounter, amused rather than moved—his indifference alike to party politics and to neighbourhood tale making him an impartial, if somewhat cold, judge—leaned forward at last and interposed.

“Now,” said he, “you have both sufficiently blackballed each other, and proved how cordially you detest each other, and how wicked you think each other. For my part, my hate is still running in such a strong current against the fellows who have broken my frames that I have none to spare for my private acquaintance, and still less for such a vague thing as a sect or a government. But really, gentlemen, you both seem very bad by your own showing—worse than ever I suspected you to be. I dare not stay all night with a rebel and blasphemer like you, Yorke; and I hardly dare ride home with a cruel and tyrannical ecclesiastic like Mr. Helstone.”

Moore’s spirits held their ground with the stubbornness of a well-fed hearth-fire. He rose before dawn in the mill, where he and Joe Scott had made shift to sleep in the counting-houses—places meant for sums and ledgers, not for breathing men; yet they had contrived beds from benches, coats, and the thin charity of folded sacking.

The master was first astir. He washed in cold water that bit like raw spirit; he set his hair with brisk fingers; and, to rouse Joe, he sang—light, French, irreverent—so that the tune ran along the empty passages and ticked against doors and iron like a spoon on a pan.

“Ye’re not custen dahn, then, maister?” Joe called, hauling himself upright.

“Not a stiver, mon garçon—which means, my lad. Up with you. We’ll walk through the mill before the hands come in, and I’ll explain my plans. We’ll have the machinery yet, Joseph. You never heard of Bruce, perhaps?”

“And th’ arrand—spider? Ay, I hev. I’ve read th’ history o’ Scotland, and happen knaw as mich on’t as ye; and I understand ye to mean to say ye’ll persevere.”

“I do.”

“Is there mony o’ your mak’ i’ your country?” Joe asked, folding away the makeshift bedding with the neatness of a mechanic who can’t bear waste or disorder.

“In my country! Which is my country?”

“Why, France—isn’t it?”

“Not it, indeed! The circumstance of the French having seized Antwerp, where I was born, does not make me a Frenchman.”

“Holland, then?”

“I am not a Dutchman. Now you are confounding Antwerp with Amsterdam.”

“Flanders?”

“I scorn the insinuation, Joe! I a Flamand! Have I a Flemish face—the clumsy nose standing out, the mean forehead falling back, the pale blue eyes à fleur de tête? Am I all body and no legs, like a Flamand? But you don’t know what they are like, those Netherlanders. Joe, I’m an Anversois. My mother was an Anversoise, though she came of French lineage, which is the reason I speak French.”

“But your father war Yorkshire, which maks ye a bit Yorkshire too; and onybody may see ye’re akin to us, ye’re so keen o’ making brass, and getting forrards.”

“Joe, you’re an impudent dog; but I’ve always been accustomed to a boorish sort of insolence from my youth up. The classe ouvrière—that is, the working people in Belgium—bear themselves brutally towards their employers; and by brutally, Joe, I mean brutalement—which, perhaps, when properly translated, should be roughly.”

“We allus speak our minds i’ this country; and them young parsons and grand folk fro’ London is shocked at wer incivility; and we like weel enough to gi’e ’em summat to be shocked at, ’cause it’s sport to us to watch ’em turn up the whites o’ their een, and spreed out their bits o’ hands, like as they’re flayed wi’ bogards—”

“You are savages, Joe. You don’t suppose you’re civilized, do you?”

“Middling, middling, maister. I reckon ’at us manufacturing lads i’ th’ north is a deal more intelligent, and knaws a deal more nor th’ farming folk i’ th’ south. Trade sharpens wer wits; and them that’s mechanics like me is forced to think. Ye know, what wi’ looking after machinery and sich like, I’ve getten into that way that when I see an effect, I look straight out for a cause—”

“You think yourself a clever fellow, I know, Scott.”

“Ay! I’m fairish. I can tell cheese fro’ chalk—”

“And because you’ve picked up a little practical mathematics, and found some scantling of chemistry at the bottom of a dyeing-vat, you fancy yourself a neglected man of science,” Moore cut in, walking as he spoke, boots striking the flagged floor with a firm, spare sound. “And you needn’t suppose that because trade doesn’t always run smooth, and you and such as you are sometimes short of work and bread, therefore your class are martyrs, and the whole form of government wrong. And you needn’t insinuate that virtue lives only under thatch. Human nature is human nature everywhere—under tile or straw—vice and virtue blended in every sample that breathes. I’ve seen villains rich, and villains poor, and villains comfortably in between. Hark—the clock. It will strike six. Away with you, Joe, and ring the mill bell.”

It was mid-February; at six, the night was only beginning to thin. The dawn did not blush; it did not even properly wake. It stole in like a pale sick child, laying a colourless hand on the hills. Rain had ceased, yet the world still dripped with it: sodden earth, brimmed pools, rivulets quick with yesterday’s spate. A raw wind shifted the long ceiling of cloud and showed, low round the horizon, not blue, but a whiter vapour—like steam that will not clear.

The mill bell rang out—loud, metallic, and unkind in the wet air. Its note belonged to iron and industry; it cut through the softer sounds—the drain’s gurgle, the hedge’s drip, the distant call of a cart—till the valley seemed to hold its breath between each clang. That sound governed bodies and hours here; it made children run.

And they did come—small figures in shawls and thin clogs, hurrying as though speed could warm them. The lane and yard filled with footfall and quick voices, the wet slap of soles, the faint cough, the squeak of a door. If the morning pinched, they had known worse—snow-storm and hard frost, rain that struck like thrown gravel. Perhaps, by contrast, this raw grey did not seem the harshest.

Moore stood at the entrance and watched them pass. He counted them—one, two, three—measuring his power and responsibility with the same eye he used on bales and bobbins. To late comers he spoke a sharp word; Joe repeated it sharper in the work-room. Yet there was no taste for cruelty in either man. They fined a delinquent who came very late—Moore held out his hand for the penny before the boy crossed the threshold, and said plainly that next time would be twopence.

Rules are necessary, and in some hands they become instruments like whips; but this master and this overlooker did not strike children. Joe, it was true, had once flogged his own lad—hard—for lying and holding to it; but in the mill there were no blows. Their rigour was cold, not feral: the chill of a system, not the heat of a tyrant.

Moore stayed among his engines, his yard, his dye-house, his warehouse, until the faint dawn strengthened into day. At last the sun rose—if one could call it so. A white disc, tintless as ice, peeped over a dark hill-crest and silvered the livid rim of cloud. It looked down, solemn and unpitying, along the whole narrow dale—the den—where the mill crouched and clattered. By eight the mill-lights were out. The signal for breakfast sounded; children were released for half an hour and fell upon their little tin cans of coffee and their small baskets of bread. Let us hope there was enough: the smell of coffee—thin, sweetened perhaps with a grudged spoon—rose like a comfort, and the sound of eager eating was softer than the machines.

Only then did Moore leave the yard and turn to his house. It lay near, yet the lane to it, banked high and hedged thick, gave a sense of retreat, as though comfort had tried to hide itself from the factory’s harsh music. The dwelling was a small whitewashed place with a green porch. In the garden the brown stalks stood like spent skewers in black soil—dead now, but promising trained creepers in summer. The grass plat was bleached, not green; the borders were mostly mould, save where, in sheltered nooks, snowdrops and crocuses pushed up—emerald points piercing the damp. Winter had been long. The last deep snow had scarcely melted before yesterday’s rains; on the hills, remnants still gleamed, white flecks in hollows, pale crowns on peaks. Three trees, not lofty but well grouped, rose beside the cottage and gave it an air of mild dignity.

It was a snug nest, made for content and thought; but Moore, built for action and gain, did not enter at once. He fetched a spade from a little shed and began to dig. For a quarter hour he worked steadily, turning the heavy earth. The spade’s bite and lift made a homely rhythm that answered, at last, to something other than the mill bell: wet soil falling back with a thick hush; breath drawn in the cold; a blackbird’s single note from the hedge.

A window opened above. A woman’s voice, quick and edged, called down in French.

“Eh, bien! Tu ne déjeûnes pas ce matin?”

“Is breakfast ready, Hortense?”

“Certainly; it has been ready half an hour.”

“Then I am ready too. I have a canine hunger.”

He dropped the spade and went in.

A narrow passage led to a small parlour. On the table waited coffee, bread and butter, and—un-English yet intimate to him—stewed pears, their syrup giving off a gentle perfume, like fruit preserved against hard seasons. The coffee’s scent was darker, roasted; it threaded the room, warm as a shawl.

Over these viands presided Hortense Moore, his sister. She seemed a little older than he—perhaps five years. Tall and stout in proportion, she had very black hair—now twisted up in curl-papers—high colour in her cheeks, a small nose, and little black eyes that looked as if they could flash indignation at a crumb out of place. The lower part of her face was large; her forehead small and corrugated; her expression fretful rather than ill-natured, provoking and yet—strangely—almost comic. Strangest to English eyes was her morning dress: a stuff petticoat and a striped cotton camisole, the petticoat short enough to show feet and ankles that were sturdy rather than shapely.

She was no slattern. Hortense was orderly, economical, and proud of both qualities. This was the dress in which, in her own country, she had always “gone her household ways,” and she did not mean to shift her habits as one shifts a bonnet, merely because she must live among English people. She believed steadfastly there was merit in keeping to old modes, as there is merit in keeping a mother-tongue alive.

She had an excellent opinion of herself—an opinion not wholly undeserved, for she possessed good, sterling qualities—but she overvalued them, and forgot to reckon the defects that travelled in their shadow. She would never have owned herself prejudiced, narrow, too touchy about dignity, too ready to take offence at trifles—yet she was all these. Still, when her claims were not crossed and her pride not pricked, she could be kind enough.

To her brothers she was devoted. As the remaining representatives of a decayed family, both were almost sacred in her eyes. Of Louis she knew less: he had been sent to England a boy, educated at an English school, and, not being shaped for trade, had taken the hard, modest course of teaching—usher once, and now, people said, tutor in a private family. Hortense spoke of Louis as having “des moyens,” but being too quiet. Of Robert she spoke with unqualified pride: he was, to her, the greatest man in Europe. Opposition to him—or to herself—was, in her mind, irrational, monstrous, infamous.

No sooner had Robert seated himself than she helped him to pears and cut him a thick tartine—bread spread in the Belgian way, not daintily, but as if butter were a necessary virtue—then poured out her horror over last night’s work: the destruction of the frames.

“What an idea! To destroy them. What a shameful act! One sees well that the workmen of this country are at once stupid and wicked. It is exactly like the English servants, the maids especially: nothing so insupportable as that Sara, for example!”

“She looks clean and industrious,” Moore remarked, blowing on his coffee.

“Looks! I do not know how she looks, and I do not say she is altogether dirty or idle, but she is insolent. She disputed with me a quarter of an hour yesterday about the cooking of the beef. She said I boiled it to rags, that English people would never be able to eat such a dish as our bouilli; that the bouillon was no better than greasy warm water; and as to the choucroute, she declares she cannot touch it. That barrel we have in the cellar—delightfully prepared by my own hands—she called a tub of hog-wash—food for pigs. I am harassed with the girl, and yet I cannot part with her lest I should get a worse. You are in the same position with your workmen, pauvre cher frère!”

“I am afraid you are not very happy in England, Hortense.”

“It is my duty to be happy where you are, brother; but otherwise there are certainly a thousand things which make me regret our native town. All the world here appears to me ill-bred. I find my habits considered ridiculous. If a girl from your mill comes into the kitchen and finds me in my jupon and camisole preparing dinner—for you know I cannot trust Sarah to cook a single dish—she sneers. If I accept an invitation out to tea—which I have done once or twice—I perceive I am put quite into the background; I have not that attention paid me which is decidedly my due. Of what an excellent family are the Gérards, as we know, and the Moores also! They have a right to claim a certain respect, and to feel wounded when it is withheld. In Antwerp I was always treated with distinction; here, one would think that when I open my lips in company I speak English with a ridiculous accent—whereas I am quite assured I pronounce it perfectly.”

“Hortense, in Antwerp we were known rich; in England we were never known but poor.”

“Precisely, and thus mercenary are mankind. Again, dear brother—last Sunday was very wet, you recollect; accordingly I went to church in my neat black sabots—objects one would not indeed wear in a fashionable city, but which in the country I have ever been accustomed to use for walking in dirty roads. Believe me, as I paced up the aisle, composed and tranquil as I am always, four ladies and as many gentlemen laughed and hid their faces behind their prayer-books.”

“Well, well! don’t put on the sabots again. I told you before I thought they were not quite the thing for this country.”

“But, brother, they are not common sabots, such as the peasantry wear. I tell you, they are black sabots, very clean, very suitable. At Mons and Leuze—cities not very far removed from the elegant capital of Brussels—it is very seldom that respectable people wear anything else for walking in winter. Let any one try to wade the mud of the Flemish chaussées in a pair of Paris brodequins—one would soon hear news of it!”

“Never mind Mons and Leuze and the Flemish chaussées; do at Rome as the Romans do. And as to the camisole and jupon, I’m not quite sure about them either. I never see an English lady dressed in such garments. Ask Caroline Helstone.”

“Caroline! I ask Caroline? I consult her about my dress? It is she who on all points should consult me. She is a child.”

“She is eighteen—or at least seventeen—old enough to know all about gowns, petticoats, and chaussures.”

“Do not spoil Caroline, I entreat you, brother. Do not make her of more consequence than she ought to be. At present she is modest and unassuming: let us keep her so.”

“With all my heart. Is she coming this morning?”

“She will come at ten, as usual, to take her French lesson.”

“You don’t find that she sneers at you, do you?”

“She does not. She appreciates me better than any one else here; but then she has more intimate opportunities of knowing me. She sees that I have education, intelligence, manner, principles—all, in short, which belongs to a person well born and well bred.”

“Are you at all fond of her?”

“For fond I cannot say. I am not one who is prone to take violent fancies, and consequently my friendship is the more to be depended on. I have a regard for her as my relative; her position also inspires interest, and her conduct as my pupil has hitherto been such as rather to enhance than diminish the attachment that springs from other causes.”

“She behaves pretty well at lessons?”

“To me she behaves very well; but you are conscious, brother, that I have a manner calculated to repel over-familiarity, to win esteem, and to command respect. Yet, possessed of penetration, I perceive clearly that Caroline is not perfect, that there is much to be desired in her.”

“Give me a last cup of coffee, and while I am drinking it amuse me with an account of her faults.”

Hortense filled his cup with an air of brisk service—as if feeding him were one of the few pleasures England had not yet soured. The coffee steamed; the pears cooled; outside, the wind pushed at the panes with a thin whistle, and beyond that, faint as a memory, the mill kept up its iron murmuring.

“Dear brother, I am happy to see you eat your breakfast with relish, after the fatiguing night you have passed. Caroline, then, is defective; but with my forming hand and almost motherly care she may improve. There is about her an occasional something—a reserve, I think—which I do not quite like, because it is not sufficiently girlish and submissive; and there are glimpses of an unsettled hurry in her nature, which put me out. Yet she is usually most tranquil, too dejected and thoughtful indeed sometimes. In time, I doubt not, I shall make her uniformly sedate and decorous, without being unaccountably pensive. I ever disapprove what is not intelligible.”

“I don’t understand your account in the least. What do you mean by ‘unsettled hurries,’ for instance?”

“An example will perhaps be most satisfactory. I sometimes make her read French poetry by way of practice in pronunciation. She has gone through much of Corneille and Racine in a very steady, sober spirit, such as I approve. Occasionally she showed, indeed, a degree of languor in the perusal of those esteemed authors, partaking rather of apathy than sobriety; and apathy is what I cannot tolerate in those who have the benefit of my instructions. The other day I put into her hands a volume of short fugitive pieces. I sent her to the window to learn one by heart; and when I looked up I saw her turning the leaves over impatiently, and curling her lip—absolutely with scorn—as she surveyed the little poems. I chid her. ‘Ma cousine,’ said she, ‘tout cela m’ennuie à la mort.’ I told her this was improper language. ‘Dieu!’ she exclaimed, ‘il n’y a donc pas deux lignes de poésie dans toute la littérature française?’ I inquired what she meant. She begged my pardon with proper submission. Ere long she was still. I saw her smiling to herself over the book. She began to learn assiduously. In half an hour she came and stood before me, presented the volume, folded her hands—as I always require—and commenced the repetition of that short thing by Chénier, ‘La Jeune Captive.’ If you had heard the manner in which she went through this, and in which she uttered a few incoherent comments when she had done, you would have known what I mean by the phrase ‘unsettled hurry.’ One would have thought Chénier more moving than all Racine and all Corneille. You, brother, who have so much sagacity, will discern that this disproportionate preference argues an ill-regulated mind; but she is fortunate in her preceptress. I will give her a system, a method of thought, a set of opinions; I will give her the perfect control and guidance of her feelings.”

“Be sure you do, Hortense.” Moore drank. “Here she comes. That was her shadow passed the window, I believe.”

“Ah! truly. She is too early—half an hour before her time.—My child, what brings you here before I have breakfasted?”

Caroline entered then—a young girl wrapped in a winter mantle, its folds gathered with a natural grace about a slender figure. The cold followed her in a moment: a damp breath from outside, the faint scent of wet wool, and, beneath that, something clean and pale as frost—an outdoor smell of lane and hedge.

“I came in haste to see how you were, Hortense, and how Robert was too. I was sure you would be both grieved by what happened last night. I did not hear till this morning. My uncle told me at breakfast.”

“Ah! it is unspeakable,” Hortense returned, gathering herself into injured propriety. “You sympathize with us? Your uncle sympathizes with us?”

“My uncle is very angry—but he was with Robert, I believe, was he not? Did he not go with you to Stilbro’ Moor?”

“Yes,” said Moore. “We set out in very martial style, Caroline; but the prisoners we went to rescue met us half-way.”

“Of course nobody was hurt?”

“Why, no; only Joe Scott’s wrists were a little galled with being pinioned too tightly behind his back.”

“You were not there? You were not with the wagons when they were attacked?”

“No. One seldom has the fortune to be present at occurrences at which one would particularly wish to assist.”

“Where are you going this morning? I saw Murgatroyd saddling your horse in the yard.”

“To Whinbury. It is market day.”

Mademoiselle Moore had that morning a pupil whose thoughts would not keep to their prescribed furrow. Caroline’s eyes wandered from the page as if some other lesson—unwritten, unsanctioned—were being whispered to her from beyond the glass. Yet when Hortense rebuked her, the girl took it with a docility that seemed warmed and sweetened by the very sun slanting in at the window. It lay on her like a benediction: not a grand, churchy thing, but a domestic grace—heat on skin, light on hair—making her quiet face both happier and better.

She was fair, and it was the sort of fairness that did not insist upon being understood before it was liked. Youth had shaped her gently: slight and pliant, every movement neat without effort, every curve in proper measure. Her eyes—brown and clear—had at times a beam that spoke where speech failed, winning not by boldness, but by that soft, untranslatable language some looks carry straight to the affections. Her mouth was pretty, her skin delicate, her brown hair abundant; she arranged it with a taste that made its curls seem less an adornment than a natural right. Her dress, too, had that modest correctness which is its own refinement: not costly, not loud in fashion, but chosen in colour and cut to suit the wearer. To-day she wore winter merino of the same soft brown as her hair; a little collar lay over a pink ribbon, tied in a pink knot; she had no other ornament, and needed none.

Of what lay beneath—character, intellect—time would speak. But her place in this household could be told at once, as one tells the plain ingredients before the cooking begins.

She was the child of parents who had separated soon after her birth, their temperaments having proved a discord that no patience could reconcile. Her mother had been half-sister to Mr. Moore’s father; thus Caroline stood in a distant cousinship to Robert, Louis, and Hortense—no blood mixed close, yet kin enough for duty to have a voice. Her father, brother to Mr. Helstone the rector, was of a kind whose memory friends prefer to leave buried once death has closed the account: a man who made his wife unhappy, and whose true faults lent plausibility to false stories whispered of his better-principled brother. Caroline had not known her mother; she was taken from her in infancy and never saw her again. Her father died while she was still young, and then her uncle, Mr. Helstone, became her guardian.

He was not made—by nature or habit—for the charge of a young girl. He neglected what others would have managed with care; and had Caroline not felt in herself the ache of want—want of knowledge, want of guidance—he might have let her grow up with scarcely more teaching than chance threw in her way. Even with her own efforts, a depressing sense haunted her: that she was behind other girls of her age and station, irregularly taught, inferior. So when Hortense, newly arrived at Hollow’s Mill, offered to teach her French and fine needle-work, Caroline seized the offer eagerly, as one catches at bread when hunger has made pride small.

Mademoiselle delighted in the arrangement—not merely from kindness, though she was capable of that in her fashion, but because it placed her in command. She liked to be important; she liked to rule a docile pupil. She took Caroline precisely at her own estimate: an ignorant English girl, unevenly brought up, to be corrected and formed. When she discovered Caroline learned quickly and with keen eagerness, she attributed it not to the scholar’s mind or application, but to the teacher’s superior method. When she found, too, that Caroline possessed stray knowledge—desultory, varied, gathered who could tell where—Hortense still contrived to believe it had all been gleaned unconsciously from herself, even when Caroline knew much on subjects whereof Hortense knew little. It was not logical; but Hortense’s faith in her own influence was complete.

Proud of having “un esprit positif,” and professing preference for dry studies, Mademoiselle held her cousin close to grammar as if it were virtue itself. She assigned, as the most improving exercise imaginable, interminable analyses logiques. Caroline would have learned French, she thought, without forever being driven through that thorny hedge of propositions principales and incidents; of hunting down the incidente déterminative and the incidente applicative; of deciding whether a sentence was pleine, elliptique, implicite. When she grew lost in the maze—and she did, more than once—she sometimes took advantage of those odd intervals when Hortense, in an unaccountable fervour, would be upstairs rummaging drawers: arranging, disarranging, rearranging, as if domestic order were a puzzle she must solve anew each day. Then Caroline would slip her book away to the counting-house, where Robert sat among ledgers and samples, ink and figures, the industry of the place making its own austere music: the scratch of pen, the soft thud of a book closed, the measured creak of floorboards under a man who kept his step controlled.

Mr. Moore had a clear, tranquil brain. He glanced at her difficulty, and it seemed to melt—two minutes, two words, and the knot was untied. She would stand there with her lesson open, warmed by the strange comfort of his competence, thinking how swiftly she might learn if Hortense taught like him. When she left, she repaid him with an admiring smile that fell, shyly, rather at his feet than lifted to his face. And walking back to the rectory cottage, she would wish—foolishly, fervently—that nature had made her a boy, so she might ask to be his clerk and sit in the counting-house beside him, instead of sitting in the parlour under Hortense’s rule.

Sometimes—rarely—she spent an evening at Hollow’s Cottage. Often Robert was away: at market, at Mr. Yorke’s, or shut up with male visitors in another room. But sometimes he was at home, free, and then those hours passed with a lightness that made the clock seem indulgent. There was no room in England, Caroline thought, so pleasant as that little parlour when the three cousins occupied it. Hortense, when not teaching, scolding, or cooking, could relax into something almost kindly. And there was a sure way to soften her further: persuade her to take her guitar and sing. Then her voice—well-toned, trained—filled the room. It might have been wholly agreeable, had not her formal, self-important nature shaped even her music, as it shaped her manners and stamped her features: the notes came correct, yet with a certain stiffness, as if even melody must be kept respectable.

Robert, when released for an hour from the yoke of business, was not precisely lively; but he could be willing—willing to watch Caroline’s liveliness, to listen while she talked, to answer her questions with patient exactness. It was agreeable merely to sit near him, to hover on the edge of his notice, to address him and look at him. Sometimes he became more than agreeable—almost animated, certainly gentle, friendly.

Yet the next morning would freeze him again. However much he seemed, in his quiet way, to enjoy those evenings, he rarely contrived their recurrence. Caroline, inexperienced, puzzled over it. If happiness lay ready to hand, why not take it often, keep it bright with use, instead of letting it lie aside until it rusted?

Still she never acted on her own thought. She did not go to Hollow’s Cottage unasked. Even when Hortense pressed her to come, Caroline would refuse if Robert did not second the request—or did so only faintly. To-day was different. That morning, for the first time, he had invited her of his own unprompted will; and he had spoken so kindly that the sound of his voice had been like a sustaining draught, keeping her glad through the day’s dull stretches.

The hours moved in their usual manner. Mademoiselle, perpetually breathless with occupation, bustled between kitchen and parlour: scolding Sarah, then bending over Caroline’s exercise, then demanding a repetition lesson. However faultlessly Caroline performed, Hortense never praised. Praise, she held, was beneath a teacher’s dignity; blame—severe or slight—was indispensable to it. Reprimand was the rail that kept authority upright. If there were no fault in the lesson, then there was always something else: the pupil’s carriage, her air, her dress, her mien.

The customary skirmish over dinner took place. When Sarah finally brought the meal in, she set it down with a look that said plainly, I never dished such stuff in my life, and it’s not fit for dogs. Yet the repast was savoury enough in its foreign way. There was soup first: a purée of dried peas, patiently simmered until the hard little beads surrendered into a thick, smooth body, and seasoned so that it smelled of warmth and thrift together. Hortense prepared it amidst lamentations that in this desolate England no haricot beans could be had—no proper beans, as she called them, with the conviction of a food historian defending an ancestral pantry.

Then came a dish of meat—its nature not declared, though its chopped miscellany suggested odds and ends gathered without ceremony. It was minced small and mixed with crumbs, then seasoned with something sharp and aromatic, and baked in a mould so that it turned out in a compact, respectable shape, queer to the eye but not unpalatable. Greens, bruised oddly as if they had been punished rather than cooked, accompanied it. And last there was a pâté of fruit conserved by a recipe credited to Madame Gérard Moore’s grand’mère; its sweetness had a dark, treacly edge, as if mélasse had stood in for sugar.

Caroline did not dislike this Belgian cookery; she rather liked it for its difference, for the way it broke the sameness of English plainness. It was well she did, for had she shown any distaste, Hortense would have marked it as a crime. A symptom of disrelish for foreign comestibles would have injured Caroline in her good graces beyond repair. Here, taste was loyalty; appetite was a kind of speech, and to speak wrongly at table was to offend a whole lineage.

After dinner, Caroline coaxed her cousin-governess upstairs to dress. The manoeuvre required care. To hint that the jupon, camisole, and curl-papers were odious would have been felony. One must not attack what Hortense considered merit. So Caroline pretended she needed a change of scene, drew Hortense aloft, and once in the bedroom persuaded her it was not worth returning again: she might as well make her toilet now. While Mademoiselle delivered a solemn homily on her own surpassing virtue in disregarding frivolities of fashion, Caroline’s hands did their quiet work: off came the camisole, on went a decent gown; collar adjusted, hair smoothed, the whole made presentable with a deftness born of longing to please.

But Hortense insisted on her own finishing touches. These consisted of a thick handkerchief tied round the throat and a large black apron, servant-like in its amplitude, spoiling the grace Caroline had tried to secure. Hortense would not appear in her own house without those two things. The fichu was morality itself—improper to go without; the apron was the ensign of a good housewife, as if by wearing it she saved money simply by being seen in it. She had made and presented similar items to Caroline; and the only serious quarrel they had ever had, a sore still living in Hortense’s mind, had arisen from Caroline’s refusal to accept and wear them.

“I wear a high dress and a collar,” Caroline had pleaded on that occasion; “I should feel suffocated with a handkerchief besides. And my short aprons do quite as well as that very long one. I would rather make no change.”

Hortense, by perseverance, might have forced her, but Mr. Moore chanced to overhear their dispute and decided that Caroline’s little aprons would suffice. As she was still a child, he said, she might dispense with the fichu for the present—especially as her curls were long and almost touched her shoulders.

There was no appeal from Robert. Hortense yielded, but disapproved deeply of the piquant neatness of Caroline’s dress and the ladylike grace of her appearance. Something more solid, more homely, she would have pronounced beaucoup plus convenable.

The afternoon was devoted to sewing. Hortense, like most Belgian ladies, was skilful with the needle; she considered it no waste to spend long hours on fine embroidery, lace-work that strained the eyes, netting and knitting, and above all on elaborate stocking-mending. She would give a day to the repair of two holes and count her mission nobly fulfilled. To Caroline it was another trial to be condemned to learn that foreign style of darning—stitch by stitch, imitating exactly the knit of the stocking, as if one were rebuilding cloth fibre by fibre. Wearisome, yes; but to Hortense and her ancestresses for generations it was one of the first duties of a woman. She herself had been given a needle and a fearfully torn stocking while still wearing a child’s coif on her little black head; her haut faits in the darning line had been displayed to company before she was six; and when she discovered Caroline was profoundly ignorant of this essential attainment, she could have wept with pity over such a neglected youth.

No time was lost in hunting up a hopeless pair of hose with heels entirely gone and setting the ignorant English girl to repair the deficiency. Caroline had begun the task two years ago, and the stockings were still in her work-bag. Each day she did a few rows as penance, expiating her sins stitch by stitch. They were a grievous burden. She would have liked to fling them into the fire; once Robert, seeing her sitting and sighing over them, had proposed a private cremation in the counting-house. But Caroline knew it would be impolitic to agree; the ashes would only be replaced by a fresh pair in worse condition. She kept, therefore, to the ills she knew.

All afternoon they sat, fingers moving, heads bent, until eyes and hands—and even the spirits of one of them—grew weary. After dinner the sky had darkened; now rain poured fast again, a dense rush on the panes and roof that made the world outside sound like a river. The cottage had its own small acoustic ecology: the rain’s drumming, the wind’s long sighing in the roof-trees, the occasional click of a needle, the faint scrape of thread pulled tight. Inside, the parlour fire threw a ruddy glow on the clear hearth, as if twilight had arrived early.

As five o’clock struck and slipped away, Caroline’s secret fears rose. Robert might be persuaded by Mr. Sykes or Mr. Yorke to remain at Whinbury till it cleared—and there seemed no chance of clearing.

“It will not be fair till the moon rises,” pronounced Mademoiselle Moore. “Consequently I feel assured that my brother will not return till then. Indeed I should be sorry if he did. We will have coffee. It would be vain to wait for him.”

Caroline’s fingers paused on the hateful stocking.

“I am tired. May I leave my work now, cousin?”

“You may, since it grows too dark to see to do it well. Fold it up; put it carefully in your bag; then step into the kitchen and desire Sarah to bring in the goûter, or tea, as you call it.”

“But it has not yet struck six. He may still come.”

“He will not, I tell you. I can calculate his movements. I understand my brother.”

Suspense is a poor diet, and disappointment bitter; Caroline felt both. She obeyed and went into the kitchen.

Sarah sat at the table making a dress for herself, her scissors snipping with brisk, cheerful defiance of the rain.

“You are to bring in coffee,” Caroline said spiritlessly. Then, as if she could not carry her own weight of waiting, she leaned her arm and head against the mantelpiece and hung over the fire.

“How low you seem, miss! But it’s all because your cousin keeps you so close to work. It’s a shame!”

“Nothing of the kind, Sarah,” Caroline answered briefly.

“Oh! but I know it is. You’re fit to cry this minute, for nothing else but because you’ve sat still the whole day. It would make a kitten dull to be mewed up so.”

“Sarah,” Caroline asked, forcing steadiness, “does your master often come home early from market when it is wet?”

“Never, hardly; but just to-day, for some reason, he has made a difference.”

“What do you mean?”

“He is come. I am certain I saw Murgatroyd lead his horse into the yard by the back-way when I went to get water at the pump five minutes since. He was in the counting-house with Joe Scott, I believe.”

“You are mistaken.”

“What should I be mistaken for? I know his horse surely?”

“But you did not see himself?”

“I heard him speak, though. He was saying something to Joe Scott about having settled all concerning ways and means, and that there would be a new set of frames in the mill before another week passed, and that this time he would get four soldiers from Stilbro’ barracks to guard the wagon.”

The words frames, soldiers, guard fell like hard pellets into Caroline’s mind; even in the warm kitchen, with the domestic smell of cloth and starch and the faint comfort of the fire, industry and conflict found their way in, a harsh undertone beneath the rain.

“Sarah, are you making a gown?” Caroline asked suddenly, as if to steady herself by fastening on some homely thing.

“Yes. Is it a handsome one?”

“Beautiful. Get the coffee ready. I’ll finish cutting out that sleeve for you, and I’ll give you some trimming for it. I have some narrow satin ribbon of a colour that will just match it.”

“You’re very kind, miss.”

“Be quick; there’s a good girl. But first put your master’s shoes on the hearth: he will take his boots off when he comes in. I hear him; he is coming.”

“Miss, you are cutting the stuff wrong.”

“So I am; but it is only a snip. There is no harm done.”

At that moment the kitchen door opened. Mr. Moore entered wet with rain and chilled with wind. The smell he brought in was of cold outdoors—damp wool, horse, and air sharpened by weather. Caroline half turned, but bent again over the dress for an instant, as if to gain one more second to settle her face, to veil whatever might betray her too plainly. The attempt failed. When she looked up at last, her countenance beamed.

“We had ceased to expect you. They asserted you would not come,” she said.

“But I promised to return soon. You expected me, I suppose?”

“No, Robert; I dared not when it rained so fast. And you are wet and chilled. Change everything. If you took cold, I should—we should blame ourselves in some measure.”

“I am not wet through: my riding-coat is waterproof. Dry shoes are all I require. There—the fire is pleasant after facing the cold wind and rain for a few miles.”

He stood on the hearth, letting warmth creep back into him. Caroline stood beside him. Robert’s eyes, as he enjoyed the glow, rested a moment on the glittering brasses on the shelf above—bright, scrubbed, domestic trophies. Then he glanced down and saw, uplifted towards him, a face flushed with relief and happiness, shaded by silky curls, lit by eyes that could not hide what they felt.

Sarah had gone into the parlour with the tray; a lecture from her mistress detained her there. The kitchen’s sounds softened: only the rain’s steady assault outside, and the fire’s small crackle within.

Mr. Moore laid his hand for a moment on Caroline’s shoulder, stooped, and kissed her forehead.

The touch seemed to unseal her, as if some long-held restraint broke like a thread.

“Oh!” she said. “I was miserable when I thought you would not come. I am almost too happy now. Are you happy, Robert? Do you like to come home?”

“I think I do—to-night, at least.”

“Are you certain you are not fretting about your frames, and your business, and the war?”

“Not just now.”

“Are you positive you don’t feel Hollow’s Cottage too small for you, and narrow, and dismal?”

“At this moment, no.”

“Can you affirm that you are not bitter at heart because rich and great people forget you?”

“No more questions. You are mistaken if you think I am anxious to curry favour with rich and great people. I only want means—a position—a career.”

“Which your own talent and goodness shall win you. You were made to be great; you shall be great.”

“I wonder now,” he said, a darker edge coming into his voice, “if you spoke honestly out of your heart, what recipe you would give me for acquiring this same greatness; but I know it—better than you know it yourself. Would it be efficacious? Would it work? Yes—poverty, misery, bankruptcy. Oh, life is not what you think it, Lina!”

“But you are what I think you.”

“I am not.”

“You are better, then?”

“Far worse.”

“No; far better. I know you are good.”

“How do you know it?”

“You look so, and I feel you are so.”

“Where do you feel it?”

“In my heart.”

“Ah! You judge me with your heart, Lina: you should judge me with your head.”

“I do; and then I am quite proud of you. Robert, you cannot tell all my thoughts about you.”

Colour rose in his dark face. His lips smiled, and yet pressed together as if he mistrusted the smile. His eyes laughed, and yet he knit his brow, resisting the softness he had just allowed himself.

“Think meanly of me, Lina,” he said. “Men, in general, are a sort of scum, very different to anything of which you have an idea. I make no pretension to be better than my fellows.”

“If you did, I should not esteem you so much. It is because you are modest that I have such confidence in your merit.”

“Are you flattering me?” he demanded suddenly, turning sharply, searching her face with that acute look which seemed to pierce past words to intention.

“No,” she answered softly, laughing at his swiftness. She did not seem anxious to defend herself; as if truth, once spoken, needed no adornment, no further translation.

“You don’t care whether I think you flatter me or not?”

“No.”

“You are so secure of your own intentions?”

“I suppose so.”

“What are they, Caroline?”

“Only to ease my mind by expressing for once part of what I think, and then to make you better satisfied with yourself.”

“By assuring me that my kinswoman is my sincere friend?”

“Just so. I am your sincere friend, Robert.”

“And I am—what chance and change shall make me, Lina.”

“Not my enemy, however?”

The answer did not come whole. Sarah and her mistress entered the kitchen together in a small commotion. They had employed the time in disputing over café au lait: Sarah declaring it the queerest mess ever seen, a waste of God’s good gifts—coffee’s nature, she insisted, was to be boiled in water; Hortense maintaining it was un breuvage royal, far too good for a mean person who objected to it. Their voices cut across the intimate quiet like a sudden clang, making the kitchen seem smaller.

Robert and Caroline withdrew to the parlour. Before Hortense followed, Caroline had only time to repeat, quick as a pulse:

“Not my enemy, Robert?”

And Mr. Moore, Quaker-like, answered with another question, low and steady:

“Could I be?”

Then he seated himself at the table and, with a quiet authority that felt like kindness, settled Caroline at his side.

Caroline Helstone was just eighteen, an age at which life has not yet set its pot to a true, steady boil. Before then one lives as if in an old tale told by a winter hearth—now sweet as sugared milk, now bitter as burnt crust—but always with a taste of unreality on the tongue. The world is too coloured, too strong in scent and sound: moons look like silver fruit you might pluck; suns blaze like ovens where gods bake their bread. You believe in marvels as you believe in morning.

At eighteen the edge of that country is reached. Behind lies Elf-land with its bright deceit; ahead, the shores of Reality lift themselves—blue and soft at a distance, like meadows seen through steam rising from a kettle. You imagine, if you once step there, you will never hunger, never thirst, never be chilled again. You do not know how many stony fields must be crossed before any table is spread; how often the very water you must drink will be cold as grief. Joy, when it comes at all, is earned; it is not handed over like a loaf still warm from the baker’s board. There are prizes for which one must sweat, and bleed, and still keep one’s hands steady.

Caroline did not yet know this with the knowledge that sits in the bones. Hope, when she leaned near and promised, was believed as easily as a child believes the dinner-bell. Love, when he wandered like a lost angel to the door, was taken in and cherished; his quiver went unseen. If his arrows struck, the wound felt like a new life, a quickening. No one warns you then about the barbs no hand can draw out; no one tells you how passion can be an ache that never quite ceases.

She had been convoyed home by Robert Moore, and when she reached the rectory she had no longing to spend the remainder of the evening with her uncle. The room where he sat was sacred ground—more like a vestry than a parlour in her mind—and she seldom entered it unless summoned. She kept aloof until the bell rang for prayers.

Part of the evening church service was read, as usual, in Mr. Helstone’s household. He delivered it in that clear, loud, nasal monotone which made devotion seem as mechanical as a mill-wheel. When it was done, Caroline went to him in her accustomed way.

“Good-night, uncle.”

“Hey! You’ve been gadding abroad all day—visiting, dining out, and what not!”

“Only at the cottage.”

“And have you learned your lessons?”

“Yes.”

“And made a shirt?”

“Only part of one.”

“Well, that will do. Stick to the needle, learn shirt-making and gown-making and piecrust-making, and you’ll be a clever woman some day. Go to bed now. I’m busy with a pamphlet here.”

Piecrust-making. The words had a homely sound; she could almost smell flour and butter and the clean heat of an oven. Yet even that domestic counsel came from him like a rule barked out, not like kindness. She went up to her small bedroom, bolted the door, and exchanged day-dress for a white dressing-gown. Then she loosened her hair. It fell thick and soft, a heavy wave to her waist; and as she combed, the slow drawing of the teeth through it—like the hush of a brush on wheat—seemed to quiet the room.

She paused, elbow on knee, cheek on hand, eyes on the carpet. At eighteen, such pauses are not empty. Visions rose as naturally as scent from warm bread.

Her thoughts spoke to her, and she listened with a smile. Something brighter than her own young face seemed present there: the spirit of Hope, full of flattering prophecies. According to that sweet deceiver she had done with chill and disappointment. The dawn she had entered was no false light; it was the true beginning of morning, and her sun would rise quickly.

“When people love, the next step is they marry,” she argued with herself, as if laying out ingredients on a board in the order they must be used. “Now, I love Robert, and I feel sure Robert loves me. I have thought it many a time before; to-day I felt it. When I looked up after repeating Chénier’s poem, his eyes—what handsome eyes he has!—sent the truth straight through my heart.”

She remembered the lane, the evening air, their footsteps together, and the hush between them that had not been awkward, only deep, like the pause before music resumes.

“Sometimes I am afraid to speak to him,” she went on, “lest I be too frank—lest I seem forward. I have regretted words—overflowing, superfluous words—and feared he would disapprove what he might deem my indiscretion. But to-night I could have ventured anything: he was so indulgent. How kind he was as we walked up the lane! He does not flatter, or say foolish things. His love-making—friendship, I mean; of course I don’t yet account him my lover, but I hope he will be so some day—is not like what we read of in books. It is far better—original, quiet, manly, sincere.”

She drew the comb once more through her hair, and the sound, regular and soft, seemed to keep time with her certainties.

“I do like him; I would be an excellent wife to him if he did marry me. I would tell him of his faults—for he has a few faults—but I would study his comfort, and cherish him, and do my best to make him happy. Now, I am sure he will not be cold to-morrow. I feel almost certain that to-morrow evening he will either come here, or ask me to go there.”

Turning her head, she caught herself in the glass. Such reflections sober the plain; but Caroline, seen so—head inclined, hair loose, the softness of youth still unclouded—looked lovely. The sight seemed to confirm Hope’s whisper. She lay down and slept with gladness unbroken.

In the morning she rose with the same warmth in her veins. When she entered her uncle’s breakfast-room and wished him good-morning with a gentle cheerfulness, even he—bronze little man that he was—thought, for an instant, that she was growing “a fine girl.” Usually she was quiet with him, docile and uncommunicative; but to-day words came to her. They were small words—safe words—like the modest fare one sets before a fastidious guest.

She told him she had walked early in the garden and that certain flowers were beginning to spring; she asked when the gardener would come to trim the borders; she spoke of starlings building in the church-tower, and wondered that the tolling of the bells did not frighten them away. Through the open window the village soundscape drifted in—the faint clink of a pail, the far call of a voice, the rustle of trees disturbed by morning wind; and near at hand, the steady hush of the old house breathing.

Mr. Helstone opined that “they were like other fools who had just paired—insensible to inconvenience just for the moment.”

Caroline, made a little bolder by her good spirits, hazarded a remark she had never before dared.

“Uncle,” said she, “whenever you speak of marriage you speak of it scornfully. Do you think people shouldn’t marry?”

“It is decidedly the wisest plan to remain single, especially for women.”

“Are all marriages unhappy?”

“Millions of marriages are unhappy. If everybody confessed the truth, perhaps all are more or less so.”

“You are always vexed when you are asked to come and marry a couple. Why?”

“Because one does not like to act as accessory to the commission of a piece of pure folly.”

He spoke with a readiness that suggested relish in the topic—like a man pleased to season his discourse with pepper for the mere sting of it. Caroline, surprised to find herself not struck dumb, went further.

“But why should it be pure folly? If two people like each other, why shouldn’t they consent to live together?”

“They tire of each other—they tire of each other in a month. A yokefellow is not a companion; he or she is a fellow-sufferer.”

There was no naïve innocence in what Caroline said next; it sprang from real dislike—almost anger—at his cold conclusions.

“One would think you had never been married, uncle. One would think you were an old bachelor.”

“Practically, I am so.”

“But you have been married. Why were you so inconsistent as to marry?”

“Every man is mad once or twice in his life.”

“So you tired of my aunt, and my aunt of you, and you were miserable together?”

He pushed out his cynical lip, wrinkled his brown forehead, and gave an inarticulate grunt.

“Did she not suit you? Was she not good-tempered? Did you not get used to her? Were you not sorry when she died?”

“Caroline,” said Mr. Helstone, bringing his hand slowly down and then smiting the mahogany table with a sudden blow, “understand this: it is vulgar and puerile to confound generals with particulars. In every case there is the rule and there are the exceptions. Your questions are stupid and babyish. Ring the bell, if you have done breakfast.”

The breakfast things were cleared away. Usually uncle and niece separated until dinner; but to-day Caroline did not leave the room. She went to the window-seat and sat looking out. Mr. Helstone glanced round uneasily, wishing her gone; but she stared through the panes as if she had forgotten him, and he returned to his newspaper. It was full of the Peninsula—dispatches from General Lord Wellington, columns of marching and counter-marching. He read with the absorbed interest of a man who prefers the ordered noise of war on paper to the unruly music of feeling in his own house.

He did not know what stir his niece’s mind endured. The talk of marriage had revived old thoughts—had not sown them, for they had lived there long—like bees in a hive disturbed.

She reviewed his character and his scorn. And on the far side of the gulf between them, she saw another figure—dim, sinister, half-remembered: the image of her father, James Helstone, her uncle’s brother.

Rumours had reached her from servants: hints dropped and swallowed again. She knew he had not been good, and that he had never been kind to her. She remembered—darkly, but with a chill that never quite left—some weeks spent with him in a great town. No maid attended her there. She had been shut up day and night in a high garret without a carpet; an uncurtained bed, bare boards, scant furniture; the air close with the stale smell of poverty and neglect, as if no pot ever simmered, no clean hearth ever warmed the place. He went out early each morning and would forget to return to give her dinner; hunger, for a child, is a loud thing, yet the house kept its silence as if it were deaf. At night he came back either raging—furious, terrible—or, worse, vacant and senseless, as if the man within had been blotted out.

She knew she had fallen ill there. One night, when she was very sick, he came raving into the room and said he would kill her, for she was a burden. Her screams had brought aid. From the moment she was rescued, she never saw him again—except as a dead man in his coffin.

That was her father.

She had a mother too, though Mr. Helstone never spoke of her, and Caroline could not remember having seen her; yet she knew she lived. That mother was then the drunkard’s wife. What had their marriage been? Watching the starlings—without truly seeing them—Caroline turned from the lattice and, in a low voice, with bitterness subdued into sadness, broke the silence.

“You term marriage miserable, I suppose, from what you saw of my father and mother. If my mother suffered what I suffered when I was with papa, she must have had a dreadful life.”

Mr. Helstone wheeled about in his chair and looked over his spectacles. He was taken aback.

Her father and mother—those names he had avoided for twelve years—spoken at last in the open breakfast-room. He could not imagine the thoughts were self-matured; he could not credit that she remembered, or reasoned.

“Your father and mother? Who has been talking to you about them?”

“Nobody; but I remember something of what papa was, and I pity mamma. Where is she?”

That question had been in Caroline’s throat a hundred times; she had swallowed it always, as one swallows a mouthful too hot. Now it came out.

“I hardly know,” returned Mr. Helstone. “I was little acquainted with her. I have not heard from her for years: but wherever she is, she thinks nothing of you; she never inquires about you. I have reason to believe she does not wish to see you. Come, it is school-time. You go to your cousin at ten, don’t you? The clock has struck.”

Perhaps Caroline would have said more; but Fanny came in to say the churchwardens wanted to speak with him in the vestry. He rose and went quickly, glad of the interruption; and Caroline soon set out for the cottage.

The road from the rectory to Hollow’s Mill inclined downward. She ran almost all the way, as if speed could shake off what she had heard. The air was fresh; the hedges held a green dampness; birds stitched their small sounds through the morning—sounds that soothed, because they belonged to old, living things, not to pamphlets and cynicism. And beneath these, as she drew nearer, there began to come the heavier voice of labour: the mill’s thunder, the rush of the watercourse, that constant industrial roar that filled the valley like a hard wind that never changed.

Exercise and the thought of seeing Robert—if only of being near him, within his precincts—revived her quickly. When the white house came in sight, and the sound of the wheel and stream grew loud enough to press against the ear, the first thing she saw was Moore standing at his garden-gate. He wore a belted Holland blouse and a light cap—an undress that suited him, making him seem less a master and more a man of the place. He was looking down the lane, not in the direction from which she approached.

Caroline paused and drew a little behind a willow. She studied him.

“He has not his peer,” she thought, the words tasting like admiration itself. “He is as handsome as he is intelligent. What a keen eye he has! What clearly-cut, spirited features—thin and serious, but graceful! I do like his face, I do like his aspect, I do like him so much—better than any of those shuffling curates, for instance—better than anybody; bonny Robert!”

She stepped out and went to him.

For his part, when he saw her, it seemed he would have vanished like a phantom if he could; but being solid—tall fact, not fiction—he had to stand and meet her. His greeting was brief. It was cousin-like, brother-like, friend-like—anything but lover-like. The nameless charm of last night had fled from his manner. He was no longer the same man; or, if the man remained, the heart within had shut its door.

The disappointment struck sharp. At first she would not believe it, though she saw and felt it as plainly as one tastes salt. It was hard to withdraw her hand from his until he gave something—some warmer pressure, some tenderness—to justify it. It was hard to turn her eyes away until his eyes had spoken what she longed to hear. But his look was cool welcome only; his hand did not linger.

What could she do? A man may demand explanation; a woman—if she has any instinct for self-preservation—keeps silence. To speak would be to betray herself and then to be punished afterwards by that inward thunder of self-contempt. Nature, in such hours, is an austere guardian: she seals the lips, commands a smooth face, orders the heart to endure without sob or outcry.

Caroline endured. Robert had done her no wrong; he had spoken no lie. If she had made a romance of his kindness, it was her own weaving. He had sometimes seemed fond of her—because she had tried so hard to be pleasing, and even his efforts could not always prevent his manner from showing a warmth his judgment disallowed. Now he meant to withdraw, to disentangle himself, to avoid a marriage he believed imprudent.

So she did not cling, did not question, did not reproach. She left him quietly; went on to her lessons with Hortense as usual; and at dinner-time returned home without lingering.

When she had dined, and found herself alone in the rectory drawing-room—her uncle lingering over a temperate glass of port—she met a new difficulty: How am I to get through this day?

Last night she had expected the evening to resemble yesterday’s. She had hoped for Robert, for that sweetness of companionship that seemed to feed her better than any meal. This morning had taught her her mistake; yet she could not persuade her heart to settle into certainty. False Hope kept whispering: he may come still.

He had walked up after tea more than once to spend an hour with Mr. Helstone. The bell had rung at twilight; his voice had been heard in the passage when she least expected such a happiness. Twice it had happened after he had treated her with peculiar reserve; and though he seldom spoke to her in her uncle’s presence, he had looked at her with something like relenting as he sat opposite her work-table. A few words, dropped casually, had comforted her; his good-night had been genial.

To-day—why not to-day again?

She tried to read, but the lines slipped away from meaning. She tried to sew, but each stitch was an ennui, each draw of thread a little act of endurance; needlework, which might have been soothing if her mind had been quiet, became mere torture of repetition. She opened her desk and attempted a French composition; she wrote nothing but mistakes, as if even grammar revolted.

Then the door-bell rang sharply.

Her heart leaped; she sprang to the drawing-room door and opened it softly, peeping through the narrow gap. Fanny was admitting a visitor—a gentleman—a tall man—Robert’s height. For one second Caroline believed it was Robert; for one second she exulted.

But the voice asking for Mr. Helstone undeceived her. It was an Irish voice—Malone’s, the curate’s—not Moore’s. He was shown into the dining-room, where, no doubt, he would presently help the rector to empty the decanters.

Moore was in the saddle before the morning had found its voice.

The night still lay cold and folded in the hollows when he rode out, and the road to Whinbury rang under his horse’s shoes with a sound like struck iron—thin, solitary, and too sharp for peace. The hedges were black laces against a paling sky; here and there a cottage chimney sighed up a first ribbon of peat-reek; a cock tried his call and was answered far off, as if the very air were feeling its way toward day. Moore took in all these small, homely signals without softening. Whatever business he did in Whinbury—under the dim lamp of a clerk’s office, with paper breathed on by cold fingers and sealed with quick impatience—he kept to himself when he returned.

By the time Hortense had warmed the milk and coffee together till it gave up that faint sweet steam—half dairy, half roast—he had already washed the road dust from his hands and put away the look of a man who has been bargaining with something hard. She cut the tartines neatly, her knife making a crisp whisper against crust; she set the café au lait before him, and the smell was gentle, domestic, almost pleading. He ate, but his appetite seemed a duty; he drank, but as if warmth could not travel far in him. Hortense asked nothing. Between brother and sister there was a compact older than affection: his movements were not to be questioned; her silence was not to be interpreted.

When breakfast was done, he went to the counting-house.

Henry—Joe Scott’s lad—brought in the letters and the daily paper. Moore sat down, broke seals, glanced, and the short lines of ink might as well have been drops of sourness: his nostrils gave a little derisive snuff, and in his eye there stirred that keen, almost gay defiance which looks like a jest until it turns to steel. Without changing the placidity of his face, he chose a pen and, in a brief spasm of finger-fury, stripped away the feathered top. He wrote answers swiftly—decisive strokes, as if each word were a nail driven home—sealed them, and went out to walk through the mill.

Within, the day’s work had begun its thick chant: looms thudding like great hearts, wheels making their relentless, circular whir. Sound did not merely fill the place; it ruled it, pressed on ribs and temples, ate into thought. Outside the wide counting-house door stood open to sunshine and mild air, but what the breeze carried was not spring. Now and then, along with the clean edge of cold, there came a sulphur-puff from the gaunt chimney—soot-thick smoke rushing up in a sable column—an industrial breath that soured the very idea of weather.

He came back, sat down, and tried the newspaper. It would not hold him. More than once he laid it across his knee, folded his arms, and stared into the fire. He looked towards the window. He looked at his watch. His attention moved like a restless tongue tasting for something it could not find.

A dark-blue apparition—the ghost of Joe Scott, fresh from a dyeing vat, half man and half indigo—showed itself at the open door.

“He’s comed, sir,” said Joe, and vanished as quickly as if he had been absorbed into the mill’s own blue.

Moore did not lift his eyes from the paper.

A large man entered—broad-shouldered, massive-limbed—clad in fustian, with grey worsted stockings and a hat so battered it looked as if weather had chewed it. He removed the hat, stowed it under his chair with care, and wiped his forehead with a spotted cotton handkerchief that had been living in the hat-crown.

“Raight dahn warm for Febewerry,” he remarked, and sat with the air of one who has a right to be where he is.

Moore made a sound that might serve for assent.

The visitor set, in the corner beside him, an official-looking staff, and then began to whistle—an aimless, comfort-seeking tune, as if to make the air his own.

“You have what is necessary, I suppose?” Moore asked.

“Ay, ay! all’s right.”

Whistling resumed. Paper rustled. Fire ticked. The mill, beyond, ground on—an unceasing, nerve-wearing bass.

After a time Moore reached to the cupboard at his side without rising, and took out a black bottle, a tumbler, and a jug. He placed them on the table with the economy of a man who wastes no motion.

“Help yourself; there’s water in that jar in the corner.”

“I dunnut knaw that there’s mich need, for all a body is dry in a morning,” the fustian gentleman replied, already on his feet. He mixed with a practised hand, the clear water clouding as it took the spirit; he tested it by a deep draught, and sank back with the satisfaction of a man who believes he has made life simpler.

“Will you tak naught yourseln, Mr. Moore?” he asked, mouth mellowed by the burn and sweetness of the hollands.

Moore answered with a slight negative movement; he did not drink.

“Yah’d as good,” urged the visitor. “It ‘uld set ye up. Uncommon good hollands. Ye get it fro’ furrin parts, I’se think?”

“Ay!”

“Tak my advice and try a glass on’t. Them lads ‘at’s coming ‘ll keep ye talking, nob’dy knows how long. Ye’ll need propping.”

“Have you seen Mr. Sykes this morning?” Moore inquired, as if his mind had never touched the bottle at all.

“I seed him—hauf an hour—nay, happen a quarter sin’—just afore I set off. He said he aimed to come here, and I sudn’t wonder but ye’ll have old Helstone too. I seed ’em saddling his little nag as I passed at back o’ t’ rectory.”

Five minutes later the yard answered with the brisk trot of a small horse. Hoofs tapped a quick, bright rhythm on the stones, and then a nasal voice called out, “Boy—take my horse and lead him into the stable.”

Helstone entered as if the cold had sharpened him. He marched nimbly and erect, looking browner, keener, and more alive than his years promised.

“Beautiful morning, Moore. How do, my boy? Ha! whom have we here?” His glance fell on the man with the staff. “Sugden! What! you’re going to work directly? On my word, you lose no time. But I come to ask explanations. Your message was delivered to me. Are you sure you are on the right scent? How do you mean to set about the business? Have you got a warrant?”

“Sugden has.”

“Then you are going to seek him now? I’ll accompany you.”

“You will be spared that trouble, sir; he is coming to seek me. I’m just now sitting in state waiting his arrival.”

“And who is it? One of my parishioners?”

Joe Scott had slipped in without sound—silent as dye sinking into cloth. He leaned on the desk, indigo-dark and watchful. Moore answered Helstone’s question with a smile—one of those brief, dry curves that carried no kindness.

Joe took the word, his pawky quietness like a small knife.

“It’s a friend of yours, Mr. Helstone—a gentleman you often speak of.”

“Indeed! His name, Joe? You look well this morning.”

“Only the Rev. Moses Barraclough; t’ tub orator you call him sometimes, I think.”

Helstone drew out his snuff-box and took a long pinch, as if the tobacco’s sting might guard him against surprise.

“Ah! Couldn’t have supposed it. Why, the pious man never was a workman of yours, Moore. He’s a tailor by trade.”

“And so much the worse grudge I owe him,” Moore returned, “for interfering and setting my discarded men against me.”

“And Moses was actually present at the battle of Stilbro’ Moor? He went there, wooden leg and all?”

“Ay, sir,” said Joe, relishing the telling. “He went there on horseback, that his leg mightn’t be noticed. He was the captain, and wore a mask. The rest only had their faces blackened.”

“And how was he found out?”

“I’ll tell you, sir,” Joe said, glancing sideways at Moore with a look that begged leave and took it all the same. “T’ maister’s not so fond of talking. I’ve no objections. He courted Sarah, Mr. Moore’s sarvant lass, and so it seems she would have nothing to say to him; she either didn’t like his wooden leg or she’d some notion about his being a hypocrite. Happen—for women is queer hands; we may say that amang werseln when there’s none of ’em nigh—she’d have encouraged him, in spite o’ his leg and his deceit, just to pass time like. I’ve known some on ’em do as mich; and some o’ t’ bonniest and mimmest-looking too—ay, I’ve seen clean, trim young things, that looked as denty and pure as daisies, and wi’ time a body fun’ ’em out to be nowt but stinging, venomed nettles.”

“Joe’s a sensible fellow,” Helstone put in drily.

“Howsiver, Sarah had another string to her bow. Fred Murgatroyd—one o’ our lads—is for her; and as women judge men by their faces—and Fred has a middling face, while Moses is none so handsome, as we all knaw—the lass took on wi’ Fred. A two-three months sin’, Murgatroyd and Moses chanced to meet one Sunday night; they’d both come lurking about these premises wi’ the notion o’ counselling Sarah to tak a bit of a walk wi’ them. They fell out, had a tussle, and Fred was worsted—for he’s young and small, and Barraclough, for all he has only one leg, is almost as strong as Sugden there—indeed, anybody that hears him roaring at a revival or a love-feast may be sure he’s no weakling.”

“Joe, you’re insupportable,” Moore cut in, his patience fraying at the edges. “You spin out your explanation as Moses spins out his sermons. The long and short of it is, Murgatroyd was jealous of Barraclough; and last night, as he and a friend took shelter in a barn from a shower, they heard and saw Moses conferring with some associates within. From their discourse it was plain he had been the leader, not only at Stilbro’ Moor, but in the attack on Sykes’s property. Moreover they planned a deputation to wait on me this morning, which the tailor is to head, and which, in the most religious and peaceful spirit, is to entreat me to put the accursed thing out of my tent. I rode over to Whinbury this morning, got a constable and a warrant, and I am now waiting to give my friend the reception he deserves. Here, meantime, comes Sykes. Mr. Helstone, you must spirit him up. He feels timid at the thoughts of prosecuting.”

A gig rolled into the yard, its wheels making a hollow, anxious rattle on the stones. Mr. Sykes entered—a tall, stout man of about fifty, comely enough in feature, but with a physiognomy that seemed made of concessions. His face had the look of a man who has spent many nights listening for footsteps where none should be.

“Have they been? Are they gone? Have you got him? Is it over?” he asked at once, words tripping over each other.

“Not yet,” Moore returned, unruffled. “We are waiting for them.”

“They’ll not come; it’s near noon. Better give it up. It will excite bad feeling—make a stir—cause perhaps fatal consequences.”

“You need not appear,” said Moore. “I shall meet them in the yard when they come; you can stay here.”

“But my name must be seen in the law proceedings. A wife and family, Mr. Moore—a wife and family make a man cautious.”

Moore’s mouth tightened with a disgust he did not trouble to disguise.

“Give way, if you please. Leave me to myself. I have no objection to act alone; only be assured you will not find safety in submission. Your partner Pearson gave way, and conceded, and forbore. Well, that did not prevent them from attempting to shoot him in his own house.”

“My dear sir,” said Helstone, “take a little wine and water.”

The “wine and water” was hollands and water—clean-looking, deceptively mild. Sykes compounded a brimming tumbler and swallowed it. It went down like liquid fire and false courage; in two minutes it painted his cheeks and set his tongue to trumpeting. Noise—chemical, easy noise—rose inside him and drove out fear, as a louder sound drives out a softer one.

He declared he hoped he was above being trampled on by common people; he would endure the insolence of the working-classes no longer; he had considered and made up his mind to go all lengths; if money and spirit could put down these rioters, they should be put down; Moore might do as he liked, but he—Christie Sykes—would spend his last penny in law before he would be beaten; he would settle them, or he’d see.

“Take another glass,” Moore urged, watching him with a sort of cool amusement.

Sykes did not mind if he did. This was a cold morning—though Sugden had called it warm—one must be careful at this season; it was proper to take something to keep damp out; he had a cough already (he produced it obligingly, as proof); something of this sort was excellent taken medicinally; he didn’t make a practice of drinking spirits in a morning, but occasionally it really was prudent to take precautions.

“Quite prudent, and take them by all means,” Moore replied.

Sykes, warmed and inflated, turned to Helstone, who stood on the hearth, his shovel-hat still on his head, his keen eyes watching the whole scene as if it were a lesson in some unpleasant catechism.

“You, sir, as a clergyman,” said Sykes, “may feel it disagreeable to be present amidst scenes of hurry and flurry—and, I may say, peril. I dare say your nerves won’t stand it. You’re a man of peace, sir; but we manufacturers, living in the world, and always in turmoil, get quite belligerent. Really, there’s an ardour excited by the thoughts of danger that makes my heart pant. When Mrs. Sykes is afraid of the house being attacked and broke open—as she is every night—I get quite excited. I couldn’t describe to you, sir, my feelings. Really, if anybody was to come—thieves or anything—I believe I should enjoy it, such is my spirit.”

Helstone’s response was a laugh—hard, brief, low—cut like flint. It did not flatter Sykes; it did not even quite insult him; it simply told the truth as sound.

Moore would have pressed a third tumbler on the heroic mill-owner, but Helstone lifted a hand with quiet authority.

“Enough is as good as a feast, is it not, Mr. Sykes?”

Sykes assented, and sat watching Joe Scott remove the bottle at a sign from Helstone. A self-satisfied simper lingered on his lips, and a regretful glisten in his eye followed the black glass as it vanished. Moore looked, for one instant, as if he would have liked to make sport of him to the very top of his bent.

And somewhere beyond this room—somewhere in a quieter house, where reading by lamplight and gentle music could make an evening seem a refuge—there was a young woman to whom Moore had lately been, for one soft interval, almost tender. If she had seen him now—with that mischievous, sardonic edge showing in his face—would she have recognized the same man? The thought passed like a shadow and was gone. Men, like cloth, showed different sides according to the light.

A gathering sound rose in the yard—a trample, a thickening hum—then a pause, as if the crowd drew breath together. Moore stepped to the window; Helstone followed.
