They stood so that they could see without being seen: the tall young man behind the undersized old one, both looking out with the caution of those who have long listened to danger and learned its notes.

Their only comment was a cynical smile exchanged—stern eyes flashing it like a signal.

Outside came a flourishing, oratorical cough. Then an interjection—“Whisht!”—aimed at stilling several voices. Moore opened the casement an inch or two, to let sound in more freely. The voices in the yard had a particular texture: not the round speech of the educated, but the thick, vowel-heavy dialect of the district—language carrying old fields, old hearths, old resentments. In it lived a whole memory that no polished lexicon could translate without killing something vital; yet here it was being used as a weapon, turned and sharpened by men who knew how to make words herd other men.

“Joseph Scott,” began a snuffling voice, “might we inquire if your master be within, and is to be spoken to?”

Joe, sentinel at the counting-house door, answered with lazy indifference.

“He’s within, ay.”

“Would you then, if you please”—and the emphasis on “you” was a small act of power—“have the goodness to tell him that twelve gentlemen wants to see him.”

“He’d happen ax what for,” Joe suggested, enjoying himself. “I mught as weel tell him that at t’ same time.”

“For a purpose.”

Joe came in.

“Please, sir, there’s twelve gentlemen wants to see ye, ‘for a purpose.’”

“Good, Joe; I’m their man.—Sugden, come when I whistle.”

Moore went out, chuckling dryly, as if what awaited him were sport rather than peril. He advanced into the yard with one hand in his pocket and the other in his waistcoat; his cap brim shaded his eyes, but could not hide the deep, dancing ray of scorn in them.

Twelve men stood waiting—some in shirt-sleeves, some in blue aprons—faces weathered, hands stained with labour, bodies carrying the posture the mill had taught them: shoulders slightly forward, ears tuned for command. In the van were two conspicuous leaders.

One was a little dapper strutting man with a turned-up nose and a self-important air—as if words themselves were a coat he wore for show. The other was broad-shouldered, and more notable still: a demure face, eyes like a cat’s—watchful and faithless—plus a wooden leg and a stout crutch. A leer lived about his lips, as though he laughed inwardly at the very people he pretended to represent. He did not look like a man who tasted honesty; he looked like a man who tasted advantage.

“Good-morning, Mr. Barraclough,” Moore said, debonairly, in a tone that made courtesy sound like mockery.

“Peace be unto you!” answered Barraclough, closing his already half-shut eyes as he delivered the blessing, as if it were a performance he could do better blind.

“I’m obliged to you. Peace is an excellent thing; there’s nothing I more wish for myself. But that is not all you have to say to me, I suppose? I imagine peace is not your purpose?”

“As to our purpose,” began Barraclough, “it’s one that may sound strange and perhaps foolish to ears like yours, for the childer of this world is wiser in their generation than the childer of light—”

“To the point, if you please, and let me hear what it is.”

“Ye’se hear, sir. If I cannot get it off, there’s eleven behint can help me. It is a grand purpose, and”—his voice shifted from a half-sneer to a whine—“it’s the Looard’s own purpose, and that’s better.”

“Do you want a subscription to a new Ranter’s chapel, Mr. Barraclough? Unless your errand be something of that sort, I cannot see what you have to do with it.”

“I hadn’t that duty on my mind, sir; but as Providence has led ye to mention the subject, I’ll make it i’ my way to tak ony trifle ye may have to spare; the smallest contribution will be acceptable.”

He doffed his hat and held it out like a begging-box; a brazen grin crossed his countenance.

“If I gave you sixpence you would drink it,” Moore said.

Barraclough uplifted the palms of his hands and the whites of his eyes—a burlesque of sanctity so broad it was almost a jest upon religion itself.

“You seem a fine fellow,” Moore returned, cool and dry. “You don’t care for showing me that you are a double-dyed hypocrite, that your trade is fraud. You expect indeed to make me laugh at the cleverness with which you play your coarsely farcical part, while at the same time you think you are deceiving the men behind you.”

Moses’ countenance lowered. He saw he had overshot his mark. He would have answered, but the second leader—impatient at having been kept in shadow—stepped forward.

This man did not look like a traitor; conceit, rather than cunning, sat on him. He spoke through throat and nose, very slowly, as if savouring each phrase before letting it go, and as if his listeners ought to be grateful for the elegance of his speech.

“Mr. Moore,” he commenced, “it might, perhaps, justly be said that reason rather than peace is our purpose. We come, in the first place, to request you to hear reason; and should you refuse, it is my duty to warn you, in very decided terms, that measures will be had resort to which will probably terminate in— in bringing you to a sense of the unwisdom, of the—the foolishness which seems to guide and guard your proceedings as a tradesman in this manufacturing part of the country. Hem! Sir, I would beg to allude that as a furriner, coming from a distant coast, another quarter and hemisphere of this globe, thrown, as I may say, a perfect outcast on these shores—the cliffs of Albion—you have not that understanding of huz and wer ways which might conduce to the benefit of the working-classes. If, to come at once to partic’lars, you’d consider to give up this here miln, and go without further protractions straight home to where you belong, it ’ud happen be as well. I can see naught ageean such a plan.—What hev ye to say tull’t, lads?”

The deputation answered with a unanimous “Hear, hear!”—a sound like a wave breaking.

“Brayvo, Noah o’ Tim’s!” muttered Joe Scott behind Moore, delighting in the absurd grandiosity. “Moses’ll niver beat that. Cliffs o’ Albion, and t’ other hemisphere! My certy! Did ye come fro’ th’ Antarctic Zone, maister? Moses is dished.”

But Moses refused to be dished. He cast an ireful glance at Noah of Tim’s and began again, this time serious, dropping the sly sarcasm that had failed.

“Or iver you set up the pole o’ your tent amang us, Mr. Moore, we lived i’ peace and quietness—yea, I may say, in all loving-kindness. I am not myself an aged person as yet, but I can remember as far back as maybe some twenty year, when hand-labour were encouraged and respected, and no mischief-maker had ventured to introduce these here machines which is so pernicious. Now, I’m not a cloth-dresser myself, but by trade a tailor. Howsiver, my heart is of a softish nature. I’m a very feeling man, and when I see my brethren oppressed, like my great namesake of old, I stand up for ’em; for which intent I this day speak with you face to face, and advises you to part wi’ your infernal machinery, and tak on more hands.”

“What if I don’t follow your advice, Mr. Barraclough?” Moore asked.

“The Looard pardon you! The Looard soften your heart, sir!”

“Are you in connection with the Wesleyans now, Mr. Barraclough?”

“Praise God! Bless His name! I’m a joined Methody!”

“Which in no respect prevents you from being at the same time a drunkard and a swindler,” Moore said, his voice still even, but cutting. “I saw you one night a week ago laid dead-drunk by the roadside, as I returned from Stilbro’ market; and while you preach peace, you make it the business of your life to stir up dissension. You no more sympathize with the poor who are in distress than you sympathize with me. You incite them to outrage for bad purposes of your own; so does the individual called Noah of Tim’s. You two are restless, meddling, impudent scoundrels, whose chief motive-principle is a selfish ambition, as dangerous as it is puerile. The persons behind you are some of them honest though misguided men; but you two I count altogether bad.”

Barraclough made a movement to speak.

“Silence!” Moore snapped, and the word fell like a lash. “You have had your say, and now I will have mine. As to being dictated to by you, or any Jack, Jem, or Jonathan on earth, I shall not suffer it for a moment. You desire me to quit the country; you request me to part with my machinery. In case I refuse, you threaten me. I do refuse—point-blank! Here I stay, and by this mill I stand, and into it will I convey the best machinery inventors can furnish. What will you do? The utmost you can do—and this you will never dare to do—is to burn down my mill, destroy its contents, and shoot me. What then? Suppose that building was a ruin and I was a corpse—what then, you lads behind these two scamps? Would that stop invention or exhaust science? Not for the fraction of a second of time! Another and better gig-mill would rise on the ruins of this, and perhaps a more enterprising owner come in my place. Hear me! I’ll make my cloth as I please, and according to the best lights I have. In its manufacture I will employ what means I choose. Whoever, after hearing this, shall dare to interfere with me may just take the consequences. An example shall prove I’m in earnest.”

He whistled—shrill and loud. The sound sliced through the yard, through the men’s murmurs, through the mill’s distant grind. At that signal Sugden came forward, staff in hand, warrant ready: law embodied in fustian and paper.

Moore turned sharply to Barraclough.

“You were at Stilbro’,” he said. “I have proof of that. You were on the moor, you wore a mask, you knocked down one of my men with your own hand—you! a preacher of the gospel!—Sugden, arrest him!”

Sugden seized Moses.

There was a cry and a surge—a rush forward as if the whole deputation would become one body to pull him free. Boots scraped stone; voices rose—raw, dialect-thick—each man calling from his own fear and fury. It was a sound with history in it, old as hunger, old as threatened hearths; and yet here it met the newer, harsher music of industry and law.

All this time Moore’s right hand had lain hidden in his breast. Now it came out holding a pistol, its metal catching a cold glint of day.

“Both barrels are loaded,” he said, very quietly. “I’m quite determined! Keep off!”

Messrs. Helstone and Sykes met Mr. Moore again with a kind of hearty raillery, as if the whole morning’s sharp passage with the men had been but a bout at cudgels pleasantly concluded. They congratulated him—on firmness, on spirit, on the good fortune of being the man to say No when a crowd required Yes.

He listened as one listens to wind that does not cool. His face had the look of a still, dark day—no beam, no breeze; and Helstone, who could read an eye as readily as he could read a line of print, checked his own geniality as if it were an ill-timed joke over a dish left untouched. He buttoned up his compliments with his coat, turned to Sykes, and, with that decisive civility which is dismissal in a fine voice, said—

“Come, sir; your road and mine lie partly together. Had we not better bear each other company? We’ll bid Moore good-morning, and leave him to the happy fancies he seems disposed to indulge.”

“And where is Sugden?” Moore demanded, lifting his gaze at last, as if the name were a necessary ingredient in the day’s reckoning.

“Ah, ha!” cried Helstone, pleased to be useful. “I’ve not been quite idle while you were busy. I’ve been helping you a little; I flatter myself not injudiciously. I thought it better not to lose time; so, while you were parleying with that down-looking gentleman—Farren I think his name is—I opened the back window, shouted to Murgatroyd in the stable to bring Mr. Sykes’s gig round; then I smuggled Sugden and brother Moses—wooden leg and all—through the aperture, and saw them mount the gig (always with our good friend Sykes’s permission, of course). Sugden took the reins—he drives like Jehu—and in another quarter of an hour Barraclough will be safe in Stilbro’ jail.”

“Very good; thank you,” said Moore, in the same low, even tone, as if thanks were a coin he paid when due, not when he felt rich. “Good-morning, gentlemen.”

He conducted them to the door with punctilious politeness, and watched them clear his premises; then he turned back as a man might turn from a bright road into a passage where no sun falls.

All the rest of the day he remained taciturn and serious. He did not so much as bandy a repartee with Joe Scott. Joe, for his part, spoke to his master only what business demanded; yet his eyes kept sliding, sidelong and watchful, like a cook’s glance from the hearth to a guest who will not eat. He came more than once to poke the counting-house fire—raising and shifting the coals till they burned with a redder heart; and, when the mill was locked for the evening (work going but short time now, trade slack as a loaf that will not rise), he ventured, as if offering a simple restorative—

“It’s a grand evening, sir. I could wish you’d take a bit of a walk up th’ Hollow. It would do you good.”

At that, Moore gave a short laugh—sharp, unseasonable, like steel struck in a quiet room. He demanded of Joe what all this solicitude meant, and whether he took him for a woman or a child; then he snatched the keys from Joe’s hand and thrust him by the shoulders towards the door.

“Out of my way.”

Joe obeyed; but Moore called him back before he reached the yard-gate, as if some thought, buried all day under work and pride, had suddenly moved and knocked from within.

“Joe—do you know those Farrens? They are not well off, I suppose?”

“They cannot be well off, sir, when they’ve not had work as a three month. Ye’d see yoursel’ ‘at William’s sorely changed—fair paired. They’ve selled most o’ t’ stuff out o’ th’ house.”

“He was not a bad workman?”

“Ye never had a better, sir, sin’ ye began trade.”

“And decent people—the whole family?”

“Niver dacenter. Th’ wife’s a raight cant body, and as clean—ye mught eat your porridge off th’ house floor. They’re sorely comed down. I wish William could get a job as gardener or summat i’ that way; he understands gardening weel. He once lived wi’ a Scotchman that tached him the mysteries o’ that craft, as they say.”

Moore’s mouth tightened, not in anger, but in a restraint that was worse than anger—like hunger forced to sit still at a table spread for others.

“Now, then, you can go, Joe. You need not stand there staring at me.”

“Ye’ve no orders to give, sir?”

“None, but for you to take yourself off.”

Joe went.

Spring evenings can be treacherous. The day had been fine—warm even in the morning and in the noon sunshine; yet at sunset the air chilled as if some unseen door had opened to a cold pantry. The ground crisped; and before dusk a hoar frost crept, soft-footed, over grass and bud, laying its pale dust on what had dared to unfold. It whitened the pavement before Briarmains—Mr. Yorke’s house—doing its silent mischief among tender plants in his garden and on the mossy level of his lawn. The great tree by the gable nearest the road, strong in trunk and broad in arm, seemed to defy such a night; its boughs were bare still, and frost found little to bite. Behind, the grove of walnut-trees rose tall and leafless, stern as old tenants who have seen many seasons and do not flatter any.

It was dusk, moonless, though starry. Lights shone vividly from windows. The scene was neither dark nor lonely—nor even silent. Briarmains stood near the highway. It was an old place, built before that highway was cut, when a lane winding through fields had been the only approach. Briarfield lay scarce a mile off; its hum came on the wind, its glare was plain. Briar Chapel—a large, new, raw Wesleyan building—rose within a hundred yards; and a prayer-meeting was in full tumult inside, the chapel windows casting bright reflections on the road while the sound poured out: hymns that seemed less sung than shouted, as if zeal had become a kind of music and noise had been mistaken for spirit.

The words came in snatches between clamorous prayers and fearful groans—odd fragments, like crumbs from a table at which the diners ate too fast to taste.

“Oh! who can explain  
This struggle for life,  
This travail and pain,  
This trembling and strife?  
Plague, earthquake, and famine,  
And tumult and war,  
The wonderful coming  
Of Jesus declare!”

Then more: fierce images, hammered out on lungs already hoarse.

“For every fight  
Is dreadful and loud:  
The warrior’s delight  
Is slaughter and blood,  
His foes overturning,  
Till all shall expire:  
And this is with burning,  
And fuel, and fire!”

A pause—if pause it could be called—filled with groans and ejaculations. Then a cry of triumph rang from within: “I’ve found liberty!” “Doad o’ Bill’s has fun’ liberty!” and out broke the assembly again, louder, quicker, as if the building itself were an instrument played too hard.

“What a mercy is this! What a heaven of bliss!  
How unspeakably happy am I!  
Gathered into the fold,  
With Thy people enrolled,  
With Thy people to live and to die!”

And again, with the same buoyant ease of those who will not linger to savour one tune before leaping to the next:

“Oh, the goodness of God  
In employing a clod  
His tribute of glory to raise;  
His standard to bear,  
And with triumph declare  
His unspeakable riches of grace!”

They shouted of fire and flame; they clapped their hands amidst imagined burning; and the night-air carried it all, an acoustic storm rolling across hedge and stone. The roof did not fly off—which speaks volumes for the chapel’s solid slating; but the ears of any quiet creature within hearing might well have wished for the mercy of thick walls and deep rooms.

Briarmains was alive too—though with a different life. In some lower windows, candles glimmered behind curtains. Voices and laughter came muffled and domestic, not eruptive. We may pass the front door and find the heart of the house.

No company made it lively. There was none within save Mr. Yorke’s own family, assembled in their usual evening room—the back parlour, farthest to the right.

By day the windows would show stained glass: purple and amber predominating, glittering around grave medallions—Shakespeare’s suave head in one, Milton’s serene in another. On the walls hung Canadian views—green forest and blue water—between which blazed a painted night-eruption of Vesuvius, ardent and terrible, set against the cool foam and azure of cataracts and the shadowy depths of woods.

And on the hearth burned a fire such as a southerner seldom sees in a private room: a clear, hot coal fire, heaped high in an ample chimney. Mr. Yorke would have such heat even in summer, as if he believed warmth to be a necessary seasoning to thought. He sat beside it with a book in hand and a small round stand at his elbow bearing a candle; but he did not read. His eyes were on his children.

Opposite sat his wife. She might be described at length; yet one feels the description would be like setting down the measurements of a cloud. She was large, of gravest aspect; care sat on her brow and shoulders, but it was not the care that falls like necessity—it was the care that some take up as a duty and wear, exemplary, as a cloak. Mrs. Yorke held it profane to be mirthful and frivolous to be cheerful. She drew no distinctions. Yet she was a good wife, a careful mother, sincerely attached to her husband. Only, had she been able, she would have permitted him no friend but herself. His relations were insupportable to her; she kept them at arm’s length.

And still the pair agreed—perhaps because Mr. Yorke had a shadowy side as well as a sunny one, and his shadow found sympathy in his wife’s constant overcast. She was strong-minded, never weak, never trite; sternly democratic in view, cynically plain in estimate of human nature; sure of her own correctness and doubtful of everyone else’s. Her chief fault was suspicion—eternal, brooding, immitigable suspicion of men, creeds, parties, things; a mist before her eyes, a false guide at every turning.

Their children, then, were not likely to be commonplace. There were six.

The youngest was a baby on the mother’s knee—still all her own. That one she had not yet begun to doubt, suspect, condemn. It clung to her, drew its life from her, loved her with the whole blind fidelity of infancy; and because she was sure of that love, she returned it.

The two next were girls—Rose and Jessy—both now at their father’s knee. They seldom approached their mother except when duty drove them, as children are sometimes driven to bitter medicine. Rose, twelve years old, was like her father the most of all: a granite head copied in ivory. Yorke’s face was harsh; his daughter’s was softened in line and colour. It was not quite pretty; it was simple, childlike in feature; the round cheeks bloomed. But the grey eyes were not childlike—there lived in them a serious soul, young yet, but destined to mature if the body held. It held, too, something neither parent possessed in equal measure—purity, aspiration, a strength that might one day outgrow both father’s temper and mother’s gloom. Rose was still, sometimes stubborn. Her mother would have made of her a woman of dark and dreary duties; Rose had a mind thick-sown with ideas her mother had never known. To have those ideas trampled and repressed was agony. She had never rebelled; but if hard driven, she would one day rebel once for all. She loved her father; he did not rule her with iron. He was good to her, and sometimes feared she would not live—so keen and bright were the sparks that at moments flashed from her glance and lit her speech. That fear made him tender.

Jessy—little Jessy—was gay and chattering, arch, original; passionate when provoked, most affectionate when caressed; by turns gentle and rattling; exacting yet generous; fearless—of her mother, for instance, whose hard rule she had more than once defied—yet trusting to any who would help her. With her piquant face and winning ways she was made to be a pet; and her father’s pet she was. It was odd that she resembled her mother feature for feature, as Rose resembled her father, and yet the physiognomy—how different in expression, in promise.

If, in that warm back parlour with its coal-fire and stained glass, some magic mirror had been held up—if it had shown those two girls twenty years hence, you might have turned away from the picture as from a dish too bitter to finish.

First, Jessy: not here, not in England. Cypress and willow and yew; stone crosses; dim garlands of everlasting flowers. A gray marble headstone in green sod. Jessy sleeps below. She lived through an April day—much loved, much loving. Tears came often in her brief life, and sorrows; yet between she smiled and brightened whatever saw her. Her death was tranquil and happy in Rose’s guardian arms. They were alone then—two English girls watching and dying in a foreign country—and the soil of that country took Jessy’s small body and made it a grave.

And Rose, two years later: still farther. The crosses and garlands looked strange; but stranger still were the hills and woods. This was remote from England—some region of the southern hemisphere, wild and luxuriant, a virgin solitude. Unknown birds fluttered along the forest skirts. No European river ran there, and yet Rose sat by its bank thinking—quiet Yorkshire Rose, made lonely emigrant. Would she ever come back? The mirror shut, as mirrors do, when one most longs to press questions upon them.

The three eldest were boys—Matthew, Mark, and Martin—seated together in a corner, engaged in a game; and the noise of their play rose and fell like the clatter of knives on a board when impatient hands prepare a meal too quickly.

Their heads were alike at first glance, different at second, contrasted at third. Dark hair, dark eyes, red cheeks, small English features; each bore a blended resemblance to both parents; and yet each had his own stamp.

Matthew, the first-born, was no plain boy. Jet-black hair, white brow, high-coloured cheek, quick dark eyes—these were good points. And yet, as you looked, his face suggested—sinisterly and again and again—one object in the room more than any other: that painted eruption of Vesuvius. Flame and shadow seemed the elements of his soul—no daylight, no sunshine, no cool moonbeam. Cross him in the game and see the scowl. Mr. Yorke saw it and lowered his voice, pleading, “Mark and Martin, don’t anger your brother.”

This was the rule always. Both parents decried partiality in theory; no rights of primogeniture were to be allowed in their house. Yet Matthew was never to be vexed, never to be opposed. They averted provocation from him as they would avert fire from a barrel of gunpowder. “Concede, conciliate,” was their motto wherever he was concerned. Their republican household was fast making a tyrant of its own blood. The younger boys felt it, though they could not read the motive; they saw only injustice. Dragon’s teeth were sown among those olive-branches; discord would one day be the harvest.

Mark was bonny, the most regular-featured. He was calm—so calm his smile could be shrewd and his driest, cutting sayings came in the quietest tone. Yet a heavy brow spoke temper; smooth water is not always safe. He was too still to be happy. Life would bring little joy to Mark. At five-and-twenty he would wonder why people laughed, and think all fools who seemed merry. Poetry—whether in books or living—would sound to him rant and jargon. Enthusiasm he would despise. Though his body was fourteen, his soul was already thirty.

Martin, youngest of the three, promised another fate: brief or long, his life would be brilliant. He was not handsome now; there was a husk upon him, a dry shell he would wear till near twenty, then cast it off. He would make himself handsome. Uncouth manners and perhaps homely garments would cling to him for a time; but the chrysalis held its power of transfiguration, and in due season the butterfly would come. For a space he would be vain, eager for pleasure and admiration, athirst too for knowledge. He would drink deep at both fountains—enjoyment and lore. What then? The seer could not say. Martin might be remarkable; whether he would be, no open vision told.

Taken together, the six young heads held an unusual store of mental power, originality, vigour. Divided among half a dozen commonplace broods, it would have made each child above average. Mr. Yorke knew it and was proud. Yorkshire has such families among hill and wold—racy, vigorous, strong in brain and blood; turbulent in pride, intractable in native force; wanting polish, wanting docility, but sound and spirited and true-bred as eagle on cliff or steed on steppe.

A low tap came at the parlour door. The boys had been noisy over their game; and Jessy, besides, had been singing—sweetly—a Scotch song to her father. Mr. Yorke delighted in Scotch and Italian airs, and had taught his musical little daughter the best; so the ring at the outer door had gone unheard.

“Come in,” said Mrs. Yorke, in that conscientiously constrained voice of hers—solemn as if she were pronouncing sentence, though she might be but ordering pudding or calling children to sew.

And in came Robert Moore.

His habitual gravity, and his abstemiousness—for when he visited, no case of spirit decanters was ever ordered up—had so far recommended him to Mrs. Yorke that she had not yet made him the subject of private animadversion with her husband. She had not yet discovered that he was hampered by any secret intrigue, nor labelled him wolf in sheep’s clothing—discoveries she had made early in marriage about most of Mr. Yorke’s bachelor friends, and had thereafter excluded them from her board with a severity that had, perhaps, both harshness and sense.

“Well, is it you?” she said as he approached and offered his hand. “What are you roving about at this time of night for? You should be at home.”

“Can a single man be said to have a home, madam?” he asked.

“Pooh!” returned Mrs. Yorke, who despised conventional smoothness as much as her husband did, and practised it as little. “Pooh! you need not talk nonsense to me; a single man can have a home if he likes. Pray, does not your sister make a home for you?”

“Not she,” put in Mr. Yorke. “Hortense is an honest lass. But when I was Robert’s age I had five or six sisters, all as decent and proper as she is; but you see, Hesther, for all that it did not hinder me from looking out for a wife.”

“And sorely he has repented marrying me,” added Mrs. Yorke, who liked at times to crack a dry jest against matrimony, even at her own cost. “He has repented it in sackcloth and ashes, Robert Moore, as you may well believe when you see his punishment”—and she pointed to the children. “Who would burden themselves with such a set of great, rough lads as those, if they could help it? It is not only bringing them into the world, though that is bad enough, but they are all to feed, to clothe, to rear, to settle in life. Young sir, when you feel tempted to marry, think of our four sons and two daughters, and look twice before you leap.”

“I am not tempted now, at any rate,” said Moore. “I think these are not times for marrying or giving in marriage.”

This lugubrious sentiment obtained Mrs. Yorke’s approbation. She nodded and groaned assent, as if gloom were a broth that nourished her. Then, after a moment, she added—

“I make little account of the wisdom of a Solomon of your age; it will be upset by the first fancy that crosses you. Meantime, sit down, sir. You can talk, I suppose, as well sitting as standing?”

It was her way of inviting him to a chair. He had scarcely obeyed when little Jessy slipped from her father’s knee and ran straight into Mr. Moore’s arms. He held them out promptly, and lifted her lightly to his own knee, as if she were something warm and dear he did not fear to touch.

“You talk of marrying him,” she said to her mother, indignantly, with a child’s quick jealousy over what she counts her own, “and he is married now, or as good. He promised that I should be his wife last summer, the first time he saw me in my new white frock and blue sash. Didn’t he, father?” (These children were not accustomed to say papa and mamma; their mother allowed no such “namby-pamby.”)

Time went on as it always did in that part of the world—slowly, as if each day must be kneaded and proved before it could be baked into the next. Spring deepened; the hedges thickened; the fields took their green again, and gardens broke into an honest, domestic riot of leaf and bloom. In the mornings you could smell damp earth and crushed young grass, and in the evenings the air had that cool, rinsed clearness which makes a hearth-fire seem not a luxury but a right.

Yet beneath the pleasant surface England was sore. War still drew its long, rasping breath across the land; it was heard in the thud of distant taxes, in the mutter of anxious men, in the clink of coin counted too often and too late. The poor were no less poor for the blossoming of lilac; the masters were no less harassed for the singing of larks. Trade—at least certain kinds of it—felt as if a hand lay on its throat. News from the Peninsula came in broken measures: a far-off report of success, then a long, dead stretch of silence, and between, Bonaparte’s loud proclamations, like brass instruments blaring over a village where people wanted, above all things, quiet.

Those who suffered most from the war’s grind found the struggle intolerable and, to their minds, hopeless. They clamoured for peace at any price. Men like Yorke and Moore—men pushed by circumstance to the lip of bankruptcy and looking down—insisted upon peace with the ferocity of frightened self-preservation. Meetings were held; speeches made; petitions prepared and carried about like baskets meant to collect a harvest. Terms did not matter; relief did.

A man alone may be capable of generosity; men in a mass are commonly capable chiefly of appetite. The merchant was not exempt. The mercantile classes—so clever at measuring out cloth or calculating freight—were apt to measure national honour by the yard and reckon dignity by the pound. They professed to loathe war because it was bloody, because it was barbarous, because they were, as they would have it, peculiarly humane. But the same lips that spoke of gentleness could be remarkably cold when they spoke of any class not their own. Many of them grudged the poor even their bread, as if hunger were an impertinence; they questioned the very right of those who did not “trade” to live at all. Whoever did not make money was accused of eating idleness like daily food. Long might it be, one hoped, before England became only a nation of shopkeepers.

Moore, as has been seen, was no self-sacrificing patriot; the position into which he had been forced made him doubly eager to save himself. When he felt ruin creeping near for the second time, none fought more fiercely to keep his feet. He did what he could to stir northern agitation against the war, and prodded men richer than he, men with more names and more influence, to do more.

Now and then a flash of clearer sense crossed him. When he heard of Europe threatened and arming; when he saw Russia rising in her stern, frozen strength to defend even her dark despotism from foreign heel and yoke, he knew England—free England—could not then go cap in hand with concessions to an unjust conqueror. And when dispatches came—Lord Wellington’s measured, modest words, truth spoken without trumpet—Moore’s heart confessed, if only for a moment, that a steady power was with Britain’s troops: slow, vigilant, enduring, the kind of power that wins in the end.

In the end. But that end looked distant, and between Moore and it lay the immediate crush of his own affairs. He could not live on eventual victories. He had his own fate to follow; he followed it with an almost savage vigour.

He followed it so far that he came to a decisive rupture with his old Tory friend, the rector. They quarrelled at a public meeting; later, their anger hardened into pungent letters printed for all to read. Mr. Helstone denounced Moore as a Jacobin, ceased to see him, and would not speak to him when they met. He made it plain, too, and without softening, that Caroline’s intercourse with Hollow’s Cottage must cease for the present. She was to give up her French lessons.

French, he observed, was a bad and frivolous tongue at the best; the literature of it was bad and frivolous likewise, and injurious to weak female minds. He wondered—parenthetically, but with pointed scorn—what noodle had first made it fashion to teach women French at all. Nothing could be more improper; it was, he said, like feeding a rickety child on chalk and water gruel. Caroline must give up the language, and give up her cousins. They were dangerous people.

He expected opposition—at least tears. He seldom troubled himself about Caroline’s movements; still, a vague certainty possessed him that she liked going to Hollow’s Cottage, and that she liked, too, Robert Moore’s occasional presence at the rectory. The “Cossack” had noticed something else as well: when Malone stepped in of an evening to be sociable—pinching the ears of an aged black cat that shared with Miss Helstone’s feet the footstool, borrowing a fowling-piece and cracking off shots at a tool-shed door while daylight lasted, carrying in, with boisterous brusquerie, the smell of gunpowder and the draft of open passage-doors—Caroline had a trick of vanishing. She would slip upstairs and remain invisible until she was called down to supper.

But when Robert Moore was the guest, there were no fireworks of noise. He did not torment the cat; he sometimes coaxed it from the stool to his knee and let it purr and climb, rubbing its head against his cheek with the complacence of a creature that knows gentleness by touch. There was no sulphurous perfume, no bang of firearms, no boasting. Yet Caroline stayed in the room and seemed to find deep content in stitching Jew-basket pin-cushions and knitting socks meant for missionary baskets.

She was very quiet; Robert scarcely addressed her. Nevertheless, Mr. Helstone was not one of those elderly gentlemen easily blinded. He watched them when they bade each other good-night. He had seen their eyes meet once—only once.

Some natures would have taken pleasure in that glance, because it held no harm and a little innocent delight. It was not a glance of mutual intelligence; there were no secrets between them. There was no craft, no concealment—only Moore’s eyes, looking into Caroline’s, recognising clearness and gentleness; and Caroline’s eyes, meeting his, confessing manliness and search. Each acknowledged the other’s charm in his or her own way. Moore smiled slightly; Caroline coloured slightly.

Mr. Helstone could have rated them both on the spot. They annoyed him. Why? He could not have explained. Asked what Moore deserved, he would have answered “a horsewhip.” Asked what Caroline deserved, he would have adjudged her a box on the ear. Pressed for a reason, he would have stormed against flirtation and love-making and vowed there should be no such folly under his roof.

Political reasons, plus these private irritations, fixed his resolution to separate the cousins. He announced his will one evening while Caroline sat at work near the drawing-room window. The light fell full upon her face, and, noticing it, he was struck by something he had not till then admitted to himself: she looked paler—quieter—than she used to look. He had observed, too, that Robert Moore’s name had not, for some three weeks, dropped from her lips, and that during the same space of time Moore had not come to the rectory. A suspicion of clandestine meetings haunted Mr. Helstone’s mind—because he had an indifferent opinion of women, and, where he did not understand them, he suspected. He believed they required constant watching.

In a tone dryly significant, he told her she was to cease her daily visits to the Hollow. He expected a start and a look of depreciation. The start came, but slight. No look at all was directed to him.

“Do you hear me?” he asked.

“Yes, uncle.”

“Of course you mean to attend to what I say?”

“Yes, certainly.”

“And there must be no letter-scribbling to your cousin Hortense—no intercourse whatever. I do not approve of the principles of the family. They are Jacobinical.”

“Very well,” said Caroline quietly.

She acquiesced. There was no vexed flush, no rising tears. The thoughtful shadow that had lain on her features before he spoke remained undisturbed; she was obedient.

Perfectly—because his mandate chimed with her own judgement already formed; because it had become pain to her to go to Hollow’s Cottage. Nothing met her there now but disappointment. Hope and love had quitted that small tenement, for Robert seemed to have deserted it.

Whenever she asked after him—which she did very seldom, since the utterance of his name made heat rush to her face—the answer was always the same: he was from home, or he was absorbed in business. Hortense feared he was killing himself by application. He scarcely ever took a meal in the house; he lived in the counting-house.

At church only did Caroline have the chance of seeing him; and there she rarely looked. It was too much pain and too much pleasure to look; it roused too much feeling, and she had learned, sharply, that her feeling was wasted.

Once, on a dark, wet Sunday—when the congregation was thin, and when especially certain ladies were absent whose observing eyes and tomahawk tongues Caroline feared—she allowed her gaze to seek Robert’s pew and rest awhile on its occupant. He sat alone. Hortense, guided by prudent considerations touching the rain and a new spring chapeau, had stayed at home.

During the sermon, he sat with folded arms, his eyes cast down, looking sad and abstracted. When depressed, the very colour of his face seemed darker; to-day cheek and forehead wore their most sober olive, as if all warmth had been drawn inward and locked away.

By instinct, as she studied that clouded countenance, Caroline knew his thoughts were running in no familiar channel; they were far from her and far from anything she could comprehend or share. Nothing they had ever talked of together was in his mind now. He was wrapped from her by interests and responsibilities in which she could have no part—not because she was unwilling, but because the world would not admit it.

She pondered on the subject with earnestness that tried to be practical. She speculated on his life, his fears, his fate; she mused over the mystery called “business,” and made herself attempt to understand it—not as a romantic riddle, but as a daily discipline: its liabilities, duties, exactions; the state of mind of a man of business, the sleeplessness it brewed, the sharpness it demanded. She wished, sincerely, to see things as they were. Here and there she caught a thin ray of truth and hoped it might be enough to guide her.

“Different, indeed,” she concluded, “is Robert’s condition to mine. I think only of him; he has no room, no leisure, to think of me. Love has been for two years the predominant feeling of my heart—always there, always awake. Quite other feelings absorb his reflections. He is rising now: service is over. Will he turn his head toward this pew? No—not once. He has not one look for me. That is hard. A kind glance would have made me happy till to-morrow. I have not got it; he would not give it; he is gone. Strange that grief should almost choke me because another human being’s eye has failed to greet mine.”

That Sunday evening, as Mr. Malone came, as usual, to spend it with the rector, Caroline withdrew after tea to her chamber. Fanny, knowing her habits, had lit a cheerful little fire, for the weather was gusty and chill. The flame caught on the coals with a faint crackle—one of those small, living sounds that make loneliness bearable; outside, the east wind sobbed at the casement, and the rain tapped with a steady, thin insistence, like fingers too tired to knock properly.

Closeted there, silent and solitary, what could she do but think? She paced noiselessly on the carpet, head drooped, hands folded. It was irksome to sit; reflection ran through her mind like water through a narrow channel—rapid, continuous, mutely excited.

Mute was the room, mute the house. The double door of the study muffled the gentlemen’s voices to a low rumour, as if male talk were a far-off sea. The servants were quiet in the kitchen, engaged with books their young mistress had lent them—books she had said were fit for Sunday reading. On Caroline’s own table lay another of the same sort, open; but she could not read it. Its theology was incomprehensible; besides, her mind was already teeming with another language—one made of remembered look and tone, of footsteps on gravel, of the hush in Hollow’s copse when the birds paused, and then began again.

Her imagination filled with pictures: Moore’s face and form; scenes where he and she had been together. She saw winter firelight in the rectory parlour, when the kettle sang its thin, comforting song and the cat purred like a small engine of content; she saw a hot summer afternoon in the bosom of Nunnely Wood, where the air smelled of resin and leaf-mould and bruised fern; she saw mild spring and mellow autumn moments in Hollow’s Copse, when the May cuckoo called and called as if naming something that could not be answered in human words.

And she saw, with a tenderness that was half hunger, those simple woodland feasts she had made without calling them such: September’s treasure of nuts and ripe blackberries gathered into a little basket, shaded with green leaves and fresh blossoms. She had taken pleasure in arranging it—choosing the darkest berries, wiping them with her handkerchief, cracking the nuts, testing their sweetness; and then, later, giving them to Moore berry by berry, nut by nut, like a bird feeding its fledgling—an intimate rite of sustenance that asked no declaration and yet meant more than declarations.

Robert’s voice seemed close in her ear; his few caresses appeared renewed. Then, because these joys were but air, they collapsed. The pictures faded; the voice failed; the visionary clasp melted cold from her hand. Where lips had once left a warm seal on her forehead, she felt now as if a sleety raindrop had struck and slid away.

She returned from an enchanted region to the real. For Nunnely Wood in June, she saw her narrow chamber; for bird-song, she heard rain on the pane; for the sigh of a south wind, the sob of the east; and for Moore’s manly presence, she had only the thin illusion of her own dim shadow on the wall.

Turning from that pale phantom—her outline, her drooped attitude, her colourless tresses—she sat down, because inaction suited the decline of her spirits. She said to herself, with a clearness that frightened her, “I have to live, perhaps, till seventy years. As far as I know, I have good health; half a century of existence may lie before me. How am I to occupy it? What am I to do to fill the interval of time which spreads between me and the grave?”

She reflected.

“I shall not be married, it appears,” she went on. “If Robert does not care for me, I shall never have a husband to love, nor little children to take care of. Till lately I reckoned securely on the duties and affections of wife and mother to occupy my life. I considered—somehow—as a matter of course, that I was growing up to the ordinary destiny, and never troubled myself to seek any other. Now I perceive plainly I may have been mistaken. Probably I shall be an old maid. I shall live to see Robert married to some one else—some rich lady. I shall never marry. What was I created for? Where is my place in the world?”

Again she mused, and the rain kept its fine, ceaseless patter, as if the sky were sifting a handful of grains over the roof.

“Ah! I see,” she pursued presently; “that is the question which most old maids are puzzled to solve. Other people solve it for them by saying, ‘Your place is to do good to others, to be helpful whenever help is wanted.’ That is right in some measure—and very convenient for the people who hold it. Certain sets of human beings are very apt to maintain that other sets should give up their lives to them and their service, and then they requite them by praise; they call them devoted and virtuous. Is this enough? Is it to live? Is there not a terrible hollowness—mockery—want—in that existence which is given away to others for want of something of your own to bestow it on? I suspect there is. Does virtue lie in the abnegation of self? I do not believe it. Undue humility makes tyranny; weak concession creates selfishness.”

Her thoughts—always sensitive to the way words were used as a bridle—moved on with a troubled precision.

“The Romish religion especially teaches renunciation of self and submission to others, and nowhere are found so many grasping tyrants as in the ranks of the Romish priesthood. Each human being has his share of rights. I suspect it would conduce to the happiness and welfare of all if each knew his allotment, and held to it as tenaciously as the martyr to his creed. Queer thoughts these that surge in my mind. Are they right thoughts? I am not certain.”

The fire sank; its small red heart beat lower and lower. The room, with its muffled male voices far away and the steady rain near at hand, felt like a world reduced to two kinds of sound: authority at a distance, weather at the window. She listened, without meaning to, and the very listening sharpened her sense of how much a life could be governed by what it habitually heard—by peace or by din, by gentle domestic noises or by harsh, public clamours.

“Well,” she continued, “life is short at the best. Seventy years, they say, pass like vapour, like a dream when one awaketh; and every path trod by human feet terminates in one bourne—the grave, the little chink in the surface of this great globe, the furrow where the mighty husbandman with the scythe deposits the seed shaken from the ripe stem; and there it falls, decays, and thence springs again when the world has rolled round a few times more.”

So much for the body. The soul meantime—so she had been taught—wings its long flight upward, folds its wings on the brink of the sea of fire and glass, and, gazing down through burning clearness, finds mirrored there the vision of the Christian’s triple Godhead—the sovereign Father, the mediating Son, the Creator Spirit. Such words, at least, had been chosen to express what is inexpressible, to describe what baffles description. The soul’s real hereafter—who should guess?

Her fire was down to its last cinder. Malone had departed. The study bell rang for prayers.

The next day Caroline spent altogether alone, her uncle being gone to dine with his friend Dr. Boultby, vicar of Whinbury. The hours moved, and still she talked inwardly in the same strain—looking forward, asking what she was to do with life. Fanny, passing in and out on housemaid errands, perceived that her young mistress sat very still, always in the same place, bent industriously over some work. She did not lift her head to speak, as was her custom; and when Fanny remarked that the day was fine and she ought to take a walk, she only answered, “It is cold.”

“You are very diligent at that sewing, Miss Caroline,” continued the girl, coming nearer to the little table.

“I am tired of it, Fanny.”

“Then why do you go on with it? Put it down. Read—or do something to amuse you.”

“It is solitary in this house, Fanny. Don’t you think so?”

“I don’t find it so, miss. Me and Eliza are company for one another; but you are quite too still. You should visit more. Now be persuaded: go upstairs and dress yourself smart, and go and take tea, in a friendly way, with Miss Mann or Miss Ainley. I am certain either of those ladies would be delighted to see you.”

“But their houses are dismal: they are both old maids. I am certain old maids are a very unhappy race.”

“Not they, miss. They can’t be unhappy; they take such care of themselves. They are all selfish.”

“Miss Ainley is not selfish, Fanny. She is always doing good. How devotedly kind she was to her step-mother as long as the old lady lived; and now, when she is quite alone in the world, without brother or sister or any one to care for her, how charitable she is to the poor—as far as her means permit! Still nobody thinks much of her, or has pleasure in going to see her; and how gentlemen always sneer at her!”

“They shouldn’t, miss. I believe she is a good woman. But gentlemen think only of ladies’ looks.”

“I’ll go and see her,” cried Caroline, starting up as if the very act of moving might shake off the heaviness. “And if she asks me to stay to tea, I’ll stay. How wrong it is to neglect people because they are not pretty and young and merry! And I will certainly call to see Miss Mann too. She may not be amiable, but what has made her unamiable? What has life been to her?”

Fanny helped her put away her work, and then assisted her to dress. She smoothed the soft, full curls until they shone, and tied the sash of Caroline’s brown silk frock with a practised neatness.

“You’ll not be an old maid, Miss Caroline,” she said, with a confidence that had more kindness than reason in it. “There are no signs of an old maid about you.”

Caroline looked into the little mirror and thought she saw some signs. In the last month she had altered: her complexion was paler; a wan shade circled her eyes; her face held a dejected quietness. She was not, in short, so fresh as she used to be. She hinted as much to Fanny, who would not answer directly—only said people’s looks did vary, and that at Caroline’s age a little falling away signified nothing; she would soon come round again, plumper and rosier than ever.

Having delivered this reassurance, Fanny wrapped her with singular zeal in warm shawls and handkerchiefs, piling them on as if to keep out not merely the chill of the air but the chill of thought. Caroline, nearly smothered with the weight, was fain at last to resist further additions; and thus prepared, she went out, carrying with her, into the spring day’s thin sunshine and soft wind, a heart full of unanswered questions and a resolve—small, perhaps, but honest—to seek companionship where the world least praised it.

Yet Caroline would not lie down and let the sorrow trample her. There was a native stoutness in her, a stubborn, quiet grain of strength that belonged not to counsel or company, but to solitude. When there is no witness to applaud, no hand to steady, and no voice to say, “This will pass,” the heart fights hardest, because defeat then seems not merely pain, but shame.

So she laboured at herself as one labours at a refractory dough: pressing, folding, beating down the air-bubbles of wild thought, trying to make the mass smooth enough to bear handling. She was never so busy. She made duties where none were demanded, and if the house offered none, she went out and sought them on the roads. She walked in all weathers—long, lonely walks—through drizzle that smelled of wet stones and peat, through sharp winds that combed the hedgerows and set the bare twigs clicking like brittle bones, through sunlight that warmed the green rise of Nunnely Common as if it were a hearthstone. Day after day she came home in the evening pale and worn; yet, once her bonnet and shawl were cast aside, she would not rest. She paced her room as if movement were a charm against thinking. Sometimes she did not sit down till faintness forced her.

She said she meant to tire herself into sleep. But night was her enemy. When other heads sank into pillows, hers was lifted, listening—listening to the house’s minute sounds, the settling timbers, the thin stir of a branch against a pane, the far-off dog that answered another across the fields. The silence was not kind silence; it had hollows in it where her own thoughts echoed. Often—unhappy girl—she cried in that pitch of despair which is not dramatic, only intolerable; a rushing helplessness that smote her down and made her childlike, as if all her careful daytime discipline were a crust that broke the instant darkness touched it.

Then temptation came, not with any grand shape, but with the small, plausible whisper of ink and paper. Write to Robert. Say you are unhappy. Say you fear he will forget you. Beg him—only his friendship, of course; she always told herself that word, as though it were a cold cloth to fever—beg him to remember, to write sometimes. One or two such letters she did write. She formed his name with a trembling precision, and the strokes looked to her like a kind of touch. But she never sent them. Shame sealed the envelope, and good sense locked it away.

At last the life she led tightened like a cord. She felt she could bear no more—that she must find change, or her heart and head would fail together under the strain. She wanted to leave Briarfield—far, far away. Another longing, deeper and more secret, grew alongside this: to discover her mother, to know her. Yet dread went with the desire. If she found her, could she love her? No one had ever spoken warmly of Mrs. James Helstone. The few times Caroline had heard her mentioned, the tone had been cool, as of something puzzling and best left untouched. Her uncle’s very silence carried an aversion. An old servant, who had lived with Mrs. Helstone after her marriage, spoke with chilling reserve: “queer,” sometimes; “I never understood her,” often. Those words fell like frost on the daughter’s heart. They suggested that perhaps it was safer never to know a parent than to know and fail to cherish her.

Still, one plan took shape, the only one she could imagine that promised relief: she would take a situation—become a governess. She could do nothing else. A small incident—one of those moments when a soul that has been wavering is suddenly pricked into decision—brought her at last to the courage of speaking.

Her walks were always on solitary roads, but whichever way she went—along the drear skirts of Stilbro’ Moor or the brighter stretch of common—she so contrived her homeward path that it passed near the Hollow. She rarely went down into the den, but almost every twilight found her on its brink, as regularly as the first stars climbed over the hill-crests. Her resting-place was by a certain stile under a certain old thorn. There she could look down on the cottage, the mill, the dark, still dam holding the sky’s reflection as a pan holds broth; the little garden plot lying dewy and quiet. From that point she could see the counting-house window; and at a fixed hour, from its panes, a ray would suddenly shoot—lamp-light. She went to watch for that ray as other women might go to listen for a footstep.

Sometimes it was bright and steady in clear air; sometimes it shivered thin through mist; sometimes rain slanted between her eyes and the lattice, breaking it into winking fragments. She came in all weathers.

There were nights when it did not appear. Then she knew Robert was away, and she went home with sorrow doubled, as if absence were not merely distance but refusal. When the light kindled, her spirits rose on the instant, on no rational ground—only on the sweet, foolish persuasion that where he was, hope must also be. If, while she gazed, a shadow crossed between lamp and lattice, her heart leaped. That shadow was Robert; she had seen him. She would go back comforted, carrying a clearer image of his face, a more distinct recollection of his voice and smile, his carriage—little fragments of him, like crumbs gathered and hoarded because there is no loaf. Along with them often came the thought—too tender to be called expectation, too strong to be called fancy—that if she were near him, he might yet welcome her; that now, at this moment, he might be willing to stretch out his hand and draw her to him and shelter her at his side as once he used. On such a night, even if she wept, her tears seemed less scalding; the pillow felt softer; her temples ached less against it.

The shortest path from the Hollow to the rectory wound near a certain mansion, tenantless by its owner for ten years, yet no ruin—Fieldhead. Mr. Yorke had had it kept in repair; an old gardener and his wife lived there, tended the grounds, and kept the house habitable, so that its emptiness was not squalor but a kind of maintained pause, like a table laid and waiting.

If Fieldhead had few other virtues, it was picturesque. Its regular grey front was softened by time into mossy, pencilled colour; its old latticed windows, stone porch, roof and chimneys had the richness of things long looked at and long weathered. Behind, trees rose bold and spreading; in front, the cedar stood grand and dark. Granite urns on the garden wall and the fretted arch of the gateway seemed made to catch an artist’s eye.

One mild May evening, near moonrise, Caroline passed close by, weary, yet unwilling to go home, where the night’s thorny bed of grief waited. She sat down on the mossy ground near the gate and looked through towards cedar and mansion. The night was still, dewy, cloudless; the westward gables held a clear amber from the horizon. The oaks behind were black; the cedar blacker—its dense boughs like a canopy cut from raven cloth. Under them the sky opened, gravely blue, and there hung the moon, solemn and mild, looking down on Caroline as if it had watched women suffer there before.

She found it mournfully lovely. She wished she could be happy; she wished she could have that inward peace which seems, to those who lack it, as simple as bread and as unattainable. She wondered—half child, half rebel—whether Providence had no pity on her, and would not help or console her. Old ballads rose in her mind—lovers meeting at twilight under trees, vows whispered, hands clasped. Such a tryst, in such a scene, she thought, would be bliss.

Where was Robert now? Not at the Hollow; she had watched long, and his lamp had not appeared. Would she and Moore ever meet and speak again? The question had scarcely formed when the door within the stone porch opened. Two men came out—one elderly and white-headed, the other young, dark-haired, and tall. They crossed the lawn, went out through a portal in the garden wall, crossed the road, passed the stile, descended the fields, and disappeared. Robert Moore had passed her with his friend Mr. Yorke. Neither had seen her.

It was over almost before she could draw breath; but that brief sight ran through her like fire. It found her despairing; it left her desperate—two states, and yet different as numbness from pain.

“Oh, had he but been alone! had he but seen me!” was her cry. “He would have said something. He would have given me his hand. He does—he must—love me a little. In his eye, on his lips, I should have read comfort. But the chance is lost. The wind, the cloud’s shadow, do not pass more silently, more emptily than he. I have been mocked, and Heaven is cruel!”

In utter sickness of longing and disappointment she went home.

The next morning, at breakfast, she appeared white-cheeked and wretched, like one who had walked with a ghost. She asked her uncle, as steadily as she could, “Have you any objection, uncle, to my inquiring for a situation in a family?”

Mr. Helstone, ignorant as the table that held his coffee-cup of all she had undergone, scarcely believed his ears.

“What whim now?” he demanded. “Are you bewitched? What can you mean?”

“I am not well, and need a change,” she answered.

He looked at her then—really looked. He saw, though he did not understand. The rose had dwindled to a mere snowdrop; bloom had gone, flesh wasted; she sat drooping, colourless, thin. Only her brown eyes remained soft, and her hair still flowed in abundance.

“What on earth is the matter with you?” he said. “What is wrong? How are you ailing?”

She could not speak. The brown eyes filled; the lips, faintly tinted, trembled.

“Look out for a situation, indeed! What situation are you fit for? What have you been doing with yourself? You are not well.”

“I should be well if I went from home.”

“These women are incomprehensible,” he broke out. “They startle you with unpleasant surprises. To-day they’re bouncing, buxom, red as cherries, round as apples; to-morrow they’re effete as dead weeds, blanched and broken down. And why? That’s the puzzle. She has her meals, her liberty, a good house, good clothes. A while since that sufficed to keep her handsome and cheery; and there she sits now, a poor little pale puling chit. Provoking! Then comes the question, what is to be done? I suppose I must send for advice. Will you have a doctor, child?”

“No, uncle. I don’t want one. A doctor could do me no good. I merely want change of air and scene.”

“Well, if that be the caprice, it shall be gratified. You shall go to a watering-place. I don’t mind the expense. Fanny shall accompany you.”

“But, uncle, some day I must do something for myself. I have no fortune. I had better begin now.”

“While I live, you shall not turn out as a governess, Caroline. I will not have it said that my niece is a governess.”

“But the later in life one makes a change of that sort, the more difficult and painful it is. I should wish to get accustomed to the yoke before any habits of ease and independence are formed.”

“I beg you will not harass me. I mean to provide for you. I have always meant it. I will purchase an annuity. Bless me! I am but fifty-five; my health and constitution are excellent. There is plenty of time. Don’t make yourself anxious respecting the future. Is that what frets you?”

“No, uncle; but I long for a change.”

He laughed shortly. “There speaks the woman! A change! a change! Always fantastical and whimsical. Well, it’s in her sex.”

“But it is not fantasy and whim, uncle.”

“What is it then?”

“Necessity, I think. I feel weaker than formerly. I believe I should have more to do.”

“Admirable! She feels weak, and therefore she should be set to hard labour—‘clair comme le jour,’ as Moore—confound Moore! You shall go to Cliff Bridge; and there are two guineas to buy a new frock. Come, Cary, never fear. We’ll find balm in Gilead.”

“Uncle, I wish you were less generous and more—”

“More what?”

Sympathizing was the word on Caroline’s lips; she swallowed it. She knew the sound of it would make him laugh the more. He would have taken it for a kind of soft nonsense, a foreign importation into a plain English house. Finding her silent, he concluded, “The fact is, you don’t know precisely what you want.”

“Only to be a governess.”

“Pooh! mere nonsense! I’ll not hear of governessing. Don’t mention it again. It is rather too feminine a fancy. I have finished breakfast. Ring the bell. Put all crotchets out of your head, and run away and amuse yourself.”

“With what? My doll?” she asked herself, as she left the room.

A week or two passed; her health neither worsened nor improved. She had reached that strange middle state when, if the seeds of some fatal malady had been in her constitution, misery might have forced it rapidly to bloom and kill; but if no such seed existed, sorrow could do its worst and still not take life—only its colour, its ease, its spring. She felt no dying sickness; there was no sharp pain—only diminished appetite, which she understood because she wept at night; lessened strength, which she could explain because sleep was shy and when it came brought distressing dreams. In the far future she still believed—dimly, stubbornly—that this passage might be crossed, and that she might one day be calm again, though perhaps never again happy.

Her uncle urged her to visit—to accept the frequent invitations of their acquaintance. She evaded them. She could not be cheerful in company, and she felt observed with curiosity rather than tenderness. Old ladies offered advice and nostrums; young ladies looked at her in a way she understood and shrank from. Their eyes said they knew she had been “disappointed,” as custom phrases it; by whom they were not sure.

Commonplace young ladies can be as hard as commonplace young gentlemen—worldly, selfish, quick to despise what hurts. They seem to regard calamity as a judgment on inferiority. With them, to “love” is to scheme for a match; to be “disappointed” is to have the scheme detected and thwarted. They suppose others feel as they do, and judge accordingly.

Caroline knew this, partly by instinct, partly by observation. She kept her pale face and wasted figure as much out of sight as possible. Living thus in seclusion, she ceased to hear the neighbourhood’s small news.

One morning her uncle came into the parlour where she sat trying to coax pleasure out of painting—a little group of wild flowers she had gathered under a hedge at the top of the Hollow fields. Their scent still clung to her fingers: crushed stem and green bitterness, with a faint honey from the smallest bloom—tiny comforts she could at least translate into colour. Mr. Helstone spoke abruptly.

“Come, child, you are always stooping over palette, or book, or sampler; leave that tinting work. By-the-bye, do you put your pencil to your lips when you paint?”

“Sometimes, uncle, when I forget.”

“Then it is that which is poisoning you. The paints are deleterious. There is white lead and red lead, and verdigris, and gamboge, and twenty other poisons in those colour-cakes. Lock them up! Lock them up! Get your bonnet on. I want you to make a call with me.”

“With you, uncle?”

Surprise made her voice thin. She was not accustomed to accompany him on visits; she neither rode nor walked out with him for such purpose.

“Quick! quick! I am always busy, you know. I have no time to lose.”

She gathered up her brushes and saucers, asked where they were going.

“To Fieldhead.”

“Fieldhead! What—to see old James Booth, the gardener? Is he ill?”

“We are going to see Miss Shirley Keeldar.”

“Miss Keeldar! Is she coming to Yorkshire? Is she at Fieldhead?”

“She is. She has been there a week. I met her at a party last night—that party to which you would not go. I was pleased with her. I choose that you shall make her acquaintance. It will do you good.”

“She is now come of age, I suppose?”

“She is come of age, and will reside for a time on her property. I lectured her on the subject; I showed her her duty. She is not intractable. She is rather a fine girl; she will teach you what it is to have a sprightly spirit. Nothing lackadaisical about her.”

“I don’t think she will want to see me, or to have me introduced to her. What good can I do her? How can I amuse her?”

“Pshaw! Put your bonnet on.”

“Is she proud, uncle?”

“Don’t know. You hardly imagine she would show her pride to me, I suppose? A chit like that would scarcely presume to give herself airs with the rector of her parish, however rich she might be.”

“No. But how did she behave to other people?”

“Didn’t observe. She holds her head high, and probably can be saucy enough where she dare. She wouldn’t be a woman otherwise. There! Away now for your bonnet at once!”

Caroline’s confidence had never been robust; weakness of body and depression of spirits did not strengthen it. As they walked up the broad paved approach from Fieldhead’s gateway to its porch, she quailed, despite all rebukes she tried to give herself. She followed Mr. Helstone reluctantly through the porch into the old vestibule.

It was very sombre—long, vast, and dark; one latticed window admitted a dim, grudging light. The wide chimney held no fire in that warm weather; willow boughs filled it instead, their pale leaves smelling faintly of sap and water. Above, a gallery ran opposite the entrance, lost towards the ceiling in shadow. Carved stags’ heads with real antlers looked down grotesquely from the walls. The house was neither grand nor comfortable: antique, rambling, incommodious. Yet it carried an authority of age. A thousand a year belonged to it, descending for lack of male heirs upon a female; mercantile families nearby could boast twice the income, but the Keeldars, by antiquity and the title of lords of the manor, took precedence.

They were shown into a parlour lined with oak—dark, glossy panels that made the room seem at once rich and chilly, as if it had been polished with patience but warmed by no affection. The furniture was old, solid, sincere. On each side of the high mantelpiece stood antique chairs like little wooden thrones; in one sat a lady.

If this were Miss Keeldar, she must have come of age twenty years ago. She was matronly in form, capless, with auburn hair still undimmed and features small and naturally young-looking; yet her aspect was not youthful, nor did she seem to desire it. Her gown, though of handsome material, was arranged in scanty folds after an obsolete mode; the oddity suggested eccentricity at first glance.

She received them with a mixture of ceremony and diffidence peculiarly English: unsure of her own power to please, anxious to be proper, desirous—if possible—of being agreeable without daring to trust herself. Yet her embarrassment went beyond ordinary shyness. Caroline felt it, and, knowing what is kind to the timid, sat quietly near her and began to talk in a gentle, easy way—an ease that came to Caroline now because she was, for once, the steadier of two.

Alone, they might have suited at once. The lady had the clearest voice imaginable—soft and tuneful, more like a well-kept instrument than could have been expected from forty years—and her form inclined to comfortable fulness. Caroline liked that voice; it made amends for the formal correctness of her language. In ten minutes, liking would have been mutual, and friendship possible.

But Mr. Helstone stood on the rug, looking at both—particularly at the strange lady—with a sarcastic, keen eye that expressed impatience of ceremony and annoyance at her want of boldness. His hard gaze and rasping tone discomfited her more and more. She tried to offer little speeches about the weather and the country, but Mr. Helstone affected not to hear distinctly. Whatever she said he made her repeat; each elaborately-constructed nothing had to be spoken twice, as if words were heavy loads and she had to lift them again. The effort soon overwhelmed her. She rose at last in a flutter, murmuring nervously that she did not know what detained Miss Keeldar and that she would go and look for her—when Miss Keeldar spared her.

A glass door from the garden opened, and in came a girl erect and slight, bringing with her the outdoor air—leaf-coolness, earth-damp, the faint aromatic crush of bruised herbs. She carried in her left hand a little silk apron full of flowers, gathered as if she had been busy among borders and beds, and she gave Mr. Helstone her right hand with pleasant readiness.

“I knew you would come to see me,” she said, “though you do think Mr. Yorke has made me a Jacobin. Good-morning.”

“But we’ll not have you a Jacobin,” returned he. “No, Miss Shirley; they shall not steal the flower of my parish from me. Now that you are amongst us, you shall be my pupil in politics and religion; I’ll teach you sound doctrine on both points.”

Shirley proved she had meant what she said—meant it with the frank obstinacy of a nature used to having its affections answered—by coming often to the rectory, and, oftener still, by contriving that Caroline should come to Fieldhead. Had Shirley waited, she might have waited long; for Miss Helstone did not readily believe herself desired. She carried, like a pinched shawl about her shoulders, a shy persuasion that she could not be wanted—could not enliven any circle—least of all the bright circle that seemed to move about Shirley Keeldar as naturally as summer air about a lark.

Yet no one, however rich, however young, however dazzling in talk and motion, is independent of genial companionship. Shirley had, within a month, been called on, visited, invited, and claimed by the neighbourhood. She knew all the Misses Sykes, all the Misses Pearson, and those two faultless Misses Wynne of Walden Hall; she could thread their drawing-rooms as easily as she threaded the shrubbery walks at Fieldhead. But it appeared she found none of them, in her own phrase, “genial.” She would say, with a careless candour that was part pride, part mischief, to Mrs. Pryor, “If I had the bliss of being Shirley Keeldar, Esq., lord of the manor of Briarfield, there is not a single fair one in this parish, nor in the two adjoining, whom I should feel disposed to request to become Mrs. Keeldar, lady of the manor.”

Mrs. Pryor, who received most of her pupil’s outbursts with a quietness that neither chilled nor indulged, answered, as she sat with her work laid straight upon her knee, “My dear, do not allow that habit of speaking of yourself as a gentleman to be confirmed. It is a strange one. Those who do not know you, hearing you, would think you affected masculine manners.”

Shirley never laughed at Mrs. Pryor; and if she had, she would have shown weakness. Quiet worth, even when it is formal, has a claim to reverence; the very little prim ways of that lady—harmless as the orderly folding of a napkin—were respectable in Shirley’s eyes. She took the remonstrance in silence, stood near the window, and looked out upon the great cedar on the lawn. A bird was on one of the lower boughs; its throat swelled and fell with a clean, content sound that seemed to rinse the room of its pauses.

Presently Shirley began to answer it—at first a low chirrup, then clearer; then, forgetting herself, she whistled. The whistle struck into a tune, and she carried it with a sweet precision, as if she had a musician’s palate for notes—knowing which should be crisp, which lingering, which bright as a drop of water on stone.

“My dear!” expostulated Mrs. Pryor.

“Was I whistling?” said Shirley, stopping short. “I forgot. I beg your pardon, ma’am. I had resolved to take care not to whistle before you.”

“But, Miss Keeldar, where did you learn to whistle? You must have got the habit since you came down into Yorkshire. I never knew you guilty of it before.”

“Oh! I learned to whistle a long while ago.”

“Who taught you?”

“No one. I took it up by listening, and I had laid it down again. But lately—yesterday evening—as I was coming up our lane, I heard a gentleman whistling that very tune in the field on the other side of the hedge, and that reminded me.”

“What gentleman was it?”

“We have only one gentleman in this region, ma’am, and that is Mr. Moore—at least he is the only gentleman who is not gray-haired. My two venerable favourites, Mr. Helstone and Mr. Yorke, it is true, are fine old beaus, infinitely better than any of the stupid young ones.”

Mrs. Pryor was silent; her silence had always an edge—as if it were cut from something firm.

“You do not like Mr. Helstone, ma’am?” Shirley pursued, turning from the window.

“My dear, Mr. Helstone’s office secures him from criticism.”

“You generally contrive to leave the room when he is announced.”

“Do you walk out this morning, my dear?”

“Yes. I shall go to the rectory, and seek and find Caroline Helstone, and make her take some exercise. She shall have a breezy walk over Nunnely Common.”

“If you go in that direction, my dear, have the goodness to remind Miss Helstone to wrap up well; there is a fresh wind, and she appears to me to require care.”

“You shall be minutely obeyed, Mrs. Pryor. Meantime, will you not accompany us yourself?”

“No, my love; I should be a restraint upon you. I am stout, and cannot walk so quickly as you would wish to do.”

So Shirley went alone, and she brought Caroline back—brought her, at first, like some timid creature coaxed from its shelter by a hand that offers no snare. Once they were fairly out on the quiet road, with the open sweep of Nunnely Common spreading before them—green turf flowing away into ridges of heath—Shirley made conversation as naturally as she drew breath. The first diffidence once overcome, Caroline felt relief in speaking; there was a comfort in Shirley’s companionship that did not exact perpetual effort, but warmed and encouraged, as a well-tended hearth warms without scorching.

A first exchange of slight remarks sufficed to give each an idea of the other. Shirley said she liked the green sweep of the common turf, and, better still, the heath on its ridges, because it reminded her of moors—moors she had seen in travelling near the Scottish border.

“I remember,” she said, “a district we crossed one long afternoon, on a sultry but sunless summer day. We journeyed from noon till sunset over what seemed a boundless waste of deep heath; and we saw nothing but wild sheep, and heard nothing but the cries of wild birds.”

“I know how the heath would look on such a day,” said Caroline, the quiet accuracy of her mind finding its picture at once: “purple-black—a deeper shade of the sky-tint; and that would be livid.”

“Yes—quite livid; with brassy edges to the clouds, and here and there a white gleam, more ghastly than the lurid tinge; and as you looked you momentarily expected it would kindle into blinding lightning.”

“Did it thunder?”

“It muttered distant peals; but the storm did not break till evening, after we had reached our inn—an isolated house at the foot of a range of mountains.”

“Did you watch the clouds come down over the mountains?”

“I did. I stood at the window an hour watching them. The hills seemed rolled in a sullen mist; and when the rain fell in whitening sheets, suddenly they were blotted from the prospect—they were washed from the world.”
