“I have seen such storms in hilly parts of Yorkshire,” Caroline answered. “At their riotous climax—while the sky was all cataract, the earth all flood—I have remembered the Deluge.”

“It is singularly reviving,” said Shirley, “after such hurricanes, to feel calm return; and from the opening clouds to receive a consolatory gleam—softly testifying that the sun is not quenched.”

“Miss Keeldar,” Caroline said then, stopping on the brow where the wind met them, clean and salted with earth and leaf, “just stand still now, and look down at Nunnely dale and wood.”

They halted. Beneath them lay the valley robed in May—meads pearled with daisies, and others golden with king-cups; the young verdure smiling clear in sunlight, as if it had a taste of its own sweetness. On Nunnwood—the one remaining remnant of an antique British forest, where once the lowlands had been all chase and the highlands breast-deep in heather—slept the shadow of a cloud. Beyond, hills were dappled; the horizon was shaded and tinted like mother-of-pearl—silvery blues, soft purples, evanescent greens and rose-shades melting into fleeces of white cloud, pure as azury snow. The whole scene had its own hush and its own music: not the shriek and stamp of wheels, but the honest wind, the far-off call of birds, the faint stir of leaves; a sound-world that soothed by being true.

“Our England is a bonny island,” said Shirley, “and Yorkshire is one of her bonniest nooks.”

“You are a Yorkshire girl too?”

“I am—Yorkshire in blood and birth. Five generations of my race sleep under the aisles of Briarfield Church. I drew my first breath in the old black hall behind us.”

Caroline, with a sudden pleasure that was like tasting something homely and remembered, held out her hand. “We are compatriots,” she said.

“Yes,” Shirley agreed, gravely nodding, and taking that hand as if the clasp had sealed more than a light morning’s intimacy.

“And that,” Caroline asked, pointing, “that is Nunnwood?”

“It is.”

“Were you ever there?”

“Many a time.”

“In the heart of it?”

“Yes.”

“What is it like?”

“It is like an encampment of forest sons of Anak. The trees are huge and old. When you stand at their roots, the summits seem in another region. The trunks remain still and firm as pillars, while the boughs sway to every breeze. In the deepest calm their leaves are never quite hushed; and in high wind a flood rushes, a sea thunders above you.”

“Was it not one of Robin Hood’s haunts?”

“Yes; and there are mementos of him still. To penetrate into Nunnwood is to go far back into the dim days of old. Can you see a break in the forest, about the centre?”

“Yes—distinctly.”

“That break is a dell—a deep hollow cup, lined with turf as green and short as the sod of this common. The very oldest of the trees—gnarled, mighty oaks—crowd about the brink; and in the bottom lie the ruins of a nunnery.”

“We will go,” said Caroline, and her voice, though soft, had a longing in it like hunger—“you and I alone, early some fine summer morning, and spend a long day there. We can take pencils and sketch-books, and any interesting reading book we like; and of course we shall take something to eat. I have two little baskets, in which Mrs. Gill—my housekeeper—might pack our provisions; and we could each carry our own. It would not tire you too much to walk so far?”

“Oh no,” Caroline answered quickly—pleased, not only by the plan, but by the domestic certainty of it: the baskets, the packing, the thought of food made ready with care. “Especially if we rested the whole day in the wood. And I know all the pleasantest spots. I know where we could get nuts in nutting time; I know where wild strawberries abound; I know certain lonely, quite untrodden glades carpeted with strange mosses—some yellow as if gilded, some a sober gray, some gem-green. I know groups of trees that ravish the eye with their picture-like effects—rude oak, delicate birch, glossy beech clustered in contrast; ash trees stately as Saul, standing isolated; and superannuated wood-giants clad in bright shrouds of ivy. Miss Keeldar, I could guide you.”

“You would be dull with me alone?”

“I should not. I think we should suit; and what third person is there whose presence would not spoil our pleasure?”

“Indeed, I know of none about our own ages—no lady at least; and as to gentlemen—”

“An excursion becomes quite a different thing when there are gentlemen of the party,” Caroline interrupted, with a quick, almost frightened truthfulness.

“I agree with you—quite a different thing to what we were proposing.”

“We were going simply to see the old trees, the old ruins; to pass a day in old times, surrounded by olden silence, and above all by quietude.”

“You are right; and the presence of gentlemen dispels the last charm, I think. If they are of the wrong sort—like your Malones, your young Sykes, and Wynnes—irritation takes the place of serenity. If they are of the right sort, there is still a change; I can hardly tell what change—one easy to feel, difficult to describe.”

“We forget Nature, imprimis.”

“And then Nature forgets us,” said Shirley, “covers her vast calm brow with a dim veil, conceals her face, and withdraws the peaceful joy with which—if we had been content to worship her only—she would have filled our hearts.”

“What does she give us instead?”

“More elation and more anxiety; an excitement that steals the hours away fast, and a trouble that ruffles their course.”

“Our power of being happy lies a good deal in ourselves, I believe,” Caroline replied, with the sober sense she often used as a defence. “I have gone to Nunnwood with a large party—all the curates and some other gentry of these parts, with sundry ladies—and I found the affair insufferably tedious and absurd. And I have gone quite alone, or accompanied only by Fanny—who sat in the woodman’s hut and sewed, or talked to the goodwife, while I roamed about and made sketches or read—and I have enjoyed much happiness of a quiet kind all day. But that was when I was young—two years ago.”

“Did you ever go with your cousin, Robert Moore?”

“Yes; once.”

“What sort of a companion is he on these occasions?”

“A cousin, you know, is different to a stranger.”

“I am aware of that; but cousins, if they are stupid, are still more insupportable than strangers, because you cannot so easily keep them at a distance. But your cousin is not stupid?”

“No; but—”

“Well?”

“If the company of fools irritates, as you say,” Caroline answered, and her colour changed as if some nearer heat than the sun touched her, “the society of clever men leaves its own peculiar pain also. Where the goodness or talent of your friend is beyond and above all doubt, your own worthiness to be his associate often becomes a matter of question.”

“Oh! there I cannot follow you. That crotchet is not one I should choose to entertain for an instant,” said Shirley, with the proud generosity of her temperament. “I consider myself not unworthy to be the associate of the best of them—of gentlemen, I mean—though that is saying a great deal. Where they are good, they are very good, I believe. Your uncle, by-the-bye, is not a bad specimen of the elderly gentleman. I am always glad to see his brown, keen, sensible old face, either in my own house or any other. Are you fond of him? Is he kind to you? Now, speak the truth.”

“He has brought me up from childhood,” said Caroline. “I doubt not, precisely as he would have brought up his own daughter if he had had one; and that is kindness. But I am not fond of him. I would rather be out of his presence than in it.”

“Strange—when he has the art of making himself so agreeable.”

“Yes, in company; but he is stern and silent at home. As he puts away his cane and shovel-hat in the rectory hall, so he locks his liveliness in his bookcase and study-desk: the knitted brow and brief word for the fireside; the smile, the jest, the witty sally for society.”

“Is he tyrannical?”

“Not in the least. He is neither tyrannical nor hypocritical. He is simply a man who is rather liberal than good-natured, rather brilliant than genial, rather scrupulously equitable than truly just—if you can understand such superfine distinctions.”

“Oh yes,” said Shirley. “Good-nature implies indulgence, which he has not; geniality, warmth of heart, which he does not own; and genuine justice is the offspring of sympathy and considerateness, of which my bronzed old friend is quite innocent.”

“I often wonder,” Caroline went on, with that inward question she could never quite put away, “whether most men resemble my uncle in their domestic relations; whether it is necessary to be new and unfamiliar to them in order to seem agreeable or estimable in their eyes; and whether it is impossible to their natures to retain a constant interest and affection for those they see every day.”

“I don’t know,” Shirley replied. “I can’t clear up your doubts. I ponder over similar ones myself sometimes. But, to tell you a secret—if I were convinced they are necessarily and universally different from us—fickle, soon petrifying, unsympathizing—I would never marry. I should not like to find out that what I loved did not love me; that it was weary of me; and that whatever effort I might make to please would hereafter be worse than useless, since it was inevitably in its nature to change and become indifferent. That discovery once made, what should I long for? To go away—to remove from a presence where my society gave no pleasure.”

“But you could not if you were married.”

“No, I could not. There it is. I could never be my own mistress more. A terrible thought! It suffocates me! Nothing irks me like the idea of being a burden and a bore—an inevitable burden, a ceaseless bore. Now, when I feel my company superfluous, I can comfortably fold my independence round me like a mantle, and drop my pride like a veil, and withdraw to solitude. If married, that could not be.”

“I wonder we don’t all make up our minds to remain single,” said Caroline. “We should, if we listened to the wisdom of experience. My uncle always speaks of marriage as a burden; and I believe whenever he hears of a man being married he invariably regards him as a fool—or, at any rate, as doing a foolish thing.”

“But, Caroline, men are not all like your uncle. Surely not. I hope not.”

Shirley paused a moment, musing; the wind moved the heath with a low, continuous whisper, like a remembered language spoken under breath—intimate, yet half lost.

“I suppose we each find an exception in the one we love, till we are married,” Caroline suggested.

“I suppose so. And this exception we believe to be of sterling materials. We fancy it like ourselves; we imagine a sense of harmony. We think his voice gives the softest, truest promise of a heart that will never harden against us; we read in his eyes that faithful feeling—affection. I don’t think we should trust to what they call passion at all, Caroline. I believe it is a mere fire of dry sticks, blazing up and vanishing. But we watch him, and see him kind to animals, to little children, to poor people. He is kind to us likewise—good, considerate. He does not flatter women; but he is patient with them, and he seems to be easy in their presence, and to find their company genial. He likes them not only for vain and selfish reasons, but as we like him—because we like him. Then we observe he is just; that he always speaks the truth; that he is conscientious. We feel joy and peace when he comes into a room; we feel sadness and trouble when he leaves it. We know this man has been a kind son, that he is a kind brother. Will any one dare to tell me he will not be a kind husband?”

“My uncle would affirm it unhesitatingly. ‘He will be sick of you in a month,’ he would say.”

“Mrs. Pryor would seriously intimate the same.”

“Mrs. Yorke and Miss Mann would darkly suggest ditto.”

“If they are true oracles, it is good never to fall in love.”

“Very good—if you can avoid it.”

“I choose to doubt their truth.”

“I am afraid that proves you are already caught.”

“Not I. But if I were—do you know what soothsayers I would consult?”

“Let me hear.”

“Neither man nor woman—elderly nor young: the little Irish beggar that comes barefoot to my door; the mouse that steals out of the cranny in the wainscot; the bird that in frost and snow pecks at my window for a crumb; the dog that licks my hand and sits beside my knee.”

“Did you ever see any one who was kind to such things?” Caroline asked.

“Did you ever see any one whom such things seemed instinctively to follow—like—rely on?”

“We have a black cat and an old dog at the rectory,” Caroline answered, and now her voice softened, as if the simple household creatures drew her nearer to confession. “I know somebody to whose knee that black cat loves to climb—against whose shoulder and cheek it likes to purr. The old dog always comes out of his kennel and wags his tail, and whines affectionately when somebody passes.”

“And what does that somebody do?”

“He quietly strokes the cat, and lets her sit while he conveniently can; and when he must disturb her by rising, he puts her softly down, and never flings her from him roughly. He always whistles to the dog and gives him a caress.”

“Does he? It is not Robert?”

“But it is Robert.”

“Handsome fellow!” said Shirley, with sudden enthusiasm; her eyes sparkled as if the very name had struck fire on flint. “Is he not handsome? Has he not fine eyes and well-cut features, and a clear, princely forehead?”

“He has,” said Caroline—low.

“He has all that, Caroline. Bless him! he is both graceful and good.”

“I was sure you would see that he was. When I first looked at your face I knew you would.”

“I was well inclined to him before I saw him. I liked him when I did see him. I admire him now. There is charm in beauty for itself; when it is blent with goodness, there is a powerful charm.”

“When mind is added, Shirley?”

“Who can resist it?”

“Remember my uncle; Mesdames Pryor, Yorke, and Mann.”

“Remember the croaking of the frogs of Egypt. He is a noble being. I tell you, when they are good, they are the lords of the creation—they are the sons of God. Moulded in their Maker’s image, the minutest spark of His spirit lifts them almost above mortality. Indisputably, a great, good, handsome man is the first of created things.”

“Above us?”

“I would scorn to contend for empire with him—I would scorn it. Shall my left hand dispute for precedence with my right? Shall my heart quarrel with my pulse? Shall my veins be jealous of the blood which fills them?”

“Men and women—husbands and wives—quarrel horribly, Shirley.”

“Poor things! Poor, fallen, degenerate things! God made them for another lot—for other feelings.”

“But are we men’s equals, or are we not?”

“Nothing ever charms me more than when I meet my superior—one who makes me sincerely feel that he is my superior.”

“Did you ever meet him?”

“I should be glad to see him any day. The higher above me, so much the better. It degrades to stoop; it is glorious to look up. What frets me is, that when I try to esteem, I am baffled; when religiously inclined, there are but false gods to adore. I disdain to be a pagan.”

They had reached the rectory gates; the familiar sound of the place met Caroline’s ear—the faint clink from within, the subdued stir of a house lived in quietly, the outer world kept at a managed distance.

“Miss Keeldar, will you come in?” Caroline asked.

“Not to-day,” Shirley answered. “But to-morrow I shall fetch you to spend the evening with me. Caroline Helstone—if you really are what at present to me you seem—you and I will suit. I have never in my whole life been able to talk to a young lady as I have talked to you this morning. Kiss me—and good-bye.”

* * *

Mrs. Pryor, too, appeared disposed to cultivate Caroline’s acquaintance. She who went nowhere else called, early, at the rectory. She came in the afternoon, when the rector happened to be out. The day was close; the air lay thick as if it had been breathed over many times and not renewed. Mrs. Pryor’s face was flushed, and she seemed fluttered—more by entering a strange house than by any fatigue. Her habits were so secluded, so retired, that a new threshold affected her like a sudden change of climate.

When Caroline went into the dining-room she found her seated on the sofa, trembling, fanning herself with her handkerchief, and contending with a nervous discomposure that threatened hysteria.

Caroline marvelled at so unusual a want of self-command in a lady of Mrs. Pryor’s years; and, too, at the lack of real strength in one who appeared almost robust. Mrs. Pryor hastened to allege the fatigue of her walk—the heat of the sun—this, that, and the other, as reasons for her indisposition; and still, with more hurry than coherence, she repeated these causes, while Caroline, gently and quietly, sought to relieve her—unfastening the shawl, removing the bonnet, making the close room seem, by these small orderly offices, more breathable.

Attentions of this sort Mrs. Pryor would not have accepted from every one. Usually she recoiled from touch or close approach with a mixture of embarrassment and coldness that repelled the very kindness offered. To Caroline’s little light hand, however, she yielded tractably, and seemed soothed by the contact. In a few minutes she ceased to tremble, grew quiet, and resumed her habitual composure, as if the disturbance had been some sudden, passing storm of the nerves.

Then she began to talk of ordinary topics. In mixed company Mrs. Pryor rarely spoke, or, if obliged to speak, spoke under restraint and therefore not well. In dialogue she was different: a good converser—formal in phrase, but choosing her words with care; just in sentiment; various and accurate in information. Caroline found it pleasant to listen—more pleasant than she had expected; it was as though, behind Mrs. Pryor’s reserve, there existed a mind that could season common things with intelligence, as a careful hand seasons plain food so that it nourishes and satisfies.

On the wall opposite the sofa hung three pictures: in the centre, over the mantelpiece, the portrait of a lady; on either side, male portraits.

“That is a beautiful face,” said Mrs. Pryor at length, breaking a brief pause that had followed half an hour’s animated conversation. “The features may be termed perfect; no statuary’s chisel could improve them. It is a portrait from the life, I presume?”

“It is a portrait of Mrs. Helstone,” Caroline answered.

“Of Mrs. Matthewson Helstone—of your uncle’s wife?”

“It is; and is said to be a good likeness. Before her marriage she was accounted the beauty of the district.”

“I should say she merited the distinction. What accuracy in all the lineaments! It is, however, a passive face. The original could not have been what is generally termed ‘a woman of spirit.’”

“I believe she was a remarkably still, silent person.”

“One would scarcely have expected, my dear, that your uncle’s choice should have fallen on a partner of that description. Is he not fond of being amused by lively chat?”

“In company he is. But he always says he could never do with a talking wife. He must have quiet at home. You go out to gossip, he affirms; you come home to read and reflect.”

“Mrs. Matthewson lived but a few years after her marriage, I think I have heard?”

“About five years.”

“Well, my dear,” said Mrs. Pryor, rising to go, “I trust it is understood that you will frequently come to Fieldhead. I hope you will. You must feel lonely here, having no female relative in the house; you must necessarily pass much of your time in solitude.”

“I am inured to it,” Caroline replied. “I have grown up by myself. May I arrange your shawl for you?”

Mrs. Pryor submitted to be assisted; and, when Caroline had set the folds straight, and placed the clasp with quiet neatness, the older lady added, in her grave tone, “Should you chance to require help in your studies, you may command me.”

There were hours in Shirley Keeldar’s life when she seemed to live on air—on the mere fact of being, as if existence itself, unhelped by effort, were a feast laid before her. After a morning brisk with tasks and talk, she would sometimes drift out of doors, and, choosing the foot of a tree whose shadow fell like a cool hand, stretch herself at length upon the turf. She asked for no company beyond Caroline—only that Caroline should be near enough to call; she asked for no spectacle but the blue height overhead, with its thin flocks of cloud travelling like white sails; and no sound but the field’s low music—the bee’s steady drone, the leaf’s confidential whisper, the light, pure hush that holds the heart together. In such leisure she did not read printed pages; she read memory, and that other volume, unopened yet, expectation. Her face, at moments, wore a smile as though she tasted something sweet on the tongue of thought—some morsel of the past, some promise of the future; and all seemed kindly, warmed with hope.

Yet on one of these afternoons Caroline, coming to rouse her—thinking the stillness had lasted long enough—found, to her astonishment, dew upon Shirley’s cheek that was not dew. Those brilliant eyes were full; the lashes glittered with moisture.

“Shirley! why do you cry?” Caroline asked; and the emphasis fell, despite herself, on you.

Miss Keeldar turned her head upon the grass, and smiled up—half mocking, half soft. “Because it pleases me mightily to cry,” said she. “My heart is both sad and glad. But why, you good patient child—why do you not bear me company? I weep tears, delightful and soon wiped away; you might weep gall, if you chose.”

“Why should I weep gall?”

“Mateless, solitary bird!” was all the answer she gave—an answer like a proverb in a dialect no one has written down.

“And are not you too mateless, Shirley?”

“At heart—no.”

“Oh! who nestles there, Shirley?”

Shirley only laughed, and sprang up with sudden lightness, as if she had shaken off the drop that trembled on her eyelash. “I have dreamed,” she said—“a mere day-dream—certainly bright, probably baseless!”

Time passed; and Caroline, though stripped now of the easy illusions which once made the future a plain road, still obeyed old habit and old association. Many an evening, as naturally as one goes to the hearth when the air cools, she found her feet carrying her to the field-style and the thorn above the Hollow. That place had become to her what a certain savour is to memory—one smell of crushed thyme, and whole summers return; one glimpse of a familiar stile, and whole histories wake.

The night after the incident of the note she went again, and watched, as she had watched before, for her beacon. She watched in vain. No lamp glimmered; no kindly point of light answered her. At length the rising of certain constellations—clear and cold as a clock-face—warned her how late it was, and sent her away.

Passing Fieldhead on her return, she paused in spite of herself. The house lay under moonlight with a beauty so composed it seemed enchanted into calm. The building, made pale by that serene radiance, wore a pearly gilding; around it the darker mass of trees gathered in a mellow brown gloom, as if night itself were protective. The broad pavement before the door shone like lighted stone—so bright that it might have been a strip of white shore beyond some black flood. Across it lay two sharp shadows, long and sable, cast by two figures.

At first those figures were motionless; then they moved together—step answering step—and spoke low, their voices in the same key, subdued, intimate, guarded by the night air that carried every syllable away before it could be caught.

Caroline’s gaze fixed—earnest, unwilling, yet unable to turn aside. As the pair came from behind the cedar trunk, the moon made their outlines and faces distinct.

“Is it Mrs. Pryor and Shirley?” she thought.

Shirley it certainly was. No one else wore so proud a grace even in carelessness; no one else had that lithe movement, that half-mirthful, half-musing face where tenderness and mockery could dwell together without quarrel. She had not covered her hair against the dew; her curls lay free, veiling neck and shoulder with their dark rings. A gold ornament gleamed through the folds of the scarf across her bust, and a bright gem flashed from the white hand that held it close. Yes—Shirley.

Then the companion—Mrs. Pryor?

Mrs. Pryor, if Mrs. Pryor could command six feet of height and had exchanged her widow’s sober weeds for a man’s figure and carriage. It was a man. Tall, young, stately. Robert Moore.

They spoke softly; their words could not be made out. To linger was not to listen in malice; the moon shone openly upon them as if it had been invited to witness their meeting. And Caroline—who had once walked with Moore herself through summer evenings—could not resist. She stayed.

She remembered other nights: when he had been used to go up the Hollow with his cousin after sunset, to breathe the earth’s freshness where the terrace grew thick with fragrant herbage; and below, from the ravine’s rifted darkness, came that lonely water’s voice, always the same and never the same—moaning among wet stones, between weedy banks, under alder-boughs that made a roof of shadow. That sound, a small native music of the place, had once soothed her; now it seemed to belong to a life she had lost, like an old song she could no longer sing in its original tongue.

“But I used to be closer to him,” she thought. “He felt no obligation to treat me with homage; I needed only kindness. He used to hold my hand; he does not touch hers. And yet Shirley is not proud where she loves. There is no haughtiness in her look now—only a little in her port, what is natural to her and inseparable. Robert must think, as I think, that he is looking down on a fine face; but he must think it with a man’s brain, not with mine. She has such generous, yet soft fire in her eyes. She smiles—what makes her smile so sweet? I saw that Robert felt its beauty, and he must have felt it with his man’s heart, not with my dim woman’s perceptions. They look to me like two great happy spirits. That silver pavement is like the white shore we fancy beyond the death-flood. They have reached it; they walk there united. And what am I—standing here in shadow, shrinking into concealment, my mind darker than my hiding-place? A poor doomed mortal, asking in ignorance and hopelessness wherefore she was born and to what end she lives; thinking always how she shall encounter death, and by whom be sustained through it.

“This is the worst passage I have come to yet; still I was prepared. I gave Robert up—I gave him up to Shirley—the first day I heard she was come, the first moment I saw her—rich, youthful, lovely. She has him now. He is her lover. She is his darling. She will be more his darling when they are married. The more he knows her, the more his soul will cleave to her. They will both be happy; I do not grudge them their bliss—but I groan under my own misery. Some of my suffering is very acute. Truly I ought not to have been born; they should have smothered me at the first cry.”

Here Shirley stepped aside to gather a dewy flower; then she and her companion turned into the path that lay nearer the gate. A few words of their talk floated clearer. Caroline would not stay to hear. With a noiseless step she withdrew; and the moonlight, which had held her shadow against the wall, kissed the stone again when the darkness of her presence moved away.

But the night had ears; and the reader may remain where Caroline would not, and catch what can be caught of their discourse.

“I cannot conceive why nature did not give you a bulldog’s head,” said Shirley, “for you have all a bulldog’s tenacity.”

“Not a flattering idea. Am I so ignoble?”

“And something too you have of the same animal’s silent ways of going about its work. You give no warning; you come noiselessly behind, seize fast, and hold on.”

“This is guess-work. You have witnessed no such feat on my part. In your presence I have been no bulldog.”

“Your very silence indicates your race. How little you talk, and yet how deeply you scheme! You are far-seeing; you are calculating.”

“I know the ways of these people. I have gathered information of their intentions. My note last night informed you that Barraclough’s trial had ended in his conviction and sentence to transportation. His associates will plot vengeance. I shall lay my plans so as to counteract—or at least be prepared for—theirs. That is all. Having now given you as clear an explanation as I can, am I to understand that for what I propose doing I have your approbation?”

“I shall stand by you so long as you remain on the defensive. Yes.”

“Good! Without any aid—even opposed or disapproved by you—I believe I should have acted precisely as I now intend to act, but in another spirit. I now feel satisfied. On the whole, I relish the position.”

“I dare say you do. That is evident. You relish the work which lies before you still better than you would relish the execution of a government order for army-cloth.”

“I certainly feel it congenial.”

“So would old Helstone. It is true there is a shade of difference in your motives—many shades, perhaps. Shall I speak to Mr. Helstone? I will, if you like.”

“Act as you please. Your judgment, Miss Keeldar, will guide you accurately. I could rely on it myself in a more difficult crisis. But I should inform you Mr. Helstone is somewhat prejudiced against me at present.”

“I am aware—I have heard all about your differences. Depend upon it, they will melt away. He cannot resist the temptation of an alliance under present circumstances.”

“I should be glad to have him; he is of true metal.”

“I think so also.”

“An old blade, and rusty somewhat, but the edge and temper still excellent.”

“Well, you shall have him, Mr. Moore—that is, if I can win him.”

“Whom can you not win?”

“Perhaps not the rector; but I will make the effort.”

“Effort! He will yield for a word—a smile.”

“By no means. It will cost me several cups of tea, some toast and cake, and an ample measure of remonstrances, expostulations, and persuasions. It grows rather chill.”

“I perceive you shiver. Am I acting wrongly to detain you here? Yet it is so calm—I even feel it warm—and society such as yours is a pleasure to me so rare. If you were wrapped in a thicker shawl—”

“I might stay longer, and forget how late it is, which would chagrin Mrs. Pryor. We keep early and regular hours at Fieldhead, Mr. Moore; and so, I am sure, does your sister at the cottage.”

“Yes; but Hortense and I have an understanding, the most convenient in the world, that we shall each do as we please.”

“How do you please to do?”

“Three nights in the week I sleep in the mill—but I require little rest—and when it is moonlight and mild I often haunt the Hollow till daybreak.”

“When I was a very little girl,” said Shirley, “my nurse used to tell me tales of fairies being seen in that Hollow. That was before my father built the mill, when it was a perfectly solitary ravine. You will be falling under enchantment.”

“I fear it is done,” Moore answered; and his voice sank, as if he spoke into the hush between two heartbeats.

“But there are worse things than fairies to be guarded against,” pursued Miss Keeldar.

“Things more perilous,” he agreed.

“Far more so. For instance—how would you like to meet Michael Hartley, that mad Calvinist and Jacobin weaver? They say he is addicted to poaching, and often goes abroad at night with his gun.”

“I have already had the luck to meet him. We held a long argument together one night. A strange little incident it was; I liked it.”

“Liked it? I admire your taste! Michael is not sane. Where did you meet him?”

“In the deepest, shadiest spot in the glen, where the water runs low under brushwood. We sat down near that plank-bridge. It was moonlight, but clouded, and very windy. We had a talk.”

“On politics?”

“And religion. I think the moon was at the full, and Michael was as near crazed as possible. He uttered strange blasphemy in his Antinomian fashion.”

“Excuse me, but I think you must have been nearly as mad as he, to sit listening to him.”

“There is a wild interest in his ravings. The man would be half a poet, if he were not wholly a maniac; and perhaps a prophet, if he were not a profligate. He solemnly informed me that hell was foreordained my inevitable portion; that he read the mark of the beast on my brow; that I had been an outcast from the beginning. God’s vengeance, he said, was preparing for me, and affirmed that in a vision of the night he had beheld the manner and the instrument of my doom. I wanted to know further, but he left me with these words—‘The end is not yet.’”

“Have you ever seen him since?”

“About a month afterwards, returning from market, I encountered him and Moses Barraclough, both in an advanced stage of inebriation. They were praying in frantic sort at the roadside. They accosted me as Satan, bid me avaunt, and clamoured to be delivered from temptation. Again, but a few days ago, Michael took the trouble of appearing at the counting-house door, hatless, in his shirt-sleeves—his coat and castor having been detained at the public-house in pledge. He delivered himself of the comfortable message that he could wish Mr. Moore to set his house in order, as his soul was likely shortly to be required of him.”

“Do you make light of these things?”

“The poor man had been drinking for weeks, and was in a state bordering on delirium tremens.”

“What then? He is the more likely to attempt the fulfilment of his own prophecies.”

“It would not do to permit incidents of this sort to affect one’s nerves.”

“Mr. Moore, go home!”

“So soon?”

“Pass straight down the fields, not round by the lade and plantations.”

“It is early yet.”

“It is late. For my part, I am going in. Will you promise me not to wander in the Hollow to-night?”

“If you wish it.”

“I do wish it. May I ask whether you consider life valueless?”

“By no means. On the contrary, of late I regard my life as invaluable.”

“Of late?”

“Existence is neither aimless nor hopeless to me now, and it was both three months ago. I was then drowning, and rather wished the operation over. All at once a hand was stretched to me—such a delicate hand I scarcely dared trust it; its strength, however, has rescued me from ruin.”

“Are you really rescued?”

“For the time. Your assistance has given me another chance.”

“Live to make the best of it. Don’t offer yourself as a target to Michael Hartley; and good-night!”

The next day Caroline was under promise to spend the evening at Fieldhead; and she kept her word. Between that moonlit walk and this homely visit lay hours black as a soot-cloud. Most of the interval she passed shut in her own room, only coming out to join her uncle at meals. When Fanny showed signs of curiosity, Caroline forestalled questions by saying she was altering a dress and preferred sewing upstairs, that she might not be interrupted.

She did sew. The needle ran ceaselessly; the muslin—thin, obedient—was tucked and plaited with patient fingers. Yet her brain worked faster than her hands. Again she felt, with a sharper craving, that she must have fixed occupation—something that would press upon her from morning to night and allow no leisure for thought, however harsh, however irksome. Her uncle must be entreated once more; but first she would consult Mrs. Pryor. While she stitched, the summer dress spread on the little white couch at the foot of which she sat, she planned and replanned, as if life were cloth one could cut into a new shape if only the pattern were right. Now and then a tear fell on the muslin and darkened it for an instant; then she wiped it away, re-threaded the needle, smoothed the tuck, and worked on.

Late in the afternoon she dressed and went to Fieldhead. She entered the oak parlour as tea was being brought in, and Shirley asked why she came so late.

“Because I have been making my dress,” Caroline answered. “These fine sunny days began to make me ashamed of my winter merino, so I have furbished up a lighter garment.”

“In which you look as I like to see you,” said Shirley. “You are a lady-like little person, Caroline.—Is she not, Mrs. Pryor?”

Mrs. Pryor rarely paid compliments, rarely spoke at all of faces and figures; but she drew Caroline nearer with a grave tenderness, swept the curls from her cheek, traced with her hand the oval line as if to assure herself it was still there, and said quietly, “You get somewhat thin, my love, and somewhat pale. Do you sleep well? your eyes have a languid look.” She gazed anxiously as she spoke.

“I sometimes dream melancholy dreams,” replied Caroline; “and if I lie awake for an hour or two in the night, I am continually thinking of the rectory as a dreary old place. You know it is very near the churchyard. The back part of the house is extremely ancient, and it is said that the out-kitchens there were once enclosed in the churchyard, and that there are graves under them. I rather long to leave the rectory.”

“My dear, you are surely not superstitious?”

“No, Mrs. Pryor; but I think I grow what is called nervous. I see things under a darker aspect than I used to do. I have fears I never used to have—not of ghosts, but of omens and disastrous events; and I have an inexpressible weight on my mind which I would give the world to shake off, and I cannot do it.”

“Strange!” cried Shirley. “I never feel so.” Mrs. Pryor was silent.

“Fine weather, pleasant days, pleasant scenes, are powerless to give me pleasure,” Caroline went on. “Calm evenings are not calm to me. Moonlight, which I used to think mild, now only looks mournful. Is this weakness of mind, Mrs. Pryor, or what is it? I cannot help it. I often struggle against it. I reason; but reason and effort make no difference.”

“You should take more exercise,” said Mrs. Pryor.

“Exercise! I exercise sufficiently. I exercise till I am ready to drop.”

“My dear, you should go from home.”

“Mrs. Pryor, I should like to go from home, but not on any purposeless excursion or visit. I wish to be a governess, as you have been. It would oblige me greatly if you would speak to my uncle on the subject.”

“Nonsense!” broke in Shirley. “What an idea! Be a governess! Better be a slave at once. Where is the necessity of it? Why should you dream of such a painful step?”

“My dear,” said Mrs. Pryor, “you are very young to be a governess, and not sufficiently robust. The duties a governess undertakes are often severe.”

“And I believe I want severe duties to occupy me.”

“Occupy you!” cried Shirley. “When are you idle? I never saw a more industrious girl than you. You are always at work. Come—come and sit by my side, and take some tea to refresh you. You don’t care much for my friendship, then, that you wish to leave me?”

“Indeed I do, Shirley; and I don’t wish to leave you. I shall never find another friend so dear.”

At that, Shirley’s hand went into Caroline’s with impulsive warmth; her face softened as though some proud shield had slipped aside for an instant.

“If you think so, you had better make much of me,” said she, “and not run away from me. I hate to part with those to whom I am become attached. Mrs. Pryor there sometimes talks of leaving me, and says I might make a more advantageous connection than herself. I should as soon think of exchanging an old-fashioned mother for something modish and stylish. As for you—why, I began to flatter myself we were thoroughly friends; that you liked Shirley almost as well as Shirley likes you, and she does not stint her regard.”

“I do like Shirley. I like her more and more every day. But that does not make me strong or happy.”

“And would it make you strong or happy to go and live as a dependent amongst utter strangers? It would not. And the experiment must not be tried; I tell you it would fail. It is not in your nature to bear the desolate life governesses generally lead; you would fall ill. I won’t hear of it.”

She paused, having spoken with a decision that was almost command. Then, still a little ruffled, she began again. “Why, it is my daily pleasure now to look out for the little cottage bonnet and the silk scarf glancing through the trees in the lane, and to know that my quiet, shrewd, thoughtful companion and monitress is coming back to me; that I shall have her sitting in the room to look at, to talk to, or to let alone, as she and I please. This may be a selfish sort of language—I know it is—but it is the language which naturally rises to my lips, therefore I utter it.”

“I would write to you, Shirley.”

“And what are letters? Only a sort of pis aller. Drink some tea, Caroline. Eat something—you eat nothing. Laugh and be cheerful, and stay at home.”

Caroline shook her head and sighed. She felt already how hard it would be to persuade anyone to help her to that change of life which she believed would be cure. Could she follow her own judgment, she thought she might find something harsh, perhaps, but effectual—some discipline that would leave her no room to ache. Yet that judgment rested on circumstances she could fully explain to none—least of all to Shirley. To other eyes it would look fanciful, unaccountable; and it was opposed accordingly.

There was indeed no present need of money to drive her from a comfortable home into service; and there was every likelihood her uncle would in some manner provide for her. So her friends thought, and as far as their sight reached they reasoned well. Of Caroline’s inward suffering—of racked nights and leaden days—they had no suspicion. She could not make it plain. It seemed untranslatable even to herself; it had no name in their language. To wait and endure was the only plan left. Many who wanted bread and clothing had livelier hearts and brighter prospects than hers; many, pinched by poverty, were less straitened than she.

“Now, is your mind quieted?” asked Shirley. “Will you consent to stay at home?”

“I shall not leave it against the approbation of my friends,” Caroline replied; “but I think in time they will be obliged to think as I do.”

During all this Mrs. Pryor looked far from easy. Habitual reserve held her tongue as a tight band holds back speech. Questions crowded her mind which she never ventured to put; advice formed itself silently and died there. Had she been alone with Caroline she might have spoken more directly; but Shirley’s presence—familiar as it was—sealed her lips with inexplicable scruple. Her concern showed itself only by small indirect ministries: asking whether the fire made Caroline too warm, setting a screen between her chair and the hearth, shutting a window from which she fancied a draught might come, and glancing often and restlessly at the pale face she watched as one watches a flame one fears may go out.

Shirley, however, would not let the matter die in sadness.

“Having destroyed your plan,” she said, “which I hope I have done, I shall construct a new one of my own. Every summer I make an excursion. This season I propose spending two months either at the Scotch lochs or the English lakes—that is, I shall go there provided you consent to accompany me. If you refuse, I shall not stir a foot.”

“You are very good, Shirley,” Caroline answered.

Of course, she knew he would marry Shirley.

The thought rose with Caroline as naturally as breath when she opened her eyes to the pale morning; it lay upon her heart as steadily as the counterpane upon her knees. He would marry her; he ought. Shirley had means, land, consequence—hands that could reach where his could not; a name that could stand between him and ruin as a stout wall stands between a hearth and the wind.

“She can help him,” Caroline said aloud, to give the sentence the ring of reason, to make it sound like duty and not like pain.

And then, with a sharpness that made her sit up, came the next truth, cruel as iron laid on a tongue: when they were married, she herself would be—nothing.

Forgotten; not maliciously, perhaps, not with deliberate unkindness, but softly, inevitably; as yesterday’s bread is pushed aside when the new loaf comes warm and fragrant to table. Where should she turn then? She had no art of making herself necessary; she had only the dumb, clinging fidelity that asks to be cherished and is ashamed of asking. To call him hers even in thought seemed presumption. She was poverty and incapacity; Shirley was wealth and power—and beauty too, and the right to be ardently loved.

Caroline could not summon a single honest objection. It was not a bargain, not a sordid traffic; Shirley’s feeling, whatever name it might yet bear, had nobleness in it. She would love—nay, she already loved—in a way that would make any man proud to be loved. Let it be so, then.

But afterwards—afterwards she could not stay.

That hollow comfort people offer—“you will still be his cousin, his sister in all but name”—she despised. She would be all or nothing to a man like Robert; she could not hover about the edges of his happiness, smiling with a hypocrite’s mouth while her heart was wrung. She could not take the place of friend without wounding herself, nor of foe without degrading him. She must either cleave to him as if she were part of him, or be sundered utterly—wide as the poles.

“Sunder me then, Providence,” she pleaded in her mind, with a desperation that was still prayer. “Part us speedily.”

Late that afternoon, while these thoughts worked like a hidden fever, the very form that haunted them passed the parlour window—Shirley Keeldar, strolling by with that air of careless wistfulness which, when she was quiet, seemed native to her as the scent is native to a crushed leaf. When she was animated, the careless part vanished; the wistfulness took a brighter tincture, and her mirth, when it came, never sounded thin or crackling, but full and flavoured, like something warmed through at the hearth.

She came in without waiting to be invited.

“What do you mean by not coming to see me this afternoon, as you promised?” were her first words.

“I was not in the humour,” Caroline answered; and because it was true, it was plain.

Shirley’s eye, quick as a blade, fixed on her.

“No,” she said. “I see you are not in the humour for loving me. You are in one of your sunless, inclement moods—when one feels a fellow-creature’s presence is not welcome. You have such moods. Are you aware of it?”

“Do you mean to stay long, Shirley?”

“Yes. I am come to have my tea, and must have it before I go. I shall take the liberty, then, of removing my bonnet, without being asked.”

She did so, and stood on the rug with her hands clasped behind her, regarding Caroline as if she would read her to the last line.

“A pretty expression you have,” she went on, not unkindly, rather with a sort of pity that pricked. “Wonderfully self-supported you look—you solitude-seeking wounded deer. Are you afraid Shirley will worry you if she discovers that you are hurt, and that you bleed?”

“I never do fear Shirley.”

“But sometimes you dislike her; often you avoid her. Shirley can feel when she is slighted and shunned. If you had not walked home in the company you did last night, you would have been a different girl to-day. What time did you reach the rectory?”

“By ten.”

“Humph! You took three-quarters of an hour to walk a mile. Was it you, or Moore, who lingered so?”

“Shirley, you talk nonsense.”

“He talked nonsense—that I doubt not; or he looked it, which is a thousand times worse. I see the reflection of his eyes on your forehead at this moment. I feel disposed to call him out, if I could only get a trustworthy second. I feel desperately irritated. I felt so last night, and have felt it all day.”

Caroline’s colour stirred, partly from shame, partly from the pain of hearing Robert’s name flung about as if it were a plaything.

“You don’t ask me why,” Shirley pursued after a pause. “You little silent, over-modest thing; and you don’t deserve that I should pour out my secrets into your lap without an invitation. Upon my word, I could have found it in my heart to have dogged Moore yesterday evening with dire intent. I have pistols, and can use them.”

“Stuff, Shirley! Which would you have shot—me or Robert?”

“Neither, perhaps. Perhaps myself—more likely a bat or a tree-bough. He is a puppy, your cousin—a quiet, serious, sensible, judicious, ambitious puppy. I see him standing before me, talking his half-stern, half-gentle talk, bearing me down—as I am very conscious he does—with his fixity of purpose; and then—I have no patience with him!”

She began to pace the room rapidly, repeating that she had no patience with men in general and with her tenant in particular; her dress brushed the furniture as a gust brushes leaves. Caroline, watching her, felt that the house itself was changed by her presence: it was not only sight, but sound—Shirley’s step, Shirley’s breath, Shirley’s quick, impatient turnings—made an atmosphere; and Caroline’s own troubled silence seemed to listen to it as much as to words.

“You are mistaken,” she urged, anxious in spite of herself. “Robert is no puppy, no male flirt; I can vouch for that.”

“You vouch for it!” Shirley wheeled round. “Do you think I’ll take your word on the subject? There is no one’s testimony I would not credit sooner than yours. To advance Moore’s fortune you would cut off your right hand.”

“But not tell lies. And if I speak the truth, I must assure you that he was just civil to me last night—that was all.”

“I never asked what he was. I can guess. I saw him from the window take your hand in his long fingers, just as he went out at my gate.”

“That is nothing. I am not a stranger. I am an old acquaintance—and his cousin.”

“I feel indignant, and that is the long and short of the matter,” Shirley said. “All my comfort is broken up by his manoeuvres. He keeps intruding between you and me. Without him we should be good friends; but that six feet of puppyhood makes a perpetually recurring eclipse of our friendship. Again and again he crosses and obscures the disc I want always to see clear; ever and anon he renders me to you a mere bore and nuisance.”

“No, Shirley, no.”

“He does. You did not want my society this afternoon, and I feel it hard. You are naturally somewhat reserved; but I am a social personage who cannot live alone. If we were but left unmolested, I have that regard for you that I could bear you in my presence for ever, and not for the fraction of a second do I ever wish to be rid of you. You cannot say as much respecting me.”

“Shirley, I can say anything you wish. Shirley, I like you.”

“You will wish me at Jericho to-morrow, Lina.”

“I shall not. I am every day growing more accustomed to—fonder of you. You know I am too English to get up a vehement friendship all at once; but you are so much better than common—you are so different to every-day young ladies—I esteem you, I value you; you are never a burden to me—never. Do you believe what I say?”

“Partly,” Shirley replied, smiling, still a little incredulous. “But you are a peculiar personage. Quiet as you look, there is both a force and a depth somewhere within, not easily reached or appreciated. Then you certainly are not happy.”

“And unhappy people are rarely good. Is that what you mean?”

“Not at all. I mean rather that unhappy people are often preoccupied, and not in the mood for discoursing with companions of my nature. Moreover, there is a sort of unhappiness which not only depresses, but corrodes; and that, I fear, is your portion. Will pity do you any good, Lina? If it will, take some from Shirley; she offers largely, and warrants the article genuine.”

Something in Caroline, long pinched and silent, softened like butter set near warmth. The house was still; through the window there came the country’s accustomed sounds—the faint call of a bird, a cart far off on the lane—clean, living noises that belonged to the old fields and hedgerows. Industrial rumour, when it came, came harshly and made the heart shrink; but these were the gentler voices that let a mind breathe.

“Shirley,” she said, and the word shook, “I never had a sister—you never had a sister; but it flashes on me at this moment how sisters feel towards each other—affection twined with their life, which no shocks of feeling can uproot; which little quarrels only trample an instant, that it may spring more freshly when the pressure is removed; affection that no passion can ultimately outrival, with which even love itself cannot do more than compete in force and truth. Love hurts us so, Shirley. It is so tormenting, so racking; it burns away our strength with its flame. In affection is no pain and no fire—only sustenance and balm. I am supported and soothed when you—that is, you only—are near, Shirley. Do you believe me now?”

Shirley’s eyes altered; the irritability in them gave way to a pleased softness.

“I am always easy of belief when the creed pleases me. We really are friends, then, Lina, in spite of the black eclipse?”

“We really are,” Caroline answered, drawing her down to sit beside her, and holding her as if she feared to lose the comfort of that nearness. “Chance what may.”

“Come, then; we will talk of something else than the Troubler.”

But at that moment the rector entered; his presence, like a draught of colder air, changed the tone of the room. Whatever Shirley had meant to speak of next was checked. Conversation took a safer course till, when evening brought Shirley’s departure, she detained Caroline a few minutes in the passage, away from other ears.

“Caroline,” she said in a lower voice, “I wish to tell you that I have a great weight on my mind; my conscience is quite uneasy—as if I had committed, or was going to commit, a crime. It is not my private conscience, you must understand, but my landed-proprietor and lord-of-the-manor conscience. I have got into the clutch of an eagle with iron talons. I have fallen under a stern influence which I scarcely approve, but cannot resist. Something will be done ere long, I fear, which it by no means pleases me to think of. To ease my mind, and to prevent harm as far as I can, I mean to enter on a series of good works. Don’t be surprised, therefore, if you see me all at once turn outrageously charitable. I have no idea how to begin, but you must give me some advice. We will talk more on the subject to-morrow; and just ask that excellent person, Miss Ainley, to step up to Fieldhead. I have some notion of putting myself under her tuition. Won’t she have a precious pupil? Drop a hint to her, Lina, that, though a well-meaning, I am rather a neglected character; and then she will feel less scandalized at my ignorance about clothing societies and such things.”

On the morrow Caroline found Shirley at a desk, grave as a little clerk of fate, with an account-book spread open, a bundle of bank-notes laid straight as ribbon, and a purse so well-filled it seemed to hump its own leather with importance. She looked serious—yet not at ease; her brow was knit, not from stinginess, but from perplexity.

“I have been casting an eye over the weekly expenditure in housekeeping,” she announced, tapping the account-book as if it were a witness that must answer. “Trying to find out where I could retrench. And I have just given audience to Mrs. Gill, the cook, and sent her away with a notion that my brain is certainly crazed. I have lectured her on the duty of being careful—in a way quite new to her. So eloquent was I on the text of economy that I surprised myself; for you see, it is altogether a fresh idea. I never thought, much less spoke, on the subject till lately.”

Shirley’s nostrils quivered, as if the very smell of kitchen plenty rose between her and her resolutions: butter, dripping, meat, candles, soap—commodities that had their own domestic tyranny, their own soft insistence.

“But it is all theory,” she continued, “for when I came to the practical part I could retrench nothing. I had not firmness to take off a single pound of butter, or to prosecute to any clear result an inquest into the destiny of either dripping, lard, bread, cold meat, or other kitchen perquisite whatever. I know we never get up illuminations at Fieldhead, but I could not ask the meaning of sundry quite unaccountable pounds of candles. We do not wash for the parish; yet I viewed in silence items of soap and bleaching-powder calculated to satisfy the solicitude of the most anxious inquirer after our position in reference to those articles. Carnivorous I am not, nor is Mrs. Pryor, nor is Mrs. Gill herself, yet I only hemmed and opened my eyes a little wide when I saw butchers’ bills whose figures seemed to prove that fact—falsehood, I mean.”

She half laughed, then checked herself, ashamed of laughing when she meant to be stern.

“Caroline, you may laugh at me, but you can’t change me. I am a poltroon on certain points; I feel it. There is a base alloy of moral cowardice in my composition. I blushed and hung my head before Mrs. Gill, when she ought to have been faltering confessions to me. I found it impossible to get up the spirit even to hint, much less to prove, to her that she was a cheat. I have no calm dignity, no true courage about me.”

“Shirley, what fit of self-injustice is this?” Caroline protested. “My uncle—who is not given to speak well of women—says there are not ten thousand men in England as genuinely fearless as you.”

“I am fearless, physically. I am never nervous about danger. I was not startled from self-possession when Mr. Wynne’s great red bull rose with a bellow before my face, as I was crossing the cowslip lea alone—stooped his begrimed, sullen head, and made a run at me; but I was afraid of seeing Mrs. Gill brought to shame and confusion of face. You have twice—ten times—my strength of mind on certain subjects, Caroline. You, whom no persuasion can induce to pass a bull, however quiet he looks, would have firmly shown my housekeeper she had done wrong; then you would have gently and wisely admonished her; and at last, I dare say—provided she had seemed penitent—you would have very sweetly forgiven her. Of this conduct I am incapable.”

She pushed the account-book away with a gesture that dismissed her own weakness.

“However, in spite of exaggerated imposition, I still find we live within our means. I have money in hand; and I really must do some good with it. The Briarfield poor are badly off; they must be helped. What ought I to do, think you, Lina? Had I not better distribute the cash at once?”

“No, indeed, Shirley. You will not manage properly. I have often noticed that your only notion of charity is to give shillings and half-crowns in a careless, free-handed sort of way, which is liable to continual abuse. You must have a prime minister, or you will get yourself into a series of scrapes. You suggested Miss Ainley yourself; to Miss Ainley I will apply. And, meantime, promise to keep quiet, and not begin throwing away your money.”

Shirley glanced at the notes and the purse as if they were both a burden and a temptation.

“What a great deal you have, Shirley! You must feel very rich with all that?”

“Yes; I feel of consequence. It is not an immense sum, but I feel responsible for its disposal; and really this responsibility weighs on my mind more heavily than I could have expected. They say that there are some families almost starving to death in Briarfield. Some of my own cottagers are in wretched circumstances. I must and will help them.”

“Some people say we shouldn’t give alms to the poor, Shirley.”

“They are great fools for their pains. For those who are not hungry, it is easy to palaver about the degradation of charity, and so on; but they forget the brevity of life, as well as its bitterness. We have none of us long to live. Let us help each other through seasons of want and woe as well as we can, without heeding in the least the scruples of vain philosophy.”

“But you do help others, Shirley. You give a great deal as it is.”

“Not enough. I must give more, or, I tell you, my brother’s blood will some day be crying to Heaven against me. For, after all, if political incendiaries come here to kindle conflagration in the neighbourhood, and my property is attacked, I shall defend it like a tigress—I know I shall. Let me listen to Mercy as long as she is near me. Her voice once drowned by the shout of ruffian defiance, and I shall be full of impulses to resist and quell.”

Caroline caught in that word—voice—because she too knew what it was to be altered by sound: how harsh cries could unmake mercy, how a clamour could stampede thought. Shirley’s imagination was already hearing the mob as if it had gathered under her windows.

“If once the poor gather and rise in the form of the mob,” Shirley went on, “I shall turn against them as an aristocrat; if they bully me, I must defy; if they attack, I must resist—and I will.”

“You talk like Robert.”

“I feel like Robert, only more fierily. Let them meddle with Robert, or Robert’s mill, or Robert’s interests, and I shall hate them. At present I am no patrician, nor do I regard the poor around me as plebeians; but if once they violently wrong me or mine, and then presume to dictate to us, I shall quite forget pity for their wretchedness and respect for their poverty, in scorn of their ignorance and wrath at their insolence.”

“Shirley, how your eyes flash!”

“Because my soul burns. Would you, any more than me, let Robert be borne down by numbers?”

Caroline’s answer was low, but it had in it a steadiness that surprised herself—like a quiet hearth that keeps its heat though no flame shows.

“If I had your power to aid Robert, I would use it as you mean to use it. If I could be such a friend to him as you can be, I would stand by him, as you mean to stand by him, till death.”

“And now, Lina,” Shirley said, softened and yet keenly observant, “though your eyes don’t flash, they glow. You drop your lids; but I saw a kindled spark. However, it is not yet come to fighting. What I want to do is to prevent mischief. I cannot forget, either day or night, that these embittered feelings of the poor against the rich have been generated in suffering: they would neither hate nor envy us if they did not deem us so much happier than themselves. To allay this suffering, and thereby lessen this hate, let me, out of my abundance, give abundantly; and that the donation may go farther, let it be made wisely. To that intent, we must introduce some clear, calm, practical sense into our councils. So go and fetch Miss Ainley.”

Without another word Caroline put on her bonnet and departed.

It might seem strange that neither she nor Shirley thought of consulting Mrs. Pryor; but they abstained wisely. To involve Mrs. Pryor would be to draw her into an embarrassment that would pain her without profiting the plan. She was better informed, more read, a deeper thinker than Miss Ainley; but of administrative energy—of executive activity—she had none. Secret almsgiving suited her; she would subscribe her modest mite willingly; but public plans, large schemes, councils and conferences—these would have only reminded her of a deficiency she already knew too well. Shirley understood this by instinct, and spared her.

Miss Ainley, when summoned to Fieldhead to deliberate on projects so entirely her own element, had a day bright as harvest to her spirit. She sat with honour and deference at a table furnished with paper, pen, ink—and, best of all, money—requested to draw up a regular plan for administering relief to the destitute poor of Briarfield. She knew them all: knew whose cupboard held only dry meal, whose children went to bed with hollow bellies, whose fire had gone out for want of coal, whose sickness had eaten the little strength their bodies owned. She had long studied, with a quiet perseverance, what would succour them best if ever succour could be had.

Now, as she answered the eager questions of the two young ladies, her knowledge came out clear and serviceable; and a meek exultation, the purest kind of pride, warmed her as she found herself at last able not only to pity but to act. When Shirley placed at her disposal three hundred pounds, Miss Ainley’s eyes filled with joyful tears; for she saw, as if already, the hungry fed, the naked clothed, the sick visited and comforted. Her fingers trembled over the notes, not with greed, but with reverence, as if they were a new language in which mercy could speak.

She drew up, quickly and sensibly, a simple plan for expenditure; and she assured them brighter times would now come round, for she doubted not the lady of Fieldhead’s example would be followed. She would try to get additional subscriptions, to form a fund. But first—the clergy must be consulted. On that point she was peremptory. Mr. Helstone, Dr. Boultby, Mr. Hall must be consulted; not only Briarfield must be relieved, but Whinbury and Nunnely also. It would be presumption in her, she said, to take a single step unauthorized by them.

In Miss Ainley’s eyes the clergy were sacred beings. The individual might be small; the office made him holy. Even the curates—trivial, arrogant, absurd as they could be—she viewed, in her sincere enthusiasm, as sucking saints. Point out their little vices and huge follies, and still she could not see them; the white surplice covered a multitude of sins. Her faith in their station was a kind of dialect she could not unlearn: words like “reverence” and “authority” sounded to her with meanings that would not translate into any colder tongue.

Shirley, knowing this harmless infatuation in her newly chosen minister, stipulated expressly that the curates were to have no voice in disposing of the money—that their meddling fingers were not to be inserted into the pie. The rectors, of course, were paramount and might be trusted: they had experience, some sagacity; and Mr. Hall at least had sympathy and loving-kindness for his fellow-men. But the youths under them must be set aside, kept down, and taught that subordination and silence best became their years and capacity.

Miss Ainley heard such language with horror; her very hands lifted a little, as if to ward off disrespect. Caroline, with a mild word or two in praise of Mr. Sweeting, calmed her. Sweeting was indeed her own favourite. The slices of sponge-cake and glasses of cowslip or primrose wine she had at different times administered to him, when he came to see her in her little cottage, had been offered with sentiments of truly motherly regard—simple fare, but made precious by the kindness steeped into it, and by the remembered scent of spring hedges in the drink.

The same innocuous collation she had once presented to Malone; but that personage had shown such open scorn of the offering she had never ventured to renew it. To Donne she always served the treat, and was happy to see his approbation proved beyond doubt by the fact of his usually eating two pieces of cake and putting a third in his pocket.

Indefatigable where good was to be done, Miss Ainley would immediately have set out on a walk of ten miles round to the three rectors, to show her plan and humbly solicit approval. Miss Keeldar interdicted that at once. No; she proposed, instead, to collect the clergy in a small select reunion that evening at Fieldhead. Miss Ainley was to meet them there; the plan should be discussed in full privy council.

Shirley managed accordingly to get the senior priesthood together; and before the old maid arrived, she had further talked all the gentlemen into the most charming mood imaginable. She herself took in hand Dr. Boultby and Mr. Helstone. The first was a stubborn old Welshman—hot, opinionated, obstinate—yet one who did a great deal of good, though not without making some noise about it. The second was Helstone, as he always was. Shirley had a friendly feeling for both, especially for old Helstone, and it cost her no effort to be delightful: she took them round the garden, gathered them flowers, spoke to them like a kind daughter. The air among the beds held the clean, bruised scents of leaf and bloom; their steps on the gravel made a homely music under the open sky.

Mr. Hall she left to Caroline—or rather, it was to Caroline’s care that Mr. Hall consigned himself.

The morning after, Shirley sat with Caroline where the light fell kindly, warming the polish of the old furniture and laying a soft gilt edge on the very air. Through the half-open window came the garden’s mingled breath—box, damp earth, and some late wallflower refusing to be done with spring. A rook cawed beyond the trees; nearer at hand a bee worried at a blossom with the steady industry of a cook who knows exactly what must be done and when.

Shirley, still in that pleased humour which follows a well-managed table, spoke with the bright ease of a hostess who liked her own skill.

“I was glad last night answered,” she said. “I do like a little circle of gentlemen round a judiciously contrived meal. It is diverting to watch them grow amiable over what has been honestly considered—what has been seasoned, and warmed, and timed. Men pretend to live on glory and business; give them a well-roasted bird and a glass properly chosen, and you see what truly reaches them. We—women—are expected to be content with the thinking of it; but I own I take pleasure in the contrivance.”

Caroline smiled; she had enjoyed the dinner too, though her enjoyment was of the quiet kind that does not ask to be observed.

“And yet,” Shirley went on, turning the subject with that suddenness which was her way, “I watch Moore sometimes—your cousin Moore—to discover where he may be touched. But he has none of that child’s simplicity about food, of being pleased on the surface and showing it. Did you ever find his accessible point, Lina? You have seen more of him than I.”

“It is not, at any rate, like my uncle’s, nor Dr. Boultby’s,” Caroline returned, and because Shirley had led her there, she allowed herself that shy indulgence—the speaking of Robert’s name without being blamed by her own reserve. “But I cannot tell what it is. I never watched him in my life but my watching was soon baffled by finding he was watching me.”

“There!” Shirley’s eyes kindled with the quick triumph of recognition. “You cannot fix your gaze on him but his will presently flash on you. He is never off his guard. Even when he does not look, his mind seems moving among your own thoughts—tracing words back to their spring, weighing motives as if they were grain in a measure. I know that sort of man. It piques me. How does it affect you?”

Caroline had once been fluttered by these direct thrusts; now she had learned, like a little Quaker in a plain gown, to parry with gentleness.

“Pique you? In what way does it pique you?” she asked, letting the question return upon its owner.

Shirley’s attention flew elsewhere as suddenly as a bird startled from a hedge.

“Here he comes!” she cried, springing up and running to the window. “A diversion! I never told you of a conquest I have made lately—made at those parties you never will come to with me; and it was done without effort, without intention—so I protest. There is the bell—and by all that is delicious! there are two of them. Do they never hunt except in couples? You may have one, Lina, and you may take your choice. Listen to Tartar!”

Down the hall burst the deep bay of her great dog—black-muzzled, tawny, and huge—filling the house as though the very boards had learned to bark. After the bay came a growl, low and long, like thunder muttering behind a hill.

“Listen!” Shirley laughed. “You would think it the prelude to a bloody onslaught. They will be frightened. They do not know old Tartar as I do. They are not aware his uproars are all sound and fury, signifying nothing.”

But in the hollow of the hall there was indeed a scuffle, a sharp command, and then a crack of a cane or whip; immediately followed a yell, a run, the clatter of hurried feet, and the startled cries of servants.

“O Malone—Malone!”

“Down, sir! Down!” rang out a high, imperious voice—more indignant than brave.

“He really is worrying them,” Shirley exclaimed, half amused, half alarmed now. “They have struck him. A blow is what he is not used to, and will not take.”

Out she ran. The scene, for all its comic frenzy, had something too true of panic in it. One gentleman fled up the polished oak staircase with the speed of terror; another backed towards the stairfoot, flourishing a knotty stick as though he believed noise and timber could keep teeth away. Tartar—big as a calf, stubborn as a stone—bellowed, sprang, and set the hall in uproar. From the kitchen came the servants, tumbling out with aprons and startled faces, as if the very business of preparing dinners were being invaded by war.

The dog made a spring; the second gentleman turned and rushed after the first. The first was already safe in a chamber and held the door against his companion—nothing so merciless as fear. The other struggled hard; the door was about to yield.

“Gentlemen,” Shirley’s voice rang clear, silvery, and vibrating, “spare my locks, if you please. Calm yourselves! Come down. Look at Tartar—he won’t harm a cat.”

She was already at the dog. At her touch he crouched, fore paws stretched before him, tail still switching in stubborn resentment, nostrils snorting, dull fire in his eyes. He was honest, phlegmatic, stupid, and resolute; he loved his mistress and the man who fed him, and cared for little else. Quiet enough—unless threatened with a stick. Then some bulldog inheritance rose and made a demon of him.

“Mr. Malone, how do you do?” Shirley called up to the gallery, lifting her mirth-lit face. “That is not the way to the oak parlour. That is Mrs. Pryor’s apartment. Request your friend Mr. Donne to evacuate. I shall have the pleasure of receiving him in a lower room.”

A hollow laugh answered—Peter Augustus Malone leaning over the balustrade with offended dignity.

“Really that animal alarmed Donne,” he said, stiffening himself with an effort at composure. “He is—ah—a little timid. I thought it better to follow in order to reassure him.”

“It appears you did. Now come down, if you please. John—go upstairs and liberate Mr. Donne. And take care, Mr. Malone; the stairs are slippery.”

The caution came late. The oak had been polished by years of careful housewifery until it shone like a table newly rubbed for company. Malone slipped in his stately descent and saved himself only by clutching the banisters so that the whole great framework groaned.

Tartar, sensitive to any commotion, growled again. Malone, for all his surprise, was no coward; now he passed the dog with suppressed fury rather than fear, and if looks could have strangled, Tartar would have died where he lay. Forgetting politeness in his rage, Malone pushed into the parlour before Miss Keeldar, glanced at Caroline as if bending his head were a personal indignity, and glared at both ladies with such a dark promise of domestic tyranny that Shirley thought, with a momentary pity, how certain men look when crossed—as if they had been born to clutch and punish.

Shirley stopped laughing. Caroline, true lady, would not smile at any mortification, however deserved. Tartar was dismissed with a word; Malone was soothed—for Shirley could put a bridle on tempers with her voice, as deftly as she could order a meal. Malone had sense enough to perceive that, since he could not challenge the owner of the dog, he had better be civil. He tried to be civil; being received, he became very civil indeed, and presently quite himself. He had come, in truth, meaning to be charming and fascinating. If his entrance had been lion-like, he resolved to depart lamb-like.

He chose his seat for air—or for escape—neither on the sofa where Shirley offered him a sort of throne, nor near the fire, to which Caroline, by a gentle sign, invited him, but on a chair close to the door. Once his sullen heat had cooled, constraint returned; he spoke by starts, choosing the most painfully common topics, and between each fragment of talk he sighed as if the world were a burden and he a martyr to sensibility.

To help himself to grace, he drew from his pocket a large silk handkerchief—red and yellow—an ample bandana. His hands, having nothing wiser to do, set to work on it with earnest invention. He folded it cornerwise, whipped it open with a flourish, folded it narrower, made of it a band; and then, with solemn originality, he bound it round his crossed legs—circled his athletic Irish calves and tied them together as though he feared his limbs might run away without permission. He repeated this device more than once, as if expecting applause. Shirley fled to the window to laugh unseen; Caroline turned her face aside so her curls might hide the smile rising in spite of herself.

And Caroline observed, too, what was more instructive than comic: how completely, abruptly, and without shame Malone’s homage had wheeled from herself to the heiress. The little sum he had once calculated upon in Miss Helstone’s future dwindled to nothing beside Miss Keeldar’s estate and hall. He did not even trouble to disguise his arithmetic; it was as open as a butcher’s scale.

John’s steps were long absent above; Mr. Donne, it seemed, did not descend willingly. At length he appeared at the parlour door—cold, calm, and not in the least ashamed. Shame, indeed, required some warmth of nature, and Donne had none. He was of that immovable, complacent, self-satisfied kind which cannot blush because it cannot feel. No humiliation could touch him; he had no fire in his blood, no modesty in his soul—decorous, arrogant, insipid, and inane; and yet he had arrived at the notion of wooing Miss Keeldar as one might decide to purchase a chair.

His idea of courtship was mechanical: a certain number of visits, then a letter, then acceptance for love of his office; then marriage; then mastery of Fieldhead, servants at command, the best eating and drinking, and importance. He would have concealed his aims better had he possessed even that small instinct which teaches men how to conceal; but he entered with an injured impertinence, as if Fieldhead had wronged him.

“A very dangerous dog that, Miss Keeldar,” he said. “I wonder you should keep such an animal.”

“Do you?” Shirley replied coolly. “Perhaps you will wonder more when I tell you I am very fond of him.”

“I should say you are not serious. Can’t fancy a lady fond of that brute—’tis so ugly—a mere carter’s dog. Pray hang him.”

“Hang what I am fond of!”

“And purchase instead some sweetly pretty pug or poodle—something appropriate. Ladies generally like lap-dogs.”

“Perhaps I am an exception.”

“Oh, you can’t be, you know. All ladies are alike in those matters. That is universally allowed.”

“Tartar frightened you terribly, Mr. Donne. I hope you won’t take harm.”

“That I shall, no doubt. He gave me a turn I shall not soon forget. When I sor him”—so Mr. Donne pronounced it—“about to spring, I thought I should have fainted.”

“Perhaps you did faint in the bedroom; you were a long time there.”

“No. I bore up that I might hold the door fast. I was determined not to let any one enter. I thought to keep a barrier between me and the enemy.”

“But what if your friend Mr. Malone had been worried?”

“Malone must take care of himself.”

At that moment, past the glass door opening on the garden, Tartar walked, stiff and tawny and black-muzzled as ever, still in bad humour, emitting a growl and a half-strangled whistle—some stubborn note from his bulldog ancestry.

“What is that?” Donne cried. “I declare your man told a falsehood! The dog is there!”

“There are other visitors coming,” Shirley observed, with the provoking serenity of those who own the terror and can command it.
