Tartar sprang down the paved walk towards the gate, bellowing with explosion. Shirley opened the glass door and stepped out, chirruping to him as a cook might hush the sudden roar of a kettle. The bellow was silenced at once; the great brute lifted his blunt head to be patted.

“What! Tartar, Tartar!” said a cheery, rather boyish voice. “Don’t you know us? Good-morning, old boy!”

Little Mr. Sweeting came through the gate, fearless through sheer good nature, caressing the guardian as if they were old friends. His vicar, Mr. Hall, followed with quiet assurance; Tartar had no ill-will to him. After sniffing both with grave ceremony, the dog drew aside to the sunny front of the hall, leaving the archway clear. Sweeting would have played with him, but Tartar, obstinately indifferent to all hands save Shirley’s, ignored the caresses as if they were air.

Shirley met Messrs. Hall and Sweeting cordially, shaking hands, and heard their news—success that morning in obtaining subscriptions for the fund. Mr. Hall’s plain face shone with benignity; his eyes beamed through his spectacles. When Caroline, seeing who had come, ran out and put both her hands into his, he looked down on her with a gentle affection which made him seem at once grave and fatherly—like some mild scholar who had carried peace about with him into a rough world.

Instead of returning to the house, they wandered into the garden, the ladies on either side of Mr. Hall. The day was breezy and sunny; the wind freshened their cheeks and lifted their ringlets into graceful disorder. Shirley looked gay; Caroline looked quietly pretty. Mr. Hall spoke most to his brilliant companion, yet his eyes often sought the quieter one, as if accustomed to read her silence.

Flowers grew profusely in the enclosure, and their perfume—rose, mignonette, damp leaves bruised underfoot—rose and fell with the passing gusts. Shirley gathered handfuls, pressing the stems into her palm till the sap scented her fingers. She gave some to Caroline.

“Choose a nosegay for Mr. Hall,” she said.

Caroline, with her lap filled with delicate and splendid blossoms, sat down on the steps of a summer-house. Mr. Hall stood near, leaning on his cane, and there, in that ordinary garden ease—talk, wind, scent, and bird-notes—there was something almost sacred in their quiet companionship, as if the day were repeating itself from year to year, and friendship were a tradition kept like a household recipe, handed down and never improved upon because it was already right.

Shirley, remembering hospitality even while she strayed, called out the neglected pair still in the oak parlour. She convoyed Donne past his dread enemy, who lay snoring under the meridian sun, nose on fore paws, as harmless as any dog could look when asleep. Donne was glad of the safeguard; gratitude, as usual, did not occur to him.

Desiring to be impartial, Shirley offered the curates flowers. They accepted them with native awkwardness. Malone, holding bouquet in one hand and shillelah in the other, looked as if the two objects were sworn enemies and he did not know which to honour. Donne’s “Thank you!” was rich to hear—so fatuous, so self-important, as though a handful of blossoms were homage to his virtues and an attempt to win his priceless affection. Sweeting alone received his posy like the smart, sensible little man he was, and popped it gallantly into his buttonhole as if he had always belonged among gardens and ladies.

As a reward for that good manner, Shirley beckoned him apart and gave him some commission. Sweeting’s eyes sparkled; away he flew round by the courtyard to the kitchen, as much at home there as if he had been born to carry dishes. Presently he reappeared with a round table, which he set under the cedar; then he collected six garden-chairs from nooks and bowers, arranging them in a circle with cheerful fuss. The parlour-maid—Shirley kept no footman—came out with a tray covered by a napkin. Sweeting’s nimble fingers helped dispose glasses, plates, knives, and forks; then the napkin was lifted, and there lay the neat luncheon: cold chicken with its skin pale and savoury, slices of ham cut clean, and tarts whose browned edges promised crispness before the tongue even tried them.

This impromptu regale was Shirley’s delight. Nothing pleased her more than to have an alert little friend like Sweeting running about her hand, catching hints and executing them briskly. Between the house and the kitchen there was a little world of sound—soft clink of glass, the hush of linen unfolded, the knife’s clean tap against a plate—domestic music, wholesome and unpretending. It contrasted with the harsher noises beyond Fieldhead: the memory of factory clatter and public tumult that could batter the nerves like incessant hammering. Here, under the cedar, sound was proportioned—voices, birds, wind, and the small civil noises of eating—each in its place, each permitting thought and feeling to breathe.

Sweeting’s devotion to the heiress was, for once in such a world, quite disinterested; it did not shake his faithful allegiance to the magnificent Dora Sykes.

The repast proved merry. Donne and Malone contributed little to its gaiety—except what concerned knife, fork, and wine-glass; but where four such natures as Mr. Hall, David Sweeting, Shirley, and Caroline met in health and amity on a green lawn, under sun, amid a wilderness of flowers, no dulness could long hold ground. Even the very food seemed to set a tone—honest, kindly, and communal, as if salt and bread had a way of making people speak more truly.

In the course of talk Mr. Hall reminded them Whitsuntide was approaching, when the grand united Sunday-school tea-drinking and procession of the three parishes—Briarfield, Whinbury, and Nunnely—were to take place. Caroline would be at her post as teacher, he knew, and he hoped Miss Keeldar would not be wanting. He hoped she would make her first public appearance amongst them then.

Shirley’s eyes brightened. She liked festive excitement—the gathering of happiness, the arrangement of pleasant details, the sight and sound of gladness in a mass: children’s voices, hymns, the shuffle of many feet on grass, the rustle of holiday clothes, the clatter of cups and the sweet steam of tea rising from urns like a homely incense.

“They may count on me with security,” she said. “I do not know what I shall have to do, but they may dispose of me as they please.”

“And,” Caroline added, “you will promise to come to my table and sit near me, Mr. Hall?”

“I shall not fail, Deo volente,” he replied. Then, turning to Shirley, with a smile that showed how long memory can keep the same affection under altered years: “I have occupied the place on her right hand at these monster tea-drinkings for the last six years. They made her a Sunday-school teacher when she was a little girl of twelve. She is not particularly self-confident, as you may have observed; the first time she had to ‘take a tray’ and make tea in public there was piteous trembling and flushing. I saw the speechless panic—the cups shaking in the little hand, the teapot too full from the urn, slopping over in spite of her care. I went to her aid, sat near her, managed the urn and slop-basin, and in fact made the tea for her like any old woman.”

“I was very grateful,” Caroline interposed, and the quiet warmth of her tone did more than protestations.

“You were,” he said. “And you told me so with an earnest sincerity that repaid me well; for there are little ladies of twelve whom you may help and caress for ever without their showing a quicker sense of kindness than if they were wax and wood. She kept close to me the rest of the evening, Miss Keeldar—walked with me over the grounds where the children played; followed me into the vestry when all were summoned into church; she would, I believe, have mounted with me to the pulpit, had I not taken the precaution of conducting her to the rectory pew.”

“And he has been my friend ever since,” Caroline said.

“And always sat at her table, near her tray, and handed cups—that is the extent of my services,” Mr. Hall continued. “The next thing I do for her will be to marry her some day to some curate or mill-owner. But mind, Caroline”—and now there was a gentle seriousness under the jest—“I shall inquire about the bridegroom’s character; and if he is not a gentleman likely to render happy the little girl who walked with me, hand in hand, over Nunnely Common, I will not officiate. So take care.”

“The caution is useless,” Caroline answered, half smiling, half earnest. “I am not going to be married. I shall live single, like your sister Margaret.”

“You might do worse,” he said. “Margaret is not unhappy. She has her books for a pleasure and her brother for a care, and she is content. If ever you want a home—if the day should come when Briarfield rectory is yours no longer—come to Nunnely vicarage. Should the old maid and bachelor be still living, they will make you tenderly welcome.”

Caroline, who had kept aside the nosegay she had chosen, offered it now.

“There are your flowers. You don’t care for a bouquet, but you must give it to Margaret; only—let me be sentimental for once—keep that little forget-me-not. It is a wild flower I gathered from the grass; and—to be still more sentimental—let me take two or three of the blue blossoms and put them in my souvenir.”

She drew out a small book with an enamelled cover and silver clasp, opened it, and laid the flowers between the leaves, writing round them in pencil: “To be kept for the sake of the Rev. Cyril Hall, my friend. May —, 18—.”

Mr. Hall, on his part, also placed a sprig between the leaves of his pocket Testament. He wrote only on the margin one word: “Caroline.”

“Now,” he said, smiling, “I trust we are romantic enough. Miss Keeldar, I hope you are laughing at this trait of ‘exaltation’ in the old gray-headed vicar; but the fact is, I am so used to comply with the requests of this young friend of yours, I don’t know how to refuse her when she tells me to do anything. You would say it is not much in my way to traffic with flowers and forget-me-nots; but, you see, when requested to be sentimental, I am obedient.”

“He is naturally rather sentimental,” Caroline said softly. “Margaret told me so, and I know what pleases him.”

“That you should be good and happy? Yes—one of my greatest pleasures. May God long preserve to you the blessings of peace and innocence!” His tone deepened, as if the garden’s brightness had drawn out, by contrast, a thought solemn and enduring. “By which phrase I mean comparative innocence; for in His sight none are pure. What to our human perception looks spotless as we fancy angels, is to Him but frailty, needing the blood of His Son to cleanse, and the strength of His Spirit to sustain. Let us each and all cherish humility—my young friends, as well as I; we may well do it when we look into our own hearts and see there temptations, inconsistencies, propensities even we blush to recognize.”

He paused a moment; the wind sighed in the cedar above them; somewhere nearer, a finch repeated its small phrase, clear and unbought. In the distance, beyond Fieldhead’s shelter, there came faintly—like an ill omen of another world—the harsh, far-off grind of industry, a sound that had no tenderness in it and offered no rest.

“It is not youth,” Mr. Hall went on, “nor good looks, nor grace, nor any gentle outside charm that makes either beauty or goodness in God’s eyes. Young ladies, when your mirror or men’s tongues flatter you, remember that, in the sight of her Maker, Mary Ann Ainley—a woman whom neither glass nor lips have ever panegyrized—is fairer and better than either of you. She is indeed,” he added, after a pause—“she is indeed. You young things, wrapt up in yourselves and in earthly hopes, scarcely live as Christ lived. Perhaps you cannot yet, while existence is so sweet and earth so smiling to you; it would be too much to expect. She, with meek heart and due reverence, treads close in her Redeemer’s steps.”

The fund prospered.

Not by miracle, unless one counted as miracle the stubborn, daily labour of three rectors who would not be gainsaid; the shining example of Miss Keeldar, whose purse opened with a frankness that made parsimony blush; and the noiseless, diligent soldiership of their two aides—Mary Ann Ainley and Margaret Hall—both of them women who could contrive relief with the same neat economy wherewith they pinned on caps, mended cuffs, and kept accounts in a hand as fine as stitch-work.

A handsome sum was gathered; it was managed with prudence; and, for the present, hunger was kept from certain doors. In consequence, the neighbourhood wore, for a little space, a quieter face. For a fortnight no cloth had been torn in rage; no mill-window dashed in; no midnight threatening of mansion or manufactory carried into act. Shirley—who liked action, but not blood—was sanguine. She said the worst of the storm was over; that summer would mend trade as surely as it dried the lanes; that war must end, because even war was mortal; and that peace, once returned, would pour a new life into commerce.

Such was commonly the tenor of her observations to her tenant, Gérard Moore, whenever they fell together in those chance intervals where there was room for a word aside.

Moore listened—quietly; too quietly to content her. She would turn on him an impatient glance, demanding, by mere look, that he should say more—should explain himself, should contradict her, should at least add some single spark to her bright heap of hope. Then he would smile in his way—the mouth strangely sweet under the grave brow—and answer, in substance, that he too trusted in the finite nature of war; that upon that limit his anchor was fixed; that his present course depended on it.

“For you are aware,” he would go on, “that I now work Hollow’s Mill entirely on speculation. I sell nothing; there is no market. I manufacture for a future day—I make myself ready to seize the first opening. Three months ago this was impossible: credit and capital were exhausted. You well know who came to my rescue, from what hand I received the loan which saved me. It is on the strength of that loan I can continue a bold game which, a while since, I feared I should never play again. Total ruin will follow loss—I know it; gain is doubtful; yet I am cheerful. So long as I can be active—so long as I can strive—so long, in short, as my hands are not tied, I cannot be depressed. One year—nay, six months—of the reign of the olive, and I am safe; for, as you say, peace will give an impulse to commerce. In that you are right. But as to the restored tranquillity here—as to the permanent good effect of your charitable fund—I doubt. Eleemosynary relief never yet tranquillized the working-classes; it never made them grateful; it is not in human nature. Were all things ordered aright, they ought not to be forced into need of that humiliating relief—and that is what they feel. We should feel it in their place. Besides, to whom should they be grateful? To you, to the clergy perhaps; but not to us mill-owners. They hate us worse than ever. The disaffected here correspond with the disaffected elsewhere: Nottingham is a head-quarters, Manchester another, Birmingham a third. The subalterns take orders from chiefs; they are disciplined; no blow is struck without mature deliberation. In sultry weather you have watched the sky threaten thunder day by day, and yet night after night the clouds have cleared and the sun set quietly; but danger was not gone—only delayed. The long-threatening storm will break at last. There is analogy between the moral and physical atmosphere.”

“Well, Mr. Moore,”—and so these conferences always ended—“take care of yourself. If you think I have ever done you any good, reward me by promising to take care of yourself.”

“I do; I will take close and watchful care. I wish to live, not to die. The future opens like Eden before me; and still, when I look deep into the shades of my paradise, I see a vision I like better than seraph or cherub glide across remote vistas.”

“Do you? Pray, what vision?”

“I see—”

The maid came bustling in with the tea-things, and the vision was thrust back, like a word in a native dialect that has no safe place in company—something too intimate, too perilously untranslatable for the common ear.

The early part of May had been fine; the middle wet; and in the last week, at change of moon, it cleared again. A fresh wind went over the country like a broom with a clean purpose, sweeping off the silver-white, deep-piled rain-clouds and bearing them—mass on mass—eastward, till they dwindled on the horizon and slid behind its rim. The whole blue vault was left rinsed and ready for summer. On Whitsuntide the sun rose broad; Whit-Tuesday, which was the great day of the schools’ gathering, wore splendour like a holiday garment.

Briarfield, by old custom and by Mr. Helstone’s will (which in that district passed for law), was the point of rendezvous. The two large schoolrooms, built chiefly at the present rector’s expense, had been cleaned out, whitewashed, repainted, and dressed with flowers and evergreens—some from the rectory garden; two cartloads from Fieldhead; and a wheel-barrowful from the more grudging domain of De Walden, where Mr. Wynne dwelt. Twenty long tables—each to hold twenty guests—were laid with white cloths and flanked with benches. Above them, by a fancy local and dear to Mr. Helstone’s clerk, hung some twenty cages of canaries. The clerk delighted in their piercing song, and knew that amidst confusion of tongues they always carolled loudest: a small, wild music persistently keeping its own language in a room where human voices, polite and rude, struggled for precedence.

These tables were not for the twelve hundred scholars, but for patrons and teachers. The children’s feast would be spread out of doors, where grass and sunlight made a cleaner board than any carpenter. At one the troops were to come in; at two they were to be marshalled; till four they were to parade the parish; then the open-air meal; afterwards the meeting in church with music and speechifying.

Why Briarfield had the lead was not because it was the biggest—Whinbury surpassed it—nor the oldest—Nunnely’s mossy parsonage and low-roofed temple, buried in Nunnwood oaks, were older still. It was because Mr. Helstone willed it. Boultby could not dispute precedence; Hall would not. They let their resolute brother lead and rule.

This anniversary had always been a trying day to Caroline Helstone. It dragged her into public; it compelled her to face what was wealthy, respectable, influential in the neighbourhood; and, but for Mr. Hall’s kind countenance, she would have appeared unsupported. She must be conspicuous—must walk at the head of her regiment as the rector’s niece and first teacher of the first class—must make tea at the first table for a mixed multitude of ladies and gentlemen—all without mother, aunt, or other chaperon. Being nervous, and mortally afraid of publicity, she trembled at the approach of Whitsuntide.

But this year Shirley was to be with her, and the very thought changed the trial into something like pleasure. Miss Keeldar in her single self was better than a host of ordinary friends. She moved with ease; she was self-possessed, spirited, never presuming on her importance and yet perfectly conscious of it. Courage might be borrowed by merely looking at her.

The one fear was punctuality. Shirley had a careless way of lingering behind time; and Caroline knew her uncle would not wait a second for any one. At the moment the church clock tolled two, the bells would clash and the march begin. Shirley must be secured.

Whit-Tuesday saw Caroline rise almost with the sun. She, Fanny, and Eliza made busy hands all the morning, setting the rectory parlours in first-rate company order, arranging the chairs, throwing open the windows to admit air that smelled of washed earth and lilac, and setting out a collation of cooling refreshments—wine, fruit, cakes—upon the dining-room sideboard. There was a domestic confidence in such preparations: the order of cups and plates, the pale gleam of glass, the sugared promise of confection, all set against the more anxious order of the world outside, where men argued wages and threatened mills.

Then Caroline dressed—in her freshest white muslin; the fineness of the day and the solemnity of the occasion warranted it. Her new sash—Margaret Hall’s birthday gift, which she had reason to believe Cyril himself had bought—was tied by Fanny’s dexterous fingers; and in return Caroline had indeed given Cyril a set of cambric bands in a handsome case. Her simple bonnet was trimmed to answer the sash; her scarf of white crape suited her gown. When ready, she formed a picture not dazzling, but delicately pleasing; not richly coloured, but full of purity and grace. In her brown eye and clear forehead there was the same gentle harmony that lay in the folds of her dress—a mind modest, tender, pensive, yet not discordant with the bright day.

She was, after all, a faulty human being—fair, and anxious to do right—yet, as Cyril Hall had said, neither so good nor so great as withered Miss Ainley, now putting on her best black gown and Quaker-drab shawl and bonnet in her narrow cottage chamber, as one who asked no praise and expected no recompense.

Caroline went out and took the shortest, most hidden way to Fieldhead—across sequestered fields and through lanes so screened with hedge and young leaf that the world seemed reduced to birds, grass, and her own light footfall. After the late rains all was clean, and under the present sun all was dry. No dust rose to stain the hem of her white garment; no wet clung to her sandal. The country, washed and warmed, had a smell of new bread made from earth and sun.

She reached Fieldhead and penetrated to Miss Keeldar’s dressing-room.

It was well she came. Shirley, instead of making ready, lay stretched on a couch, absorbed in reading, as if time were her servant. Mrs. Pryor stood near, urging in vain that she should rise and dress.

Caroline wasted no words. She took the book from Shirley’s hand, and with her own began the business of disrobing and re-robing her. Shirley, indolent with heat and gay with youth, wanted to talk, laugh, linger. Caroline, intent on time, persevered—fastening strings, setting pins, smoothing folds. At last, as she united the final row of hooks and eyes, she found leisure to chide her—called her naughty, incorrigible, careless. And Shirley did look the picture of carelessness—yet a very lovely picture.

She contrasted wholly with Caroline. There was style in every fold of her dress, in every line of her figure. The rich silk suited her better than simplicity; the deep embroidered scarf became her, worn negligently but with grace. The wreath on her bonnet sat like a crown. Fashion and ornament, in her case, seemed not vanity but natural expression—like the frank light in her eyes, the rallying smile about her lips, her straight carriage and lightsome step.

Caroline caught her hand; hurried her downstairs and out; and together they sped through the fields laughing, looking like a snow-white dove and a gem-tinted bird of paradise joined in social flight.

Thanks to Miss Helstone’s promptitude, they arrived in good time. While trees still hid the church, they heard the bell toll—a measured, urgent summons. There was the trooping of numbers, the trampling of many feet, the low tide-sound of voices. From a rising ground they saw, on the Whinbury road, the Whinbury school approaching—five hundred souls. Rector and curate headed it: Boultby and Donne. Boultby loomed large in full canonicals, walking under the canopy of a shovel-hat, supported by the stoutest of gold-headed canes, and clothed in the squarest, vastest black coat. He flourished his cane now and then, and inclined his hat with a dogmatical wag to his aide-de-camp. Donne, narrow compared with his principal, contrived nevertheless to look every inch a curate: self-complacent from turned-up nose and elevated chin down to black gaiters, somewhat short strapless trousers, and square-toed shoes.

Walk on, Mr. Donne; you had undergone scrutiny. Whether the white and purple figures watching from the hill thought you well set forth was another question.

Those figures—Caroline and Shirley—came running down when the regiment had marched by. The churchyard was full of children and teachers in their best holiday attire; and, distressed as the district was, bad as the times were, it was wonderful to see how respectably, how handsomely even, they had contrived to clothe themselves. That British love of decency worked miracles. Poverty which would reduce an Irish girl to rags was impotent to rob an English girl of neatness, because neatness was part of her self-respect. Besides, the lady of the manor—Shirley, now gazing with pleasure—had indeed done them good. Her seasonable bounty had consoled many a poor family against the coming holiday, and supplied many a child with a new frock or bonnet. She knew it, and it elated her—not with sanctimony, but with a bright, bodily relief, as if she had fed some hunger she could not otherwise quiet within herself. She could not be charitable like Miss Ainley; it was not in her nature. It soothed her to see there were other ways of giving—ways practicable to other characters.

Caroline too was pleased, for she had done a little good in her own narrow measure—robbed herself of more than one dress, ribbon, collar she could ill spare, to help outfit the scholars of her class; and because she could not give money she had followed Miss Ainley in giving time and industry, sewing till her fingers ached, then sewing again.

Not only was the churchyard full; the rectory garden was thronged. Ladies and gentlemen walked among waving lilacs and laburnums; the house also was occupied; at the wide-open parlour windows gay groups stood. These were patrons and teachers who would swell the procession. In the parson’s croft behind the rectory stood the musicians of the three parish bands with their instruments; and Fanny and Eliza, in the smartest caps and gowns and the whitest aprons, moved amongst them serving out quarts of ale—sound and strong, brewed some weeks since under the rector’s special superintendence. Whatever Mr. Helstone touched must be done handsomely; shabby doings of any description were not endured under his sanction. From the building of school or church to the ordering of a dinner, he advocated the lordly, liberal, effective. Miss Keeldar resembled him in this; they mutually approved each other’s arrangements.

Caroline and Shirley were soon amid the company. Caroline met it more easily than ever she had done. Instead of stealing away to her own room till the procession should be marshalled, she moved through the parlours, conversed and smiled, even spoke once or twice before she was spoken to. She seemed a new creature; and it was Shirley’s presence that transformed her. Shirley had no fear of her kind, no instinct to shrink or avoid. Men, women, children—unless rendered positively offensive by low breeding or coarse presumption—were welcome enough to her. This disposition made her a general favourite. It robbed her raillery of sting and gave her serious talk a charm; yet it did not cheapen her intimate friendship, which was a separate, rarer thing depending on another part of her character. Miss Helstone was the choice of her affection and intellect; the Misses Pearson, Sykes, Wynne, and their like were merely the profiteers of her good-nature and vivacity.

Donne came into the drawing-room while Shirley, seated on the sofa, was the centre of a wide circle. She had already forgotten her exasperation against him; she bowed and smiled good-humouredly. The disposition of the man was seen at once. He neither declined the advance with dignity, as one whose pride is justly wounded, nor met it with frankness, as one glad to forget. His punishment had impressed him with no shame, and he did not feel it now, facing his chastiser. He was not vigorous enough in evil to be actively malignant; he only passed by sheepishly with a rated, scowling look. Nothing could again reconcile him to his enemy; yet no passion of resentment could his lymphatic nature fully know.

“He was not worth a scene,” said Shirley to Caroline. “What a fool I was! To revenge on poor Donne his silly spite at Yorkshire is like crushing a gnat for attacking the hide of a rhinoceros. Had I been a gentleman, I believe I should have helped him off the premises by dint of physical force. I am glad I only employed the moral weapon. But he must come near me no more. I don’t like him. He irritates me. There’s not even amusement to be had out of him. Malone is better sport.”

It seemed Malone wished to justify the preference; for scarcely had she spoken when Peter Augustus came up—all in grand tenue, gloved and scented, hair oiled and brushed to perfection, bearing in one hand a huge bunch of cabbage-roses, five or six in full blow. He presented them to the heiress with a gravity and flourish no pencil could adequately render.

Who after this should dare say Peter was not a lady’s man? He had gathered and he had given flowers; he had offered a sentimental tribute at the shrine of Love—or Mammon. Hercules with the distaff was but a faint type of Peter with his roses. He seemed amazed at himself; he backed without a word, going away with a husky chuckle of self-satisfaction; then bethought him to stop and turn, as if to certify by sight that he really had done this gallant thing.

Yes: there were the six red cabbages on the purple satin lap; a very white hand with gold rings slightly held them; and streaming ringlets, half hiding a laughing face, drooped over them. Only half hiding. Peter saw the laugh; unmistakable. He was made a joke of—his chivalry the subject of a jest for one petticoat, for two: Miss Helstone too smiled. Moreover, he felt himself seen through; and Peter grew black as a thunder-cloud. When Shirley looked up, a fell eye was fastened on her. Malone, at least, had energy enough in hate. She read it in his glance.

“Peter is worth a scene,” she whispered to Caroline, “and shall have it, if he likes, one day.”

Now, solemn and sombre in colour though bland enough in face, appeared at the dining-room door the three rectors. They had been busy in the church; they came now to take a little refreshment ere the march commenced. The great morocco-covered easy-chair had been left vacant for Dr. Boultby; he was placed in it. Caroline, prompted by Shirley—who told her this was the time to play hostess—hastened to hand to her uncle’s vast, revered, and, on the whole, worthy friend a glass of wine and a plate of macaroons.

Boultby’s churchwardens—patrons, as he insisted on their being—were already beside him. Mrs. Sykes and other ladies hovered right and left, expressing hopes he was not fatigued and fears the day would be too warm. Mrs. Boultby, who held that when her lord dropped asleep after a good dinner his face became as the face of an angel, bent over him and wiped some perspiration, real or imaginary, from his brow. Boultby rumbled out thanks in a round voix de poitrine and assurances of tolerable health. Of Caroline he took no notice beyond accepting what she offered. He did not see her—he never did see her; he hardly knew such a person existed. He saw the macaroons, however, and being fond of sweets, possessed himself of a small handful. The wine Mrs. Boultby insisted on mingling with hot water, qualifying with sugar and nutmeg.

Mr. Hall stood near an open window, breathing fresh air and the scent of flowers, talking like a brother to Miss Ainley. To him Caroline turned with pleasure; she must serve him, not let him help himself. She brought a little salver with variety. Margaret Hall joined them; so did Miss Keeldar. The four ladies stood about their favourite pastor, thinking, in their way, that they looked upon an earthly angel. Cyril Hall was their pope, infallible to them as Dr. Thomas Boultby to his admirers.

A throng enclosed the rector of Briarfield too. Twenty pressed round old Helstone; no parson was ever more potent in a circle. The curates, herding together after their manner, made a constellation of three lesser planets. Young ladies watched them from afar, but ventured not nigh.

Mr. Helstone produced his watch.

“Ten minutes to two,” he announced aloud. “Time for all to fall into line. Come.”

He seized his shovel-hat and marched away. All rose and followed en masse.

The twelve hundred children were drawn up in three bodies of four hundred souls each; at the rear of each regiment stood a band; between every twenty there was an interval where Helstone posted teachers in pairs. To the van of the armies he summoned—

“Grace Boultby and Mary Sykes lead out Whinbury.

“Margaret Hall and Mary Ann Ainley conduct Nunnely.

“Caroline Helstone and Shirley Keeldar head Briarfield.”

Then again he gave command—

The little band was no army, no troop of men sworn to strike, and yet their instruments persisted in rousing tunes as if the very hedgerows were a battlement and every green lane a parade-ground. The brass flung out its bright defiance; the drum beat with a hard, heart-steadying certainty; and even the children’s thin piping, caught up on the air, seemed to borrow courage from the rhythm.

Shirley Keeldar walked as if she heard more than music in it. It touched something old and impatient in her—some stored-up appetite for hazard, as if peace were a diet too pale to nourish her. Dr. Helstone, turning by chance, met the look in her face, and laughed outright; she answered him with a laugh as keen.

“There is no battle toward,” he said. “No tyrant at our throat, no invader on our shore. Nothing to do—only a walk. Keep your hand steady on the reins, captain, and spare your fire; it is not wanted.”

“Take your own counsel, doctor,” Shirley returned. Then, lowering her voice for Caroline alone, she added with that sudden, half-sweet intensity she could slip into as into a softer garment: “If the world will not give me an occasion, I must borrow one. We are not soldiers—no, I do not thirst for blood—but we might be soldiers of the Cross: time rolled back, we pilgrims with staves, bound for Palestine. No—too airy. I want something rougher. Let us be Scots Lowlanders, stealing up the moors behind a Covenanting captain to pray beyond the reach of troopers. Prayer, and then battle—heaven sure even if the peat is reddened. That music—do you feel it? It shakes one awake. It makes my heart beat not as it does on common days, but as if it had been starved and suddenly fed. I almost long for danger—for a faith, a land, or—at least—a lover to defend.”

Caroline, to break the spell she feared and yet half envied, pointed quickly. “Look, Shirley! What is that red speck above Stilbro’ Brow? Your eyes are better than mine.”

Shirley narrowed her gaze. “I see it. A line of red… soldiers—cavalry.” Her voice sharpened. “Six. They ride fast. They will pass us—no, they turn off. They saw our procession and go round. Where are they bound?”

“Exercising their horses, perhaps.”

“Perhaps.” Shirley watched until the red had melted into distance. “They are gone.”

Mr. Helstone, with the brisk authority that made children step in and curates fall silent, announced their short cut. “We shall go through Royd Lane, and reach Nunnely Common by it.”

Into Royd Lane they streamed—narrow, dug in between ditches, hedges leaning close so that two abreast was almost a presumption. The soundscape changed at once: the open field’s wide breathing was traded for cramped echoes. Every footfall and whispered remark struck back from thorn and bank; the brass grew harsher, the drum more emphatic, as if the lane itself forced aggression out of any tune.

They had gained the middle when the leaders began to twitch with a kind of anticipatory excitement. Boultby’s spectacles trembled; Helstone’s old, leonine brows lifted; curates nudged and craned. Mr. Hall, gentlest of commanders, turned with a smile that tried to be light.

“What is it?” the ladies demanded.

He pointed. At the far end of the lane, another procession entered—men in black at its head, music behind, and on the air a different sort of sound: not brass and drum, but the drawn-out, plaintive rise and fall of a hymn.

Shirley’s eyes flashed. “Our double? Our wraith turned up in broad day.”

“If you wanted a battle,” Caroline whispered, laughing, “you will have one—of looks, at least.”

“They shall not pass us!” cried the curates together, as if the narrow lane were a doctrine to be defended. “We’ll not give way!”

“Give way?” Mr. Helstone’s rebuke cracked like a cane. He wheeled on them. “Who talks of giving way? Mind yourselves. The ladies, I can trust; there is no churchwoman here who will be shoved aside by these folk, not when the honour of the Establishment is concerned. What says Miss Keeldar?”

Shirley lifted her chin. “I ask only—what are they?”

“The Dissenting schools,” said Helstone, grimly pleased to name an enemy—“Methodists and the rest, joined in an unholy alliance. They have turned into this lane on purpose—to stop us, to drive us back.”

“Bad manners,” said Shirley with sudden coldness. “And I detest bad manners. They must be taught.”

“A lesson in politeness,” urged Mr. Hall, always reaching for a gentler utensil than a weapon. “Not an example of rudeness.”

But Helstone was already striding forward, his step measured and inexorable, as if the packed earth under his shoes were a pulpit and he meant to preach with his body. He neared the opposing leaders. Their chief—large, oily-faced, black hair plastered flat—called a halt. The Dissenter procession stopped as one creature stops. Out came the hymn-book; out came a verse, and then a tune, and the lane filled with the most dolorous chant, heavy as wet wool.

Helstone turned his head and signed. The brass clashed, bright and brazen; the drums answered; and on his order the band struck up “Rule, Britannia!” The children, coached into boldness by the sheer noise of it, sang with a spirit that would have startled them in their own kitchens. The two sound-worlds collided—psalm against patriotic roar—till the hymn was battered down, broken, swallowed. If victory could be measured in decibels, the Church had conquered.

“Now, follow me!” Helstone called. “Not at a run. At a firm, smart pace. Be steady—every child and woman. Keep together. Hold one another’s skirts if you must.”

And forward they went, not with the foolish heat of a charge, but with a cool, compact thrust—solid as a cartwheel. The curates, wedged between Mr. Helstone in front and Miss Keeldar behind, dared no flourish; Helstone’s cane and Shirley’s parasol were ready law. The Dissenters, astonished at first, then alarmed, gave ground. The lane’s tight walls threw every sound back on itself—panting, brass, protest, the scrape of shoes—and then, at last, the opposing procession broke, turning aside and yielding the outlet.

Boultby suffered most; for a moment his breath failed him like an ill-stoked fire, and he would have gone down. But Helstone and Malone held him up—one on each side—forcing him through whole in limb, though the lane had wrung him like a soaked cloth.

The greasy Dissenter who had started the hymn ended sitting in the ditch, winded and humbled. He was a spirit-merchant by trade, a leader among the Nonconformists, and it was said afterwards—half in malice, half in wonder—that he drank more water that afternoon than he had swallowed in twelve months past. Mr. Hall, gentle even in triumph, had kept Caroline safe; and Caroline, with her quiet courage, had watched him too, steady as a hand on a cup that threatens to spill.

When they were clear of the lane, Helstone and Shirley shook hands with real relish—warriors after a skirmish, agreeing wordlessly that the manoeuvre had been neat. The curates began to crow; Helstone shut the stable door on their exuberance at once, telling them they had never sense to know what to say, and had best hold their tongues; it had not been their business to manage, and should not become their business to boast.

By half-past three the procession turned back; by four they were again at the school. The close-shorn fields had been set as if for a rustic rite: long benches in lines; huge baskets swaddled in white cloths; great tin vessels breathing steam, their mouths lifting the scent of strong tea into the air. Before any hand reached for food, Mr. Hall pronounced a brief grace, and the children sang it—young voices untrained and yet strangely moving in the open, as if the sky itself were a chapel roof.

Then came the feast: currant buns—big, dark-studded, their sweetness warm and sticky; tea poured hot and well-sugared, not miserly but generous, a brew that carried comfort on its tannined breath. There was a rule—unspoken like the best domestic laws—that no child should be stinted. Each was given twice what small stomach and small patience could consume, so there would be a reserve to carry home: a bun wrapped like treasure for an old grandmother; a crust and a pocket of sweetness for a sick brother. Meanwhile buns and beer went round among musicians and singers; and afterwards, when benches were removed, the players were turned loose into laughter with official permission.

A bell called the teachers, patrons, and patronesses into the schoolroom. It had been dressed for the occasion in a homely splendour: white walls brightened with evergreens and flowers; silver teapots catching the light; porcelain gleaming; trays laid out; and everywhere movement—women pressed into service as waiters, neighbours’ servants and clerks’ wives, eager to look their smartest. Their dresses rustled; their shoes clicked; their voices ran in a cheerful current, not loud, but lively. Above it all the canary birds trilled in their high cages—sharp, insistent music, like little coins dropped on metal, a reminder that even gaiety has its shrillness when shut indoors.

Caroline, as the rector’s niece, took her place at one of the first tables. Mrs. Boultby and Margaret Hall presided at the others. Equality was not served here with the tea: the élite had their benches, and the rest their own places, as in most parishes and most hearts. Miss Helstone removed bonnet and scarf, oppressed by the heat. Her curls fell on her neck like a veil; her muslin dress was modest as a novice’s, needing no shawl to complete its chastity.

The room filled. Mr. Hall took his station beside Caroline. As she set cups straight and counted spoons with quick fingers, she murmured to him about Royd Lane—trying to make light of it, because she saw the gravity in his face. Shirley sat near—oddly still, not laughing, not scattering sparks, but watching the door with a vigilance that felt like hunger disguised. More than once she spread her satin skirt over too much bench, or laid her gloves and embroidered handkerchief upon the space beside her, as if claiming it by occupation.

Caroline noticed at last. “Whom do you expect, Shirley?”

Shirley leaned so close her whisper was warm at Caroline’s ear, and softened by some secret tenderness. “Mr. Moore. I saw him last night. I made him promise to come with his sister—and to sit at our table. He will not fail me; but he will come too late and be separated. Here—people are arriving; every place will be taken. It is provoking.”

Indeed, the magistrate, Mr. Wynne, entered with his family in full ceremony—wife, son, and two daughters, as if they were an institution rather than persons. Their place was, of course, at the first table; and they were led there, filling what remained.

To Shirley’s discomfort, Sam Wynne—her particular aversion, made worse because he had the dull presumption to aim at her hand—planted himself precisely in the seat she had reserved, settling heavily on her gown, her gloves, her handkerchief, and all her patience. Rumour had even carried to her ears the old gentleman’s public jest that Fieldhead and the De Walden estate were delightfully contagious—his own mistaken word repeating itself through the neighbourhood like a bad tune no one can stop humming.

Caroline’s ears still rang with that whisper—“I expect Mr. Moore”—when the organ pealed over the hum. Dr. Boultby, Mr. Helstone, and Mr. Hall rose; all rose; grace was sung, music lifting and holding the room for a minute as if it could cleanse the air of its heat and small rivalries. Then tea began in earnest.

Caroline was kept busy, pouring and serving, until the last cup was filled. Only then did she glance about with restless eagerness. Some ladies and gentlemen still stood, unseated. She recognized, among them, Miss Mann—her solitary spinster friend—drawn from her drearness by weather or persuasion. Miss Mann looked worn with standing; a lady in a yellow bonnet brought her a chair.

Caroline knew that satin-yellow bonnet, knew the black hair and the kind face that could yet look opinionated and froward; knew the black silk gown and the gray linen shawl. Hortense Moore. The recognition struck her like a sudden sweetness: she felt an impulse to spring up, run to her, kiss her—once for Hortense herself, twice for her brother’s sake.

She half rose with a smothered exclamation. But a hand, light and decisive, pressed her back into her seat; and a voice behind her whispered, “Wait till after tea, Lina, and then I’ll bring her to you.”

When she looked up—when she dared—Robert Moore stood close behind, smiling at her eagerness. He looked better than she had ever seen him: his face clearer, his air steadier. To her partial eyes he was so handsome it hurt, as if the brightness of him were too keen for the day’s ordinary light, and branded itself on her memory as if lightning had done the engraving.

He moved on to speak to Shirley. Shirley, annoyed by Sam Wynne’s attentions, by his weight on her belongings, and by Moore’s late arrival, was in no mood to be gracious. She shrugged and spoke bitterly of his “insupportable tardiness.” Moore neither excused himself nor snapped back; he stood quietly, waiting, as if giving her time to come back to herself. In a few minutes she did. She offered him her hand. He took it with a smile that corrected without scolding, and thanked without servility; the faint shake of his head said one thing, and the gentlest pressure of his fingers another.

“You may sit where you can now, Mr. Moore,” Shirley said, smiling as if nothing sharp had passed. “There is not an inch of room for you here. I see plenty at Mrs. Boultby’s table—between Miss Armitage and Miss Birtwhistle. Go. John Sykes shall be your vis-à-vis, and you will sit with your back to us.”

He did not go. He lingered near, taking a turn down the long room, exchanging greetings with other men condemned to stand; and each time he returned to Shirley as if drawn by some magnet, bringing observations he chose to deliver in a low voice at her ear.

Sam Wynne, meanwhile, looked increasingly wretched. Shirley could not keep still: she fanned herself; complained of heat, lack of air, lack of space; declared that when people had finished tea they ought to leave the tables; announced, with perfect distinctness, that she expected to faint if matters continued. Sam offered to take her into the open air; she told him he would give her her death of cold. In short, having swallowed his tea, he judged retreat wise and evacuated the coveted seat.

Moore ought to have taken it at once. He was at the far end of the room, in talk with Christopher Sykes. Timothy Ramsden—the great corn-factor, stout and puffy, a man as large in property as in person—advanced instead, weary of standing, and minded to sit.

Shirley’s ingenuity answered like a match struck in a dark pantry. With a sweep of her scarf she upset her teacup. Tea spilled over bench and satin, steaming and staining. A waiter must be called; napkins fluttered; apologies were made; there was a little domestic tempest. Mr. Ramsden held aloof from the commotion with an air that implied he did not sit where women made accidents.

Shirley, usually indifferent to such trifles, now acted the delicate, distressed creature to perfection—declaring herself on the verge of swooning, fussing over the damage with a tremor no one had ever seen in her over any real hurt. Ramsden opened his mouth, reconsidered, withdrew, and beat a heavy retreat.

Moore returned at last. He surveyed the bustle calmly, scanned Shirley’s face with a faintly quizzical look, and remarked that indeed this end of the room was the hottest; that the climate would agree with none but cool temperaments like his. Then, pushing waiters and napkins and all the turmoil aside with quiet decisiveness, he installed himself in the vacated place as if destiny had reserved it.

Shirley subsided at once. Her brow unknit; the restless angles of her movements softened; the mischief and wilfulness that had tormented Sam Wynne fell away as if a spell had been lifted. Yet she did not reward Moore with a gracious glance. Instead she accused him of giving her “a world of trouble,” and charged him roundly with being the cause of her losing the esteem of Mr. Ramsden and the invaluable friendship of Mr. Samuel Wynne.

“I would not have offended either gentleman for the world,” she insisted. “I have always treated both with the most respectful consideration—and see, through you, how they have been used! I shall not be happy till I make it up. To-morrow I must make a pilgrimage to Royd corn-mill, soothe the miller, and praise the grain; and next day I must call at De Walden—where I hate to go—and carry in my reticule half an oatcake for Mr. Sam’s favourite pointers.”

“You know the surest path to the heart of each swain, I doubt not,” Moore replied, perfectly composed.

His composure suited him. It made him look handsomer, not by any softness, but by a steadiness that implied equality even where inequality stared from every circumstance. He sat beside a rich woman without looking poor; his calm denied the whole vulgar arithmetic of wealth. Sometimes, when he looked down at Shirley as he spoke, there was an odd effect—as if his station, by some inward right, stood above hers. A stern light would cross his brow and glint in his eyes; and though their talk remained low, it grew animated. Shirley pressed him with questions; he refused her the full food of answers, giving her instead careful morsels. She sought his eye with a softness that asked plainly for more; he smiled pleasantly and kept his lips sealed. She turned away, piqued; within two minutes he drew her back again, offering promises—soothing, substituting—until she accepted them as a child accepts sweetmeats when denied the feast.

The heat did not suit Caroline. She grew paler as the tea-making dragged. The moment thanks were returned, she slipped from the table and hurried after Hortense, who, with Miss Mann, had already sought the open air.

Robert Moore rose when Caroline did—perhaps meaning to speak; but Shirley detained him with a parting word, and in that brief interval Caroline vanished.

Outside, Hortense received her former pupil with more dignity than warmth. She had been seriously offended by Mr. Helstone’s proceedings, and held that Caroline was to blame for obeying her uncle too literally.

“You are a very great stranger,” she said austerely, though Caroline pressed her hand with affection.

Caroline knew Hortense too well to argue. She let the punctilious chill pass, sure the deeper kindness underneath—her bonté, neither mere goodness nor mere good-nature, but something living between—would rise.

It did. Hortense examined her face, saw the change in the thinner features, and her severity melted. She kissed Caroline on both cheeks and questioned her anxiously about her health. Caroline answered gaily; yet she would have been subjected to a long, thorough cross-examination and a lecture besides, had not Miss Mann called Hortense away by asking to be conducted home. The poor invalid was already fatigued; fatigue made her sharp. Caroline’s white dress and brighter look displeased her; she preferred her young friend in brown stuff or gray gingham, with the familiar air of melancholy. She scarcely seemed to recognize her tonight, and took leave with a cool nod. Hortense promised to accompany her, and they departed together.

Caroline, left, looked round for Shirley. She saw the rainbow scarf and purple dress in the midst of a throng of ladies—women Caroline knew well, and just as systematically avoided when she could. Shy by nature, and shyer in this moment, she had no courage to press into them. Yet she could not stand solitary where others paired and clustered.

She approached instead a group of her own scholars—older girls, nearly young women—standing to watch the younger children at blind-man’s buff. Caroline knew they liked her; nonetheless she felt awkward with them out of school, as if her authority belonged to the classroom air and would not travel into the open. They, by instinct and good feeling, respected her timidity. They did not tease or take advantage; they stood round her with civil friendliness, taking her slight smiles and hurried attempts at talk as kindly as if she had given them eloquence. Their good breeding sprang, as it so often does, from good heart.

Mr. Sam Wynne came up in haste, insisting that the elder girls should join the game as well as the younger; off they went, and Caroline was again alone. She was meditating a quiet retreat to the house when Shirley, seeing from afar her isolation, hastened to her side.

“Let us go to the top of the fields,” Shirley said. “I know you do not like crowds, Caroline.”

“But it will deprive you of pleasure,” Caroline answered, looking past Shirley to the group that courted her. “All those fine people seek you so assiduously; you can be pleasant to them without effort. Why should I steal you away?”

The evening had that close, honeyed warmth which makes the very air feel poured and settled, as if it had been simmered all day in a great copper pan and now lay thick over field and hedge. The sun, sinking, bled purple and Indian-red through a gauze of cloud; the horizon wore colours too rich for Yorkshire propriety—rose and saffron, bruised plum, a last flare like coals being raked. Every surface took the tint as if it were a glaze: the white fronts of houses, the boles of trees, the curves of the road, the broad backs of pasture-ground lifting and dipping like loaves.

Shirley Keeldar and Caroline Helstone came down from the fields at a lingering pace, as though they were reluctant to leave that open, breathing space. When they reached the churchyard the bells had fallen silent; the people had flowed inside and shut the door on their own murmurs. Outside, among the grey stones, the world held its hush.

“How pleasant and calm it is,” Caroline said, letting the stillness wash her like cool water.

“And how hot it will be in the church,” Shirley returned, fanning herself with her hat as if she could stir the air. “And what a dreary long speech Dr. Boultby will make; and how the curates will hammer over their prepared orations—each syllable like a pestle pounding stale meal. For my part, I would rather not enter.”

“But my uncle will be angry if he observes our absence.”

“I will bear the brunt of his wrath; he will not devour me. I shall be sorry to miss his pungent speech, though—sense for the Church, and causticity for schism. He will not forget the battle of Royd Lane. And I should be sorry, too, to deprive you of Mr. Hall’s friendly homily, with all its racy Yorkshireisms—words like good brown bread, honest and heavy. Still—here I must stay. Look at the church, Cary. Look at the stones. Grey upon grey—yet the west is laying a crimson relish over them, as if Nature had dressed even death for supper.”

She stood among the tombs as if she belonged there—unafraid of names cut in stone, unsoftened by them either, only brightened by the light. Her eyes, fixed on the burning west, had that quick, eager fire she got when some inward fancy seized her.

“Nature is now at her evening prayers,” Shirley went on, her voice dropping. “She is kneeling before those red hills. I see her prostrate on the great steps of her altar, praying for a fair night for mariners at sea, for travellers in deserts, for lambs on moors, and unfledged birds in woods. Caroline, I see her—and I will tell you what she is like. She is like what Eve was when she and Adam stood alone on earth.”

“And that is not Milton’s Eve, Shirley.”

“Milton’s Eve! Milton’s Eve!” Shirley repeated with a little flare of impatience, as though the name were a dish too often served. “No—by the pure Mother of God, she is not. Cary, we are alone; we may speak what we think. Milton was great; but was he good? His brain was right—how was his heart? He saw heaven; he looked down on hell. He saw Satan, and Sin his daughter, and Death their horrible offspring. Angels serried their battalions; the long lines of adamantine shields flashed back on his blind eyeballs the unutterable splendour of heaven. Devils gathered their legions in his sight; their dim, discrowned armies passed rank and file before him. Milton tried to see the first woman—but, Cary, he saw her not.”

“You are bold to say so, Shirley.”

“Not more bold than faithful. It was his cook that he saw—or it was Mrs. Gill, as I have seen her, making custards in the heat of summer, in the cool dairy with rose-trees and nasturtiums about the latticed window; preparing a cold collation for rectors—preserves and dulcet creams—puzzled what choice to choose for delicacy best; what order so contrived as not to mix tastes not well-joined, inelegant, but bring taste after taste, upheld with kindliest change.”

Caroline could not help a smile; even in Shirley’s irreverence there was something domestic—something that took the grand and brought it close to hand, like a great idea tested by the tongue.

“All very well too, Shirley.”

“I would beg to remind him that the first men of the earth were Titans—and that Eve was their mother. From her sprang Saturn, Hyperion, Oceanus; she bore Prometheus—”

“Pagan that you are! What does that signify?”

“It signifies that there were giants on the earth in those days—giants that strove to scale heaven. The first woman’s breast that heaved with life on this world yielded the daring which could contend with Omnipotence; the strength which could bear a thousand years of bondage; the vitality which could feed that vulture death through uncounted ages; the unexhausted life and uncorrupted excellence—sisters to immortality—which, after millenniums of crimes, struggles, and woes, could conceive and bring forth a Messiah. The first woman was heaven-born. Vast was the heart whence gushed the well-spring of the blood of nations, and grand the undegenerate head where rested the consort-crown of creation.”

“She coveted an apple and was cheated by a snake,” Caroline said, half laughing, half vexed; “but you have got such a hash of Scripture and mythology into your head there is no making any sense of you. You have not yet told me what you saw kneeling on those hills.”

“I saw—I now see—a woman-Titan,” Shirley answered, and her tone was changed, softened by awe. “Her robe of blue air spreads to the outskirts of the heath where yonder flock is grazing; a veil white as an avalanche sweeps from her head to her feet, and arabesques of lightning flame on its borders. Under her breast I see her zone, purple like that horizon; through its blush shines the star of evening. Her steady eyes I cannot picture. They are clear; they are deep as lakes; they are lifted and full of worship; they tremble with the softness of love and the lustre of prayer. Her forehead has the expanse of a cloud and is paler than the early moon, risen long before dark gathers. She reclines her bosom on the ridge of Stilbro’ Moor; her mighty hands are joined beneath it. So kneeling, face to face, she speaks with God. That Eve is Jehovah’s daughter, as Adam was His son.”

“She is very vague and visionary. Come, Shirley, we ought to go into church.”

“Caroline, I will not. I will stay out here with my mother Eve—in these days called Nature. I love her—undying, mighty being! Heaven may have faded from her brow when she fell in paradise, but all that is glorious on earth shines there still. She is taking me to her bosom and showing me her heart. Hush, Caroline! You will see her and feel as I do, if we are both silent.”

“I will humour your whim,” Caroline said; “but you will begin talking again ere ten minutes are over.”

Shirley, on whom the warm evening seemed to work with unwonted power, leaned against an upright headstone. The stone held the day’s stored heat; it warmed her shoulder through her sleeve like a hand. She fixed her eyes on the deep-burning west and sank into a still, pleasurable trance.

Caroline wandered a little apart beneath the rectory garden wall, where the earth smelt of mint bruised underfoot and of box and old damp brick; bees went home in a low, drowsy drone; a thrush, somewhere hidden, tried a few notes and fell silent again. The word Shirley had spoken—mother—did not summon to Caroline any Titan of hill and cloud. It called up, instead, a gentle human form: a face she had never seen, yet had been shaping in fancy since childhood; a voice she had never heard, yet had longed for till longing itself felt like hunger.

Oh that the day would come when she would remember her child! Oh that she might know her—and, knowing, love her!

The craving that had kept her awake in her crib—when the house was quiet and the candle wicks died down and even the cats ceased to stir—returned with sudden, aching warmth. For years she had tried to smother it, afraid of the folly of hoping; yet now it rekindled, and her heart, like a hearth revived, glowed with the thought that perhaps one day her mother would come, would send for her, would look upon her with eyes not strange but fond, and say, in a sweet voice, “Caroline, my child, I have a home for you; you shall live with me. All the love you have needed and not tasted from infancy I have saved for you carefully. Come; it shall cherish you now.”

A sound cut across that inward softness—metal and hoof and discipline, the road speaking in an altered tongue. Both girls started, as if some unseen cook had dropped a pan in a quiet kitchen. They listened: the measured tramp of horses; then, through the trees, a glint as of steel catching fire. Between leaves they saw flashes of martial scarlet; a helm shone, a plume waved. Silent and orderly, six soldiers rode softly by, their very restraint more alarming than noise.

“The same we saw this afternoon,” Shirley whispered. “They have been halting somewhere till now. They wish to be as little noticed as possible, and are seeking their rendezvous at this quiet hour, while the people are at church. Did I not say we should see unusual things ere long?”

Scarcely had the last hoof-beat been swallowed by the hedges when another disturbance broke the night-hush—no disciplined sound, but the raw cry of impatience: a child’s scream, sharp as a knife on a plate. They turned. A man had come out of the church carrying an infant—a robust, ruddy boy of about two—roaring with all the lung-power that good milk and sound sleep can furnish. Two little girls followed, nine and ten, neat as if dressed for a fair-day. The cooler air, the change of scene, and the bribery of flowers snatched from a grave—little white heads with green stems, still damp—soon quieted the boy. The man sat down with him and dandled him on his knee as tenderly as any woman; the girls took their places close, one on either side, as if to fence their small brother in with their bodies.

Shirley narrowed her eyes a moment, then her face opened.

“Good-evening, William.”

The man lifted his hat and grinned with a broad, pleased simplicity. It was Farren—rough-headed, hard-featured, not old but weather-beaten, as if wind and work had tanned him into his own coat. His clothes were clean; his children’s, singularly neat—washed, mended, and buttoned with a thrift that had pride in it.

“You are not going into the church?” he asked, looking at them with a complacency touched by bashfulness—not awe of their station, but a kind of shy pleasure in their youth and elegance, as one might be shy before a fine flower not meant for one’s own garden.

William, with gentlemen, was often dogged; with proud or insolent ladies he could be unmanageable, resentful. But good-humour and civility melted him. His stubborn nature was repelled by inflexibility in others; for that reason he had never been able to like his former master, Moore. Unaware that Moore, behind his cold face, had done him a service—recommending him as gardener to Mr. Yorke and so opening him a path—William still nursed his grudge against austere manners as if they were an injury.

Latterly he had often worked at Fieldhead. Shirley’s frank hospitality charmed him; Caroline he had known from childhood, and without knowing it he kept her as his ideal of a lady. There was something in her gentleness—her step, her gesture, the very way her dress fell—that moved quiet artist-fibres in his peasant heart. He liked to look at her as he liked to examine rare flowers or stand and drink in a pleasant landscape. Both young women liked William; they lent him books, gave him plants, and preferred his talk to that of many coarse, hard, pretentious people immeasurably higher in station.

“Who was speaking, William, when you came out?” Shirley asked.

“A gentleman ye set a deal of store on, Miss Shirley—Mr. Donne.”

“You look knowing, William. How did you find out my regard for Mr. Donne?”

“Ay, Miss Shirley, there’s a gleg light i’ your een sometimes which betrays you. You look raight down scornful sometimes when Mr. Donne is by.”

“Do you like him yourself, William?”

“Me? I’m stalled o’ t’ curates, and so is t’ wife. They’ve no manners. They talk to poor folk fair as if they thought they were beneath them. They’re allus magnifying their office. It’s a pity but their office could magnify them; but it does nought o’ t’ soart. I fair hate pride.”

“But you are proud in your own way yourself,” Caroline put in gently. “You are what you call house-proud: you like to have everything handsome about you. Sometimes you look as if you were almost too proud to take your wages. When you were out of work, you were too proud to get anything on credit. But for your children, I believe you would rather have starved than gone to the shops without money; and when I wanted to give you something—what a difficulty I had in making you take it!”

He shifted the child higher on his knee, as if the boy’s weight steadied him.

“It is partly true, Miss Caroline. Ony day I’d rather give than take—especially from sich as ye. Look at t’ difference between us. Ye’re a little, young, slender lass, and I’m a great strong man; I’m rather more nor twice your age. It is not my part, then, I think, to tak fro’ ye—to be under obligations, as they say, to ye. And that day ye came to our house and called me to t’ door and offered me five shillings, which I doubt ye could ill spare—for ye’ve no fortin’, I know—that day I war fair a rebel, a radical, an insurrectionist; and ye made me so. I thought it shameful that, willing and able as I was to work, I suld be i’ such a condition that a young cratur about t’ age o’ my own eldest lass suld think it needful to come and offer me her bit o’ brass.”

“I suppose you were angry with me, William?”

“I almost was, in a way. But I forgave ye varry soon. Ye meant well. Ay, I am proud—and so are ye; but your pride and mine is t’ raight mak—what we call i’ Yorkshire clean pride—such as Mr. Malone and Mr. Donne knows nought about. Theirs is mucky pride. Now, I shall teach my lasses to be as proud as Miss Shirley there, and my lads to be as proud as myseln; but I dare ony o’ ’em to be like t’ curates. I’d lick little Michael if I seed him show any signs o’ that feeling.”

“What is the difference, William?” Shirley asked, not unkindly, though her eyes laughed.

“Ye know t’ difference weel enow, but ye want me to get a gate o’ talking. Mr. Malone and Mr. Donne is almost too proud to do aught for theirseln; we are almost too proud to let anybody do aught for us. T’ curates can hardly bide to speak a civil word to them they think beneath them; we can hardly bide to tak an uncivil word fro’ them that thinks themseln aboon us.”

“Now, William, be humble enough to tell me truly how you are getting on,” Shirley said. “Are you well off?”

“Miss Shirley, I am varry well off. Since I got into t’ gardening line, wi’ Mr. Yorke’s help, and since Mr. Hall—another o’ t’ raight sort—helped my wife to set up a bit of a shop, I’ve nought to complain of. My family has plenty to eat and plenty to wear.” He said it with the quiet satisfaction of a man who hears, in his own kitchen, the good sounds of well-being: the steady bubble of broth, the scrape of a knife on a board, the clink of a cup set down without fear of it being the last. “My pride makes me find means to have an odd pound now and then against rainy days; for I think I’d die afore I’d come to t’ parish; and me and mine is content. But t’ neighbours is poor yet. I see a great deal of distress.”

“And consequently there is still discontent, I suppose?” Shirley asked.

“Consequently—ye say right—consequently. In course starving folk cannot be satisfied or settled folk. The country’s not in a safe condition—I’ll say so mich!”

“But what can be done? What more can I do, for instance?”

“Do? Ye can do not mich, poor young lass! Ye’ve gi’en your brass; ye’ve done well. If ye could transport your tenant, Mr. Moore, to Botany Bay, ye’d happen do better. Folks hate him.”

“William, for shame!” Caroline cried, warmth rising in her as quickly as a blush. “If folks do hate him, it is to their disgrace, not his. Mr. Moore himself hates nobody. He only wants to do his duty and maintain his rights. You are wrong to talk so.”

“I talk as I think. He has a cold, unfeeling heart, yond’ Moore.”

“But,” Shirley said, turning the matter as neatly as a cook turns an argument in a pan, “supposing Moore was driven from the country and his mill razed to the ground—would people have more work?”

“They’d have less. I know that, and they know that. And there is many an honest lad driven desperate by t’ certainty that whichever way he turns he cannot better himself; and there is dishonest men plenty to guide ’em to the devil—scoundrels that reckons to be t’ people’s friends, and that knows nought about the people, and is as insincere as Lucifer. I’ve lived aboon forty year i’ t’ world, and I believe that the people will never have any true friends but theirseln, and them two or three good folk i’ different stations that is friends to all the world. Human natur’, taking it i’ th’ lump, is nought but selfishness. It is but excessive few—it is but just an exception here and there, now and then—sich as ye two young uns and me—that, being in a different sphere, can understand t’ one t’ other, and be friends wi’out slavishness o’ one hand or pride o’ t’ other. Them that reckons to be friends to a lower class than their own fro’ political motives is never to be trusted; they always try to make their inferiors tools. For my own part, I will neither be patronized nor misled for no man’s pleasure. I’ve had overtures made to me lately that I saw were treacherous, and I flung ’em back i’ the faces o’ them that offered ’em.”

“You won’t tell us what overtures?” Shirley asked, quickened.

“I will not. It would do no good. It would mak no difference. Them they concerned can look after theirseln.”

“Ay, we’se look after werseln,” said another voice.

Joe Scott had sauntered out of the church to get a breath of air, and there he stood—overlooker and oracle by his own valuation—his very posture a reprimand to the world.

“I’ll warrant ye, Joe,” William said, with a smile that took the edge off his bluntness.

“And I’ll warrant my maister,” Joe returned, with a stiff assurance. Then, to the young ladies, assuming a lordly air: “Ye’d better go into th’ house.”

“I wonder what for?” Shirley asked. Joe’s pragmatical manners were familiar to her; she was often at war with him. He held supercilious theories about women, and the fact that his master’s mill and his master himself were, in a manner, under petticoat government, was wormwood and gall to his secret soul. He had especially resented those business visits of the heiress to the Hollow’s counting-house, as if her very step among ledgers were an affront.

“Because there is nought agate that fits women to be consarned in.”

“Indeed! There is prayer and preaching agate in that church. Are we not concerned in that?”

“Ye have been present neither at the prayer nor preaching, ma’am, if I have observed aright. What I alluded to was politics. William Farren here was touching on that subject, if I’m not mista’en.”

“Well, what then? Politics are our habitual study, Joe. Do you know I see a newspaper every day, and two of a Sunday?”

“I should think you’ll read the marriages, probably, miss, and the murders, and the accidents, and sich like?”

“I read the leading articles, Joe, and the foreign intelligence; and I look over the market prices. In short, I read just what gentlemen read.”

Joe looked as though he considered this talk mere chatter—like a pie bird’s noise in a kitchen where men were weighing sacks. He replied by a disdainful silence.

“Joe,” Shirley continued, “I never yet could ascertain properly whether you are a Whig or a Tory. Pray, which party has the honour of your alliance?”

“It is rayther difficult to explain where you are sure not to be understood,” Joe answered, his pride stiffening his vowels; “but as to being a Tory, I’d as soon be an old woman, or a young one—which is a more flimsier article still. It is the Tories that carries on the war and ruins trade; and if I be of any party—though political parties is all nonsense—I’m of that which is most favourable to peace, and, by consequence, to the mercantile interests of this here land.”

“So am I, Joe,” Shirley said, delighted to bait him. “Partly, at least. I have rather a leaning to the agricultural interest too—as good reason is, seeing that I don’t desire England to be under the feet of France; and that, if a share of my income comes from Hollow’s Mill, a larger share comes from the landed estate around it. It would not do to take any measures injurious to the farmers, Joe, I think?”

“The dews at this hour is unwholesome for females,” Joe observed, as if changing the subject could shut a woman’s mouth like a lid on a pot.

“If you make that remark out of interest in me,” Shirley replied, “I have merely to assure you I am impervious to cold. I should not mind taking my turn to watch the mill one of these summer nights, armed with your musket, Joe.”

Joe’s chin, always rather prominent, poked out at this speech some inches farther than usual.

“But—to go back to my sheep,” she continued, “clothier and mill-owner as I am, besides farmer, I cannot get out of my head a certain idea that we manufacturers and persons of business are sometimes a little—a very little—selfish and short-sighted in our views, and rather too regardless of human suffering; rather heartless in our pursuit of gain. Don’t you agree with me, Joe?”

“I cannot argue where I cannot be comprehended,” was again the answer.

“Man of mystery! Your master will argue with me sometimes, Joe. He is not so stiff as you are.”

“Maybe not. We’ve all our own ways.”

“Joe, do you seriously think all the wisdom in the world is lodged in male skulls?”

“I think that women are a kittle and a froward generation,” Joe said, with the air of quoting a sound authority, “and I’ve a great respect for the doctrines delivered in the second chapter of St. Paul’s first Epistle to Timothy.”

“What doctrines, Joe?” Shirley demanded.

“‘Let the woman learn in silence, with all subjection. I suffer not a woman to teach, nor to usurp authority over the man, but to be in silence. For Adam was first formed, then Eve.’”

“What has that to do with the business?” Shirley cut in. “That smacks of rights of primogeniture. I’ll bring it up to Mr. Yorke the first time he inveighs against those rights.”

“And,” Joe continued, doggedly pleased with his weapon, “Adam was not deceived, but the woman being deceived was in the transgression.”

“More shame to Adam to sin with his eyes open!” Shirley cried. “To confess the honest truth, Joe, I never was easy in my mind concerning that chapter. It puzzles me.”

“It is very plain, miss. He that runs may read.”

“He may read it in his own fashion,” Caroline said, entering quietly now, but with a firmness that had its own flavour. “You allow the right of private judgment, I suppose, Joe?”

“My certy, that I do! I allow and claim it for every line of the holy Book.”

“Women may exercise it as well as men?”

“Nay. Women is to take their husbands’ opinion, both in politics and religion. It’s wholesomest for them.”

“Oh! oh!” both Shirley and Caroline exclaimed together, as if he had offered them some bitter draught and called it wholesome.

“Consider yourself groaned down and cried shame over for such a stupid observation,” Shirley said. “You might as well say men are to take the opinions of their priests without examination. Of what value would a religion so adopted be? It would be mere blind, besotted superstition.”

“And what is your reading, Miss Helstone, o’ these words o’ St. Paul’s?” Joe demanded, turning on Caroline with the satisfaction of a man who thinks he has found a softer adversary.

“Hem! I—I account for them in this way,” Caroline answered, a little flurried, yet earnest. “He wrote that chapter for a particular congregation of Christians, under peculiar circumstances; and besides, I dare say if I could read the original Greek I should find many of the words have been wrongly translated—perhaps misapprehended altogether. It would be possible, I doubt not, with a little ingenuity, to give the passage quite a contrary turn—to make it say, ‘Let the woman speak out whenever she sees fit to make an objection.’ ‘It is permitted to a woman to teach and to exercise authority as much as may be. Man, meantime, cannot do better than hold his peace;’ and so on.”

“That willn’t wash, miss.”

“I dare say it will. My notions are dyed in faster colours than yours, Joe. Mr. Scott, you are a thoroughly dogmatical person, and always were. I like William better than you.”

“Joe is well enough in his own house,” Shirley said, half conciliatory, half amused. “I have seen him as quiet as a lamb at home. There is not a better nor a kinder husband in Briarfield. He does not dogmatize to his wife.”

“My wife is a hard-working, plain woman,” Joe replied, “time and trouble has ta’en all the conceit out of her. But that is not the case with you, young misses. And then you reckon to have so much knowledge; and i’ my thoughts it’s only superficial sort o’ vanities you’re acquainted with.”

He leaned forward a little, as if to speak confidentially, yet with malice enough to season his words.

“I can tell—happen a year sin’—one day Miss Caroline coming into our counting-house when I war packing up summat behind t’ great desk, and she didn’t see me, and she brought a slate wi’ a sum on it to t’ maister.
