It war only a bit of a sum in practice, that our Harry would have settled i’ two minutes. She couldn’t do it. Mr. Moore had to show her how. And when he did show her, she couldn’t understand him.”

“Nonsense, Joe!” Shirley cried, quick to defend.

“Nay, it’s no nonsense. And Miss Shirley there reckons to hearken to t’ maister when he’s talking ower trade—so attentive like—as if she followed him word for word, and all war as clear as a lady’s looking-glass to her een; and all t’ while she’s peeping and peeping out o’ t’ window to see if t’ mare stands quiet; and then looking at a bit of a splash on her riding-skirt; and then glancing glegly round at wer counting-house cobwebs and dust, and thinking what mucky folk we are, and what a grand ride she’ll have just i’ now ower Nunnely Common. She hears no more o’ Mr. Moore’s talk nor if he spake Hebrew.”

Dusk had come softly down on Briarfield, as if laid like a cool cloth over a heated brow. The air, washed clean by no rain but by the day’s own sinking, grew so clear that the first stars pricked through it easily—pin-bright points in a sky still faintly pearl where the west would not quite let go.

“There will be just light enough to show me the way home,” said Miss Keeldar at the rectory garden door, settling her shawl as one might settle courage on the shoulders.

“You must not go alone, Shirley; Fanny shall accompany you.”

“That she shall not. In my own parish—what am I to fear? I would walk from Fieldhead to the church any fine midsummer night, three hours later than this, for the mere pleasure of seeing the stars—and the chance of meeting a fairy.”

“But only wait till the crowd is cleared away.”

“Agreed. There go the five Misses Armitage, streaming like a flock. And here comes Mrs. Sykes’s phaeton—Mr. Wynne’s close carriage—Mrs. Birtwhistle’s. I do not wish to go through the ceremony of bidding them all good-bye. Let us step into the garden and take shelter amongst the laburnums for an instant.”

They slipped back among blossoms and drooping gold, letting the polite commotion spend itself. The church porch emptied: rectors, curates, churchwardens; handshakes, praises for speeches, anxious cautions against the night air. Wheels crunched, harness jingled, voices receded. The village, that had been so full of words, began to empty into distance.

Miss Keeldar was just leaving her flowery refuge when Mr. Helstone came briskly into the garden and met her, as though he had been hunting her among the shrubs.

“Oh, I want you!” he said. “I was afraid you were already gone.—Caroline, come here.”

Caroline came quickly, both she and Shirley expecting rebuke for her invisibility in church. But the rector’s mind had flown to other matters.

“I shall not sleep at home to-night,” he began. “I have just met with an old friend, and promised to accompany him. I shall return probably about noon to-morrow. Thomas, the clerk, is engaged, and I cannot get him to sleep in the house, as I usually do when I am absent for a night. Now—”

“Now,” broke in Shirley, with that lightness that would not wholly hide her quick intelligence, “you want me as a gentleman—the first gentleman in Briarfield, in short—to supply your place, be master of the rectory and guardian of your niece and maids while you are away?”

“Exactly, captain. I thought the post would suit you. Will you favour Caroline so far as to be her guest for one night? Will you stay here instead of going back to Fieldhead?”

“And what will Mrs. Pryor do? She expects me home.”

“I will send her word. Come, make up your mind to stay. It grows late; the dew falls heavily. You and Caroline will enjoy each other’s society, I doubt not.”

“I promise you, then, to stay with Caroline,” said Shirley. “As you say, we shall enjoy each other’s society. We will not be separated to-night. Now, rejoin your old friend, and fear nothing for us.”

Mr. Helstone’s eye—half fatherly, half keen—rested on her as if measuring the steel under the ribbon.

“If there should chance to be any disturbance in the night, captain; if you should hear the picking of a lock, the cutting out of a pane of glass, a stealthy tread of steps about the house (and I need not fear to tell you, who bear a well-tempered, mettlesome heart under your girl’s ribbon sash, that such little incidents are very possible in the present time), what would you do?”

“Don’t know; faint, perhaps—fall down, and have to be picked up again. But, doctor, if you assign me the post of honour, you must give me arms. What weapons are there in your stronghold?”

“You could not wield a sword?”

“No; I could manage the carving-knife better.”

“You will find a good one in the dining-room sideboard—a lady’s knife, light to handle, and as sharp-pointed as a poniard.”

“It will suit Caroline. But you must give me a brace of pistols. I know you have pistols.”

“I have two pairs. One pair I can place at your disposal. You will find them suspended over the mantelpiece of my study in cloth cases.”

“Loaded?”

“Yes, but not on the cock. Cock them before you go to bed. It is paying you a great compliment, captain, to lend you these. Were you one of the awkward squad you should not have them.”

“I will take care. You need delay no longer, Mr. Helstone. You may go now.”

He went out at the garden gate with the haste of a man who has timed his own absence to some darker clock than the church’s.

“He is gracious to me to lend me his pistols,” Shirley remarked, watching him go. Then, turning, as if to lock away thought by opening another cupboard: “But come, Lina, let us go in and have some supper. I was too much vexed at tea with the vicinage of Mr. Sam Wynne to be able to eat, and now I am really hungry.”

Within, the dining-room lay dim and warm, as though it had kept the breath of the day. The windows were open, and the evening air stole in with the garden’s perfumes—laburnum and late roses, damp box, the faintly acrid sweetness of crushed leaves. From far down the road came the last thinning fall of footsteps. Under it all—distinct in the stillness, like a thread of sound that did not break—ran the beck in the Hollow, fretting on its stones.

“Shirley,” Caroline said, pausing at the casement, listening as if she were learning a language the daylight drowned, “I hear the beck in the Hollow.”

Then she rang for a candle and asked for bread and milk—Shirley’s common supper, and her own. When Fanny brought the tray, she would have shut the windows and fastened the shutters, but was told to leave them.

The twilight was too calm; its breath too balmy to be excluded yet. They ate in silence: the soft dip of bread, the small clink of china, the faint sweet animal warmth of milk newly brought—comforting and homely, and therefore, in a time like this, strangely precious. Caroline rose once to move a glass of flowers from the sideboard to the window-sill; the room was sultry, and the blossoms exhaled too richly, as if their scent pressed on the lungs.

When she returned, she half opened a drawer and drew out something that caught what little light there was and gave it back coldly—steel, bright and narrow in her hand.

“You assigned this to me, then, Shirley, did you? It is bright, keen-edged, finely tapered; it is dangerous-looking. I never yet felt the impulse which could move me to direct this against a fellow-creature. It is difficult to fancy that circumstances could nerve my arm to strike home with this long knife.”

“I should hate to do it,” Shirley answered; and the words were quiet, but not light now. “But I think I could do it, if goaded by certain exigencies which I can imagine.”

She sipped her milk thoughtfully, a little pale—though, indeed, she was rarely anything else than pale; her bloom was not in colour but in energy.

When they had eaten, Fanny was called again, and she and Eliza were told to go to bed. Both were weary—there had been much cutting of currant-buns, filling of urns and teapots, carrying of trays; domestic labour, like kneading, had its own rhythm, and it leaves the arms aching with honest fatigue. Presently the maids’ chamber door shut.

Caroline took a candle and went quietly through the house, fastening windows, barring doors. She did not avoid the haunted back kitchen nor the vault-like cellars; she traversed them as if fear were a thing to be checked off like a latch.

When she returned she said, trying to smile and failing: “There is neither spirit nor flesh in the house at present which should not be there. It is now near eleven o’clock, fully bedtime; yet I would rather sit up a little longer, if you do not object, Shirley. Here—I have brought the brace of pistols from my uncle’s study. You may examine them at your leisure.”

She laid the pistols on the table as one lays down a fact.

“Why would you rather sit up longer?” Shirley asked, taking them up, looking them over with a composed hand, and laying them down again.

“Because I have a strange, excited feeling in my heart.”

“So have I.”

“Is this state of sleeplessness and restlessness caused by something electrical in the air, I wonder?”

“No; the sky is clear, the stars numberless. It is a fine night.”

“But very still. I hear the water fret over its stony bed in Hollow’s Copse as distinctly as if it ran below the churchyard wall.”

“I am glad it is so still a night. A moaning wind or rushing rain would vex me to fever just now.”

“Why, Shirley?”

“Because it would baffle my efforts to listen.”

“Do you listen towards the Hollow?”

“Yes; it is the only quarter whence we can hear a sound just now.”

“The only one, Shirley.”

They sat near the open lattice, both leaning their arms on the sill, both inclining their heads as though the ear were a kind of mouth and silence could be tasted. They could see each other’s young faces by starlight and that dim June remainder which does not wholly leave the west till the east begins to promise morning.

“Mr. Helstone thinks we have no idea which way he is gone,” Shirley murmured, “nor on what errand, nor with what expectations, nor how prepared. But I guess much; do not you?”

“I guess something.”

“All those gentlemen—your cousin Moore included—think that you and I are now asleep in our beds, unconscious.”

“Caring nothing about them—hoping and fearing nothing for them,” Caroline added.

They said no more for a long half-hour. The night said little too; only the church clock measured it, dividing time into quarters as a careful cook divides dough—without passion, without pity. Once they drew their scarves closer and put on their bonnets again, because the air cooled and the dew thickened, and their vigil had the stillness of people who listen not for pleasure but for meaning.

Towards midnight the house-dog began that teasing, monotonous bark which wears on the nerves like a dripping tap. Caroline rose and went noiselessly through dark passages to the kitchen, carrying herself as if every board had ears. She appeased him with a piece of bread and returned.

The dining-room was now dark; Shirley had put out the candle. Her outline showed near the open window, leaning out. Caroline asked no question; she went to her side. The dog recommenced barking, then stopped abruptly, as if some nearer authority had silenced him. The two at the window listened—and now it was not merely the beck in the Hollow.

A muffled sound came up the road below the churchyard: measured, beating, approaching. A dull tramp of marching feet.

It drew nearer, and the extent of it grew on the ear. It was not two men, nor a dozen, nor a score. It was the tread of hundreds. The garden shrubs made a leafy screen, so that sight was denied them; but hearing, sharpened by stillness, brought everything close. The troop seemed passing the rectory, and then a human voice, speaking one word only, broke the hush:

“Halt!”

The march stopped. A low conference followed, indistinct, yet charged with intent.

“We must hear this,” Shirley said.

She turned, took the pistols from the table, and passed out through the middle window—indeed a glass door—into the garden. She stole down the walk and stood listening beneath the lilacs. Caroline, who would not have quitted the house alone, followed because Shirley went; she glanced at the long knife on the sideboard, left it, and soon stood at Shirley’s shoulder. They did not dare look over the wall; they crouched behind it and heard words that seemed to come from mouths close to the bricks, rough and low, with that dialect that carries a whole parish’s history in its consonants.

“It looks a rambling old building. Who lives in it besides the damned parson?”

“Only three women—his niece and two servants.”

“Do you know where they sleep?”

“The lasses behind; the niece in a front room.”

“And Helstone?”

“Yonder is his chamber. He was burning a light, but I see none now.”

“Where would you get in?”

“If I were ordered to do his job—and he desarves it—I’d try yond’ long window; it opens to the dining-room. I could grope my way upstairs, and I know his chamber.”

“How would you manage about the women folk?”

“Let ’em alone except they shrieked, and then I’d soon quieten ’em. I could wish to find the old chap asleep. If he waked, he’d be dangerous.”

“Has he arms?”

“Firearms, allus—and allus loadened.”

“Then you’re a fool to stop us here. A shot would give the alarm. Moore would be on us before we could turn round. We should miss our main object.”

“You might go on, I tell you. I’d engage Helstone alone.”

There was a pause. Someone dropped a weapon; it rang sharply on stone. At that ring the rectory dog burst into furious barking again, as though the sound had struck his instinct like a hand on a drum.

“That spoils all!” said the voice. “He’ll awake. A noise like that might rouse the dead. You did not say there was a dog. Damn you! Forward!”

Forward they went—tramp, tramp—slow-filing, manifold. The night swallowed them.

Shirley rose, looked over the wall along the road.

“Not a soul remains,” she said. After a moment: “Thank God!”

Caroline repeated it, but her voice shook. Her heart beat thickly; her face was cold, her forehead damp with a sweat that felt like dew fallen inward.

“Thank God for us!” she whispered. “But what will happen elsewhere? They have passed us by that they may make sure of others.”

“They have done well,” Shirley returned with composure that had iron under it. “The others will defend themselves. They can do it. They are prepared for them. With us it is otherwise. My finger was on the trigger of this pistol. I was quite ready to give that man, if he had entered, such a greeting as he little calculated on; but behind him followed three hundred. I had neither three hundred hands nor three hundred weapons. I could not have effectually protected either you, myself, or the two poor women asleep under that roof. Therefore I again earnestly thank God for insult and peril escaped.”

She stood a second, thinking as quickly as she had acted.

“What is it my duty and wisdom to do next? Not to stay here inactive, I am glad to say, but, of course, to walk over to the Hollow.”

“To the Hollow, Shirley?”

“To the Hollow. Will you go with me?”

“Where those men are gone?”

“They have taken the highway; we should not encounter them. The road over the fields is as safe, silent, and solitary as a path through the air would be. Will you go?”

“Yes,” said Caroline mechanically—not from readiness, but because she could not bear to let Shirley go alone.

“Then we must fasten up these windows, and leave all as secure as we can behind us. Do you know what we are going for, Cary?”

“Yes—no—because you wish it.”

“Is that all? And are you so obedient to a mere caprice of mine? What a docile wife you would make to a stern husband! The moon’s face is not whiter than yours at this moment, and the aspen at the gate does not tremble more than your busy fingers; and so, tractable and terror-struck, and dismayed and devoted, you would follow me into the thick of real danger! Cary, let me give your fidelity a motive. We are going for Moore’s sake—to see if we can be of use to him, to make an effort to warn him of what is coming.”

“To be sure!” Caroline cried, shame and love meeting in her voice. “I am a blind, weak fool, and you are acute and sensible, Shirley. I will go with you; I will gladly go with you!”

“I do not doubt it. You would die blindly and meekly for me, but you would intelligently and gladly die for Moore. But, in truth, there is no question of death to-night; we run no risk at all.”

Caroline barred and shuttered the dining-room windows with hands that, for all their trembling, were quick and capable. Then they were out, running, taking the fields as if the earth belonged to them in that hour. Walls checked them but did not baffle them. Shirley was surefooted, springing like a deer when she chose. Caroline, more timid and less practised, fell once or twice and bruised herself; she rose instantly each time, insisting she was not hurt, as if pain were a luxury she could not afford.

A quickset hedge bounded the last field. They lost time hunting a gap; when found, it was narrow, and they worked through it, snagging hair and tearing tender skin and catching silks and muslins on thorns. What they regretted most was not the damage but the delay; every impediment felt like a hand thrust between them and Robert Moore.

Beyond the hedge the beck met them, deeper than it sounded, its bed rough with stones. A narrow plank was the only bridge. Shirley had crossed it often without thought; Caroline had never yet dared.

“I will carry you across,” Shirley said. “You are light, and I am not weak. Let me try.”

“If I fall in, you may fish me out,” Caroline answered, and squeezed her hand. Then, without pausing to bargain with fear, she stepped onto the trembling plank as if it were firm turf. Shirley followed, no more resolute than Caroline, no safer. Their blood was too hot for caution; on this errand, fire and water were not masters.

But a sound stopped them. Hardly had they reached the opposite bank when a shot split the air from the north. A second later another burst from the south. Within three minutes, like harsh birds calling across the valley, similar signals boomed from east and west.

“I thought we were dead at the first explosion,” Shirley said, drawing a long breath. “I felt myself hit in the temples, and I concluded your heart was pierced; but the reiterated voice is an explanation. Those are signals—it is their way—the attack must be near. We should have had wings. Our feet have not borne us swiftly enough.”

They cleared a portion of copse, branches whispering overhead with the small nocturnal rustle that belongs to leaves, to nesting birds, to insects—yet beneath those natural sounds lay another layer now: the human multitude moving like a tide. When they emerged, the mill lay below them. They could see the buildings and yard, and the road beyond.

The first glance told Shirley she had guessed rightly. They were too late to warn.

The road, which should have shown pale, was dark with a moving mass. The rioters were gathered in front of the closed yard gates, and within stood a single figure, apparently addressing them. The mill itself was black and still; no light burned, no movement showed at lattice or door.

“Surely he is prepared,” Shirley whispered. “Surely that is not Moore meeting them alone?”

“It is,” Caroline breathed, and her whole body leaned forward as if longing could make a path. “We must go to him. I will go to him.”

“That you will not.”

“Why did I come, then? I came only for him. I shall join him.”

“Fortunately it is out of your power. There is no entrance to the yard.”

“There is a small entrance at the back besides the gates in front. It opens by a secret method which I know. I will try it.”

“Not with my leave.”

Shirley clasped her round the waist with both arms and held her back with an authority that was tenderness armed. “Not one step shall you stir. At this moment Moore would be both shocked and embarrassed if he saw either you or me. Men never want women near them in time of real danger.”

“I would not trouble—I would help him.”

“How?—by inspiring him with heroism? Pooh! These are not the days of chivalry. It is not a tilt at a tournament we are going to behold, but a struggle about money, and food, and life.”

“It is natural that I should be at his side.”

“As queen of his heart? His mill is his lady-love, Cary! Backed by his factory and his frames, he has all the encouragement he wants or can know. It is not for love or beauty, but for ledger and broadcloth, he is going to break a spear. Don’t be sentimental; Robert is not so.”

“I could help him; I will seek him.”

“Off then—I let you go—seek Moore. You’ll not find him.”

She loosened her hold. Caroline darted forward; behind her rang Shirley’s short, gibing laugh, with a warning flung after it: “Look well there is no mistake!”

But there was a mistake. Caroline stopped, stared; the figure within the gate had suddenly retreated and was running back towards the mill.

“Make haste, Lina!” Shirley called. “Meet him before he enters!”

Caroline returned slowly, her face changing as certainty replaced hope. “It is not Robert,” she said. “It has neither his height, form, nor bearing.”

“I saw it was not Robert when I let you go,” Shirley replied, and there was relief now in her tone, almost mirth at her own quick deception. “How could you imagine it? It is a shabby little figure of a private soldier; they had posted him as sentinel. He is safe in the mill now. I saw the door open and admit him. My mind grows easier. Robert is prepared. Our warning would have been superfluous; and now I am thankful we came too late to give it. It has saved us the trouble of a scene. How fine to have entered the counting-house toute éperdue, and to have found oneself in presence of Messrs. Armitage and Ramsden smoking, Malone swaggering, your uncle sneering, Mr. Sykes sipping a cordial, and Moore himself in his cold man-of-business vein! I am glad we missed it all.”

“I wonder if there are many in the mill, Shirley!”

“Plenty to defend it. The soldiers we have twice seen to-day were going there, no doubt, and the group we noticed surrounding your cousin in the fields will be with him.”

“What are they doing now, Shirley? What is that noise?”

“Hatchets and crowbars against the yard gates. They are forcing them. Are you afraid?”

“No; but my heart throbs fast. I have a difficulty in standing. I will sit down. Do you feel unmoved?”

“Hardly that; but I am glad I came. We shall see what transpires with our own eyes. We are here on the spot, and none know it. Instead of amazing the curate, the clothier, and the corn-dealer with a romantic rush on the stage, we stand alone with the friendly night—its mute stars—and these whispering trees, whose report our friends will not come to gather.”

“Shirley, Shirley, the gates are down!” Caroline cried. “That crash was like the felling of great trees. Now they are pouring through. They will break down the mill doors as they have broken the gate. What can Robert do against so many? Would to God I were a little nearer him—could hear him speak—could speak to him! With my will—my longing to serve him—I could not be a useless burden in his way; I could be turned to some account.”

“They come on!” Shirley said, and her voice sank, as if instinctively she would not waste sound. “How steadily they march in! There is discipline in their ranks. I will not say there is courage—hundreds against tens are no proof of that quality—but” (she spoke lower still) “there is suffering and desperation enough amongst them. These goads will urge them forwards.”

“Forwards against Robert; and they hate him. Shirley, is there much danger they will win the day?”

“We shall see. Moore and Helstone are of earth’s first blood—no bunglers—no cravens—”

A crash—smash—shiver—cut her off. A volley of stones, hurled together, struck the broad front of the mill. All its windows answered at once, not with light but with ruin: every pane in every lattice shattered and fell, pounded into fragments that glittered for an instant and then were gone.

A yell followed—a rioters’ yell: harsh, long, packed with hunger, anger, and the thick accent of the West-Riding clothing district. It poured into the Hollow and filled it, drowning the beck’s fretting voice as surely as a flood would drown a shallow stream.

Caroline rose; Shirley put her arm round her, and they stood together as still as the straight stems of two trees. When the yell ended, the night was not quiet; it was full of the swaying murmur of a crowd, like wind in a heavy crop.

“What next?” they asked each other without words. The mill remained mute as a tomb.

“He cannot be alone,” Caroline whispered.

“I would stake all I have that he is as little alone as he is alarmed,” Shirley answered.

Shots were discharged by the rioters; small flashes, sharp reports. Had the defenders waited for that signal? It seemed so. The hitherto inert mill woke: fire leaped from its empty window-frames; a volley of musketry pealed through the Hollow.

“Moore speaks at last!” said Shirley. “And he seems to have the gift of tongues. That was not a single voice.”

The two girls met no living creature on their homeward way. The lanes, so lately loud with footfall, shout, and musket-crack, now lay washed and vacant beneath a paling sky. They let themselves into the rectory as if they were thieves of quiet; they went upstairs without a board’s complaint; the dawn—thin, grey, and honest—gave light enough for such stealth.

Shirley, once in the chamber set apart for her, asked no questions of its strangeness. She had never slept there before; the air was not her own, nor the curtains, nor the chair where someone else’s gown had been folded; yet her nature, strong in health, took the night’s terror as a gust that bends and passes. She threw herself upon the couch, laid down her bright head, and in the instant after—almost before Caroline had smoothed the cover—sleep took her, deep and wholesome, like a cool drink swallowed in one draught.

Caroline sat beside her and watched. In Shirley’s repose there was no twitch of fear, no shadow of after-thought—only that serene, childlike beauty which comes when a heart, though quick to kindle, is not made to burn itself out. To Caroline, that calm face was like a lamp seen through clear glass: it told of a mind which, however shaken, would spring back to its poise.

For herself—made of finer, more tremulous fibre—there was no rest. A mere tea-drinking, a school-gathering, any little ripple of social movement, would have kept her wakeful; what she had seen and heard at the Hollow clung to her senses as the smell of gunpowder clings to a cloak. She could not lie still: the pillow pricked like a thorn. At last she sat upright by Shirley’s side, counting slow minutes, and listening.

Listening—yes, that was the tyranny. The rectory, as day broke, began to give up its usual sounds one by one: a shutter opened below with a wooden sigh; a pail clinked; some early bird in the garden tried its first notes, timidly competing with the far-off roll of a cart. Yet beneath these homely murmurs Caroline’s ear still held the remembered night: the sudden roar, the scattered shots, the rush and cry that had torn the darkness. The mind, when it cannot feed on hope, gnaws itself; it turns even prayer into a kind of hunger. She framed petitions, earnest and plain, such as she had been taught; and, because relief did not come at once, she felt as if Heaven were silent—felt, at moments, that dreadful chill of being forsaken, as if the very language of comfort had lost its meaning and the words she spoke were not understood.

When the house at last stirred fully—when the day’s business began to call—she rose with that faint revival which action gives even to the most weary. She dressed with care; she tried, by neat hair and orderly gown, to conceal the inward forlornness. When they were ready, she looked fresh enough—only her eyes were languid, where Shirley’s were bright.

“To-day I shall have much to say to Moore,” Shirley declared, standing before the glass, winding her naturally waved hair round her fingers into curls. Her voice had the ring of a person for whom life is made of interest. “He will have to undergo cross-examination. He thinks he has outwitted me—men always do. Concealing danger, as if we were children to be frightened or amused by turns. They imagined we little knew where they were to-night. We know they little guessed where we were.”

She paused, then resumed the theme with quick warmth, while Caroline fastened her dress and clasped her girdle.

“If men could see us as we really are, they would be amazed. The cleverest of them live under an illusion. Their ‘good woman’ is half doll, half angel; their ‘bad woman’ is a fiend. And then they fall into raptures over each other’s inventions—those heroines in poem, novel, drama—worship them as if they were nature. Fine it may be; divine it may be; but often it is as false as the rose in my best bonnet. If I spoke my mind of some first-rate female characters in first-rate works, I should be stoned under a cairn in half an hour.”

“Shirley, you chatter so I cannot fasten you. Be still,” Caroline said, trying for mildness. “Besides, authors’ heroines are almost as good as authoresses’ heroes.”

“Not at all. Women read men more truly than men read women. I’ll prove it in a magazine paper some day—if I have time. Only it will be ‘declined with thanks,’ and left at the publisher’s for me.”

“To be sure. You could not write cleverly enough. You don’t know enough. You are not learned.”

“God knows I can’t contradict you, Cary; I’m as ignorant as a stone. There’s one comfort—you are not much better.”

So they went down to breakfast.

“I wonder how Mrs. Pryor and Hortense Moore have passed the night,” Caroline said, measuring coffee into the pot and letting the boiling water fall with its dark, comforting hiss—one of the few sounds that felt kindly after the harsh noise of the Hollow. The smell rose—bitter, warm, steadying. “Selfish being that I am, I never thought of either till now. They must have heard all the tumult. Hortense is timid in such matters—so is Mrs. Pryor.”

“Take my word for it, Lina, Moore got his sister out of the way,” Shirley answered. “She went home with Miss Mann; he has quartered her there. As to Mrs. Pryor—yes, I’m uneasy about her; but in another half-hour we will be with her.”

By then the neighbourhood’s tongue had wakened as fully as the sun. Fanny, sent to Fieldhead for milk, came back panting, her cap-strings flying, with tidings—confident, contradictory, terrible. There had been a battle at Mr. Moore’s mill; some said twenty men were killed. While Fanny was gone, Eliza had been told by the butcher’s boy the mill was burnt to the ground. Each brought her tale as if she had carried it in both hands. Both ended by being “sure master must have been in it all”—master and Mr. Thomas the clerk; and Mr. Malone had not been heard of since yesterday afternoon; and Joe Scott’s wife was in a fine taking, not knowing what had become of her husband.

Scarce had the women finished when there came a knock at the kitchen door; the Fieldhead errand-boy stood there, hot and breathless, holding a billet from Mrs. Pryor. It was hurried, anxious. Miss Keeldar was urged to return directly: the house and neighbourhood were in confusion, and orders must be given which only the mistress could regulate. Then, in a postscript, Mrs. Pryor begged that Miss Helstone might not be left alone at the rectory; she had better accompany Miss Keeldar.

“There are not two opinions on that head,” Shirley said, tying on her bonnet and running to fetch Caroline’s.

“But what will Fanny and Eliza do? And if my uncle returns?”

“Your uncle will not return yet; he has other fish to fry. He will be galloping between Briarfield and Stilbro’ all day, rousing magistrates and officers. Fanny and Eliza can have Joe Scott’s wife and the clerk’s wife in with them. Besides—there’s no real danger now. Weeks will pass before those men can rally again; and I am much mistaken if Moore and your uncle do not take advantage of last night to quell them altogether. They will frighten the authorities into energetic measures. I only hope they won’t be too severe—won’t pursue the discomfited too relentlessly.”

“Robert will not be cruel. We saw that last night,” Caroline said.

“But he will be hard,” Shirley returned; “and so will your uncle.”

They hurried by meadow and plantation path to Fieldhead. From a rise they could see the highway already alive—equestrians and pedestrians flowing, like a drawn tide, towards the usually solitary Hollow. The very air seemed full of hurried feet and eager voices; Caroline, with her strained ear, felt the day’s soundscape thickening—human noise laying claim to the fields, like smoke after fire.

At the hall they found the backyard gates open. The court and kitchen were crowded with excited milk-fetchers—men, women, children—cans at hip, buckets in hand, talking as if talk could feed them. Mrs. Gill, the housekeeper, moved among them trying, and failing, to persuade them away. (For it was then the custom on such an estate that the cottagers received their milk and butter from the manor dairy; Miss Keeldar kept a herd for that very convenience—deep-dewlapped Craven cows, sleek as if polished, fed on the sweet herbage and clear waters of Airedale; and she was proud of them, proud of the steady lowing from the byre at milking-time, proud of the clean, foamy pails and the cool, animal warmth that belonged to morning work.)

Shirley, seeing that a clearance must be made, stepped in among the gossiping groups. She bade them good-morning with frank ease—the manner she wore with working-people, cooler with equals, proudest with those above her. She asked if they had all got their milk measured out; learning they had, she demanded what they were waiting for.

“We’re just talking a bit over this battle there’s been at your mill, mistress,” a man answered.

“Talking a bit! Just like you!” Shirley said. “It is a queer thing—how all the world is so fond of talking over events. You talk if anybody dies sudden; you talk if a fire breaks out; you talk if a mill-owner fails; you talk if he’s murdered. What good does your talking do?”

They liked it—the plain, downright rating. Flattery they would have sniffed at; honest chiding they received as if it were hearty bread. Smiles broke out.

“We’re no war nor some ‘at is aboon us, are we?” asked one.

“Nor a whit better. You that should be models of industry are as gossip-loving as the idle. Fine rich folk that have nothing to do may be partly excused for wasting time; you who earn your bread with the sweat of your brow are inexcusable.”

“That’s queer, mistress. Suld we never have a holiday because we work hard?”

“Never,” she returned promptly; then softened it with a smile that half belied her severity—“unless you knew how to use it better than getting together over rum and tea if you are women, or beer and pipes if you are men, and talking scandal at your neighbours’ expense. Come, friends—oblige me: take your cans and go home. I expect several persons to call to-day, and it will be inconvenient to have the avenues crowded.”

They yielded, as Yorkshire folk will, to persuasion where they resist compulsion. In five minutes the yard was clear.

“Thank you, and good-bye to you, friends,” Shirley said, closing the gates on a quiet court.

Entering by the back way, the young ladies passed through the kitchen—“the house,” as the inner kitchen is called—towards the hall. Mrs. Pryor came running down the oak staircase to meet them. She was unnerved. Her sanguine colour had drained; her usually placid, timid blue eye wandered with a frightened uncertainty. Yet she did not break into wild exclamations. What pressed heaviest on her was a conscientious dissatisfaction with herself—that she could not be firmer, cooler, more equal to what was demanded.

“You are aware,” she began, her voice trembling, yet her anxiety being to exaggerate nothing, “that a body of rioters attacked Mr. Moore’s mill to-night. We heard the firing and confusion plainly here; none of us slept. It was a sad night. The house has been in bustle all the morning—people coming and going. The servants applied to me for orders and directions, which I did not feel warranted in giving. Mr. Moore has, I believe, sent up for refreshments for the soldiers and others engaged in the defence, and for conveniences for the wounded. I could not undertake the responsibility. I fear delay may have been injurious; but this is not my house. You were absent, my dear Miss Keeldar. What could I do?”

“Were no refreshments sent?” Shirley asked; and her countenance, so clear and tranquil a moment before, darkened, as if a cloud passed over a summer field.

“I think not, my dear.”

“And nothing for the wounded—no linen, no wine, no bedding?”

“I think not. I cannot tell what Mrs. Gill did; but it seemed impossible to me, at the moment, to venture to dispose of your property by sending supplies to soldiers. Provisions for a company of soldiers sounds formidable. How many there are I did not ask; but I could not think of allowing them to pillage the house, as it were. I meant to do right; yet I did not see the case clearly.”

“It lies in a nutshell, notwithstanding,” Shirley said. “These soldiers have risked their lives in defence of my property: they have a right to my gratitude. The wounded are our fellow-creatures: we should aid them. Mrs. Gill!”

The call rang through the thick oak of hall and kitchen doors like a bell that permits no delay. Mrs. Gill came from bread-making with hands and apron in culinary case, dough clinging, flour whitening her; she had not dared stop to wipe them. Her mistress had called so but once before—when Tartar, Shirley’s old dog, had been set upon by two carriers’ curs and their owners stood by, egging them on. Then Shirley had gone out bonnetless and, scorning the men more than the beasts, had put her hands about the thick neck of the larger brute and tried to choke it off, while Tartar’s eye bled and the fangs held fast. Five or six men had come at last; she thanked none. “They might have come before, if their will had been good,” she said. The rest of that day she had sat by the hall fire tending the gory, stiff, swollen warrior at her feet, murmuring soft words whose music he knew, licking her hand and then his own red wounds. John had borne a cold shoulder for a week.

Remembering this, Mrs. Gill came “all of a tremble.” Shirley’s questions and orders were firm, brief. That Fieldhead should have shown the inhospitality of a miser’s hovel—at such a time—stung her pride keenly.

“How long is it since the message came from the mill?”

“Not an hour yet, ma’am,” Mrs. Gill answered, soothing.

“Not an hour! You might almost as well say not a day. They will have applied elsewhere by this time. Send a man instantly down to tell them everything this house contains is at Mr. Moore’s, Mr. Helstone’s, and the soldiers’ service. Do that first.”

While the order was executed, Shirley moved away and stood at the hall-window, silent, unapproachable. When Mrs. Gill returned, Shirley turned; a purple flush glowed on her pale cheek; displeasure lit her dark eye.

“Let the contents of the larder and wine-cellar be brought up, put into the hay-carts, and driven down to the Hollow. If there is not much bread or meat, go to butcher and baker and desire them to send what they have. But I will see for myself.”

She went off.

“All will be right soon; she will get over it in an hour,” Caroline whispered to Mrs. Pryor. “Go upstairs, dear madam, and try to be calm and easy. The truth is, Shirley will blame herself more than you before the day is done.”

With gentle assurances and persuasion Caroline soothed the agitated lady, led her to her room, and promised to return when things were settled. Then she went down again “to see if she could be useful.”

She proved she could. Fieldhead’s servants were not many; and Shirley, in her unwonted mood, found occupation for every hand—and for her own. The kitchen became a place of haste and smell: crusts lifted from loaves, brown and crackling; ham cut with a firm knife that clicked against bone; cheese unwrapped and giving out its sharp, homely scent; bottles drawn from the cool dark of the cellar, their corks damp with age. Baskets were brought and filled; cloths and linen shaken out; the air grew warm with baking and human breath. Caroline’s deftness and quiet good-nature steadied the maids, who were half scared by their lady’s sharpness; it helped Mrs. Gill; it even appeased Shirley. A chance glance from Caroline, a small smile—no more—drew from Shirley an answering smile, like sun through cloud.

Caroline was carrying a heavy basket up the cellar-stairs when Shirley caught sight of her.

“This is a shame!” Shirley cried, running to her. “It will strain your arm.”

She took the basket and herself bore it out into the yard. When she came back, the storm had passed: the flash in her eye was melted; the shade on her forehead gone. She resumed her usual cordial cheer, and there was a touch of softened shame for the anger that had been unjust.

She was still superintending the lading of the cart when a gentleman entered the yard and approached her before she knew he was there.

“I hope I see Miss Keeldar well this morning?” he said, examining with significant scrutiny her still flushed face.

Shirley gave him a look and bent again to her employment without answer. A pleasant smile played about her lips, but she hid it. He repeated the salutation, stooping so it might reach her ear more readily.

“Well enough—if she be good enough,” she replied at last. “And so is Mr. Moore too, I dare say. To speak truth, I am not anxious about him; some slight mischance would be only his just due. His conduct has been—we will say strange, till we have time to characterise it by a more exact epithet. Meantime, may I ask what brings him here?”

“Mr. Helstone and I have just received your message that everything at Fieldhead was at our service,” Moore answered. “We judged, by the unlimited wording of the gracious intimation, that you would be giving yourself too much trouble. I perceive our conjecture was correct. We are not a regiment, remember—only about half a dozen soldiers and as many civilians. Allow me to retrench something from these too abundant supplies.”

Shirley blushed and laughed at her own over-eager generosity, her absurd calculations—how the word “soldiers” had filled her mind with ranks and companies, mouths without number. Moore laughed too, quietly; and as quietly he ordered basket after basket to be taken from the cart and returned, vessel after vessel remanded to the cellar.

“The rector must hear of this,” he said. “He will make a good story of it. What an excellent army contractor Miss Keeldar would have been!” And again, softly: “It is precisely as I conjectured.”

“You ought to be thankful, not mock me,” Shirley said. “What could I do? How could I gauge your appetites or number? For aught I knew there might have been fifty of you to victual. You told me nothing; and an application to provision soldiers naturally suggests large ideas.”

“It appears so,” Moore remarked, levelling another keen, quiet glance at her. Then, to the carter: “Now I think you may take what remains to the Hollow. Your load will be somewhat lighter than the one Miss Keeldar destined you to carry.”

As the vehicle rumbled out, Shirley, rallying herself, demanded what had become of the wounded.

“There was not a single man hurt on our side,” Moore answered.

“You were hurt yourself, on the temples,” interposed a quick, low voice—Caroline’s. She had stood within the shade of the doorway, behind the large person of Mrs. Gill, and so had escaped notice until she spoke; then Moore’s eye searched the obscurity where she withdrew.

“Are you much hurt?” she asked.

“As you might scratch your finger with a needle in sewing.”

“Lift your hair and let us see.”

He took off his hat and obeyed, showing only a narrow slip of court-plaster. Caroline, with a slight movement of the head, signified she was satisfied and withdrew into the dim interior where the scent of bread and cellar-cool wine mingled.

“How did she know I was hurt?” Moore asked.

“By rumour, no doubt. But it is too good in her to trouble herself about you,” Shirley said. “For my part, it was of your victims I was thinking when I inquired after the wounded. What damage have your opponents sustained?”

“One of the rioters—or victims, as you call them—was killed, and six were hurt.”

“What have you done with them?” Shirley demanded.

While Shirley spoke below with Moore, Caroline went back to the upper rooms and found Mrs. Pryor seated where the light was kindest, as if she had chosen the gentlest corner for a spirit that would not be comforted. The older lady’s face held itself composed, but the composure was only a smooth lid over pain. She would not call Shirley’s hastiness a wound; yet it galled, and Caroline—who had learned to read her—as surely felt it as if she had seen the blood.

To any less congruous nature, Mrs. Pryor’s stillness might have seemed indifference to consolation; for Caroline moved about quietly, set a shawl closer on her shoulders, straightened a ribbon, lifted a glass and persuaded her to sip, and met no visible response. But Caroline knew that those slight offices—like warm broth carried to a sick-room and taken in silence—were received, valued, and did their healing work.

At length Mrs. Pryor spoke, not with complaint, but with that melancholy accuracy which belongs to timid consciences.

“I am deficient in self-confidence and decision,” she said. “I always have been deficient in those qualities. Yet I think Miss Keeldar should have known my character well enough by this time to be aware that I always feel an even painful solicitude to do right, to act for the best. The unusual nature of the demand on my judgment puzzled me, especially following the alarms of the night. I could not venture to act promptly for another; but I trust no serious harm will result from my lapse of firmness.”

A gentle knock touched the door—timid at first, then more urgent. It opened half-way.

“Caroline, come here,” said a low voice.

Caroline stepped into the gallery. Shirley stood there, her proud vivacity sobered into something like a child’s shame. The very air around her seemed to have lost its swaggering music.

“How is Mrs. Pryor?” she asked.

“Rather out of spirits.”

“I have behaved very shamefully—very ungenerously—very ungratefully to her,” Shirley said, the words crowding as if they could not get out fast enough. “How insolent in me to turn on her thus for what, after all, was no fault—only an excess of conscientiousness on her part. But I regret my error most sincerely. Tell her so, and ask if she will forgive me.”

Caroline carried the message back with a warmth that made it a pleasure. Mrs. Pryor rose at once; she had the look of one who dreads a scene as she dreads thunder, yet will not refuse shelter.

“Come in, my dear,” she said falteringly.

Shirley entered, not creeping, but with a rush of penitent energy. She threw her arms round her governess and kissed her with hearty impulsiveness.

“You know you must forgive me, Mrs. Pryor. I could not get on at all if there was a misunderstanding between you and me.”

“I have nothing to forgive,” Mrs. Pryor answered, and the very gentleness of her tone showed how much she wished to end it. “We will pass it over now, if you please. The final result of the incident is that it proves more plainly than ever how unequal I am to certain crises.”

That was the sting which would remain: not Shirley’s offence, which she could pardon, but her own helplessness, which she could not. No caress, no dutiful word from Shirley, no tender watching from Caroline, could coax that thought out of its hidden place.

Before long, Shirley—doomed, as people of consequence are, to be claimed like a shared dish at a crowded table—was called downstairs again. Morning visitors succeeded each other as regularly as courses.

The rector came first. His greeting was lively, his reprimand livelier; and he took both as his due. He was so brimful of last night’s tumult that he forgot, in the heat of talk, to inquire after his niece. Riot and rioters, the mill, the magistrates, the heiress, the parish, the law—these were his family for the moment.

He spoke of the part he and his curate had played in the defence of the Hollow.

“The vials of pharisaical wrath will be emptied on our heads for our share in this business,” he said; “but I defy every calumniator. I was there only to support the law, to play my part as a man and a Briton; which characters I deem quite compatible with those of the priest and Levite, in their highest sense. Your tenant Moore has won my approbation. A cooler commander I would not wish to see, nor a more determined. Besides, the man has shown sound judgment and good sense—first, in being thoroughly prepared for the event which has taken place; and subsequently, when his well-concerted plans had secured him success, in knowing how to use without abusing his victory. Some of the magistrates are now well frightened, and, like all cowards, show a tendency to be cruel. Moore restrains them with admirable prudence. He has hitherto been very unpopular in the neighbourhood; but, mark my words, the tide of opinion will now take a turn in his favour. People will find out that they have not appreciated him, and will hasten to remedy their error; and he, when he perceives the public disposed to acknowledge his merits, will show a more gracious mien than that with which he has hitherto favoured us.”

He was gathering himself to deliver—half in jest and half in warning—some allusion to Shirley’s rumored partiality for her talented tenant, when the bell rang below with another summons. The new visitor appeared in the doorway in the shape of a white-haired old gentleman, with a truculent countenance and a disdainful eye—Mr. Yorke, the rector’s old enemy.

At once, priest and Levite seized hat and stick and made the shortest adieu to Miss Keeldar, the sternest nod to her guest, and were gone as if driven out by a gust.

Mr. Yorke was in no mild mood. His opinion of the night’s transaction came in rough gusts—each gust loaded with Yorkshire Doric, as pungent as mustard ground fresh under a pestle. Moore, the magistrates, the soldiers, the mob leaders—none were spared; but his most racy epithets were saved for the “fighting parsons,” the “sanguinary, demoniac” rector and curate. According to him, ecclesiastical guilt had come to the brim.

“The church,” he said, “was in a bonny pickle now. It was time it came down when parsons took to swaggering amang soldiers, blazing away wi’ bullet and gunpowder, taking the lives of far honester men than themselves.”

“What would Moore have done if nobody had helped him?” Shirley asked.

“Drunk as he’d brewed, eaten as he’d baked.”

“Which means you would have left him by himself to face that mob. Good! He has plenty of courage, but the greatest amount of gallantry that ever garrisoned one human breast could scarce avail against two hundred.”

“He had the soldiers, those poor slaves who hire out their own blood and spill other folk’s for money.”

“You abuse soldiers almost as much as you abuse clergymen. All who wear red coats are national refuse in your eyes, and all who wear black are national swindlers. Mr. Moore, according to you, did wrong to get military aid, and he did still worse to accept of any other aid. Your way of talking amounts to this: he should have abandoned his mill and his life to the rage of a set of misguided madmen, and Mr. Helstone and every other gentleman in the parish should have looked on, and seen the building razed and its owner slaughtered, and never stirred a finger to save either.”

“If Moore had behaved to his men from the beginning as a master ought to behave, they never would have entertained their present feelings towards him.”

“Easy for you to talk,” Shirley broke out, the blood rising as quickly as flame in dry straw—“you, whose family have lived at Briarmains for six generations, to whose person the people have been accustomed for fifty years, who know all their ways, prejudices, and preferences—easy, indeed, for you to act so as to avoid offending them. But Mr. Moore came a stranger into the district; he came here poor and friendless, with nothing but his own energies to back him, nothing but his honour, his talent, and his industry to make his way for him. A monstrous crime indeed that, under such circumstances, he could not popularize his naturally grave, quiet manners all at once; could not be jocular, and free, and cordial with a strange peasantry, as you are with your fellow-townsmen! An unpardonable transgression that when he introduced improvements he did not go about the business in quite the most politic way, did not graduate his changes as delicately as a rich capitalist might have done! For errors of this sort is he to be the victim of mob outrage? Is he to be denied even the privilege of defending himself? Are those who have the hearts of men in their breasts (and Mr. Helstone, say what you will of him, has such a heart) to be reviled like malefactors because they stand by him, because they venture to espouse the cause of one against two hundred?”

“Come, come now, be cool,” said Mr. Yorke, and even his smile had a grain of satisfaction in it; her earnestness amused him.

“Cool! Must I listen coolly to downright nonsense—to dangerous nonsense? No. I like you very well, Mr. Yorke, as you know, but I thoroughly dislike some of your principles. All that cant—excuse me, but I repeat the word—all that cant about soldiers and parsons is most offensive in my ears. All ridiculous, irrational crying up of one class, whether the same be aristocrat or democrat—all howling down of another class, whether clerical or military—all exacting injustice to individuals, whether monarch or mendicant—is really sickening to me; all arraying of ranks against ranks, all party hatreds, all tyrannies disguised as liberties, I reject and wash my hands of. You think you are a philanthropist; you think you are an advocate of liberty; but I will tell you this—Mr. Hall, the parson of Nunnely, is a better friend both of man and freedom than Hiram Yorke, the reformer of Briarfield.”

From a man, Mr. Yorke would not have borne it so patiently; nor even from some women. But he counted Shirley honest, and he counted her pretty; and where he could have flung wrath, he let himself be diverted. Besides, he had a secret liking for Moore’s welfare, and the warmth with which she defended her tenant pleased him. If he wished to avenge himself, he believed he held a weapon: a single word that might, he fancied, tame and silence her, and bring a blush up like dawn over her frank forehead.

“What more hast thou to say?” he asked, as she paused—not because her zeal had cooled, but because breath was needed.

“Say, Mr. Yorke!” she replied, pacing fast from wall to wall of the oak parlour—“say? I have a great deal to say, if I could get it out in lucid order, which I never can do. I have to say that your views, and those of most extreme politicians, are such as none but men in an irresponsible position can advocate; that they are purely opposition views, meant only to be talked about, and never intended to be acted on. Make you Prime Minister of England to-morrow, and you would have to abandon them. You abuse Moore for defending his mill. Had you been in Moore’s place you could not with honour or sense have acted otherwise than he acted. You abuse Mr. Helstone for everything he does. Mr. Helstone has his faults; he sometimes does wrong, but oftener right. Were you ordained vicar of Briarfield, you would find it no easy task to sustain all the active schemes for the benefit of the parish planned and persevered in by your predecessor. I wonder people cannot judge more fairly of each other and themselves. When I hear Messrs. Malone and Donne chatter about the authority of the church, the dignity and claims of the priesthood, the deference due to them as clergymen; when I hear the outbreaks of their small spite against Dissenters; when I witness their silly, narrow jealousies and assumptions; when their palaver about forms, and traditions, and superstitions is sounding in my ear; when I behold their insolent carriage to the poor, their often base servility to the rich—I think the Establishment is indeed in a poor way, and both she and her sons appear in the utmost need of reformation. Turning away distressed from minster tower and village spire—ay, as distressed as a churchwarden who feels the exigence of white-wash and has not wherewithal to purchase lime—I recall your senseless sarcasms on the ‘fat bishops,’ the ‘pampered parsons,’ ‘old mother church,’ etc. I remember your strictures on all who differ from you, your sweeping condemnation of classes and individuals, without the slightest allowance made for circumstances or temptations; and then, Mr. Yorke, doubt clutches my inmost heart as to whether men exist clement, reasonable, and just enough to be entrusted with the task of reform. I don’t believe you are of the number.”

“You have an ill opinion of me, Miss Shirley. You never told me so much of your mind before.”

“I never had an opening. But I have sat on Jessy’s stool by your chair in the back-parlour at Briarmains, for evenings together, listening excitedly to your talk, half admiring what you said, and half rebelling against it. I think you a fine old Yorkshireman, sir. I am proud to have been born in the same county and parish as yourself. Truthful, upright, independent you are, as a rock based below seas; but also you are harsh, rude, narrow, and merciless.”

“Not to the poor, lass, nor to the meek of the earth; only to the proud and high-minded.”

“And what right have you, sir, to make such distinctions? A prouder, a higher-minded man than yourself does not exist. You find it easy to speak comfortably to your inferiors; you are too haughty, too ambitious, too jealous to be civil to those above you. But you are all alike. Helstone also is proud and prejudiced. Moore, though juster and more considerate than either you or the rector, is still haughty, stern, and, in a public sense, selfish. It is well there are such men as Mr. Hall to be found occasionally—men of large and kind hearts, who can love their whole race, who can forgive others for being richer, more prosperous, or more powerful than they are. Such men may have less originality, less force of character than you, but they are better friends to mankind.”

“And when is it to be?” said Mr. Yorke, rising now, his hand on his hat.

“When is what to be?”

“The wedding.”

“Whose wedding?”

“Only that of Robert Gérard Moore, Esq., of Hollow’s Cottage, with Miss Keeldar, daughter and heiress of the late Charles Cave Keeldar of Fieldhead Hall.”

Colour rose in Shirley’s cheek as if some inner fire had licked the surface; yet her gaze did not fall. Her eye shone steadily—burned, indeed, with a deep, controlled brightness.

“That is your revenge,” she said slowly; then, with the quietness of one who has suddenly chosen her ground, added, “Would it be a bad match, unworthy of the late Charles Cave Keeldar’s representative?”

“My lass, Moore is a gentleman; his blood is pure and ancient as mine or thine.”

“And we two set store by ancient blood? We have family pride, though one of us at least is a republican?”

Yorke bowed. His lips refused confession, but his look yielded it: yes, pride of lineage sat in his very shoulders.

“Moore is a gentleman,” Shirley echoed, lifting her head with a glad grace—then checking herself as if a crowd of words had surged up and she would not let them pass. Yet her look spoke. It was language visible but untranslatable—like a lyric in a dialect known only to the heart that utters it. It was no ordinary love-confession, no simple rush of tenderness; there was something more intricate in it, some mingling of purpose, defiance, and feeling that baffled him. He knew, with a faint vexation, that his revenge had not struck home. He was the puzzled one; she triumphed.

“And if Moore is a gentleman, you can be only a lady; therefore—”

“Therefore there would be no inequality in our union.”

“None.”

“Thank you for your approbation. Will you give me away when I relinquish the name of Keeldar for that of Moore?”

Instead of replying, Mr. Yorke stared, as if trying to decide whether she mocked him or spoke truth. Purpose and banter, feeling and scoff, played together on her quick-changing face.

“I don’t understand thee,” he said at last, turning away.

She laughed. “Take courage, sir; you are not singular in your ignorance. But I suppose if Moore understands me that will do, will it not?”

“Moore may settle his own matters henceforward for me; I’ll neither meddle nor make with them further.”

A new thought crossed her like a shadow over sunlit grass. Her countenance changed as if a hand had drawn a veil across it. The eye darkened; the mouth set.

“Have you been asked to interfere? Are you questioning me as another’s proxy?”

“The Lord save us! Whoever weds thee must look about him! Keep all your questions for Robert; I’ll answer no more on ’em. Good-day, lassie!”

When he was gone, the house seemed for a moment to hear itself again: the faint stir of dresses, the distant clink of a tray, the muffled life of rooms. The talk of riot and guns left, behind it, a kind of uneasy silence—like the hush that follows a harsh sound in a quiet wood.

The day was fair—not sharp with sunshine, but softened by cloud-curtains; a dim blue haze lay over the hills, neither wet nor chill. While Shirley was still detained by the necessities of her rank, Caroline persuaded Mrs. Pryor to put on her bonnet and light summer shawl and come out with her, up towards the narrow end of the Hollow.

As they walked away from the house and the mill, the stream’s voice rose to meet them—broken, impatient, fretting. The glen drew its sides together; brushwood and stunted oaks thickened; the path became a mere invitation rather than a road. At the bottom, the mill-stream ran in a rough, unquiet course, struggling with stones, chafing against rugged banks, snaring itself in gnarled roots, foaming, gurgling, battling. It was a sound-world all its own—water making arguments with rock, leaves whispering their small replies, birds threading the air with quick notes. After the hard clangour of last night’s alarms, the place seemed to offer not silence, but kinder acoustics: nothing metallic, nothing that struck the nerves like hammer on anvil. Here, the ear could rest while it listened.

Half a mile from the mill, solitude deepened. The shade of trees unmolested for years received them like a sanctuary; birds sang in that shade as if it were a home built expressly for their music. No trodden way ran here. Wood-flowers kept their freshness as if no boot had bruised them; wild roses bloomed with the secluded confidence of something watched only by the wind. Blue-bells made pools of azure; pearl-white blossoms spangled the grass, humble as little stars dropped to earth.

Mrs. Pryor liked a quiet walk. High-roads, with their voices and chances, she always shunned; byways and lonely lanes she sought. Absolute solitude made her nervous—some vague fear of encounter would creep in and sour the pleasure. But she feared nothing with Caroline. As soon as they were away from human habitations and entered the still demesne of nature with this one young companion, a change stole over her: her countenance softened; her mind brightened, as if released from a tight band. With Caroline—and Caroline only—her heart seemed to lay down a burden. She could even be cheerful; sometimes she was tender. She would let fall bits of knowledge and experience, as one might share pieces of bread broken carefully and offered without ostentation—giving Caroline chances to guess what life she had lived, what cultivation had fed her mind, where her feelings were most vulnerable.

To-day, as they went along, she spoke of the birds singing above them, distinguished their species, and told something of their habits. English natural history seemed familiar to her, not in cold catalogues, but in affectionate recognition. Each wild flower near their path had a name for her; even tiny plants that sprang near stones and peeped out from old wall-chinks—plants Caroline had scarcely noticed—were pointed out and characterised. She had studied the botany of English fields and woods with minute care.

When they reached the head of the ravine, they sat on a ledge of grey, mossy rock that jutted from the base of a steep green hill towering above. Mrs. Pryor looked round and spoke of the neighbourhood as she had once seen it long ago. She noticed changes, compared this landscape with other parts of England, and in those quiet touches revealed a sense of the picturesque, a power of comparison between wild and cultured, grand and tame. Her discourse had a graphic charm, pleasant and unpretending.

Caroline listened with a reverent pleasure so sincere that it seemed to warm the elder lady by reflection. Perhaps rarely—behind that chill, repellent outside, that diffident mien, those incommunicative habits—had Mrs. Pryor known what it was to awaken earnest affection and admiring esteem in someone she could love in return. The consciousness of it made her voice more openly kind. Bending towards Caroline, she put aside a pale brown curl that had strayed from the comb and said, with more marked interest than she often allowed herself:

“I do hope this sweet air blowing from the hill will do you good, my dear Caroline. I wish I could see something more of colour in these cheeks; but perhaps you were never florid?”

“I had red cheeks once,” Caroline answered, smiling. “I remember a year—two years ago—when I used to look in the glass, I saw a different face there to what I see now—rounder and rosier. But when we are young,” added the girl of eighteen, “our minds are careless and our lives easy.”

“Do you,” Mrs. Pryor went on, and the effort it cost her showed in the slight breaking of her phrasing—“do you, at your age, fret yourself with cares for the future? Believe me, you had better not. Let the morrow take thought for the things of itself.”

“True, dear madam. It is not over the future I pine. The evil of the day is sometimes oppressive—too oppressive—and I long to escape it.”

“That is—the evil of the day—that is—your uncle perhaps is not—you find it difficult to understand—he does not appreciate—”

She could not shape the question fully; she could not bring herself to ask outright whether Mr. Helstone was harsh. But Caroline understood the broken hints.

“Oh, that is nothing,” she replied. “My uncle and I get on very well. We never quarrel—I don’t call him harsh—he never scolds me. Sometimes I wish somebody in the world loved me, but I cannot say that I particularly wish him to have more affection for me than he has. As a child, I should perhaps have felt the want of attention, only the servants were very kind to me; but when people are long indifferent to us, we grow indifferent to their indifference. It is my uncle’s way not to care for women and girls, unless they be ladies that he meets in company. He could not alter, and I have no wish that he should alter, as far as I am concerned. I believe it would merely annoy and frighten me were he to be affectionate towards me now. But you know, Mrs. Pryor, it is scarcely living to measure time as I do at the rectory. The hours pass, and I get them over somehow, but I do not live. I endure existence, but I rarely enjoy it. Since Miss Keeldar and you came I have been—I was going to say happier, but that would be untrue.”

She stopped, as if the truth itself had tightened her throat.

“How untrue? You are fond of Miss Keeldar, are you not, my dear?”

“Very fond of Shirley. I both like and admire her. But I am painfully circumstanced. For a reason I cannot explain I want to go away from this place, and to forget it.”

Only half the iron in Moore had been shown on the night he stood in the mill-yard and beat back the press of hungry men. The other half—harder, colder, and more tireless—came after, when the broken panes were mended, the splintered doors set to rights, and the clatter of danger had settled into a steadier, more ominous undertone: pursuit.

He did not spend his wrath on the multitude. Those he had recognised at the pale edge of morning—faces he might have passed on market-day, hands that had once taken wages from him and bread from their own tables—he met now on lane and street without sign. Whether justice spoke in him, or only a certain pride that scorned to strike what was already bowed by want, the result was the same: the followers went free. It was the leaders he wanted; and these were not of the parish.

Strangers, they were said to be—men blown in from the thick towns like soot on a wind: emissaries with no field, no hearth, no settled name. “Down-draughts,” folk called them—bankrupts, debt-bitten, often drink-sodden; men who had nothing to lose and much to gain if fear and confusion could be sold for coin. Moore hunted them as if the chase were a sport God had made for him alone. He liked it—liked it better than cloth. The road, the open air, the long reach of moorland under a changing sky suited him more than the steamy breath of dye-houses; and if the horse beneath him could have spoken, it might have cursed the days when its master’s temper sharpened and his seat grew harder.

He rode far and late; the magistrates, slow and cautious, were forced to move because he would not let them sleep in their indecision. He even liked to see them blanch—to hear the little crack in their voices when the word was said plain: assassination. That dread had lain over the district like a low thunder; it had made manufacturers whisper and public men keep to their own doors at dusk. Only Helstone had ever met it as if it were merely weather.

The old rector—the old Cossack—had long ago made his terms with violence. If he were shot in a lane, he would have fallen as he had lived: upright, contemptuous of pity. Moore, too, knew there was danger; and because he knew it, he despised it. He rode the moors moonlit or moonless with a kind of fierce exhilaration, his mind clearer than it ever was behind a counting-house desk, his blood fresher than it ever grew amid the rattle of looms. The consciousness that he tracked men who would strike from behind was the spur; fear could not live in him beside pride.

There were four leaders to be accounted for. Two, within a fortnight, were run down and brought to bay near Stilbro’. The other two were said to haunt farther off, nearer Birmingham; and Moore’s roads lengthened accordingly.

Yet he did not neglect the mill. Its repair was almost trivial—carpenters and glaziers, nails and putty, a new beam here, a pane there. The rioters had never broken through; his grim darlings, the machines, stood untouched, smelling of oil and iron, as if they had merely watched and waited. Business, justice, and the relentless rhythm of his days left little sign of gentler concern. He went seldom to Fieldhead; and when he did, it was briefly, as if he feared loitering. At the rectory he appeared only to confer with the rector in the study—two hard heads bent together over hard facts.

Meanwhile the year itself seemed set against peace. War’s long hurricane still swept the Continent; there was no clean break in the clouds, no sweet intermission, no softening rain except that bitter red one men talk of in metaphors and taste in their dreams. And closer to home, ruin worked like a quiet miner under Moore’s feet. Whether he crossed his own hearthstone or galloped over sullen Rushedge, he felt the ground give a hollow echo to his tread, as if it were already loosening.

While summer thus wore him thin with motion and purpose, how did it pass with those he left behind?

Let us go first to Fieldhead.

One might expect Shirley Keeldar, with her heart set where it must not be fed, to droop into the paleness of neglected girls—to sit long over a book as if it were a substitute for warmth, or to stitch with a bowed head and silent mouth, languishing into the pretty martyrdom society loves to praise. But she did nothing of the kind. If there remained about her face a wistfulness, it sat there as a passing shadow might sit on bright grass; her careless smile was not banished. The old house, dark with age and oak, she kept light with her presence, as a kitchen is kept cheerful by the certainty of supper and a good fire.

The long gallery learned new echoes—her footfall, her laugh, the light scolding she gave to herself when she remembered she had left something half-done. The dim entrance-hall with its one window grew accustomed to the frequent swish of silk as she crossed it, now with flowers in her hands to soften the barbarous peach-bloom salon, now to the dining-room to throw wide the casements and admit the scent of mignonette and sweet-briar. She had plants forever on the move: lifted from a staircase window where they had grown pale, set at the open porch-door to drink sun as if sun were milk.

She would take up her sewing sometimes; but it was Shirley’s fate never to sit over it steadily five minutes. The thimble was hardly on before some remembered trifle drove her upstairs: an old ivory-backed needle-book she did not need, a china-topped work-box she could not bear to see out of place, a drawer she must put in order because she had glimpsed that morning a confusion that offended her sense of beauty. And often—often—she went only to a particular window, because from that window Briarfield church and rectory could be seen, lying as if in a nest of trees.

Hardly would she return to her bit of cambric or her half-wrought canvas when Tartar’s scrape and strangled whistle sounded at the porch. She must open to him herself; she must put her hand on the great head, feel the heat of his breath, hear the panting that made the still hall sound alive. It was a hot day; he would want water. Shirley would lead him straight to the kitchen and see—see with her own eyes—that the bowl was filled to the brim.

And the kitchen door stood open on the court, and the court was a kind of bright farm-pageant, all sun and feathers: turkeys with their poults, peahens with their chicks, Guinea-fowl freckled like pearls, pigeons pure white or purple-necked or blue and cinnamon, stepping and cooing with that soft domestic music which is as much a part of a place as the smell of bread. The soundscape there—wings rustling, beaks tapping, the low complaint of a hen, the steady, comforting cluck of a mother—was to Shirley like an old dialect of happiness: not written anywhere, not to be translated, only to be lived.

She would run to the pantry for a roll and scatter crumbs on the step. At once her eager, plump vassals thronged her skirts. John would be about the stables; John must be spoken to; her mare must be looked at, stroked, praised, and teased. She would still be patting the warm neck when the cows came in to be milked—slow, heavy, patient shapes, their hooves dull on the yard-stones. Shirley considered that procession important; she must stand and review them as if she were an admiring historian of her own little kingdom, reading in hide and horn the lineage of comfort.

There might be calves, and little new-yeaned lambs—perhaps twins rejected by their mothers. Then Miss Keeldar must feed them herself, under John’s careful eye: the small mouths searching, the thin tongues warm, the greedy sucking that made a faint, intimate sound like rain on leaves. It is not easy to be purely miserable while one is warming milk, while one is wiping a creature’s damp nose, while the air smells of straw and animal heat and summer.

And then John, foreman and oracle, would begin to moot doubtful questions of crofts and ings and holmes. Shirley must fetch her gipsy-straw garden-hat and go with him over stile and along hedge-row to see the matter on the spot. They would stand in the grass, listening to the wind in the hedges, to distant bells, to the far-off pulse of the district’s new noises—hammers, wheels, that metallic far murmur which never quite died now. Shirley, who loved harmony, heard those sounds too; she could not help it. Sometimes, in a pause, when a lark rose and poured out its clear thread of song, the town’s din seemed for a moment an insult; and then the moment passed, because life at Fieldhead insisted on its own duties—milk to be skimmed, fruit to be gathered, bread to be cut.

So bright afternoon wore into soft evening. She came in for late tea, and after tea she never sewed. After tea she read. In that she had a steadiness she never gave to her needle. Her study was not a desk but the rug; her seat a footstool—or, often, the carpet at Mrs. Pryor’s feet. She had learned there as a child, and habit, like a family recipe, keeps its flavour through years.

Tartar lay beside her, tawny and lion-like, his black muzzle on his paws, his limbs stretched straight and strong as if he had been shaped for mountains. One hand of his mistress rested on his rude head, for if she withdrew it he groaned and fretted, and Shirley could not endure that note of dissatisfaction in the room. While she read, she scarcely lifted her eyes, scarcely moved—unless it were to answer Mrs. Pryor’s quiet, deprecatory cautions.

“My dear, you had better not have that great dog so near you; he is crushing the border of your dress.”

“Oh, it is only muslin. I can put a clean one on to-morrow.”

“My dear, I wish you could acquire the habit of sitting to a table when you read.”

“I will try, ma’am, some time; but it is so comfortable to do as one has always been accustomed.”

“My dear, let me beg of you to put that book down. You are trying your eyes by the doubtful firelight.”

“No, ma’am; my eyes are never tired.”

At last a pale light fell on the page from the window. Shirley looked up: the moon was risen—cool, remote, and sovereign. She closed the volume, rose, and walked the room.

If the book had been a good one, it had fed her—fed her in that strange way words can, when they taste of feeling and smell faintly of worlds we cannot enter. It had refilled her heart as broth refills a chilled body; it had set her mind astir, furnishing it with pictures. The still parlour, the clean hearth, the open window showing the twilight sky and the moon new-throned and glorious, sufficed to make earth seem Eden and life a poem. A deep inborn delight glowed in Shirley then—unmingled, untroubled, beyond human reach because by no human hand bestowed. Nature’s free gift; God’s secret dower. It lifted her as if on green steps up glad hills into a station where she could look down on her life and see it—not as she wished it, for she had no time to wish—only as a swift spreading glory that outran thought and outshone desire.

She did not speak while that trance held. If Mrs. Pryor addressed her then, Shirley answered softly, and went out into the dim gallery upstairs, pacing as if she would wear the splendour into her very bones.
