Had she been less indolent, less reckless, less ignorant of her own riches, she would have seized such moments with a pen and fixed them; she would have written plainly, in her queer, clear hand, the song and the story that came to her unbidden, and so made them property. But she did not. She let them pass, as some people let a beautiful phrase in a dying dialect pass because it is not written, because it is only heard, because tomorrow’s dominant language will not bother to remember.

Shirley took life easily. It was in her eye. When she was calm, that large gray sphere held softness—humour, indulgence, tenderness. Rouse her, and a red ray pierced the dew; it kindled instantly to flame.

Before July was out, she would likely have started with Caroline on the northern tour they had planned—those imagined miles like promises, those fresh inns, those mountain airs that taste like cold water in a cup of tin. But just then Fieldhead was invaded.

A genteel foraging party descended upon her stronghold and besieged her with politeness until she surrendered at discretion. An uncle, an aunt, and two cousins from the south—Mr. and Mrs. Sympson and the two Misses Sympson of Sympson Grove, ——shire—came down upon her in state. Hospitality is a law with iron under its velvet; Shirley gave in, and gave in so readily that Caroline, who knew her promptness and fertility of expedient when her own will was at stake, could not help questioning her.

“How can you submit so easily?”

“Old feelings have their power,” Shirley answered. She had spent two years of her early youth at Sympson Grove.

“And do you like them?”

“I have nothing in common with them,” she said. Only little Harry Sympson—their only brother—had once been dear to her; he was unlike his sisters. But he was not coming to Yorkshire—at least not yet.

The next Sunday, in Briarfield Church, the Fieldhead pew looked like a small exhibit of correctness. There sat a prim, trim, fidgety elderly gentleman, shifting his spectacles and changing posture every three minutes; a patient, placid elderly lady in brown satin; and two pattern young ladies in pattern attire, with pattern deportment. In the midst of them Shirley had the air of a black swan among domestic geese, or a white crow perched in a row of tame birds; and for all her spirit, her aspect was forlorn.

Having placed her thus in respectable society, we leave her there a little, and turn to Caroline.

With Miss Keeldar fenced off by fine relatives and the bustle their arrival made in the neighbourhood, Caroline could not go to Fieldhead. The visiting commotion frightened her away as a sudden loudness frightens a shy creature from its accustomed cover. She was limited again to the gray rectory; to solitary morning walks in remote by-paths where the hedges held their own quiet talk of insects and leaves; to long, lonely afternoons in a parlour the sun deserted at noon; or to the garden alcove where it shone bright yet sadly on ripening red currants trained over the trellis, and on monthly roses entwined between, and fell chequered through their leaves on Caroline’s white summer dress as she sat still as a garden statue.

She read old books from her uncle’s library. Greek and Latin were dead to her, not only as languages but as nourishment. The lighter shelf had belonged once to Aunt Mary: venerable Lady’s Magazines that had crossed the sea and been in a storm, their pages stained with salt water—as if the very print had learned the taste of brine; Methodist Magazines full of miracles and apparitions, preternatural warnings, ominous dreams, frenzied fanaticism; the equally mad letters of Mrs. Elizabeth Rowe from the Dead to the Living; and a few old English classics. In childhood Caroline had sucked honey from these faded flowers; now they were tasteless.

For change, and for good, she would sew—make garments for the poor under good Miss Ainley’s direction. Sometimes she watched her tears drop slowly onto the cloth and wondered how that excellent woman could be so serenely equal in her own solitude.

“I never find Miss Ainley oppressed with despondency or lost in grief,” Caroline thought. “Yet her cottage is still and dim, and she is without bright hope or near friend. But she told me once she had taught her thoughts to tend upwards to heaven. She allows there has been little enjoyment in this world for her; she looks, I suppose, to the bliss of the world to come. So do nuns in their close cells, with iron lamps and robes strait as shrouds, beds narrow as coffins. She says often she has no fear of death—no dread of the grave; no more, doubtless, had St. Simeon Stylites lifted up terrible on his wild column; no more has the Hindu votary stretched on iron spikes. Such people, violating nature, reverse their natural likings; they grow morbid.

“I do fear death as yet; perhaps it is because I am young. Poor Miss Ainley would cling closer to life if life had more charms for her. Surely God did not create us and cause us to live with the sole end of wishing always to die. In my heart I believe we were meant to prize life and enjoy it while we have it. Existence was not originally meant to be that blank, pale, slow-trailing thing it becomes to many—and is becoming to me.

“Nobody in particular is to blame, that I can see, for the state in which things are; and I cannot tell, however I puzzle over it, how they are to be altered at once. But I feel there is something wrong somewhere. Single women should have more to do—better chances of interesting and profitable occupation than they possess now. And when I speak thus I do not feel I displease God; that I am impious or impatient. My consolation is that God hears many a groan, and has compassion on much grief that man stops his ears against, or frowns on with impotent contempt. I say impotent; for where society cannot readily cure grievances it usually forbids their utterance, and covers its weakness with scorn like tinsel thrown over deformity. People hate to be reminded of ills they cannot or will not remedy. The reminder disturbs their ease; it shakes their self-complacency.

“Old maids, like houseless and unemployed poor, should not ask for a place and an occupation in the world; the demand disturbs the happy and rich—it disturbs parents. Look at the numerous families of girls in this neighbourhood—the Armitages, the Birtwhistles, the Sykeses. Their brothers are in business or professions; they have something to do. Their sisters have no earthly employment but household work and sewing; no earthly pleasure but unprofitable visiting; and no hope in all their life to come of anything better. This stagnant state makes them decline in health. They are never well; their minds shrink to wondrous narrowness.

“The great wish—the sole aim—of every one is to be married, but the majority will never marry. They scheme; they plot; they dress to ensnare husbands. The gentlemen turn them into ridicule; they do not want them; they hold them cheap. I have heard them say it with sneering laughs: the matrimonial market is overstocked. Fathers say so too, and are angry when they observe their daughters’ manoeuvres; they order them to stay at home. But what do they expect them to do at home? If you ask, they answer: sew and cook. Sew and cook—contentedly, regularly, uncomplainingly—all their lives long—as if they had no germs of faculties for anything else. It is as reasonable as holding that fathers have no faculties but for eating what their daughters cook or wearing what their daughters sew.

“Could men live so themselves? Would they not grow weary? And when no relief came—only reproach for the least sign of weariness—would not the weariness ferment into frenzy?

“Lucretia spinning at midnight among her maidens, and Solomon’s virtuous woman, are quoted as patterns. Lucretia, I dare say, was worthy—much like my cousin Hortense Moore—but she kept her servants up very late. I should not have liked to be one of the maidens. Hortense would work Sarah and me so if she could, and neither of us would bear it. The virtuous woman, again, had her household up in the middle of the night—Mrs. Sykes says she ‘got breakfast over’ before one o’clock a.m.—but she did more than spin and give out portions. She was a manufacturer; she made fine linen and sold it. She was an agriculturist; she bought estates and planted vineyards. That woman was a manager—what the matrons here call a clever woman. I like her better than Lucretia; and I don’t believe either Mr. Armitage or Mr. Sykes could have got the advantage of her in a bargain. Yet I like her.

“‘Strength and honour were her clothing; the heart of her husband safely trusted in her. She opened her mouth with wisdom; in her tongue was the law of kindness; her children rose up and called her blessed; her husband also praised her.’ King of Israel, your model is worthy. But are we, in these days, brought up to be like her? Men of Yorkshire—do your daughters reach that standard? Can they? Can you help them? Can you give them a field wherein their faculties may be exercised and grow?

“Men of England, look at your poor girls—many fading around you, dropping off in consumption or decline; or, what is worse, degenerating into sour old maids—envious, back-biting, wretched—because life is a desert to them; or, what is worst of all, reduced to strive by scarce modest coquetry and debasing artifice to gain by marriage that consideration which is denied to celibacy. Fathers, cannot you alter these things? Perhaps not at once; but think when the matter is brought before you. Do not dismiss it with an idle jest or an unmanly insult. You would wish to be proud of your daughters and not blush for them; then seek for them an interest and an occupation that shall raise them above the flirt, the manoeuvrer, the mischief-making tale-bearer. Keep your girls’ minds narrow and fettered, and they will still be a plague and a care—sometimes a disgrace. Cultivate them; give them scope and work; they will be your gayest companions in health, your tenderest nurses in sickness, your most faithful prop in age.”

So thought Caroline, her needle moving while her heart stood still; and outside, beyond the roses and the currants, the district’s mixed music went on—birds and bees in the garden, and, farther away, that hard industrial murmur that was changing the very air people breathed, changing what they could hear, changing what they could remember, and what words they would one day have left to name their own hunger.

One lucid summer day, Caroline Helstone had been left to the mercy of hours that would not end. Her uncle was away at Whinbury; the house at the Rectory had the decency of order, the garden the abundance of June, and yet the day felt to her like a bright desert—light everywhere, not a breath of wind anywhere, the bees almost silent as if even they had tired of labour. Needlework lay in her lap: a small brown holland slip for a child, braid ready to be stitched down with exactness. She set the edge, counted the turns, kept her hands busy because her mind would not be still. The very quiet had a sound of its own—an oppressive hush, broken only by the faint tick of her own industry and the distant, dulled clink of someone’s pail at a neighbour’s gate. In such a stillness, a heart can hear itself too plainly.

Fanny came to the garden-door, glanced over lawn and borders, and, not seeing her young mistress, called out, “Miss Caroline!”

A low voice answered, “Fanny!” from the shaded alcove. Fanny hurried there with a note.

Caroline’s fingers, a moment ago so deft with the needle, scarcely seemed to have strength to take paper. She did not ask who sent it; she did not even look. It fell among the folds of the little frock.

“Joe Scott’s son, Harry, brought it,” said Fanny.

That simple name—Harry Scott—was as if some hand had turned a key in a locked room. Caroline lifted her head with sudden life.

“Harry Scott! Who sent him?”

“He came from the Hollow.”

The note was caught up as though it might escape, the seal torn, the lines read at once—two seconds, and her whole face changed. It was from Hortense: returned from Wormwood Wells; alone for the day, Robert being gone to Whinbury market; would Caroline come to tea? It would do her good, Hortense said—so much better for her than sitting solitary since the “misunderstanding” that had separated her from her “meilleure amie, Hortense Gérard Moore.” In the postscript she was pressed to put on her bonnet and run down directly.

Caroline needed no urging. It was a kind of hunger in her—hunger not for food, though even that is dearer when shared, but for the mere warmth of other voices, for movement, for change. She laid aside the child’s slip—its plain cloth, its patient braid—rose, went upstairs, covered her curls with her straw bonnet, and threw round her shoulders her black silk scarf. The scarf lay simply, and yet it made her look more composed than she felt, its dark sheen setting off the whiteness of her dress and the fairness of her throat and cheek.

Down the green lane she went, the slope leading her to the Hollow as water runs to a known low place. The hedges were thick with summer’s provision: white may and late honeysuckle, cow-parsley fading to seed, and here and there the bruised sharpness of wild mint where someone’s foot had trodden it. She drew in the lane’s breath as if it could feed her. The birds kept their own quiet domestic economy; somewhere a thrush tried a phrase and stopped, and in the far distance there came, softened by leaves, the dull rhythm of a cart—wood on road, iron on stone—a sound that belonged to work and therefore to life.

She knew Robert would not be at the cottage. Yet it was comfort merely to go where he had been; to step into rooms he had crossed that morning; to sit, though only for an hour, within the reach of objects his hand might have touched. Separation had made the smallest trace of him precious—almost sacred. In such moments Illusion, that gentle treacherous companion, was quick to return. It seemed to her, as she hurried under the sunlit branches, that the air itself whispered: He may come home while you are there. You may at least look at his face. At least give him your hand. Perhaps, for a minute, sit beside him.

“Silence!” she said inwardly, stern with herself; and yet she loved the whisper because it soothed.

Miss Moore must have seen the white flutter of Caroline’s dress between the shrubs, for she came from the porch to meet her—straight, unbending, composed as ever. Hortense did not permit haste to disorder her dignity; but she smiled, gratified to be sought, pleased to feel Caroline’s kiss and the sincere clinging of her embrace. She led her in with an air of benevolent possession, half deceived and wholly flattered.

Had she been undeceived—had she known precisely how much of Caroline’s eagerness belonged, not to a cousin’s invitation, but to a brother’s absence—Hortense would likely have felt shocked and affronted. Sisters do not willingly see young women fall in love with their brothers; the sentiment seems to them, if not presumption, then folly—a kind of crude romance. They do not themselves love these gentlemen as others do; and the idea that another should makes them recoil, as if at an error in taste. Reason may correct the first contempt, where reason rules; where it does not, the dislike is lifelong.

“You would expect to find me alone, from what I wrote this morning,” said Hortense, conducting her towards the parlour; “but since dinner company has come in.”

She opened the door, and there, like a crimson overflow, sat Mrs. Yorke—an amplitude of red skirt spilling over the elbow-chair by the hearth. Above it, presiding with a gravity that seemed a household law, rose a cap more formidable than a crown. That cap had not travelled under a bonnet; it had come, one might imagine, in a great black silk balloon, distended with whalebone. A quarter-yard frill stood round the wearer’s face; love-ribbon, puffed and bowed in profusion, flourished about the head. It was a kind of martial adornment, and Mrs. Yorke carried it as if it were armour.

She had come “in a friendly way” to take tea with Hortense—an act of condescension as rare as it was grand; for she scorned visiting and tea-drinking, and called “gossips” the women who made such small convivialities their habit. Yet Hortense was a favourite. Mrs. Yorke had shown it by stopping to speak to her on Sundays in the churchyard; by bidding her, almost hospitably, to Briarmains; by coming herself to the Hollow to-day.

Her reasons, as she would state them, were that Miss Moore was steady, without levity; that, being a foreigner, she needed countenance. She might have added—if she had ever cared to confess the true springs of her approvals—that Hortense’s plainness, her precise dress, her phlegm, were to Mrs. Yorke a positive merit. Whatever gentlemen admired in women, she condemned; whatever they overlooked, she patronised.

Caroline advanced with diffidence. She knew little of Mrs. Yorke, and as a parson’s niece could not tell what welcome such a matron might grant. She received a cool nod—nothing more—and turned away quickly to remove her bonnet, glad to hide her sudden colour and confusion.

When she sat down, she was immediately accosted—not by Mrs. Yorke, but by a small figure in a blue frock and sash that sprang up, fairy-like, from the shelter of the red gown’s folds. The child had been seated on a footstool, quite hidden. Now she ran to Caroline, flung her arms round her neck, and demanded a kiss.

“My mother is not civil to you,” she announced, as if reporting the weather. “And Rose there takes no notice. It is their way. If, instead of you, a white angel with a crown of stars came in, mother would nod stiffly, and Rose would never lift her head. But I will be your friend. I have always liked you.”

“Jessie, curb that tongue of yours,” said Mrs. Yorke. “Repress your forwardness.”

“But, mother, you are so frozen!” Jessie protested, unabashed. “Miss Helstone has never done you harm. Why can’t you be kind? You sit so stiff and look so cold and speak so dry—what for? That’s how you treat Miss Shirley Keeldar and every other young lady at our house. And Rose there is such an aut—aut— I have forgotten the word. It means a machine in the shape of a human being. However, between you, you will drive every soul away from Briarmains. Martin often says so.”

“I am an automaton? Good!” said Rose from a corner, where she sat on the carpet at the foot of a bookcase with a volume open on her knee. She lifted her eyes briefly—remarkable gray eyes that looked older than her years—let them rest for the shortest instant on Caroline, and then dropped them again to her page. “Miss Helstone, how do you do?”

Caroline, sheltered for a moment from Mrs. Yorke’s chill by the children’s frankness, let herself look at Rose—at the absorbed brow, the stillness of her limbs, the slight unconscious movement at the corner of her mouth as she read, as if the very words had taste and she must try them, chew them, test their savour. Caroline had instinct; she felt Rose was one of the unique, to be approached not by chatter but by quiet.

She went over and knelt beside her on the carpet, looking over her shoulder without comment. The book was Mrs. Radcliffe’s The Italian.

They read together in silence for a little while—Rose turning the leaves with careful fingers, as if each page were a thin pastry-sheet that must not tear. At last Rose paused, and, before turning over, asked gravely, “Are you ready?”

Caroline nodded.

After some minutes Rose asked, “Do you like it?”

“Long since, when I read it as a child, I was wonderfully taken with it.”

“Why?”

“It seemed to open with such promise—such foreboding of a strange tale to be unfolded.”

“And in reading it you feel as if you were far away from England—really in Italy—under another sort of sky; that blue southern sky travellers describe.”

“You are sensible of that, Rose?”

“It makes me long to travel, Miss Helstone.”

“When you are a woman, perhaps you may be able to gratify your wish.”

“I mean to make a way, if one is not made for me. I cannot live always in Briarfield. The whole world is not very large compared with creation. I must see the outside of our own round planet, at least.”

“How much of its outside?”

“First this hemisphere where we live; then the other. I am resolved my life shall be a life. Not a black trance like the toad’s, buried in marble; nor a long slow death like yours in Briarfield Rectory.”

“Like mine! What can you mean, child?”

“Might you not as well be tediously dying as be for ever shut up in that glebe-house—a place that, when I pass it, always reminds me of a windowed grave? I never see movement about the door. I never hear a sound from the wall. I believe smoke never issues from the chimneys. What do you do there?”

“I sew, I read, I learn lessons.”

“Are you happy?”

“Should I be happy wandering alone in strange countries as you wish to do?”

“Much happier, even if you did nothing but wander. Remember, however, I shall have an object in view; but if you only went on and on, like some enchanted lady in a fairy tale, you might be happier than now. In a day’s wandering you would pass hill, wood, and watercourse, each perpetually altering as the sun shone out or was overcast; as weather was wet or fair. Nothing changes in Briarfield Rectory. The plaster of the parlour ceiling, the paper on the walls, the curtains, carpets, chairs—still the same.”

“Is change necessary to happiness?”

“Yes.”

“Is it synonymous with it?”

“I don’t know; but I feel monotony and death to be almost the same.”

Jessie broke in, delighted. “Isn’t she mad?”

“But, Rose,” said Caroline, gently, “I fear a wanderer’s life—for me, at least—would end like that tale, in disappointment, vanity, and vexation.”

“Does The Italian end so?”

“I thought so when I read it.”

“Better to try all things and find all empty than to try nothing and leave life blank. To do this is to commit the sin of him who buried his talent in a napkin—despicable sluggard!”

“Rose,” observed Mrs. Yorke from her chair, “solid satisfaction is only to be realized by doing one’s duty.”

“Right, mother. And if my Master has given me ten talents, my duty is to trade with them and make them ten more. Not in the dust of household drawers shall the coin be interred. I will not deposit it in a broken-spouted teapot and shut it in a china closet among tea-things. I will not commit it to your work-table to be smothered under piles of woollen hose. I will not prison it in the linen press to find shrouds among the sheets. And least of all, mother—least of all will I hide it in a tureen of cold potatoes, to be ranged with bread, butter, pastry, and ham on the shelves of the larder.”

At “cold potatoes” Caroline’s senses betrayed her; the domestic picture, so vividly placed, rose before her with its exact smell—the earthy, faintly sweet starch, the damp chill of a stone pantry, the salt tang of ham hung to cure, and that warm butter-scent that always clings to a larder shelf even when the dish is covered. The whole scene had a sort of homely mythology to it: the household gods of thrift and repetition, guarding their stores, insisting that a girl’s life be measured out like portions.

Rose went on, voice steady, eyes bright. “The Lord who gave each of us our talents will come home some day and demand an account. The teapot, the old stocking-foot, the linen rag, the willow-pattern tureen will yield up their barren deposit in many a house. Suffer your daughters, at least, to put their money to the exchangers, that they may be enabled at the Master’s coming to pay Him His own with usury.”

“Rose, did you bring your sampler with you, as I told you?” asked Mrs. Yorke.

“Yes, mother.”

“Sit down and do a line of marking.”

Rose obeyed at once. Her fingers—fingers meant for books and maps and perhaps distant journeys—took needle and thread with the same composure. For ten minutes there was only the small industrious sound of work: the prick and pull, the faint rustle of cloth, the occasional creak of Mrs. Yorke’s chair as she shifted her weight. Outside, a swallow flicked past the window with its thin cry; somewhere a kettle in the kitchen began to murmur, and the low, watery song seemed for a moment like comfort—like a domestic pulse.

Then Mrs. Yorke asked, “Do you think yourself oppressed now—a victim?”

“No, mother.”

“Yet, as far as I understood your tirade, it was a protest against all womanly and domestic employment.”

“You misunderstood it. I should be sorry not to learn to sew. You do right to teach me and to make me work.”

“Even to the mending of your brothers’ stockings and the making of sheets?”

“Yes.”

“Where is the use of ranting and spouting about it, then?”

“Am I to do nothing but that? I will do that, and then I will do more. Now, mother, I have said my say. I am twelve years old at present, and not till I am sixteen will I speak again about talents. For four years I bind myself an industrious apprentice to all you can teach me.”

“You see what my daughters are, Miss Helstone,” said Mrs. Yorke, turning her formidable cap towards Caroline. “How precociously wise in their own conceits. ‘I would rather this, I prefer that’—such is Jessie’s cuckoo song; while Rose utters the bolder cry, ‘I will, and I will not!’”

“I render a reason, mother,” said Rose, without looking up from her marking. “Besides, if my cry is bold, it is only heard once in a twelvemonth. About each birthday the spirit moves me to deliver one oracle respecting my own instruction and management. I utter it and leave it; it is for you, mother, to listen or not.”

“I would advise all young ladies,” pursued Mrs. Yorke, “to study the characters of such children before they marry and have any of their own; to consider well the responsibility of guiding the careless, the labour of persuading the stubborn, the constant burden of training even the best.”

“But with love it need not be so very difficult,” Caroline interposed. “Mothers love their children most dearly—almost better than they love themselves.”

“Fine talk—very sentimental. There is the rough, practical part of life yet to come for you, young miss.”

“But, Mrs. Yorke, if I take a little baby into my arms—any poor woman’s infant, for instance—I feel I love that helpless thing quite peculiarly, though I am not its mother. I could do almost anything for it willingly, if it were delivered over entirely to my care—if it were quite dependent on me.”

“You feel! Yes, yes! I dare say, now. You are led a great deal by your feelings, and you think yourself very sensitive, no doubt. Are you aware that with all these romantic ideas you have managed to train your features into an habitually lackadaisical expression, better suited to a novel-heroine than to a woman who is to make her way in the real world by dint of common sense?”

“No; I am not at all aware of that, Mrs. Yorke.”

“Look in the glass behind you. Compare the face you see there with that of any early-rising, hard-working milkmaid.”

“My face is pale, but it is not sentimental; and most milkmaids, however red and robust they may be, are more stupid and less practically fitted to make their way in the world than I am. I think more, and more correctly, than milkmaids in general do; consequently, where they would often, for want of reflection, act weakly, I, by dint of reflection, should act judiciously.”

“Oh no! you would be influenced by your feelings; guided by impulse.”

“Of course I should often be influenced by my feelings. They were given me to that end. Whom my feelings teach me to love I must and shall love; and I hope, if ever I have a husband and children, my feelings will induce me to love them. I hope, in that case, all my impulses will be strong in compelling me to love.”

She felt a fierce pleasure in saying it—pleasure in daring to say it there, before that chill tribunal. Her cheeks flushed, not with anger, but with the heat of a truth admitted aloud.

Mrs. Yorke answered coolly, “Don’t waste your dramatic effects. That was well said—quite fine; but it is lost on two women—an old wife and an old maid. There should have been a disengaged gentleman present. Is Mr. Robert nowhere hid behind the curtains, do you think, Miss Moore?”

Hortense, who had been much in the kitchen superintending tea—watching the kettle’s boil, measuring leaves, setting bread and butter with French exactness, making certain the cream was fresh and the cups warmed—had not caught the drift. She came back with a puzzled look and answered literally, “Robert is at Whinbury.”

Mrs. Yorke laughed her short, peculiar laugh.

“Straightforward Miss Moore!” she said patronisingly. “It is like you to understand so literally and answer so simply. Your mind comprehends nothing of intrigue. Strange things might go on around you without your being the wiser; you are not of the class the world calls sharp-witted.”

The equivocal compliment did not please Hortense. Her black brows puckered; her back stiffened. Yet she still looked bewildered, and, because she prided herself on discernment, she hastened to reclaim it.

“I have ever been noted for sagacity and discernment from childhood,” she returned.

“You never plotted to win a husband, I’ll be bound,” pursued Mrs. Yorke; “and you have not the benefit of previous experience to aid you in discovering when others plot.”

Caroline felt those words as if they had been laid, cold and heavy, on her heart. She could not parry them. To answer would have been to confess the blow had found its mark. She sat with downcast eyes, cheeks burning, posture unconsciously bent—as if the very air in the room had grown too sharp. Mrs. Yorke, seeing the tremor of humiliation, thought her fair game.

There was in Mrs. Yorke a natural antipathy to shrinking sensitiveness. Youth and prettiness did not soften her; they irritated her, like something too fragile that asks to be spared. This afternoon she was especially bilious and morose—disposed to gore, as some fierce cow in a field will lower her head at a creature that flinches.

“Your cousin Hortense is an excellent sister, Miss Helstone,” she began again. “Such ladies as come to try their life’s luck at Hollow’s Cottage may, by a very little clever female artifice, cajole the mistress of the house and have the game in their own hands. You are fond of your cousin’s society, I dare say, miss?”

“Of which cousin’s?” Caroline asked, keeping her voice even.

“Oh, of the lady’s, of course.”

“Hortense is, and always has been, most kind to me.”

“Every sister with an eligible single brother is considered most kind by her spinster friends.”

Caroline lifted her eyes slowly. The trouble cleared from their blue; the shame’s hot colour ebbed, leaving her pale, but steady. Her voice, when it came, was not loud, not rapid, but distinct as a knife laid down on a board.

“Mrs. Yorke, may I ask what you mean?”

“To give you a lesson in the cultivation of rectitude—to disgust you with craft and false sentiment.”

“Do I need this lesson?”

“Most young ladies of the present day need it. You are quite a modern young lady—morbid, delicate, professing to like retirement; which implies you find little worthy of sympathy in the ordinary world. The ordinary world—every-day honest folks—are better than you think them; much better than any bookish, romancing chit of a girl can be who hardly ever puts her nose over her uncle the parson’s garden wall.”

“Consequently of whom you know nothing,” said Caroline, and there was no tremor now, only clean anger held in bounds. “Excuse me—indeed it does not matter whether you excuse me or not—you have attacked me without provocation; I shall defend myself without apology. Of my relations with my two cousins you are ignorant. In a fit of ill-humour you have attempted to poison them by gratuitous insinuations, which are far more crafty and false than anything with which you can justly charge me. That I happen to be pale, and sometimes to look diffident, is no business of yours; that I am fond of books and indisposed for common gossip is still less your business; that I am a ‘romancing chit of a girl’ is conjecture. I never romanced to you nor to any one you know. That I am the parson’s niece is not a crime, though you may be narrow-minded enough to think it so. You dislike me. You have no just reason for disliking me; therefore keep the expression of your aversion to yourself. If at any time in future you evince it annoyingly, I shall answer even less scrupulously than I have done now.”

She ceased and sat perfectly still, white with restrained excitement. She had spoken in the clearest tones, each word shaped and placed. The speed of the blood in her veins was swift and invisible; but her bearing did not betray it.

Mrs. Yorke was not irritated. Turning coolly to Hortense, she nodded her tremendous cap as if applauding an exercise well done.

“She has spirit in her, after all,” she said. “Always speak as honestly as you have done just now, and you’ll do.”

“I repel a recommendation so offensive,” Caroline answered at once, the same pure key, the same unflinching look. “I reject counsel poisoned by insinuation. It is my right to speak as I think proper; nothing binds me to converse as you dictate. So far from always speaking as I have done just now, I shall never address any one in a tone so stern or in language so harsh, unless in answer to unprovoked insult.”

“Mother, you have found your match,” pronounced little Jessie, much edified.

Rose had listened without moving her face. She went on with her marking, then said, without malice, as if stating a fact from a book, “No; Miss Helstone is not my mother’s match, for she allows herself to be vexed. My mother would wear her out in a few weeks. Shirley Keeldar manages better. Mother, you have never hurt Miss Keeldar’s feelings yet. She wears armour under her silk dress that you cannot penetrate.”

The future does not always announce itself with a crash. Sometimes it steals in as weather does: a pressure in the air, a wrongness in the light, a thread of sound on the wind that makes the heart listen before the mind understands. And sometimes it breaks through without warning, as if the earth had parted and shown, all at once, what it had been quietly preparing to hide.

Caroline Helstone came home from Hollow’s Cottage believing herself well. The walk had been mild, the lanes sweet, the hedges breathing hawthorn and early leaf; nothing in her limbs foretold disaster. Yet on waking the next morning, she felt a weight upon her, not pain—something duller, more ominous: a languor that sat on her chest and made her arms feel as if they belonged to another. At breakfast she tried to take what was offered; the bread, warm and brown, might have been ash. Butter had no richness; tea, usually her small comfort, ran tasteless over a tongue already parched.

“Am I ill?” she whispered, and went to the glass as if it could answer. Her eyes were bright to strangeness, pupils wide; her cheeks, too, carried a bloom that looked like health and was not. She stared at that false colour and wondered why her mouth refused even the thought of food.

In her temples the pulse beat quick, an impatient hammer. Her mind, instead of sinking, raced: splinters of thought, brilliant and broken, flew like sparks from a fire that would not settle. That unnatural liveliness fed the glow in her face, and hid, for the moment, what was kindling underneath.

Then came night—hot, dry, restless. Her thirst was not quenched by water; her skin burned, and yet she shivered if the sheet shifted. Towards morning a dream took her with the sudden cruelty of a beast; she woke as if from an assault, and knew—quietly, irrevocably—that she was ill.

How the fever had entered her she could not tell. Perhaps a soft, honeyed breath from some hollow meadow had been tainted, and she had drawn it in with the heedlessness of one whose lungs are pure and unsuspecting; perhaps the very sweetness of the season, the heavy perfume of dusk and dew, had carried its own poison. Or perhaps the body, long strained by inward conflict, offers a spark to anything that will burn. The mind’s excitement, the heart’s habitual ache—these might have been dry tinder; the air merely the match.

At first it seemed a gentle flame. Two hot days, two worried nights, but no violence. Mr. Helstone frowned at the fuss, Fanny watched dutifully, the doctor spoke of a chill and prescribed with easy confidence, and when Miss Keeldar came, she laughed away the notion of danger with a brightness that had never made room for death.

“A few days,” each said, “and she will be herself.”

The few days went, and the revival did not begin. A fortnight passed. Caroline did not grow fierce with illness; she simply diminished. She wasted as snow wastes in a thaw—quietly, without struggle; she faded as a flower fades when the root can no longer drink, though the sun still shines.

Mrs. Pryor, who had been coming daily, sat one morning at the bedside and watched Caroline longer than she had ever watched her before. She took the thin hand, placed a finger on the wrist, and sat as if listening not only to the beat beneath her touch, but to something else—some inward clock that was running down.

She left the room without remark and went to Mr. Helstone’s study. They were closeted together for half the morning. When she came back, she did not keep her bonnet on. She laid shawl and bonnet aside as if she had come home. She stood by the bed with her hands folded one over the other, and rocked gently—an habitual motion, like a lullaby she gave herself when she could not give it to another.

“I have sent Fanny to Fieldhead,” she said at last, “to fetch a few things for me—such as I shall want during a short stay here. It is my wish to remain with you till you are better. Your uncle kindly permits my attendance. Will it be acceptable to you, Caroline?”

Caroline tried to smile; her smile had become an effort, but she still offered it as a courtesy.

“I am sorry you should take such needless trouble. I do not feel very ill,” she said—still clinging to that delusion as if it were strength. “But I cannot refuse. It will be such comfort to know you are in the house—to see you sometimes. Only do not confine yourself on my account, dear Mrs. Pryor. Fanny nurses me very well.”

Mrs. Pryor bent and smoothed the hair under Caroline’s cap, lifted the pillow, set the sheet straight with hands that made no sound upon linen. Caroline, with a sudden tenderness, lifted her face to kiss her.

“Are you free from pain? Are you tolerably at ease?” Mrs. Pryor asked softly.

“I think I am almost happy.”

“You wish to drink? Your lips are parched.”

She held to Caroline’s mouth a glass of something cool—water faintly flavoured, as if with a slice of lemon or a bruised leaf of balm; it seemed to take the heat down one degree as it passed.

“Have you eaten anything to-day, Caroline?”

“I cannot eat.”

“But soon your appetite will return,” Mrs. Pryor said, and her voice trembled with a will that sounded like prayer. “It must return—that is, I pray God it may.”

As she laid Caroline back, she gathered her for an instant—an embrace that seemed to escape her before she could govern it: the impulse of a heart that could not bear distance.

“I shall hardly wish to get well, that I may keep you always,” Caroline murmured.

Mrs. Pryor did not smile. A tremor ran over her face, and she fought it down as one fights down sobs in a room where they would do harm.

“You are more used to Fanny than to me,” she said after a pause. “My attendance must seem strange—officious?”

“No. Quite natural. So soothing. You must have been accustomed to wait on sick people. You move so softly; you speak so quietly; you touch me so gently.”

“I am dexterous in nothing, my dear,” Mrs. Pryor returned. “You will often find me awkward, but never negligent.”

Negligent she was not. From that hour the sick-room became her kingdom. Fanny and Eliza were present, but as shadows. Mrs. Pryor lived beside the bed; she made the small services of nursing into a continual devotion. She learned, as if by instinct, what coolness the fevered skin could bear, what draught soothed without chilling, what morsel might be tempted to the lips—toast delicately browned and dipped in weak tea, a spoonful of broth cleared and strained, a little jelly that trembled like a promise. Yet Caroline could scarcely swallow. The body refused nourishment; the very smells that should have coaxed appetite—fresh bread, warm milk, the faint, clean scent of mint—seemed to pass her by as if she were already half removed from common life.

With all this care, it was strange she did not get well. Miss Keeldar, who had never been haunted by the idea of death, began at last to feel its shape in the room. Each visit showed a further sinking; alarm seized her at last, and she went with her imperious candour to Mr. Helstone.

“She is not merely poorly,” Shirley said. “She is going away.”

Mr. Helstone resisted, as men resist what they cannot command. But energy like Shirley’s, added to Mrs. Pryor’s quiet insistence, forced him to yield. When Mrs. Pryor demanded another physician, he answered stiffly that she might send for two if she pleased.

One came—an oracle. He looked, felt, listened, spoke in dark sentences which seemed to leave the burden of meaning upon time. He wrote prescriptions and directions, with an air of authority that would have crushed hope if hope had been in his power to crush. He took his fee and went. It was likely he knew there was little to be done, and did not choose to say so.

Still the neighbourhood did not yet breathe the rumour of danger. At Hollow’s Cottage it was believed to be only a severe cold; Caroline had written to Hortense to that effect, keeping the worst from every eye but the eyes that watched her closest. Hortense answered with practical kindness: two pots of currant jam—sharp, dark, homely preserve made to tempt a stubborn palate—also a recipe for a tisane, and a note of advice in her clear, brisk hand.

Mrs. Yorke, hearing that a physician had been called, sneered as if she tasted something sour.

“Hypochondria,” she said. “Rich and idle folk send for doctors when their little finger aches—having nothing else to think of.”

But the “rich and idle,” in the person of Caroline Helstone, were sinking into a prostration that would have sobered any scoffer who had seen her. All were puzzled but one; for one alone understood how quickly a structure long undermined can fall, not by storm but by its own silent decay.

Sick people have fancies which seem to nurses mere whim, yet come from some deeper law of the heart. Caroline had such a fancy. On a certain day in the week, at a certain hour, whether she was a shade better or worse, she would beg to be raised, dressed, and placed in her chair near the window. There she would sit, wrapped in her white dressing-gown, her hands thin as winter twigs, her gaze fixed on the world outside with a patience that was itself a kind of pain. No persuasion could make her lie down again until noon had passed. When the church clock tolled twelve, she would yield at once, meek as a child.

Once put back on the couch, she would bury her face deep in the pillow and draw the coverlets round her as if to shut out the day, the sun, the sound of life. More than once, after noon, a slight convulsion shook the bed, and a faint sob broke the hush. Mrs. Pryor noticed all.

One Tuesday morning Caroline sat as usual at the lattice. The room was quiet—quiet in the way a sick-room is quiet, where even footsteps are trained to whisper. Mrs. Pryor sat a little behind, knitting, or seeming to knit. The clicking of the needles was a soft, steady sound, like a small metronome set against the fever’s irregular beat—a domestic music meant to soothe. Outside, summer made its own acoustics: a low murmur of bees in the garden, a far-off cart-wheel on the road, the intermittent call of a bird that repeated its phrase as if it had forgotten what came next.

Caroline’s face changed. A light shot into her eyes; she half rose, leaning forward with sudden eagerness.

Mrs. Pryor moved noiselessly to look over her shoulder. From that window one could see the churchyard, and beyond it the road. A horseman went sharply by, not so far off that long sight could not know him. Mrs. Pryor knew Mr. Moore. Before the figure was lost behind an intercepting rise of ground, the church clock began to strike.

Twelve.

“May I lie down again?” Caroline asked, as if the question had been waiting only for the sound.

Mrs. Pryor helped her back to bed. She drew the curtain, and then stood a moment listening. The couch trembled; the suppressed sob stirred the air.

A contraction as of anguish altered Mrs. Pryor’s face. She wrung her hands once, quickly, as if she could twist away pain. And then she understood what she had not understood before. Tuesday was Whinbury market-day. Mr. Moore must pass the rectory on his way thither, just before noon—always, habitually, as fixed as the clock.

Caroline wore continually round her neck a slender braid of silk with some trinket attached. Mrs. Pryor had seen the bit of gold glimmer now and then, but Caroline never let it out of her keeping. When dressed it lay hidden in her bosom; when in bed she held it in her hand as if it were a charm, or a last thread tying her to earth.

That Tuesday afternoon the day’s fever brought her a doze—less sleep than stupor. The air was hot; even the shaded room held the scent of summer in its fabric, honeysuckle from the garden mingled with the faint, clean smell of linen and the sharper edge of some cooling draught. In restless turning Caroline pushed the coverlets aside. Mrs. Pryor bent to replace them, and saw the small hand lying nerveless on Caroline’s breast, clasping the jealously guarded treasure. The attenuated fingers had relaxed in sleep.

With a gentleness that was nearly reverence, Mrs. Pryor disengaged the braid and drew out a tiny locket, slight and cheap enough to suit Caroline’s small purse. Beneath its crystal face lay a curl of black hair—too short, too crisp to have been cut from a woman’s head.

The silken chain twitched. The sleeper started awake. Her mind, when she woke thus, often came back in fragments, like a bird returning to a familiar bough and not at once knowing it. Half rising, terror in her eyes, she cried out:

“Don’t take it from me, Robert! Don’t! It is my last comfort; let me keep it. I never tell any one whose hair it is; I never show it.”

Mrs. Pryor had already withdrawn behind the curtain. She sat far back in a deep chair by the bedside, hidden. Caroline looked round the chamber; it seemed empty. Her scattered thoughts returned slowly, one by one, exhausted as if each had flown a long distance to reach her. Finding silence, believing herself alone, she did what in health she had never done: she spoke aloud what had been kept locked in the breast.

“Oh, I should see him once more before all is over! Heaven might favour me thus far!” she cried. “God grant me a little comfort before I die!”

Then, the helpless bitterness of it:

“But he will not know I am ill till I am gone, and he will come when they have laid me out, and I am senseless, cold, and stiff.”

And because her mind—keen even in weakness—clutched at every hint the world gave, she turned from longing to that listening which illness sharpens: the ear seeking meaning in the air.

“What can my departed soul feel then?” she went on, breath quick and thin. “Can it see or know what happens to the clay? Can spirits, through any medium, communicate with living flesh? Can the dead at all revisit those they leave? Can they come in the elements? Will wind, water, fire, lend me a path to Moore?

“Is it for nothing the wind sounds almost articulately sometimes—sings as I have lately heard it sing at night—or passes the casement sobbing, as if for sorrow to come? Does nothing, then, haunt it, nothing inspire it?

“Why, it suggested to me words one night; it poured a strain which I could have written down, only I was appalled, and dared not rise to seek pencil and paper by the dim watch-light.”

Her voice fell; then rose again with that feverish curiosity that reaches for explanations like a hand in the dark.

“What is that electricity they speak of, whose changes make us well or ill, whose lack or excess blasts, whose even balance revives? What are all those influences that are about us in the atmosphere, that keep playing over our nerves like fingers on stringed instruments, and call forth now a sweet note, and now a wail—now an exultant swell, and anon the saddest cadence?

“Where is the other world? In what will another life consist? Why do I ask? Have I not cause to think that the hour is hasting but too fast when the veil must be rent for me? Do I not know the Grand Mystery is likely to burst prematurely on me?”

Her tone changed—grew childlike, imploring, as if she spoke to the earliest God she had known.

“Great Spirit, in whose goodness I confide—whom, as my Father, I have petitioned night and morning from early infancy—help the weak creation of Thy hands! Sustain me through the ordeal I dread and must undergo! Give me strength! Give me patience! Give me—oh, give me faith!”

She fell back on the pillow.

Mrs. Pryor found means to steal from the room without being seen. When she returned, she brought with her the composed face of a woman who will not let her own heart hinder the tending of another.

The next day, callers came; news had crept out that Miss Helstone was worse. Mr. Hall and his sister Margaret arrived; both left the sick-room in tears, having found Caroline more altered than they had been prepared to see. Hortense Moore came, brisk and warm in her foreign kindness. Caroline, stirred by her presence, put on a faint show of cheerfulness. She smiled, insisted she was not dangerously ill, spoke in a low voice that tried to carry its old tone. For the length of Hortense’s visit, excitement kept up the colour in her cheeks; she almost looked better.

When Hortense rose to go, Mrs. Pryor asked, keeping her voice steady with effort, “How is Mr. Robert?”

“He was very well when he left.”

“Left?” Mrs. Pryor’s control tightened like a cord. “Is he gone from home?”

Hortense explained: some police intelligence about the rioters whom he pursued had called him away that very morning to Birmingham; perhaps a fortnight might pass before he returned.

“He is not aware that Miss Helstone is very ill?”

“Oh no,” Hortense said. “He thought, like me, she had only a bad cold.”

After that, Mrs. Pryor did not approach Caroline’s couch for more than an hour. She heard her weep, and could not bear to look upon her tears.

As evening came on, she brought tea. Mr. Helstone had made it himself, with a stiff pride in his own skill—strong, clear, and hot, as if correctness could stand in for comfort. Caroline opened her eyes from a moment’s slumber and looked at Mrs. Pryor without recognition.

“I smelt the honeysuckles in the glen this summer morning,” she said, dreamily, “as I stood at the counting-house window.”

Such words, spoken from pallid lips, pierce deeper than any dramatic cry. They carry the brightness of remembered life into the present ruin.

“My darling, do you know me?” Mrs. Pryor asked.

“I went in to call Robert to breakfast. I have been with him in the garden. He asked me to go. A heavy dew has refreshed the flowers. The peaches are ripening.”

“My darling—my darling!” Mrs. Pryor said again, as if the repetition could hold Caroline to herself.

“I thought it was daylight—long after sunrise. It looks dark. Is the moon now set?”

The moon, newly risen, was gazing full and mild into the room—calm, remote, silvering what it touched with a light that made everything seem at once more peaceful and more sorrowful.

“Then it is not morning? I am not at the cottage? Who is this? I see a shape at my bedside.”

“It is myself—your friend—your nurse—your—” Mrs. Pryor checked the word, swallowed it back as if it were a flame. “Lean your head on my shoulder. Collect yourself.” Then, in a lower tone, as if speaking not to Caroline but to Heaven: “O God, take pity! Give her life, and me strength! Send me courage! Teach me words!”

Caroline lay mute a little while in the trembling arms and on the throbbing bosom that held her. The room listened with them: the hush broken only by a faint stir of leaves outside, and the far-off, ceaseless undertone of night insects—a small chorus that went on regardless of human agony, yet seemed, to an overwrought mind, like the world’s own whisper.

“I am better now,” Caroline said at last. “Much better. I feel where I am. This is Mrs. Pryor near me. I was dreaming. I talk when I wake up from dreams; people often do in illness. How fast your heart beats, ma’am! Do not be afraid.”

“It is not fear, child—only a little anxiety. I have brought you some tea, Cary. Your uncle made it himself. You know he says he can make a better cup of tea than any housewife can. Taste it. He is concerned to hear that you eat so little; he would be glad if you had a better appetite.”

“I am thirsty. Let me drink.”

She drank eagerly, as if thirst were the one appetite left to her.

“What o’clock is it, ma’am?”

“Past nine.”

“Not later? Oh! I have yet a long night before me. But the tea has made me strong. I will sit up.”

Mrs. Pryor raised her and arranged the pillows with practised care, making a nest of softness where bones threatened to bruise.

“Thank Heaven,” Caroline said, with a faint return of her old reflective manner, “I am not always equally miserable, and ill, and hopeless. The afternoon has been bad since Hortense went; perhaps the evening may be better. It is a fine night, I think? The moon shines clear.”

“Very fine—a perfect summer night. The old church-tower gleams white almost as silver.”

“And does the churchyard look peaceful?”

“Yes, and the garden also. Dew glistens on the foliage.”

“Can you see many long weeds and nettles amongst the graves? or do they look turfy and flowery?”

“I see closed daisy-heads gleaming like pearls on some mounds. Thomas has mown down the dock-leaves and rank grass, and cleared all away.”

“I always like that to be done,” Caroline murmured. “It soothes one’s mind to see the place in order. And, I dare say, within the church just now that moonlight shines as softly as in my room. It will fall through the east window full on the Helstone monument. When I close my eyes I seem to see poor papa’s epitaph in black letters on the white marble. There is plenty of room for other inscriptions underneath.”

Mrs. Pryor’s throat tightened, but she answered steadily, offering what comfort could be offered—news of small care, small continuity.

“William Farren came to look after your flowers this morning. He was afraid, now you cannot tend them yourself, they would be neglected. He has taken two of your favourite plants home to nurse for you.”

“If I were to make a will,” Caroline said, as if speaking of dishes and linen rather than of her own leaving, “I would leave William all my plants; Shirley my trinkets—except one, which must not be taken off my neck; and you, ma’am, my books.”

After a pause she added, with a sudden longing that was like hunger:

“Mrs. Pryor, I feel a wish for something.”

“For what, Caroline?”

“You know I always delight to hear you sing. Sing me a hymn just now. Sing that hymn which begins—

‘Our God, our help in ages past,
Our hope for years to come,
Our shelter from the stormy blast,
Our refuge, haven, home!’”

Mrs. Pryor sang at once.

Her voice, even in speech, was sweet and clear; in song it became something rarer—pure, unwavering, with a tenderness that seemed to have been distilled from long restraint. The melody filled the room and changed its very air. In the kitchen the servants, hearing it, stole to the stair-foot to listen; the sound, rising through the house, softened boards, walls, and beams, as if it passed through the very grain of the wood.

Even old Helstone, walking in the garden among his borders, stood still. The hymn floated out to him over the dew-wet leaves. He could not have said why it stirred him; why it brought, like a scent carried on the night, the image of a dead wife long buried under his pride. He did not like low spirits. When they came upon him he marched them off as he would have marched off a vagrant. He remembered he had promised to pay Wynne, the magistrate, a visit that evening, and he was glad of the pretext. He quickened his pace, trying to outrun the melody.

Mrs. Pryor sang on, verse after verse, and the words, ancient and English, bore with them a whole weight of generations—language held fast against time’s erasure:

“Thy word commands our flesh to dust,—
‘Return, ye sons of men;’
All nations rose from earth at first,
And turn to earth again…”

When the hymn was ended, Caroline asked for more—like one who has been given a mouthful of warm broth after days of starvation and wants, desperately, another.

“Now sing a song—a Scottish song,” she said. “‘Ye banks and braes o’ bonnie Doon.’”

Mrs. Pryor obeyed, or tried. At the end of the first stanza she stopped. The pathos of the air—its untranslatable sadness, the way its very vowels seemed to carry a country’s mourning—broke her. Her full heart overflowed.

“You are weeping at the pathos of the air,” Caroline said, with a pitying gentleness. “Come here, and I will comfort you.”

Mrs. Pryor came and sat on the edge of the bed. Caroline’s wasted arms encircled her, and she kissed her cheek as if she had become, suddenly, the elder, the protector.

“You often soothe me; let me soothe you,” Caroline murmured. “I hope it is not for me you weep?”

Mrs. Pryor did not answer.

“Do you think I shall not get better?” Caroline asked, and her voice was small. “I do not feel very ill—only weak.”

“But your mind, Caroline—your mind is crushed,” Mrs. Pryor broke out at last. “Your heart is almost broken; you have been so neglected, so repulsed—left so desolate.”

“I believe grief is, and always has been, my worst ailment,” Caroline said simply. “I sometimes think if an abundant gush of happiness came on me I could revive yet.”

“Do you wish to live?”

“I have no object in life.”

The words fell without complaint—only with the tired truth of one who has stopped bargaining.

“You love me, Caroline?” Mrs. Pryor asked, and now there was something in her tone that shook—something urgent, desperate.

“Very much—very truly—inexpressibly sometimes. Just now I feel as if I could almost grow to your heart.”

Mrs. Pryor laid Caroline down again with hands that trembled.

“I will return directly, dear,” she said.

She glided to the door, softly turned the key, and assured herself the lock was fast. Then she came back. She drew the curtain aside to let the moonlight fall more fully over the bed. It lay on Caroline’s face like a pale blessing—showing how sharp the cheekbones had become, how the lashes lay dark against skin too white, how the lips had lost their natural colour.

Mrs. Pryor gazed as if she would fix every feature in memory against the threat of loss. Then she spoke quickly, the words pushing through the barriers of habit and fear with a force that altered her voice.

“Then, if you love me,” she said, “if you feel as if, to use your own words, you could grow to my heart—it will be neither shock nor pain for you to know that that heart is the source whence yours was filled; that from my veins issued the tide which flows in yours; that you are mine—my daughter—my own child.”

Caroline lifted her head a little, bewildered, the world wavering.

“Mrs. Pryor—”

“My own child!”

“That is—that means—you have adopted me?”

“It means that, if I have given you nothing else, I at least gave you life; that I bore you, nursed you; that I am your true mother. No other woman can claim the title; it is mine.”

“But Mrs. James Helstone—my father’s wife, whom I do not remember ever to have seen—she is my mother?”

“She is your mother. James Helstone was my husband. I say you are mine. I have proved it.” Her breath came fast now, as if long-held words were escaping at last. “I thought perhaps you were all his, which would have been a cruel dispensation for me. I find it is not so. God permitted me to be the parent of my child’s mind. It belongs to me; it is my property—my right. These features are James’s own. He had a fine face when he was young, and not altered by error. Papa, my darling, gave you your blue eyes and soft brown hair; he gave you the oval of your face and the regularity of your lineaments—the outside he conferred; but the heart and the brain are mine. The germs are from me, and they are improved—they are developed to excellence. I esteem and approve my child as highly as I do most fondly love her.”

Caroline stared, as if the moonlight had turned to water and she were drowning in it.

“Is what I hear true?” she whispered. “Is it no dream?”

Not always does the spirit, in its bold wrestle with what it cannot see, come off conqueror. There are vigils when the body sits like a spent hearth and the soul is the one coal left, burning, burning, till the sweat gathers black upon the brow; when prayer is no spoken thing, but a dumb pressure of the heart against the Unanswering—“Spare her. Heal her. Do not wrench out of me what long use has twined with every fibre of my being. Be gentle.” And after that struggle, the dawn may break as it used to break—softly, with birds calling from the hedges—only to bring, from lips grown strange and colourless, the sentence that makes the watcher’s sinews slacken.

“Oh! I have had a suffering night. This morning I am worse. I have tried to rise. I cannot. Dreams I am unused to have troubled me.”

Then the chair is drawn nearer to the pillow; the face, familiar as one’s own hand, is seen newly fashioned—as if pain had been a sculptor in the dark; and at once the mind knows, without being told, that the intolerable minute is on the road. One bows, because rebellion is useless; one subdues, because anguish must not become frenzy.

But Mrs. Pryor was not brought to that bowing. She was still praying, still kneeling in the very posture of entreaty, unconscious that the August sun had climbed above the hills, when the child in her arms woke softly—woke as one returns from a wholesome sleep, not as one drifts back from some far, dim border.

No piteous moan went before her waking—no sound that eats into the nerve till even a sworn courage dissolves in tears. No hollow pause of stupor followed. Caroline’s first look, though languid, was clear; she remembered.

“Mamma, I have slept so well. I only dreamed and woke twice.”

Mrs. Pryor rose with a start and turned her face away, that Caroline might not see the sudden, treacherous gladness gathering in her eyes at that word “mamma,” and at the calm assurance that came after it. For days—and then for more days—she dared not rejoice except with trembling. The first revivals were like the flutter of a lamp that has sunk low: it would flare for a moment, and then, as quickly, the flame would crouch down again, faint in its socket. Close upon hope came exhaustion. Caroline had always that touching desire to look better than she was, to lift herself as if will could do the work of strength; too often ability refused its service. The brave little attempts to eat, to talk, to seem cheerful, failed. Many an hour passed in which the mother feared the cords of life could never be tightened again, though their snapping might be delayed.

And all this time the neighbourhood seemed deserted, as if the very village had taken holiday from sorrow. It was the close of August; the weather was fine—fine in the way that means pitilessly dry, dust on every leaf, dust on every step, and a breath from the east that was no wholesome wind but a parching exhalation, like something blown out of far deserts. The sky held no cloud; only a pale, fixed haze, as if the air were full of powder, stole all depth from heaven’s blue, all freshness from earth’s green, all glow from day. The ear, too, suffered. There was no kindly, rustling, variable music in that stillness; the world sounded wrong—crisp, strained, over-bright. The gable of the rectory heard, night and day, the same thin sobbing at its eastern edge; the oriel of the church took it up and echoed it. Even the domestic noises—the pail set down, the latch lifted—rang as if the air were hard.

Almost every family in Briarfield was absent on some excursion. Miss Keeldar and her friends were away at the sea; so were Mrs. Yorke’s household. Mr. Hall and Louis Moore—between whom a spontaneous intimacy had sprung up, as if their minds had found each other by scent—were gone “up north,” on a pedestrian jaunt to the Lakes. Hortense, who would willingly have remained and lent her quick, capable hands to the nursing, had been entreated so earnestly by Miss Mann to accompany her once more to Wormwood Wells—hoping to ease sufferings sharpened by the unwholesome weather—that she could not refuse. It was in her nature to yield when goodness was appealed to, and when the confession of dependence stroked, too, her amour propre. Robert had gone from Birmingham to London, and still stayed there.

So long as that east wind—breath like hot, dry iron—continued to parch Caroline’s lips and fever her veins, the body could not keep pace with the mind’s returning composure. But at last a day came when the wind ceased to worry the house. A small cloud—no bigger, at first, than a man’s hand—rose in the west; gusts came after it, driving it on, spreading it wide. Rain fell; there was a short reign of wet and tempest, the sound of it on panes and leaves like a benediction after that cruel dryness. Then, when the storm had spent itself, the sun broke out with a geniality that felt like kindness; heaven recovered its true azure, earth her honest green. The livid cholera-tint vanished from nature’s face; the hills stood up clear around the horizon, absolved from the pale malaria-haze.

And with that west wind—soft as water, fresh as a new-washed linen sheet hung out to dry—something in Caroline yielded and turned. The lattice was kept ever open; it let in not only air, but the right kind of sound: the full leaf-rustle, the deeper murmur of distant trees, the wholesome stir of life moving without strain. Youth could help her now, and so could her mother’s care; both, crowned by God’s blessing, rekindled the energies that had long languished like a fire starved of fuel.

At last Mrs. Pryor saw that it was permitted to hope. A true, material convalescence had begun. It was not only that Caroline’s smile came readier, or her spirits rose for an hour; a certain look had left her face and eye—a look dreadful and indescribable, yet easily remembered by those who have watched beside dangerous sickness. Long before the wasted lines filled out, or the lost colour returned, a more subtle change passed over her: all grew softer, warmer, more human. Instead of what had seemed a marble mask and glassy eye, there lay on the pillow a face still pale, still thin—perhaps more haggard than that awful smoothness—but less terrible, because it was a sick, living girl, not a rigid piece of statuary.

Now, too, she was not for ever pleading to drink. The plaint, “I am so thirsty,” ceased to be her constant cry. Sometimes, when she had swallowed a morsel, she would say it had revived her; and those simple words were like meat and wine to the watcher’s heart. All food was no longer equally odious. She could, at times, be induced to name a preference—faintly, as if the very act of choosing were new.

With what trembling pleasure, with what anxious household skill, did Mrs. Pryor prepare what was selected! The smallest details became sacred rites. A cup was warmed so that no chill should offend a tender mouth; a spoon was dipped and wiped, dipped and wiped again, that nothing metallic should mingle with the taste; broth was skimmed till it shone clear as amber, then salted by grains, not by pinches, until it met the palate without startling it. Toast was dried, not scorched, and the crust rubbed with a touch of fresh butter that melted at once and breathed out its nutty scent. When a little custard could be borne, it was strained till it was silk—no grain, no lump—just eggs and milk coaxed over a gentle heat, lifted at the exact moment before they turned, perfumed with the faintest drop of vanilla or a shaving of lemon-peel. The kitchen itself seemed to listen; the clatter of pans was hushed; even the servants’ steps became cautious, as if sound might bruise.

Mrs. Pryor watched Caroline partake as if each spoonful were a verdict. Nourishment brought strength. Caroline could sit up; and then she began to long—not for excitement, but for air, for her flowers, for the honest sight of what had been growing while she lay so near the edge of not seeing anything again. She asked how the fruit had ripened. She asked if the late roses had held out through the east wind’s tyranny.

Her uncle, always liberal, bought a garden-chair for her express use. He carried her down in his own arms—careful, proud, tender in that rough, decisive way of his—and placed her in it himself. William Farren was there to wheel her along the walks, to show her what he had been doing among her plants, to take her directions for further work.

Caroline and William found abundance to say to each other, though their topics were such as would have seemed nothing to most people. They had, in truth, a dozen interests in common—small, minute, domestic interests—yet full, to them, of life. They watched creatures; they observed habits; they took pains not to trample what was little. They held alike those quiet doctrines about humanity to the lower creation which are not preached loudly, but practised steadily.

Under an old cherry-tree, some ground-bees had burrowed in the turf: their nest, their comings and goings, their patient industry, formed one subject. The haunts of certain hedge-sparrows, the welfare of certain pearly eggs and callow fledglings, formed another. Their world, when they spoke thus, had its own soundscape: the bees’ low, purposeful hum under the cherry shade; the sparrows’ small, anxious chatter in the hedge; the soft grit of the garden-path under the chair’s wheels; the west wind’s continual, living accompaniment. It was a music that soothed, not dazzled—a music no manufactured art could rival, and which no industrial clamour had yet learned to drown in Briarfield.

Had Chambers’s Journal existed then, it would certainly have been Miss Helstone’s and Farren’s chosen periodical. She would have subscribed; he would have borrowed each number in turn; both would have trusted the marvellous anecdotes of animal sagacity and found real savour in them, as people do who still possess the taste for wonder in common things. Let the digression stand; it explains why Caroline would have no other hand than William’s to guide her chair, and why his company, his plain talk, sufficed to make her garden airings not merely endurable but pleasant.

Mrs. Pryor, walking near, wondered—half in perplexity, half in a hidden disapprobation—how her daughter could be at such ease with “a man of the people.” She found it impossible to address him otherwise than stiffly. Between her caste and his she felt a gulf; to step across it, or meet him half-way, seemed to her like self-abasement. One day she asked Caroline, gently, as if giving counsel for safety—

“Are you not afraid, my dear, to converse with that person so unreservedly? He may presume, and become troublesomely garrulous.”

“William presume, mamma? You don’t know him. He never presumes. He is altogether too proud and sensitive to do so. William has very fine feelings.”

Mrs. Pryor smiled—sceptical, almost indulgent—at the naïve idea of that rough-handed, fustian-clad clown having “fine feelings.” But William, on his side, showed her only a sulky brow. He knew when he was misjudged. With those who failed to give him his due, he was apt to become unmanageable; and his silence had a kind of resentful weight.

Evening restored Caroline wholly to her mother, and Mrs. Pryor liked the evening. Then, when daylight softened, when the birds settled and the household sounds sank to a gentler pitch, no human shadow came between her and what she loved. In the day she had still her stiff demeanour and her cold moments, as was her habit. Between her and Mr. Helstone a respectful but rigidly ceremonious intercourse was maintained. Familiarity would have bred contempt at once in one or both; but strict civility and well-kept distance made their relations smooth.

To the servants Mrs. Pryor’s manner was not openly uncourteous, yet shy, freezing, ungenial. Perhaps it was diffidence, not pride, that made her look so haughty; but Fanny and Eliza did not trouble themselves to distinguish. She felt the effect; and it rendered her, at times, dissatisfied with herself for faults she could not help, and, dissatisfied with all else, dejected, chill, taciturn.

This mood changed under Caroline’s influence—and under that influence only. The dependent fondness of her nursling, the natural affection of her child, came over her like warmth over frost. Her rigidity unbent. She smiled; she yielded; she grew pliant. Caroline did not love with loud protestations—that would have ill suited Mrs. Pryor, who would have read wordiness as insincerity. Instead, Caroline hung on her with an easy dependence; she confided in her with fearless reliance. Those quiet claims—those simple, unquestioning trusts—contented the mother’s heart.

She liked to hear, in that voice now daily growing clearer, “Mamma, do this;” “Please, mamma, fetch me that;” “Mamma, read to me;” “Sing a little, mamma.”

No one—no living creature—had ever so claimed her services, so looked for help at her hand. Other people were always, with her, reserved and stiff, as she was reserved and stiff with them; other people betrayed consciousness of her weak points and annoyance at them. Caroline showed no such wounding sagacity now than she had shown when she was a suckling of three months old.

Yet Caroline could find fault. Blind to constitutional defects that were incurable, she was sharp-eyed for acquired habits that might be mended; and on these she would lecture, artlessly, with a young girl’s frank authority. Instead of being hurt, Mrs. Pryor felt a secret pleasure in it: the pleasure of discovering that the child dared to chide her—was so much at home.

“Mamma, I am determined you shall not wear that old gown any more. Its fashion is not becoming; it is too strait in the skirt. You shall put on your black silk every afternoon. In that you look nice; it suits you. And you shall have a black satin dress for Sundays—a real satin, not a satinet or any of the shams. And, mamma, when you get the new one, mind you must wear it.”

“My dear, I thought of the black silk serving me as a best dress for many years yet, and I wished to buy you several things.”

“Nonsense, mamma. My uncle gives me cash to get what I want. You know he is generous enough; and I have set my heart on seeing you in a black satin. Get it soon, and let it be made by a dressmaker of my recommending. Let me choose the pattern. You always want to disguise yourself like a grandmother. You would persuade one that you are old and ugly. Not at all! On the contrary, when well dressed and cheerful you are very comely indeed; your smile is so pleasant, your teeth are so white, your hair is still such a pretty light colour. And then you speak like a young lady, with such a clear, fine tone, and you sing better than any young lady I ever heard. Why do you wear such dresses and bonnets, mamma—such as nobody else ever wears?”

“Does it annoy you, Caroline?”

“Very much; it vexes me even. People say you are miserly; and yet you are not, for you give liberally to the poor and to religious societies—though your gifts are conveyed so secretly and quietly that they are known to few except the receivers. But I will be your lady’s-maid myself. When I get a little stronger I will set to work, and you must be good, mamma, and do as I bid you.”

And, sitting near, she rearranged her mother’s muslin handkerchief, resmoothed her hair with fingers that had lately been so weak they could scarcely lift a spoon. Then she went on, as if the thought of their relationship pleased her palate like sweetness—

“My own mamma—who belongs to me, and to whom I belong! I am a rich girl now. I have something I can love well, and not be afraid of loving. Mamma, who gave you this little brooch? Let me unpin it and look at it.”

Mrs. Pryor, who usually shrank from meddling fingers and too near an approach, let her have this licence with complacency.

“Did papa give you this, mamma?”

“My sister gave it me—my only sister, Cary. Would that your Aunt Caroline had lived to see her niece!”

“Have you nothing of papa’s—no trinket, no gift of his?”

“I have one thing.”

“That you prize?”

“That I prize.”

“Valuable and pretty?”

“Invaluable and sweet to me.”

“Show it, mamma. Is it here or at Fieldhead?”

“It is talking to me now, leaning on me. Its arms are round me.”

“Ah, mamma, you mean your teasing daughter, who will never let you alone; who, when you go into your room, cannot help running to seek for you; who follows you upstairs and down, like a dog.”

“Whose features still give me such a strange thrill sometimes. I half fear your fair looks yet, child.”

“You don’t; you can’t. Mamma, I am sorry papa was not good. I do so wish he had been. Wickedness spoils and poisons all pleasant things. It kills love. If you and I thought each other wicked, we could not love each other, could we?”

“And if we could not trust each other, Cary?”

“How miserable we should be! Mother, before I knew you I had an apprehension that you were not good—that I could not esteem you. That dread damped my wish to see you. And now my heart is elate because I find you perfect—almost; kind, clever, nice. Your sole fault is that you are old-fashioned, and of that I shall cure you. Mamma, put your work down; read to me. I like your southern accent; it is so pure, so soft. It has no rugged burr, no nasal twang, such as almost every one’s voice here in the north has. My uncle and Mr. Hall say that you are a fine reader, mamma. Mr. Hall said he never heard any lady read with such propriety of expression or purity of accent.”

“I wish I could reciprocate the compliment, Cary; but, really, the first time I heard your truly excellent friend read and preach I could not understand his broad northern tongue.”

“Could you understand me, mamma? Did I seem to speak roughly?”

“No. I almost wished you had, as I wished you had looked unpolished. Your father, Caroline, naturally spoke well, quite otherwise than your worthy uncle—correctly, gently, smoothly. You inherit the gift.”

“Poor papa! When he was so agreeable, why was he not good?”

“Why he was as he was—and happily of that you, child, can form no conception—I cannot tell. It is a deep mystery. The key is in the hands of his Maker. There I leave it.”

“Mamma, you will keep stitching, stitching away. Put down the sewing; I am an enemy to it. It cumbers your lap, and I want it for my head; it engages your eyes, and I want them for a book. Here is your favourite—Cowper.”
