These importunities were the mother’s pleasure. If she ever delayed compliance, it was only to hear them repeated, and to enjoy the soft, half-playful, half-petulant urgency with which Caroline insisted. And then, when she yielded, Caroline would say, with an arch little air of self-knowledge—

“You will spoil me, mamma. I always thought I should like to be spoiled, and I find it very sweet.”

So did Mrs. Pryor.

By the time the carriage rolled back from Fieldhead to Briarfield, Caroline had come up out of her illness as bread rises after the first anxious watching—still delicate, still needing warmth, but with life again in the centre of her. The news of her convalescence had reached Shirley by post; and Shirley, once home, let scarcely an hour stand between her and the rectory door.

Rain was falling—not in gusts, but in a steady, fine descent that made every late blossom hold its perfume closer, and sent the wet smell of turned earth and bruised box edging into the house. The garden wicket swung; the sound cut through the quiet like the clean click of a latch in a still kitchen. A moment after, Shirley’s figure crossed the window, and then she was in the room.

When she was deeply moved, she did not pour herself out in words. Strong feeling, with her, went first to the arms and the eyes, and only afterwards—if at all—to the tongue. She gathered Caroline up, held her as if to make sure she was truly there, and gave one swift look that seemed to take account of every change in the thin face; then one kiss.

“You are better.”

A minute later, as if her heart had spoken behind her teeth and now must be obeyed: “I see you are safe now; but take care. God grant your health may be called on to sustain no more shocks!”

Then, as though she had pressed down the weight within her, she began talking—quickly, brightly—of her journey, of roads and inns and relatives. Yet all the while, her glance slipped back and back to Caroline, measuring the frailty that still clung about her like a light shawl. Solicitude shone in it—trouble too, and a kind of amazement, as if she could not reconcile Caroline’s presence with the peril she had passed.

Suddenly that glance turned and fixed on Mrs. Pryor. It was not an idle look; it pierced, it searched.

“When will my governess return to me?” Shirley asked.

Caroline, at that, drew in breath—then turned to her mother with a look that asked leave. Mrs. Pryor’s permission was given without speech; a small gesture, quiet and resigned.

So Shirley was told. The secret—so long guarded, so long suffered—was spoken in her hearing. Mrs. Pryor, Caroline’s mother: the bond confessed at last.

Shirley’s comment came coolly, as if she were passing judgment on a point already settled in her mind. “Very good—very good! But it is no news to me.”

Caroline stared. “What! did you know?”

“I guessed long since the whole business. I have heard somewhat of Mrs. Pryor’s history—not from herself, but from others. With every detail of Mr. James Helstone’s career and character I was acquainted. An afternoon’s sitting and conversation with Miss Mann had rendered me familiar therewith; also he is one of Mrs. Yorke’s warning examples—one of the blood-red lights she hangs out to scare young ladies from matrimony.” Shirley’s voice sharpened a little, as if amused and offended together. “I believe I should have been sceptical about the truth of the portrait traced by such fingers—both these ladies take a dark pleasure in offering to view the dark side of life—but I questioned Mr. Yorke on the subject, and he said, ‘Shirley, my woman, if you want to know aught about yond’ James Helstone, I can only say he was a man-tiger. He was handsome, dissolute, soft, treacherous, courteous, cruel—’” Her eye flicked to Caroline’s face. “Don’t cry, Cary; we’ll say no more about it.”

“I am not crying, Shirley; or if I am, it is nothing. Go on; you are no friend if you withhold from me the truth. I hate that false plan of disguising, mutilating the truth.”

“Fortunately I have said pretty nearly all that I have to say,” Shirley returned; “except that your uncle himself confirmed Mr. Yorke’s words; for he too scorns a lie, and deals in none of those conventional subterfuges that are shabbier than lies.”

“But papa is dead; they should let him alone now.”

“They should; and we will let him alone.” And then, as if she could not help laying her hand, with a strange tenderness, on the very thing Caroline feared, she added: “Cry away, Cary; it will do you good. It is wrong to check natural tears. Besides, I choose to please myself by sharing an idea that at this moment beams in your mother’s eye while she looks at you. Every drop blots out a sin. Weep! your tears have the virtue which the rivers of Damascus lacked. Like Jordan, they can cleanse a leprous memory.”

Then she turned to Mrs. Pryor with that frank, fearless directness which could be kindness when it pleased, and ruthlessness when it must.

“Madam,” she said, “did you think I could be daily in the habit of seeing you and your daughter together—marking your marvellous similarity in many points, observing (pardon me) your irrepressible emotions in the presence and still more in the absence of your child—and not form my own conjectures? I formed them, and they are literally correct. I shall begin to think myself shrewd.”

“And you said nothing?” Caroline asked, when she had mastered herself again. Her voice had steadied, though it was still low, as if tears had salted it.

“Nothing. I had no warrant to breathe a word on the subject. My business it was not; I abstained from making it such.”

“You guessed so deep a secret, and did not hint that you guessed it?”

“Is that so difficult?”

“It is not like you.”

“How do you know?”

“You are not reserved; you are frankly communicative.”

“I may be communicative, yet know where to stop.” Shirley’s smile came, sudden and half veiled. “In showing my treasure I may withhold a gem or two—a curious, unbought graven stone—an amulet of whose mystic glitter I rarely permit even myself a glimpse. Good-day.”

That speech—light, strange, and final—left Caroline with a new understanding of her friend, as if a door had swung open on a chamber she had not known existed.

Soon Caroline could bear the change of scene and the small stir of company; as soon as she could, Shirley claimed her. Day by day she sued for Caroline’s presence at Fieldhead with an eagerness that was almost hunger. Whether she had grown weary of her honoured relatives she did not say; she only held Caroline fast, as if one warm, familiar soul in the house made all the difference between being at home and being merely lodged.

The Sympsons were church people—methodical as a parish calendar, punctual as the tolling bell that ordered their weeks. The rector’s niece was received with courtesy, of course; with them, courtesy was a rule like saying grace. Mr. Sympson was a man of spotless respectability, worrying temper, pious principles, and worldly views. His lady was truly good—patient, kind, well-bred—but her mind had been reared on a narrow diet. It was as though someone had set her a table of bitter herbs and then commanded her to call it feast: excellent principles pressed into a stiff crust of bigotry—wholesome, perhaps, but hard to digest—and no seasoning of imagination, no free savour of doubt or laughter. She bore it without complaint, as she bore everything.

The daughters were patterns—tall, with Roman noses, and with lives ruled as if by an invisible metronome. Their education had been faultless; all they did was well done. They had read history and solid books, and possessed principles and opinions that seemed to them beyond mending. They had learned by heart a schoolroom code for young ladies—laws of language, demeanour, propriety—and they never deviated. In others they watched for deviation with a secret, whispered horror. The Abomination of Desolation, to them, was no mystery; they had found it and named it: Originality. Where they saw its trace—in look, in word, in deed; where they heard fresh, expressive language instead of the hackneyed phrases they trusted—they recoiled, as if something wild had cried in the fields at night. Being unintelligible to them, it must be bad; therefore it must be denounced and chained.

Henry Sympson, their only son and youngest child, was fifteen. He kept mostly with his tutor; and when he escaped him, he sought Shirley. He was little, lame, and pale; his large eyes, set in a wan orbit, were often dim, yet capable of sudden blaze when feeling lit them from within. His mother loved him with a tender, almost solemn partiality, believing his peculiarities to be marks of election—a new Samuel, called from birth. Mr. Sympson and the Misses Sympson, not understanding him, left him alone. Shirley made him her pet; he made Shirley his playmate.

And in the midst of this family circle—or rather, always just outside it—moved the tutor: Louis Moore.

He was a satellite of the house of Sympson—connected, yet apart; always attending, always distant. Each member of that correct household treated him with proper dignity. The father was austerely civil and sometimes irritable; the mother was attentive, but formal; the daughters regarded him as an abstraction, not a man. They were learned, so was he—but not for them; they were accomplished, he had talents too, and these were imperceptible to their senses. His spirited sketches were blanks to their eyes. Original observations fell unheard on their ears. Their propriety was unassailable.

Caroline, watching, was astonished to discover that her cousin had no sympathizing friend at Fieldhead; that to Miss Keeldar he was as much a mere teacher, as little a gentleman, as little a man, as to the Misses Sympson. Shirley was not openly haughty; but she never noticed him. He came and went; spoke or was silent; and she rarely acknowledged his existence.

As for Louis Moore, he carried himself like one long used to such life, and determined to bear it for a time. His faculties seemed walled up—captive, and quiet in their captivity. He never laughed; he seldom smiled; he complained of nothing. He fulfilled his duties scrupulously. His pupil loved him; from the rest he asked only civility—and, it seemed, would accept nothing more in that house. When Caroline made gentle overtures of friendship, he did not encourage them; he avoided rather than sought her.

Only one living creature besides his pale, crippled scholar did he fondle at Fieldhead: the great ruffianly Tartar. Sullen and impracticable to others, the dog showed a singular partiality for the taciturn tutor. Sometimes, when Moore—summoned to a meal—entered a room and sat down unwelcomed, Tartar would rise from his lair at Shirley’s feet and betake himself to Louis’s side, as if choosing the hand that understood him. Once—only once—Shirley noticed the desertion. She held out her white hand, spoke softly, tried to coax him back. Tartar looked, slavered, sighed, but ignored her invitation and coolly settled himself by Louis. Louis drew the big black-muzzled head to his knee, patted him, and smiled one small smile to himself.

An acute observer might have remarked that, later the same evening, after Tartar had resumed his allegiance to Shirley and lay again by her footstool, the audacious tutor by one word and gesture fascinated him back. The dog pricked his ears at the word, started erect at the gesture, and came with head lovingly depressed to receive the expected caress. As it was given, that significant, quiet smile rippled again across Louis Moore’s face.

One day, when Caroline and Shirley sat alone in the summer-house—green gloom all around, and the smell of wet leaves coming up as if from a cool cellar—Caroline said, as if lightly, “Shirley, did you know that my cousin Louis was tutor in your uncle’s family before the Sympsons came down here?”

Shirley’s answer did not spring out as her answers usually did. It came after a pause. “Yes—of course; I knew it well.”

“I thought you must have been aware of the circumstance.”

“Well! what then?”

“It puzzles me to guess how it chanced that you never mentioned it to me.”

“Why should it puzzle you?”

“It seems odd. I cannot account for it. You talk a great deal—you talk freely. How was that circumstance never touched on?”

“Because it never was,” said Shirley, and laughed.

“You are a singular being! I thought I knew you quite well; I begin to find myself mistaken. You were silent as the grave about Mrs. Pryor, and now again here is another secret. But why you made it a secret is the mystery to me.”

“I never made it a secret; I had no reason for so doing. If you had asked me who Henry’s tutor was, I would have told you. Besides, I thought you knew.”

Caroline hesitated, then spoke what had been troubling her. “I am puzzled about more things than one in this matter. You don’t like poor Louis. Why? Are you impatient at what you perhaps consider his servile position? Do you wish that Robert’s brother were more highly placed?”

“Robert’s brother, indeed!” Shirley’s exclamation came sharp—scornful; and with a movement of proud impatience she snatched a rose from a branch that pushed its way in through the lattice. The rose, wet and late, gave off that faint, bruised sweetness autumn roses have, half perfume, half decay.

“Yes,” Caroline repeated, mild but firm. “Robert’s brother. He is thus closely related to Gérard Moore of the Hollow, though nature has not given him features so handsome or an air so noble as his kinsman; but his blood is as good, and he is as much a gentleman were he free.”

“Wise, humble, pious Caroline!” Shirley cried, with irony. “Men and angels, hear her! We should not despise plain features, nor a laborious yet honest occupation, should we?” She pointed through an opening in the creepers. “Look at the subject of your panegyric. He is there in the garden.”

Louis Moore was indeed coming slowly down the walk.

“He is not ugly, Shirley,” Caroline pleaded. “He is not ignoble. He is sad; silence seals his mind. But I believe him to be intelligent; and be certain, if he had not something very commendable in his disposition, Mr. Hall would never seek his society as he does.”

Shirley laughed—once, twice—each time with a slightly sarcastic sound. “Well, well. On the plea of the man being Cyril Hall’s friend and Robert Moore’s brother, we’ll just tolerate his existence; won’t we, Cary? You believe him to be intelligent, do you? Not quite an idiot—eh? Something commendable in his disposition!—id est, not an absolute ruffian. Good! Your representations have weight with me; and to prove that they have, should he come this way I will speak to him.”

He approached the summer-house. Unaware it was occupied, he sat down on the step. Tartar, now his customary companion, followed and lay across his feet, a warm, heavy presence.

“Old boy!” Louis said, pulling the tawny ear—or rather the torn remains of it, chewed in a hundred battles—“the autumn sun shines as pleasantly on us as on the fairest and richest. This garden is none of ours, but we enjoy its greenness and perfume, don’t we?”

He sat silent then, still caressing the dog. In the trees around, a faint twitter began—thin, quick notes threading through the damp hush. Something fluttered down as light as leaves. Little birds landed on the sward at a shy distance, hopping as if expectant.

“The small brown elves actually remember that I fed them the other day,” Louis soliloquized. “They want some more biscuit. To-day I forgot to save a fragment. Eager little sprites, I have not a crumb for you.”

He put his hand in his pocket and drew it out empty.

“A want easily supplied,” whispered Shirley, who had been listening.

From her reticule—never, it seemed, without some domestic provision, as if she carried a larder’s corner with her—she took a morsel of sweet-cake. It was the kind made in country houses for visitors and children: close-crumbed, faintly spiced, smelling of butter and sugar rather than of any grand confectioner’s art. She broke it carefully, crumbling it fine between her fingers, and bent over Louis’s shoulder to drop the crumbs into his hand.

“There,” she said. “There is a providence for the improvident.”

Louis, not in the least discomposed, scattered the crumbs over the grass with a calm, practised motion; the birds darted nearer, their small claws whispering in the wet. “This September afternoon is pleasant,” he observed.

“Even for you?”

“As pleasant for me as for any monarch.”

“You take a sort of harsh, solitary triumph in drawing pleasure out of the elements and the inanimate and lower animate creation.”

“Solitary, but not harsh. With animals I feel I am Adam’s son, the heir of him to whom dominion was given over ‘every living thing that moveth upon the earth.’ Your dog likes and follows me. When I go into that yard, the pigeons from your dovecot flutter at my feet. Your mare in the stable knows me as well as it knows you, and obeys me better.”

“And my roses smell sweet to you, and my trees give you shade.”

“And,” Louis continued, his voice quiet but edged with something that held pride and hurt in one, “no caprice can withdraw these pleasures from me; they are mine.”

Then he rose and walked away. Tartar followed him as if bound in affection and duty. Shirley remained standing on the summer-house step, looking after him; her face was pale, as if pride had been wounded inwardly.

“You see,” Caroline remarked, almost apologetically, “his feelings are so often hurt it makes him morose.”

“You see,” Shirley retorted, with sudden ire, “he is a topic on which you and I shall quarrel if we discuss it often; so drop it henceforward and for ever.”

Caroline said no more, but thought: he has more than once spoken thus, and it has made Shirley distant to him. Yet she wondered Shirley could not make allowance for character and circumstance—wondered that the general modesty, manliness, sincerity in Louis’s nature did not plead for him. Shirley was not often so inconsiderate, so irritable.

Two testimonies soon strengthened Caroline’s favourable view. William Farren, whose cottage Louis had visited with Mr. Hall, pronounced him “a real gentleman;” there was not such another in Briarfield. William “could do aught for that man.” And then—“to see how t’ bairns liked him, and how t’ wife took to him first minute she saw him. He never went into a house but t’ childer wor about him directly. Them little things wor like as if they’d a keener sense nor grown-up folks i’ finding our folk’s natures.”

Mr. Hall, asked what he thought of Louis Moore, replied promptly that he was the best fellow he had met with since leaving Cambridge.

“But he is so grave,” Caroline objected.

“Grave! the finest company in the world! Full of odd, quiet, out-of-the-way humour. Never enjoyed an excursion so much in my life as the one I took with him to the Lakes. His understanding and tastes are so superior, it does a man good to be within their influence; and as to his temper and nature, I call them fine.”

“At Fieldhead he looks gloomy, and, I believe, has the character of being misanthropical.”

“Oh! I fancy he is rather out of place there—in a false position. The Sympsons are most estimable people, but not the folks to comprehend him. They think a great deal about form and ceremony, which are quite out of Louis’s way.”

“I don’t think Miss Keeldar likes him.”

“She doesn’t know him—she doesn’t know him; otherwise she has sense enough to do justice to his merits.”

Well, Caroline thought, perhaps she doesn’t know him. That simple explanation served for a little while—until it could not.

One day Caroline happened to be in the schoolroom with Henry Sympson, whose affectionate, amiable temper had quickly recommended him. The boy was busy with some mechanical contrivance; his lameness made him fond of work done sitting—things made with hand and thought, when the body could not run. He began ransacking his tutor’s desk for wax or twine. Moore was absent; Mr. Hall had called for him to take a long walk. Henry searched compartment after compartment, growing impatient; at last he opened an inner drawer and came upon—not twine or beeswax—but a small bundle of marble-coloured cahiers, tied with tape.

Henry looked at them and made a face. “What rubbish Mr. Moore stores up in his desk!” he said. “I hope he won’t keep my old exercises so carefully.”

“What is it?” Caroline asked.

“Old copy-books.” And he tossed the neat packet to her.

The outside was so careful—tape smooth, covers clean—that curiosity stirred. She felt almost as if the very tidiness invited respect. “If they are only copy-books, I suppose I may open them?”

“Oh yes, quite freely. Mr. Moore’s desk is half mine—for he lets me keep all sorts of things in it—and I give you leave.”

She untied the tape. The books proved to be French compositions, written in a hand peculiar but compact, wonderfully clean and clear—each line laid down as if with a steady, exacting ear for order, as though the words themselves had to keep time. Caroline recognized the writing; yet she scarcely needed that evidence, for at the close of each theme a name was signed.

It was the name that astonished her: “Shirley Keeldar, Sympson Grove, ——shire,” and dates four years back.

Caroline retied the bundle and held it, meditating. A half shame came upon her, as if she had opened not mere schoolwork but a chamber in someone else’s memory.

“They are Shirley’s, you see,” Henry said carelessly.

“Did you give them to Mr. Moore? She wrote them with Mrs. Pryor, I suppose?”

“She wrote them in my schoolroom at Sympson Grove, when she lived with us there. Mr. Moore taught her French; it is his native language.”

“I know. Was she a good pupil, Henry?”

“She was a wild, laughing thing, but pleasant to have in the room. She made lesson-time charming. She learned fast—you could hardly tell when or how. French was nothing to her. She spoke it quick, quick—as quick as Mr. Moore himself.”

“Was she obedient? Did she give trouble?”

“She gave plenty of trouble, in a way. She was giddy, but I liked her. I’m desperately fond of Shirley.”

“Desperately fond—you small simpleton! You don’t know what you say.”

“I am desperately fond of her. She is the light of my eyes. I said so to Mr. Moore last night.”

“He would reprove you for speaking with exaggeration.”

“He didn’t. He never reproves and reproves, as girls’ governesses do. He was reading, and he only smiled into his book, and said that if Miss Keeldar was no more than that, she was less than he took her to be; for I was but a dim-eyed, short-sighted little chap.” Henry’s voice sank, and his quick pride had a plaintive undertone. “I’m afraid I am a poor unfortunate, Miss Caroline Helstone. I am a cripple, you know.”

“Never mind, Henry; you are a very nice little fellow; and if God has not given you health and strength, He has given you a good disposition and an excellent heart and brain.”

“I shall be despised. I sometimes think both Shirley and you despise me.”

“Listen, Henry. Generally, I don’t like schoolboys. I have a great horror of them. They seem to me little ruffians, who take an unnatural delight in killing and tormenting birds, and insects, and kittens, and whatever is weaker than themselves. But you are so different I am quite fond of you. You have almost as much sense as a man (far more, God wot,” she added inwardly, bitterly and softly, “than many men); “you are fond of reading, and you can talk sensibly about what you read.”

“I am fond of reading. I know I have sense, and I know I have feeling.”

At that moment Miss Keeldar entered.

“Henry,” she said, “I have brought your lunch here. I shall prepare it for you myself.”

She set upon the table a glass of new milk—so fresh it seemed still to carry the cool, animal sweetness of the byre—beside a plate of something that looked not unlike leather, and a utensil resembling a toasting-fork.

“What are you two about,” she continued, “ransacking Mr. Moore’s desk?”

“Looking at your old copy-books,” Caroline replied.

“My old copy-books?”

“French exercise-books. Look here! They must be held precious; they are kept carefully.”

She held out the tied bundle. Shirley snatched it up as if the touch of it stung. “Did not know one was in existence,” she said. “I thought the whole lot had long since lit the kitchen fire, or curled the maid’s hair at Sympson Grove.” Then, turning sharply on Henry: “What made you keep them?”

“It is not my doing. I should not have thought of it. It never entered my head to suppose copy-books of value. Mr. Moore put them by in the inner drawer of his desk. Perhaps he forgot them.”

“C’est cela. He forgot them, no doubt,” Shirley echoed; and then, smoothing the covers with a hand that tried to look indifferent, she added, with a complacency that seemed to mask something else: “They are extremely well written.”

Miss Keeldar and her uncle had never found the same tune, though they lived beneath one roof and ate from the same table. His temper was quick, his will absolute; he liked the world as it stood—rank in its place, money in its strong-box, women dutiful and compliant. Shirley’s spirit, fed on freedom like a bright fire on dry wood, would not be banked at another’s bidding. He called her romantic as if it were a fault; she thought him worldly as if it were a want.

He had not come down into Yorkshire for idle visiting. He had come with a purpose sharp as the carving-knife laid beside a roast: to portion out his niece’s fate, to settle her, to hand her over to what he named a “proper” husband, and then—his conscience satisfied—wash his hands of her future.

The mischief was that, from Shirley’s cradle onward, she and Mr. Sympson had quarrelled over the very taste of certain words. “Suitable,” to him, meant money, lineage, unencumbered estate—meat weighed and paid for, set down on the platter with a flourish. “Suitable,” to Shirley, meant something rarer and more perilous: esteem, admiration, the inward consent that makes a vow more than a sound. It was the old dispute, brought at last to the great trial.

Mr. Wynne proposed, in due form, for his son—Samuel Fawthrop Wynne.

“Decidedly suitable! most proper!” pronounced Mr. Sympson, as if he were signing a bargain for a field. “A fine unencumbered estate, real substance, good connections. It must be done!”

He sent for her to the oak parlour and shut the door upon them, as if to keep out all airy notions. The room smelt of old timber and extinguished fires; the great table, dark and polished, looked like something made to bear weight and endure. He delivered the offer, gave his opinion, and demanded her consent.

She withheld it.

“No; I shall not marry Samuel Fawthrop Wynne.”

“I ask why. I must have a reason. In all respects he is more than worthy of you.”

Shirley stood on the hearth; behind her, the white marble slab and cornice threw back her pallor, so that her face looked carved for a moment—still, proud, and cold. Only her eyes were alive, large and bright with a flash that would not soften.

“And I ask in what sense that young man is worthy of me?”

“He has twice your money, twice your common sense, equal connections, equal respectability.”

“Had he my money counted fivescore times I would take no vow to love him.”

“Please to state your objections.”

“He has run a course of despicable, commonplace profligacy. Accept that as the first reason why I spurn him.”

“Miss Keeldar, you shock me!”

“That conduct alone sinks him in a gulf of immeasurable inferiority. His intellect reaches no standard I can esteem: there is a second stumbling-block. His views are narrow, his feelings are blunt, his tastes are coarse, his manners vulgar.”

“The man is a respectable, wealthy man! To refuse him is presumption on your part.”

“I refuse point-blank! Cease to annoy me with the subject; I forbid it!”

“Is it your intention ever to marry; or do you prefer celibacy?”

“I deny your right to claim an answer to that question.”

“May I ask if you expect some man of title—some peer of the realm—to demand your hand?”

“I doubt if the peer breathes on whom I would confer it.”

“Were there insanity in the family, I should believe you mad. Your eccentricity and conceit touch the verge of frenzy.”

“Perhaps, ere I have finished, you will see me over-leap it.”

“I anticipate no less. Frantic and impracticable girl! Take warning! I dare you to sully our name by a mésalliance!”

“Our name! Am I called Sympson?”

“God be thanked that you are not! But be on your guard; I will not be trifled with!”

“What, in the name of common law and common sense, would you or could you do if my pleasure led me to a choice you disapproved?”

“Take care! take care!” He warned her with a voice and hand that trembled together.

“Why? What shadow of power have you over me? Why should I fear you?”

“Take care, madam!”

“Scrupulous care I will take, Mr. Sympson. Before I marry I am resolved to esteem—to admire—to love.”

“Preposterous stuff! indecorous, unwomanly!”

“To love with my whole heart. I know I speak in an unknown tongue; but I feel indifferent whether I am comprehended or not.”

“And if this love of yours should fall on a beggar?”

“On a beggar it will never fall. Mendicancy is not estimable.”

“On a low clerk, a play-actor, a play-writer, or—or—”

“Take courage, Mr. Sympson! Or what?”

“Any literary scrub, or shabby, whining artist.”

“For the scrubby, shabby, whining I have no taste; for literature and the arts I have. And there I wonder how your Fawthrop Wynne would suit me. He cannot write a note without orthographical errors; he reads only a sporting paper; he was the booby of Stilbro’ grammar school!”

“Unladylike language! Great God! to what will she come?” He lifted hands and eyes, as if appealing to the oak beams.

“Never to the altar of Hymen with Sam Wynne.”

“To what will she come? Why are not the laws more stringent, that I might compel her to hear reason?”

“Console yourself, uncle. Were Britain a serfdom and you the Czar, you could not compel me to this step. I will write to Mr. Wynne. Give yourself no further trouble on the subject.”

And she did. The matter, so far, was ended; but the storm within Fieldhead was only displaced, not stilled.

Fortune is said to be changeful, and yet she repeats herself with a tiresome sameness, striking again and again at the same place, as if it were a bell she liked the sound of. Miss Keeldar—or Miss Keeldar’s fortune—had made a sensation in the district. It seemed to the neighbourhood that an heiress, like a great harvest, must be gathered; and presently offers came, as sheaves are carted in—one after another, pressed, urged, exhibited as if they were proof of the land’s richness.

No less than three proposals followed Mr. Wynne’s; all more or less eligible. Mr. Sympson pressed each with the same confident hand; Shirley refused each with the same clear denial. Among them were men of decent character and ample wealth, and there were many besides her uncle who asked, with lifted brows, what she meant, and whom she expected to entrap, that she should be so fastidious.

At last the gossips thought they had found the key; and Mr. Sympson, eager for any explanation that promised to bring his niece back under the yoke of what he called reason, seized on the discovery with delight. It showed Shirley to him in a new light, and he changed his whole manner accordingly.

Fieldhead, of late, had been growing too hot to hold both uncle and niece. Mrs. Sympson, with her smooth patience, could not reconcile them. The daughters stiffened at every open clash, as if quarrels were draughts that chilled the blood. Gertrude and Isabella whispered by the hour in their dressing-room, and grew pale with decorous dread when they chanced to be left alone with their bold cousin—who, without lifting her voice, could make rebellion sound like law.

Then, all at once, a change came over the household, as when a kettle that has been singing furiously is taken from the blaze and set aside: the noise does not cease at once, but the temper of it alters. Mr. Sympson was appeased; his family’s tranquillity seemed restored.

The village of Nunnely has been mentioned—its old church, its forest, its monastic ruins, the stones that kept memory as stubbornly as old tongues keep certain untranslatable words. It had also its Hall, called the priory: older, larger, more lordly than any Briarfield or Whinbury could boast; and it had something no neighbour could rival—a title, a baronetcy. Yet for years that proud possession had been only nominal; the young baronet lived away in a distant province, unknown upon his Yorkshire land.

During Miss Keeldar’s stay at Cliffbridge, that fashionable watering-place where the sea’s voice is never wholly silent—now booming on the shingle, now whispering under the wind, now breaking into a sudden white clatter—she and her friends had met Sir Philip Nunnely and been introduced. Again and again they encountered him on the sands, on the cliffs, in the public walks, sometimes at the public balls. He seemed solitary. His manner was unpretending—too simple to be called affable; rather timid than proud. He did not condescend to their society; he seemed glad of it, as one who has lived long in echoing rooms is glad to hear, at last, a human footfall that is not his own.

With any unaffected person Shirley could swiftly knit acquaintance. She walked and talked with Sir Philip; she, her aunt, and cousins sometimes took a sail in his yacht. She liked him because he was kind and modest, and because she discovered she had power to amuse him—an influence she used with the ease of one who has always had it, and yet with some generosity too.

One slight drawback there was—where is friendship without it? Sir Philip had a literary turn. He wrote poetry—sonnets, stanzas, ballads. Perhaps Shirley thought him too fond of reading and reciting these pieces; perhaps she wished the rhyme had been truer, the measure more musical, the images fresher, the inspiration more fire. At any rate, she always winced when he returned to his poems, and usually did her best to steer the talk into another channel, as one might move a guest away from a dish that has been over-salted and served with pride.

He would beguile her into moonlight walks on the bridge, for the sole purpose, as it seemed, of pouring into her ear the longest of his ballads. He would lead her to secluded rustic seats, where the surf’s rush came softened, like distant applause, and the gardens breathed their late sweetness; and when he had her all to himself—with the sea spread before them, cliffs rising behind, and the scented shade lying round—he would draw out his last batch of sonnets and read them in a voice tremulous with feeling. He did not seem to know that, though they might be rhyme, they were not poetry. Shirley’s downcast eye and uneasy colour showed that she knew it, and was mortified by the single foible of this good and amiable gentleman.

Often she tried, gently, to wean him from that fanatic worship of his own Muse. It was his monomania; on all ordinary subjects he was sensible enough, and she was eager to meet him on those safer grounds. He questioned her sometimes about his place at Nunnely; she answered at length, delighted. She never tired of describing the antique priory, the wild silvan park, the hoary church and hamlet; the very sound of those names, spoken in their own Yorkshire air, seemed to her fuller than any elegant phrase learned elsewhere. She counselled him to come down, to gather his tenantry about him in his ancestral halls, to set his own foot on the soil that had borne his name so long.

Somewhat to her surprise, Sir Philip followed her counsel to the letter, and, towards the close of September, arrived at the priory.

He soon called at Fieldhead, and his first visit was not his last. He said—after he had made the round of the neighbourhood—that under no roof had he found such pleasant shelter as beneath the massive oak beams of the grey manor-house of Briarfield; cramped and modest enough compared with his own, but he liked it. There was a homely sound there—the quieter domestic stir, the human voices not drowned by grandeur—more wholesome to him, perhaps, than the echoing breadth of his priory.

Presently it did not suffice to sit with Shirley in her panelled parlour, where others came and went, and where he could rarely find a quiet moment to offer the latest production of his fertile muse. He must have her out among the pastures, by the still waters. Tête-à-tête ramblings she avoided, so he made parties—inviting the household and contriving chaperonage—to his own grounds, his glorious forest, to remoter scenes: woods severed by the Wharfe, vales watered by the Aire. In the hush beneath old trees, where the living world speaks in leaf-rustle and bird-note, and where the far-off grind of mills is softened into something like a bad memory, Sir Philip seemed most himself; and Shirley, who always listened to places as much as to people, felt the relief of those greener acoustics.

Such assiduity covered Miss Keeldar with distinction. Mr. Sympson’s prophetic soul began to feed on a splendid future. He already scented, from afar, the time when—left foot nursed on right knee, with a careless air—he could make dashing, familiar allusion to “my nephew the baronet.” Now his niece dawned upon him no longer “a mad girl,” but “a most sensible woman.” In confidential talk with Mrs. Sympson, he termed her “a truly superior person; peculiar, but very clever.” He treated her with exceeding deference; rose to open and shut doors; reddened his face and gave himself headaches by stooping to pick up gloves and handkerchiefs—little properties Shirley held with insecure tenure. He cut mysterious jokes about the superiority of woman’s wit over man’s wisdom; began obscure apologies for the blundering mistake he had made regarding the generalship, the tactics, of “a personage not a hundred miles from Fieldhead.” In short, he strutted and swelled as elate as any midden-cock on pattens.

Shirley watched these manoeuvres and received his innuendoes with phlegm; she seemed not half to comprehend the aim. When plainly charged with being the preferred of the baronet, she said she believed he did like her, and for her part she liked him. She had never thought a man of rank—the only son of a proud, fond mother, the only brother of doting sisters—could have so much goodness, and, on the whole, so much sense.

Time proved that Sir Philip liked her indeed. Whatever “curious charm” Mr. Hall had once noted in Shirley, it drew Sir Philip as surely as warmth draws one’s hands to a hearth. He sought her presence more and more, until at last the frequency of his visits testified that it had become to him an indispensable stimulus. About this time strange feelings hovered round Fieldhead; restless hopes and haggard anxieties haunted some of its rooms. There was an unquiet wandering of certain inmates among the still fields round the mansion; an expectancy, like a held breath, kept nerves strained.

One thing seemed clear: Sir Philip was not a man to be despised. He was amiable; if not highly intellectual, he was intelligent. Shirley could not say of him what she had said of Sam Wynne—that his feelings were blunt, his tastes coarse, his manners vulgar. There was sensibility in him; there was a very real, though not very discerning, love of the arts; there was the English gentleman in his deportment. As to wealth and lineage, both lay, of course, far beyond her claims.

At first, Shirley’s merry eye had found something to laugh at in Sir Philip’s appearance—boyish, slight, plain-featured, sandy-haired, of insignificant stature. But she soon checked herself; she would even flash into warmth if another spoke slightingly. He had “a pleasing countenance,” she maintained; “and there was that in his heart which was better than three Roman noses, than the locks of Absalom or the proportions of Saul.” One spare shaft she still kept for his unfortunate poetic propensity; but even there she would tolerate no irony except her own.

Matters had reached a point that seemed fully to warrant an observation Mr. Yorke made, about this time, to Louis Moore.

“Yond brother Robert of yours seems to me to be either a fool or a madman. Two months ago I could have sworn he had the game all in his own hands; and there he runs the country, and quarters himself up in London for weeks together, and by the time he comes back he’ll find himself checkmated. Louis, ‘there is a tide in the affairs of men, which, taken at the flood, leads on to fortune, but, once let slip, never returns again.’ I’d write to Robert, if I were you, and remind him of that.”

“Robert had views on Miss Keeldar?” asked Louis, as if the thought were new.

“Views I suggested to him myself, and views he might have realized, for she liked him.”

“As a neighbour?”

“As more than that. I have seen her change countenance and colour at the mere mention of his name. Write to the lad, I say, and tell him to come home. He is a finer gentleman than this bit of a baronet, after all.”

“Does it not strike you, Mr. Yorke, that for a mere penniless adventurer to aspire to a rich woman’s hand is presumptuous—contemptible?”

“Oh, if you are for high notions and double-refined sentiment, I’ve naught to say. I’m a plain, practical man myself, and if Robert is willing to give up that royal prize to a lad-rival—a puling slip of aristocracy—I am quite agreeable. At his age, in his place, with his inducements, I would have acted differently. Neither baronet, nor duke, nor prince should have snatched my sweetheart from me without a struggle. But you tutors are such solemn chaps; it is almost like speaking to a parson to consult with you.”

Flattered and fawned upon as Shirley now was, it appeared she was not absolutely spoiled; her better nature did not wholly desert her. Universal report had ceased to couple her name with that of Moore, and that silence seemed sanctioned by her own apparent oblivion of the absentee; yet that she had not quite forgotten him—had not quite let go her interest—was proved by the increased attention she showed, at this juncture, to Louis Moore, Robert’s brother, her own former teacher.

An attack of illness seized Louis—some fever that for a few days laid him low. Perhaps he had caught it in one of the poor cottages which he, his lame pupil Henry, and Mr. Hall were used to visit together; the air in such places could be close with damp, and with the sour breath of want. At any rate, he sickened, and after opposing the malady with a taciturn endurance for a day or two, was obliged to keep his chamber.

One evening he lay tossing on his thorny bed, while Henry, who would not leave him, watched faithfully at his side. A tap came at the door—too light to be that of Mrs. Gill or the housemaid—and Henry opened it.

“How is Mr. Moore to-night?” asked a low voice from the dark gallery.

“Come in and see him yourself.”

“Is he asleep?”

“I wish he could sleep. Come and speak to him, Shirley.”

“He would not like it.”

But the speaker stepped in. Henry, seeing her hesitate on the threshold, took her hand and drew her toward the couch with the quiet authority of a child who loves both parties.

The shaded light showed Miss Keeldar’s figure imperfectly; yet it revealed elegance—white dress, fair arms and neck, and a trembling chainlet of gold circling her throat and quivering on her breast, bright in the sickroom’s obscurity like a small star caught indoors. There was a party below; Sir Philip Nunnely was among them. The ladies were in the drawing-room; their hostess had stolen away to this hushed passage where the house’s cheerful sounds died, and only the low murmur of distant voices and the occasional soft tread of a servant came and went like far-off water.

Her mien was chastened, pensive. She spoke gently.

“Mr. Moore, how are you to-night?”

“I have not been very ill, and am now better.”

“I heard that you complained of thirst. I have brought you some grapes; can you taste one?”

“No; but I thank you for remembering me.”

“Just one.”

From the rich cluster in a small basket she severed a berry and offered it to his lips. He shook his head and turned away his flushed face, as if the sweetness would mock the bitterness in his mouth.

“But what, then, can I bring you instead? You have no wish for fruit; yet I see that your lips are parched. What beverage do you prefer?”

“Mrs. Gill supplies me with toast-and-water. I like it best.”

Silence fell for some minutes—a silence in which the house’s far-off life seemed louder: the faint clink of glass below, a door closing, the murmur of talk softened by distance, the whole domestic orchestra of an evening’s entertainment.

“Do you suffer?—have you pain?” she asked at last.

“Very little.”

“What made you ill?”

Silence again—his reserve as obstinate as if it were a lock he had vowed not to open.

“I wonder what caused this fever? To what do you attribute it?”

“Miasma, perhaps—malaria. This is autumn, a season fertile in fevers.”

“I hear you often visit the sick in Briarfield, and Nunnely too, with Mr. Hall. You should be on your guard; temerity is not wise.”

“That reminds me, Miss Keeldar, that perhaps you had better not enter this chamber or come near this couch. I do not believe my illness is infectious. I scarcely fear”—a sort of brief smile touched the edge of his mouth—“you will take it; but why should you run even the shadow of a risk? Leave me.”

“Patience, I will go soon; but I should like to do something for you before I depart—any little service—”

“They will miss you below.”

“No; the gentlemen are still at table.”

“They will not linger long. Sir Philip Nunnely is no wine-bibber, and I hear him just now pass from the dining-room to the drawing-room.”

“It is a servant.”

“It is Sir Philip; I know his step.”

“Your hearing is acute.”

“It is never dull, and the sense seems sharpened at present. Sir Philip was here to tea last night. I heard you sing to him some song which he had brought you. I heard him, when he took his departure at eleven o’clock, call you out on to the pavement, to look at the evening star.”

“You must be nervously sensitive.”

“I heard him kiss your hand.”

“Impossible!”

“No: my chamber is over the hall, the window just above the front door; the sash was a little raised, for I felt feverish. You stood ten minutes with him on the steps. I heard your discourse, every word, and I heard the salute.—Henry, give me some water.”

“Let me give it him.”

But Louis half rose to take the glass from Henry and declined Shirley’s attendance, as if her kindness were the very thing he could not bear.

“And can I do nothing?” she persisted, with that sincerity which—because it came late—had the more sting of truth in it.

“Nothing; for you cannot guarantee me a night’s peaceful rest, and it is all I at present want.”

“You do not sleep well?”

“Sleep has left me.”

“Yet you said you were not very ill?”

“I am often sleepless when in high health.”

“If I had power, I would lap you in the most placid slumber—quite deep and hushed, without a dream.”

“Blank annihilation! I do not ask that.”

“With dreams of all you most desire.”

“Monstrous delusions! The sleep would be delirium, the waking death.”

“Your wishes are not so chimerical; you are no visionary.”

“Miss Keeldar, I suppose you think so; but my character is not, perhaps, quite as legible to you as a page of the last new novel might be.”

“That is possible. But this sleep—I should like to woo it to your pillow, to win for you its favour. If I took a book and sat down and read some pages? I can well spare half an hour.”

“Thank you, but I will not detain you.”

“I would read softly.”

“It would not do. I am too feverish and excitable to bear a soft, cooing, vibrating voice close at my ear. You had better leave me.”

“Well, I will go.”

“And no good-night?”

“Yes, sir, yes. Mr. Moore, good-night.” And she went.

When the door had shut, Louis turned his face slightly away, as if the last of her light had gone with her, and spoke to Henry in a different tone—one steadier, gentler.

“Henry, my boy, go to bed now; it is time you had some repose.”

“Sir, it would please me to watch at your bedside all night.”

“Nothing less called for. I am getting better. There, go.”

“Give me your blessing, sir.”

“God bless you, my best pupil!”

“You never call me your dearest pupil!”

“No, nor ever shall.”

Possibly Shirley resented her former teacher’s rejection of her courtesy. It is certain she did not repeat the offer. Often as her light step crossed the gallery in the course of a day, it did not again pause at his door; nor did her “cooing, vibrating voice” a second time trouble the hush of his room. A sickroom, indeed, it soon ceased to be; Louis’s good constitution quickly triumphed, and in a few days he shook off the indisposition and resumed his duties as tutor.

That auld lang syne still held authority between preceptor and scholar was proved by the manner in which he would sometimes, without warning, step across the distance she liked to keep between them and, with a firm quiet hand, put down her high reserve—controlling it as he had once controlled her wilfulness over a task or a lesson.

One afternoon the Sympson family went out for a carriage airing. Shirley, never sorry to seize a reprieve from their society, remained behind, detained, she said, by business. The business—some letter-writing—was soon done after the yard gates closed on the departing wheels; and Miss Keeldar betook herself to the garden.

It was a peaceful autumn day. The Indian summer gilding lay upon the fields like a thin warm syrup, mellowing everything it touched. Woods stood russet and ripe, full-leaved still, though ready to be stripped. The heath’s purple, faded but not withered, tinged the hills. The beck wandered down to the Hollow through a district so silent that even its running seemed restrained; no wind followed its course or haunted its woody banks. Fieldhead’s gardens wore the seal of gentle decay. On the walks swept that morning yellow leaves had fluttered down again, as if the trees would not be kept tidy. The time of flowers—indeed, even of fruits—was past; yet a scantling of apples enriched the boughs, and here and there a last pale blossom opened, delicate and stubborn among the fading leaves.

These single flowers—the last of their race—Shirley culled as she wandered, thoughtful. She was fastening into her girdle a hueless, scentless nosegay—beauty grown thin, like a remembered word no longer spoken—when Henry Sympson called to her, limping from the house.

“Shirley, Mr. Moore would be glad to see you in the schoolroom and to hear you read a little French, if you have no more urgent occupation.”

He delivered the message simply, as if it were a thing of course.

“Did Mr. Moore tell you to say that?” she asked.

“Certainly; why not? And now, do come, and let us once more be as we were at Sympson Grove. We used to have pleasant school-hours in those days.”

Shirley perhaps thought much had altered since those days—names, meanings, the very weight of what was spoken and left unspoken. She made no remark; but after a brief pause she followed Henry quietly back into the house.

Shirley must have found Sir Philip’s talk agreeable the night before; for in the morning she came down as if some hidden hearth had been newly kindled in her. The breakfast-room, with its sober light and its sober faces, could not smother that inward flame. She had a brisk word for every one, a smile ready before it was asked, and—rarer still—an invitation.

“Who will walk with me?” she demanded, pushing back her chair with decision. “Isabella—Gertrude—will you?”

So uncommon a summons from Miss Keeldar to her female cousins, that both Misses Sympson stared a moment, as if the offer were a dish set before them whose name they did not know. Their mamma, however, gave assent; bonnets were fetched; and the three went out together.

They were not three who naturally mixed and mellowed by being thrown into one pot. Miss Keeldar liked few ladies’ company, and had cordial pleasure in none but Mrs. Pryor and Caroline Helstone. She could be scrupulously civil, even kind; she could remember little attentions as a careful housekeeper remembers her stores; yet commonly she had little to say to her cousins, and what she said was of the plainest grain—weather, roads, neighbours, the small homely ledger of daily things. But this morning even those ordinary matters took savour from her spirit, as the simplest broth is transformed by a handful of herbs. A sparkle ran along her phrases; she made the damp lane and the dun wood seem almost jocund.

For the day itself gave no help. It was one of those pale, waning autumn mornings when the air hangs heavy as wet wool, when the woods smell of mouldering leaves and soaked bark, when every twig drops coldly on your sleeve, and the sky is a lid of lead. The path through the coppice was dark with moisture; the ground yielded underfoot with a soft sucking sound; there was no sun—only a dim, colourless light, and a steady hush broken now and then by the drip from bough to bough. Yet in Shirley’s heart lived a warmth like southern air; in her grey eye laughed a fervour that did not belong to Yorkshire.

On their way back, as Fieldhead came into view, Shirley was detained. Some direction had to be given to her foreman, John; so she let her cousins go on, while she turned aside to speak of work—of hedges, drains, cattle—things that root a family in land and make the year’s bread. Perhaps twenty minutes passed between their parting and her return. In that interval she did speak with John; and then she lingered in the lane at the gate, as if the wet smell of earth and the soft, far murmur of the moor-wind had something to say that she would not carry indoors.

A summons to luncheon called her in at last. She excused herself from the meal, and went upstairs.

“Is not Shirley coming?” Isabella asked, surprised. “She said she was hungry.”

An hour went by, and still she did not come down. One of the cousins, uneasy, went up to seek her. Shirley was found sitting at the foot of the bed, her head resting on her hand. The colour had left her cheek; she looked not merely thoughtful, but subdued—almost sad.

“You are not ill?” was asked.

“A little sick,” said Miss Keeldar.

She was altered; no one could deny it. Two hours earlier she had been all briskness; now she was pale and still, as if a sudden shadow had crossed her. Yet that shadow did not pass like a light summer cloud. When she joined them at dinner, she talked; she sat with them in the evening; when questioned again, she declared herself perfectly recovered. It had been a mere faintness—not worth thought. But those about her felt a difference, like a change in the air before rain.

The next day, and the week, and the fortnight after, the new cloud remained. A strange quietude settled on her look, her movements, her very voice. It was not so marked as to invite constant question; but it was there, and it would not disperse. To notice it annoyed her. At first she shrank; if pressed, she repelled with that peculiar hauteur which sat on her like armour.

“Was she ill?”

“I am not.”

“Did anything weigh on her mind? Had anything happened?”

She ridiculed the notion with scorn. What did they mean by “spirits”? She had none—black, blue, grey, or white—to be affected.

“But you are so altered.”

She flung back, peremptory, that she supposed she had a right to alter at her ease. She knew she was plainer; if it suited her to grow ugly, why should others fret?

“What is the cause?”

She requested to be let alone.

Then, as if angry with herself for having let her shadow be seen, she would make an effort to appear quite gay; and when she could not wholly succeed, brief self-spurning words broke from her when alone.

“Fool! coward!” she would mutter. “Poltroon! If you must tremble, tremble in secret. Quail where no eye sees you.”

“How dare you,” she would ask her own heart, “show weakness—betray imbecile anxieties? Shake them off. Rise above them. If you cannot, hide them.”

And to hide them she did her best. She became resolutely lively in company; and when she was weary of effort and compelled to let the mask slip, she sought solitude—not the shut-in solitude of a chamber (she refused to mope between four walls), but the wide, raw solitude outdoors, which she could pursue mounted on Zoë. She took rides that lasted half a day. Her uncle disapproved; but he dared not remonstrate. It was never pleasant to face Shirley’s anger when she was healthy and bright; now, with her face grown thin and her large eye hollow, there was something in the darkening of that face and the kindling of that eye which touched as well as alarmed.

To strangers who noticed her changed looks, she gave one answer only.

“I am perfectly well; I have not an ailment.”

And, in truth, health must have been in her to endure what she now endured. Wet or fair, calm or storm, daily she rode over Stilbro’ Moor, Tartar keeping pace beside her with his wolfish gallop, long and untiring. The very sound of them—the ring of hoof on stone, the soft thud on turf, the dog’s swift pads, the rush of wind in the heather—was a kind of fierce music, a counter-chime to the town’s harsher noises. Yet even that open-air harmony could not wholly cleanse the mind. The moor might give clean scents—peat, rain, crushed ling—but it could not always give clean peace.

Twice, thrice, the eyes of gossips—those eyes that are everywhere, in closet and on hill-top—noticed that instead of turning on Rushedge, the top ridge of Stilbro’ Moor, she rode forward all the way to the town. Scouts were not wanting to mark her destination there. It was ascertained she alighted at the door of one Mr. Pearson Hall, a solicitor, related to the vicar of Nunnely—an agent of the Keeldars for generations. The village, hungry for explanations, set its teeth into the matter. Some affirmed Miss Keeldar had been drawn into business speculations connected with Hollow’s Mill, that she had lost money and must mortgage land. Others said, with a sort of relish, that she was going to be married, and that settlements were being prepared.

* * * * *

In the schoolroom Mr. Moore sat with Henry Sympson. The tutor waited for a lesson which the pupil seemed to be preparing with unusual slowness.

“Henry, make haste. The afternoon is getting on.”

“Is it, sir?”

“Certainly. Are you nearly ready with that lesson?”

“No.”

“Not nearly ready?”

“I have not construed a line.”

Mr. Moore looked up. The boy’s tone was odd—flat, yet strained, as if he held something down by force.

“The task presents no difficulties, Henry; or if it does, bring them to me. We will work together.”

“Mr. Moore, I can do no work.”

“My boy, you are ill.”

“Sir, I am not worse in bodily health than usual—but my heart is full.”

“Shut the book. Come hither, Harry. Come to the fireside.”

Harry limped forward. His tutor placed him in a chair. The child’s lips quivered; his eyes brimmed. He laid his crutch on the floor, bent his head, and wept.

“This distress is not occasioned by physical pain, you say? You have a grief. Tell it me.”

“Sir, I have such a grief as I never had before. I wish it could be relieved somehow; I can hardly bear it.”

“Who knows but that talking it over may relieve it? What is the cause? Whom does it concern?”

“The cause, sir, is Shirley; it concerns Shirley.”

“Does it? You think her changed?”

“All who know her think her changed—you too, Mr. Moore.”

“Not seriously—no. I see no alteration but such as a favourable turn might repair in a few weeks; besides, her own word must go for something: she says she is well.”

“There it is, sir. So long as she maintained she was well, I believed her. When I was sad out of her sight, I soon recovered spirits in her presence. Now—”

“Well, Harry, now. Has she said anything to you? You and she were together in the garden two hours this morning. I saw her talking and you listening. If Miss Keeldar has said she is ill and enjoined you to keep her secret, do not obey her. For her life’s sake, avow everything. Speak, my boy.”

“She say she is ill! I believe, sir, if she were dying, she would smile and aver, ‘Nothing ails me.’”

“What have you learned then? What new circumstance?”

“I have learned that she has just made her will.”

“Made her will?”

Both were silent a moment. The rain whispered at the panes; the house held its breath.

“She told you that?” asked Moore at length.

“She told me quite cheerfully—not as ominous, which I felt it to be. She said I was the only person besides her solicitor, Pearson Hall, and Mr. Helstone and Mr. Yorke, who knew anything about it; and to me, she said, she wished specially to explain its provisions.”

“Go on, Harry.”

“‘Because,’ she said—looking down on me with her beautiful eyes—oh! they are beautiful, Mr. Moore! I love them! I love her! She is my star! Heaven must not claim her! She is lovely in this world, and fitted for this world. Shirley is not an angel; she is a woman, and she shall live with men. Seraphs shall not have her! Mr. Moore, if one of the sons of God, with wings wide and bright as the sky—blue and sounding as the sea—having seen she was fair, descended to claim her, his claim should be withstood—by me—boy and cripple as I am.’”

“Henry Sympson, go on, when I tell you.”

“‘Because,’ she said, ‘if I made no will, and died before you, Harry, all my property would go to you; and I do not intend it should be so, though your father would like it. But you will have his whole estate, which is large—larger than Fieldhead. Your sisters will have nothing; so I have left them some money, though I do not love them, both together, half so much as I love one lock of your fair hair.’ And she called me her darling, and let me kiss her. She said she had left Caroline Helstone some money too; that this manor house, with its furniture and books, she had bequeathed to me, because she would not take the old family place from her own blood; and all the rest—about twelve thousand pounds, exclusive of the legacies—she had willed not to me, since I was already rich, but to a good man, who would make the best use of it any human being could—a man, she said, gentle and brave, strong and merciful; a man who might not profess to be pious, but she knew he had the secret of religion pure and undefiled before God. The spirit of love and peace was with him; he visited the fatherless and widows in their affliction, and kept himself unspotted from the world. Then she asked, ‘Do you approve what I have done, Harry?’ I could not answer. My tears choked me, as they do now.”

Mr. Moore waited till the boy’s sobs lessened.

“What else did she say?” he demanded.

“When I had signified my consent, she told me I was generous, and she was proud of me. ‘And now,’ she added, ‘in case anything should happen, you will know what to say to Malice when she comes whispering hard things in your ear—insinuating Shirley has wronged you, that she did not love you. You will know that I did love you, Harry; that no sister could have loved you better—my own treasure.’ Mr. Moore, when I remember her voice and look, my heart beats as if it would break its strings. She may go to heaven before me—if God commands it, she must; but the rest of my life—and my life will not be long, I am glad of that now—shall be a straight, quick, thoughtful journey in the path her step has pressed. I thought to enter the vault of the Keeldars before her. Should it be otherwise, lay my coffin by Shirley’s side.”

Moore answered with a weighty calm that offered strange contrast to the boy’s fervour.

“You are wrong, both of you—you harm each other. If youth once falls under a shadowy terror, it imagines there will never be full sunlight again; its first calamity it fancies will last a lifetime. What more did she say? Anything more?”

“We settled one or two family points between ourselves.”

“I should rather like to know what—”

“But, Mr. Moore, you smile. I could not smile to see Shirley in such a mood.”

“My boy, I am neither nervous, nor poetic, nor inexperienced. I see things as they are; you don’t as yet. Tell me these family points.”

“Only, sir, she asked whether I considered myself most a Keeldar or a Sympson; and I answered I was Keeldar to the core of the heart and to the marrow of the bones. She said she was glad; for, besides her, I was the only Keeldar left in England. And then we agreed—”

“Well?”

“Well, sir, that if I lived to inherit my father’s estate and her house, I was to take the name of Keeldar, and make Fieldhead my residence. Henry Shirley Keeldar I said I would be called; and I will. Her name and her manor house are ages old, and Sympson and Sympson Grove are of yesterday.”

“Come, you are neither of you going to heaven yet. I have the best hopes of you both, with your proud distinctions—a pair of half-fledged eaglets. Now, what is your inference from all you have told me? Put it into words.”

“That Shirley thinks she is going to die.”

“She referred to her health?”

“Not once; but I assure you she is wasting. Her hands are grown quite thin, and so is her cheek.”

“Does she ever complain to your mother or sisters?”

“Never. She laughs at them when they question her. Mr. Moore, she is strange—so fair and girlish—not a man-like woman at all, not an Amazon—yet lifting her head above both help and sympathy.”

“Do you know where she is now, Henry? Is she in the house, or riding out?”

“Surely not out, sir. It rains fast.”

“True; which is no guarantee she is not at this moment cantering over Rushedge. Of late she has never permitted weather to hinder her rides.”

“You remember last Wednesday, Mr. Moore—so wet and stormy she would not permit Zoë to be saddled? Yet the blast she thought too tempestuous for her mare she faced on foot; she walked nearly as far as Nunnely. I asked if she was not afraid of taking cold. ‘Not I,’ she said. ‘It would be too much good luck for me. I don’t know, Harry, but the best thing that could happen to me would be to take a good cold and fever, and so pass off like other Christians.’ She is reckless, sir.”

“Reckless indeed! Go and find where she is; and if you can speak to her without attracting attention, request her to come here a minute.”

“Yes, sir.”

He snatched his crutch and started.

“Harry!”

He returned.

“Do not deliver the message formally. Word it as, in former days, you would have worded an ordinary summons to the schoolroom.”

“I see, sir. She will be more likely to obey.”

“And, Harry—”

“Sir?”

“I will call you when I want you. Till then, you are dispensed from lessons.”

He departed. Mr. Moore, left alone, rose from his desk.

“I can be very cool and supercilious with Henry,” he said to himself. “I can make light of his apprehensions, and look down, du haut de ma grandeur, on his youthful ardour. To him I can speak as if they were both children. Let me see if I can keep up the same rôle with her.

“I have known the moment when I seemed about to forget it—when Confusion and Submission seemed about to crush me with their soft tyranny—when my tongue faltered, and I have almost let the mantle drop, and stood in her presence not master—no—but something else. I trust I shall never so play the fool. It is well for a Sir Philip Nunnely to redden when he meets her eye: he may indulge submission; he may, without disgrace, suffer his hand to tremble when it touches hers. But if one of her farmers were to show himself susceptible and sentimental, he would merely prove his need of a strait waistcoat. So far I have done well. She has sat near me, and I have not shaken—more than my desk. I have encountered her looks and smiles like—why, like a tutor, as I am. Her hand I never yet touched; never underwent that test.

“Her farmer or her footman I am not—no serf nor servant of hers have I ever been; but I am poor, and it behoves me to look to my self-respect—not to compromise an inch of it. What did she mean by that allusion to cold people who petrify flesh to marble? It pleased me—I hardly know why. I would not permit myself to inquire. I never indulge in scrutiny either of her language or countenance; for if I did, I should sometimes forget common sense and believe in romance. A strange, secret ecstasy steals through my veins at moments. I’ll not encourage it; I’ll not remember it. I am resolved, as long as may be, to retain the right to say with Paul, ‘I am not mad, but speak forth the words of truth and soberness.’”

He paused, listening.

“Will she come, or will she not come? How will she take the message—naïvely or disdainfully? Like a child or like a queen? Both characters are in her nature.

“If she comes, what shall I say? How account, firstly, for the freedom of the request? Shall I apologize? I could in all humility; but would an apology place us where we ought to stand? I must keep up the professor—otherwise— I hear a door.”

He waited. Many minutes passed.

“She will refuse me. Henry is entreating; she declines. My petition is presumption in her eyes. Let her only come, I can teach her to the contrary. I would rather she were perverse; it will steel me. I prefer her cuirassed in pride, armed with a taunt. Her scorn startles me from dreams; it makes me stand up myself. A sarcasm from her eyes or lips puts strength into every nerve and sinew I have. Some step approaches—and not Henry’s.”

The door opened. Miss Keeldar came in.

The message, it seemed, had found her with her needle; she brought her work in her hand as if she would not be dislodged from the domestic posture she had chosen. That day she had not been out; she had passed it quietly. She wore a neat indoor dress and a silk apron. This was no Thalestris from the fields; this was a household figure from the fireside, with thread drawn through cloth and a quiet hand to guide it. Mr. Moore had her at advantage; he should have seized it at once and spoken in rigid accents. Perhaps he would have done so had she looked saucy. But never had her air shown less of crânerie. A soft, youthful shyness lowered her eyelid; a faint colour mantled on her cheek. The tutor stood silent.

She stopped between the door and his desk.

“Did you want me, sir?” she asked.

“I ventured, Miss Keeldar, to send for you—that is, to ask an interview of a few minutes.”

She waited, plying her needle with small exact stitches, as if the slight resistance of the fabric steadied her.

“Well, sir,” she said at last, not lifting her eyes, “what about?”

“Be seated first. The subject I would broach is one of some moment. Perhaps I have hardly a right to approach it. It is possible I ought to frame an apology; it is possible no apology can excuse me. The liberty I have taken arises from a conversation with Henry. The boy is unhappy about your health; all your friends are unhappy on that subject. It is of your health I would speak.”

“I am quite well,” she returned briefly.

“Yet changed.”

“That matters to none but myself. We all change.”

“Will you sit down? Formerly, Miss Keeldar, I had some influence with you: have I any now? May I feel that what I am saying is not accounted positive presumption?”

“Let me read some French, Mr. Moore, or I will even take a spell at the Latin grammar, and let us proclaim a truce to all sanitary discussions.”

“No, no. It is time there were discussions.”

“Discuss away, then, but do not choose me for your text. I am a healthy subject.”

“Do you not think it wrong to affirm and reaffirm what is substantially untrue?”

“I say I am well. I have neither cough, pain, nor fever.”

“Is there no equivocation in that assertion? Is it the direct truth?”

“The direct truth.”

Louis Moore looked at her earnestly.

“I can myself trace no indications of actual disease,” he said. “But why, then, are you altered?”

“Am I altered?”

“We will try. We will seek a proof.”

“How?”

“I ask, in the first place, do you sleep as you used to?”

“I do not; but it is not because I am ill.”

“Have you the appetite you once had?”

“No; but it is not because I am ill.”

“You remember this little ring fastened to my watch-chain? It was my mother’s, and is too small to pass the joint of my little finger. You have many a time sportively purloined it. It fitted your forefinger. Try now.”

She held out her hand. The ring slid on—and then, startlingly, dropped off the wasted finger as if the flesh that once filled it had melted away. Louis picked it up and reattached it to the chain. An uneasy flush rose on his brow.

Shirley said again, as if the words were a charm she forced herself to repeat, “It is not because I am ill.”

“Not only have you lost sleep, appetite, and flesh,” proceeded Moore, “but your spirits are always at ebb. Besides, there is a nervous alarm in your eye, a nervous disquiet in your manner. These peculiarities were not formerly yours.”

“Mr. Moore, we will pause here. You have exactly hit it. I am nervous. Now—talk of something else. What wet weather we have—steady, pouring rain!”

“You nervous? Yes; and if Miss Keeldar is nervous, it is not without a cause. Let me reach it. Let me look nearer. The ailment is not physical. I have suspected that. It came in one moment. I know the day. I noticed the change. Your pain is mental.”

“Not at all. It is nothing so dignified—merely nervous. Oh! dismiss the topic.”

“When it is exhausted; not till then. Nervous alarms should always be communicated, that they may be dissipated. I wish I had the gift of persuasion, and could incline you to speak willingly. I believe confession, in your case, would be half equivalent to cure.”

“No,” said Shirley abruptly. “I wish that were at all probable; but I am afraid it is not.”

She paused in her work. Now seated, she rested her elbow on the table and leaned her head on her hand. Mr. Moore, watching, felt he had gained at last some footing on a difficult path: her wish implied an admission; she could no longer affirm that nothing ailed her.

He allowed her minutes for reflection. Once his lips moved to speak, then he checked himself and prolonged the pause. Shirley lifted her eye to his. Had he betrayed injudicious emotion, she might have locked herself into silence; but his look was calm—strong—trustworthy, like a firm hand held out without flourish. Outside, the rain continued, a constant low sound that drowned sharper noises and made the room feel more sealed, more intimate, as if the world were listening elsewhere.

“I had better tell you than my aunt,” she said at last, “or than my cousins, or my uncle. They would all make such a bustle—and it is that very bustle I dread: the alarm, the flurry, the éclat. In short, I never liked to be the centre of a small domestic whirlpool. You can bear a little shock—eh?”

“A great one, if necessary.”

Not a muscle moved; yet his heart beat fast, deep in his chest. What would she confess? Had something been done that could not be undone?

“Had I thought it right to go to you,” she continued, “I would never have made a secret of the matter one moment. I would have told you at once, and asked advice.”

“Why was it not right to come to me?”

Louis Moore had been trained by habit to quiet. Not the quiet that is a feast, chosen and seasoned to taste, but the quiet that is served to you because there is nothing else on the table. He endured it because he was made to endure: a reserved man, with an interior kingdom populous enough to people any outward vacancy. Yet to-night even his self-sufficing mind fretted, like dough that will not rise in a cold room.

Fieldhead lay hushed—hushed as a farm after milking, when the pails are scoured, the doors barred, and only the last drip from a tap answers the last footstep in the flagged passage. Miss Keeldar had gone; the Sympsons were away; Henry too. Sir Philip Nunnely had carried them all to the priory, to be presented to his mother and sisters. The baronet—kind, careful, ever anxious to do right—had even invited the tutor, out of courtesy, as one might offer a chair at a crowded supper to the person who least wants company. Louis had declined. He would rather, he thought, have kept an appointment with anything that did not eat, drink, or speak in the approved social accents of a drawing-room: the ghost of the Earl of Huntingdon under the oldest oak in Nunnely Forest; a pale nun moving soundlessly through the wet weeds of the ruined sanctuary; any legend that walked without clatter.

And yet he longed to have something near him. Not Sir Philip, with his boyish title and his manly good intentions; not Lady Nunnely, benevolent and severe as a housekeeper who counts the spoons; not the patrician sisters; not the Sympsons, whose very voices seemed to him like the scraping of chair-legs on bare boards. He wanted—he wanted one presence only, and that presence was absent.

The night itself was not calm. The equinox still wrestled with the year, and the struggle was audible. The day’s rain had spent its first fury, but the wind kept a long, rushing note, as if some invisible hand drew a bow across the ribs of the house. The moon rode high and brilliant, not in the peaceful, milk-white stillness that makes even a kennel look romantic, but in a tumult of driven cloud, silver-fleeced and breaking apart, then closing again. Her light poured in gusts. It was a weather that made a man listen against his will; it made the ear work, the nerves watch. The storm roared round the farther gable and along the hall-front; this end of Fieldhead, sheltered, received it only as a muffled bass. Louis did not want shelter. He was tired of subdued sounds, tired of screened positions, tired of the small cell of his schoolroom with its dutiful order and its poverty of life.

“All the parlours are empty,” he said, and the words came out of him as if wrung from a cloth too tightly twisted. “I am sick at heart of this cell.”

He left the schoolroom and went through the passages without a candle. He needed no lamp; the moon served him in broad, fluctuating sheets of light, whitening the floors, glossing the panels, making every picture-frame a pale blade. He wandered from room to room, as though following a phantom—not one of the priory legends, but one that had lately learned to walk in his blood.

In the oak room he stopped.

Here there was warmth. The hearth glowed ruddy; the cinders, in their intense heat, made that faint, sharp music which belongs only to burning coal—the tinkle and crack as if the fire spoke a quick dialect of its own, older than any human tongue. The sound was domestic and ancient, and it soothed him more than silence would have done. Near the rug stood a little work-table; on it a desk; by it a chair drawn as if its owner had risen for a moment and meant to sit again before the tea-water boiled or the bread browned. The air held, besides coal-smoke, a trace of something finer—wax, perhaps, and a ghost of crushed leaf, the faint green scent that comes when a fresh sprig is bruised between finger and thumb.

He stood before that chair with a look in his eye as if he had, in the household solitude, found companionship.

A small satin bag hung on the chair-back. The desk stood open; the keys were still in the lock. A pretty seal lay there; a silver pen; a couple of crimson berries on a green leaf—some idle offering of colour, like fruit set aside from the dish because it pleased the eye. A delicate glove, clean as if it had never touched dust, lay half slipped from itself. Such trifles, which strict order would have swept away, were precisely what gave charm: the disarray of a mind that does not fear its own rights.
