Yet the tone held no slipshod familiarity; there was a slight hesitation, a touch of bashfulness, which seemed to him exactly right.

“I am Martin.”

“Are your father and mother well?”—lucky she did not say papa and mamma—“and Rose and Jessie?”

“I suppose so.”

“My cousin Hortense is still at Briarmains?”

“Oh yes.”

He gave a half-smile and a comic little groan. She answered it with a faint smile of her own; she could guess in what odour Hortense was held by the young Yorkes.

“Does your mother like her?”

“They suit so well about the servants they can’t help liking each other.”

“It is cold to-night.”

“Why are you out so late?”

“I lost my way in this wood.”

Then Martin laughed—fresh, scornful.

“Lost your way in the mighty forest of Briarmains! You deserve never more to find it.”

“I never was here before, and I believe I am trespassing now. You might inform against me if you chose, Martin, and have me fined. It is your father’s wood.”

“I should think I knew that. But since you are so simple as to lose your way, I will guide you out.”

“You need not. I have got into the track now. I shall be right. Martin”—a little quickly—“how is Mr. Moore?”

Martin had heard rumours; he decided it might be amusing to try an experiment.

“Going to die. Nothing can save him. All hope flung overboard!”

She put her veil aside. Her eyes searched his, and her lips shaped the words as if they were pain.

“To die!”

“To die. All along of the women—my mother and the rest. They did something about his bandages that finished everything. He would have got better but for them. I am sure they should be arrested, cribbed, tried, and brought in for Botany Bay, at the very least.”

Perhaps she did not hear the judgment. She stood still, as if the winter had struck through her, and after two minutes she moved on without another word—no good-night, no further inquiry.

This was not amusing; not what Martin had calculated on. He had expected some scene, something demonstrative. It was hardly worth frightening the girl if she gave him no entertainment in return.

“Miss Helstone!” he called.

She did not turn. He hurried after her, overtook her among the grey trunks.

“Come; are you uneasy about what I said?”

“You know nothing about death, Martin. You are too young for me to talk to concerning such a thing.”

“Did you believe me? It’s all flummery! Moore eats like three men. They are always making sago or tapioca or something good for him. I never go into the kitchen but there is a saucepan on the fire cooking him some dainty. I think I will play the old soldier, and be fed on the fat of the land like him.”

“Martin! Martin!” Her voice trembled; she stopped short.

“It is exceedingly wrong of you, Martin. You have almost killed me.”

She leaned against a tree, shuddering, trembling, as pale as the moonlight that began to strengthen between the branches.

Martin watched her with inexpressible curiosity. In one sense it was, as he would have said, nuts to see this; it told him so much, and he had begun to relish the discovery of secrets. In another sense it pricked him with a remembrance: once he had heard a blackbird lamenting for her nestlings, which Matthew had crushed with a stone, and the sound had made him feel something he did not like. He could not find words to comfort her; so he hunted in his mind for something to do. He smiled; and in that smile, for all its boyish mischief, there was a sudden transparency.

“Eureka!” he cried. “I’ll set all straight by-and-by. You are better now, Miss Caroline. Walk forward.”

He chose a short cut—forgetting it would be harder for her than for him—that led to no gate. The path ended in a wall and hedge; he had to help her over formidable obstacles, and while he railed at her for helplessness, he liked—perfectly liked—to feel himself of use, to bear a portion of her weight, to steady her as she descended on the other side.

“Martin, before we separate,” she said at last, “assure me seriously, and on your word of honour, that Mr. Moore is better.”

“How very much you think of that Moore!”

“No—but—many of his friends may ask me, and I wish to be able to give an authentic answer.”

“You may tell them he is well enough, only idle. You may tell them that he takes mutton chops for dinner, and the best of arrowroot for supper. I intercepted a basin myself one night on its way upstairs, and ate half of it.”

“And who waits on him, Martin? Who nurses him?”

“Nurses him? The great baby! Why, a woman as round and big as our largest water-butt—a rough, hard-favoured old girl. I make no doubt she leads him a rich life. Nobody else is let near him. He is chiefly in the dark. It is my belief she knocks him about terribly in that chamber. I listen at the wall sometimes when I am in bed, and I think I hear her thumping him. You should see her fist. She could hold half a dozen hands like yours in her one palm. After all, notwithstanding the chops and jellies he gets, I would not be in his shoes. In fact, it is my private opinion she eats most of what goes up on the tray to Mr. Moore. I wish she may not be starving him.”

Caroline was silent—silent in a way that was thought, not sulkiness. Martin watched her slyly, as if her quiet were another page to be read.

“You never see him, I suppose, Martin?” she asked.

“I? No. I don’t care to see him, for my own part.”

Silence again; and in the quiet, the faint sounds of the countryside came clear—some far dog’s bark, the thin rasp of a branch, the small crunch of their own steps. The world was still enough that a heart might hear itself.

“Did not you come to our house once with Mrs. Pryor, about five weeks since, to ask after him?” Martin inquired.

“Yes.”

“I dare say you wished to be shown upstairs?”

“We did wish it. We entreated it; but your mother declined.”

“Ay! she declined. I heard it all. She treated you as it is her pleasure to treat visitors now and then. She behaved to you rudely and harshly.”

“She was not kind; for you know, Martin, we are relations, and it is natural we should take an interest in Mr. Moore. But here we must part; we are at your father’s gate.”

“Very well—what of that? I shall walk home with you.”

“They will miss you, and wonder where you are.”

“Let them. I can take care of myself, I suppose.”

Martin knew he had already earned his punishment: the lecture, and dry bread for tea. No matter; the evening had furnished him with an adventure—better than muffins and toast.

He walked home with Caroline; and on the way he promised—against the dragon who guarded the chamber—that he would see Mr. Moore. He named an hour the next day when Caroline was to come again to Briarmains Wood for tidings; he would meet her at a certain tree. The scheme led to nothing; still he liked it—liked the secrecy, the appointment, the little thread spun between his blunt boyhood and her anxious woman’s heart.

When he reached home, the dry bread and the lecture were duly administered, and he was dismissed to bed early. He accepted his punishment with the toughest stoicism.

Before he went up, he slipped into the dining-room—still, cold, stately, seldom used, for the family customarily ate in the back parlour where the kitchen smells could reach and the talk was freer. In the dining-room the air had no savour of supper; it held only polish, dust, and the faint ghost of old fires. He stood before the mantelpiece and lifted his candle to two pictures hung above: female heads. One was serene beauty—happy, innocent; the other more lovely, but forlorn, desperate, with a look as if love had been made into pain.

“She looked like that,” he murmured, staring at the latter sketch, “when she sobbed, turned white, and leaned against the tree.”

For Martin Yorke’s scheme to run smoothly, it was indispensable that he remain at home that day—present in body, absent in duty. Therefore, at breakfast he discovered, with the quick ingenuity of fifteen, that his appetite had vanished, and, just as the hour for school drew near, a pain, sharp and solemn, fixed itself upon his heart. It was a pain that made a journey to Mr. Summer’s grammar class not only imprudent but nearly impossible; a pain that required—so Martin alleged—the soft authority of his father’s arm-chair, the fire’s respectful warmth, and the morning paper spread broad as a shield across his knees.

Mark departed for school, unsuspecting. Mr. Yorke and Matthew withdrew to the counting-house. The house, thus cleared of its more formidable masculine obstacles, still left Martin with several exploits to achieve before four o’clock: first, to obtain the breakfast he had theatrically refused; and then, in good order, to remove his mother, Miss Moore, and Mrs. Horsfall from the field, so that one private hour might be won without interruption.

The first of these tasks pressed most urgently upon his empty stomach. A young plotter may plan on an empty head, but not on an empty belly; and Martin, who could be fastidious in his pleasures, was resolved that his delayed meal should be no pale penitence of bread-and-milk. He knew the way to the larder. He took it.

The kitchen doors were shut; within, the servants were breakfasting with the heavy, closed-mouthed solemnity of people who have work ahead and no time for elegance. Outside, upon the lawn, Mrs. Yorke and Miss Moore paced and aired themselves, and—being women and therefore, in their own view, keepers of domestic order—commented upon those same shut doors with dissatisfaction.

Safe in the larder’s cool hush, where the air smelled of stored fruit, sugar, and yesterday’s roasted meat, Martin made a connoisseur’s survey. On a shelf lay rosy apples nested in straw; he chose three—firm, with that faint cider-scent that promises crispness. Pastry sat under a cover: he lifted it and selected an apricot puff, its glaze catching what little light there was, and a damson tart, dark as wine beneath its lattice. He passed the plain loaf without a glance, but paused at currant tea-cakes—plump, speckled, and faintly sticky with butter—and condescended to one.

Then came the savoury. With the help of his clasp-knife—an instrument as useful to boyhood as to brigandage—he carved himself a wing of fowl and a slice of ham, the latter edged with fat that had set into a pearly rim. A cantlet of cold custard-pudding, yellow and trembling even in its chill, seemed to him an excellent finish: sweet after salt, softness after chew. Armed with this assorted treasure, he sallied into the hall.

He was already almost across it—three steps more and he would have anchored in the back parlour’s safe harbour—when the front door opened and Matthew stood there.

It would have been easier to confront the Old Gentleman, horned and hoofed, than Matthew Yorke with his dry eye and quicker sarcasm. Matthew had not subscribed, even for a moment, to that sudden “pain about the heart.” Earlier he had muttered words in which “shamming Abraham” had rung distinctly, and Martin’s succession to the arm-chair and paper had given him something like a mental cramp.

Now he saw the evidence: apples, tarts, tea-cake, fowl, ham, custard-pudding—an invalid’s provisions, indeed.

Martin paused one instant, trapped; the next, he recovered. He had the instinct—half wicked, half brilliant—of turning any mishap into leverage. A collision with Matthew, he knew, was a spark that never failed to set his mother’s nerves alight. Mrs. Yorke, roused to tears and tremors in the morning, would seek repose in bed by afternoon; and that bed, in Martin’s economy, was as good as a locked door. He could, with a quarrel, remove her from the day.

So the collision took place there in the hall, as if summoned. Matthew’s sneer came like a slap; Martin met it with a languid, cutting reply. They rushed to battle. Usually Martin made little noise in such engagements, but now he clattered like a pot-lid orchestra—raising the kind of domestic alarm that brings skirts and aprons running.

The servants flew in. Mrs. Yorke appeared. Miss Moore followed. No female hand could separate them. Mr. Yorke was summoned from his work, irritated, authoritative, and weary of boyish drama.

“Sons,” he said, standing between them, “one of you must leave my roof if this occurs again. I will have no Cain and Abel strife here.”

Martin allowed himself to be taken off as the injured party—youngest, slightest, cool as a small knife. He had no passion left; he even smiled. In his mind the hardest portion of his labour was done.

Once, later in the morning, he seemed to flag. “It is not worth while to bother myself for that Caroline,” he remarked, as if the name were a penny he could choose to spend or keep.

Yet a quarter of an hour afterwards he was again in the dining-room, watching his mother’s dishevelled head and eyes turbid with despair, and the sight restored his resolution.

“Yes,” he told himself, “I made her sob, shudder, almost faint. I’ll see her smile before I’ve done with her; besides, I want to outwit all these womenites.”

After dinner, Mrs. Yorke fulfilled his calculation. She withdrew to her chamber, to recover with pillows and solitude from the morning’s storm.

Now for Hortense.

Miss Moore—Hortense—was comfortably settled in the back parlour with her stocking-mending, the steady snick of needles and the low domestic quiet around her. Martin lay stretched upon the sofa, a book in his hand, affecting the voluptuous ease of a young pacha in exile, too delicate to sit upright.

Lazily, in the course of talk that seemed to wander without intention, he introduced Sarah, the maid at the Hollow. He insinuated—always by degrees, always with that air of letting fall mere trifles—that Sarah was said to have three suitors: Frederic Murgatroyd, Jeremiah Pighills, and John-of-Mally’s-of-Hannah’s-of-Deb’s; and that Miss Mann had affirmed she knew for a fact that, now the girl was left in sole charge of the cottage, she often had her swains to meals and entertained them with the best the house afforded.

It needed no more. Miss Moore’s sense of propriety, sharpened by curiosity and a sort of moral appetite, could not endure an hour longer without inspection. The scandal was like an odour carried on a draught—she must follow it to the source. Up she rose, bonneted and indignant, and set off for the Hollow with the speed of a woman convinced she goes on righteous business.

One remained: Mrs. Horsfall, Mr. Moore’s nurse—set and stubborn in her duties, and not easily coaxed away by gossip.

Martin, now master of the field, obtained from his mother’s work-basket a bunch of keys. With them he opened the sideboard cupboard and brought out a black bottle and a small glass. He arranged them on the table with brisk precision—bait laid neatly. Then he ran upstairs to Mr. Moore’s door and tapped. The nurse opened.

“If you please, ma’am,” said Martin, with an excellent imitation of respectful seriousness, “you are invited to step into the back parlour and take some refreshment. You will not be disturbed; the family are out.”

He watched her down. He watched her in. Then he shut the door upon her as calmly as if he had locked away a threat.

The hard work was done. Now for the pleasure.

He snatched his cap and went out—away, away—to the wood. It was only half-past three.

The morning had been fine, but now the sky thickened with a sullen darkness. Snow began to fall; the wind blew cold, and the wood looked dismal, the old tree grim. Yet Martin approved the shadow. He liked the spectral aspect of the doddered oak; it suited his drama, and made him feel more important, like a conspirator in a legend.

To and fro he walked along the narrow track as flakes fell faster, while the wind, which at first had only moaned, now howled pitifully through bare branches—an untuned, violent music that made the heart feel lonely even when the body was warm.

“She is long in coming,” he muttered, scanning the path.

Then, as if he could not resist making his own motives sound darker than they were, he added, “I wonder what I wish to see her so much for? She is not coming for me. But I have power over her, and I want her to come that I may use that power.”

He walked on. The storm thickened. The oak creaked like an old ship.

“Now,” he resumed when more time had passed, “if she fails to come, I shall hate and scorn her.”

The church clock, far away, struck four. At the same moment he heard a step so quick, so light, that but for the faint rustle of leaves it would scarcely have sounded on the wood-walk. The wind blew fiercely now; the snow drove in bewildering sheets; but on she came, not dismayed.

“Well, Martin,” Caroline Helstone said eagerly, breath quick from haste and cold, “how is he?”

“It is queer how she thinks of him,” reflected Martin. “The blinding snow and bitter cold are nothing to her, I believe; yet she is but a chitty-faced creature, as my mother would say. I could find in my heart to wish I had a cloak to wrap her in.”

So occupied was he with this sudden, inconvenient tenderness that he neglected to answer at once.

“You have seen him?” she insisted.

“No.”

“Oh! you promised you would.”

“I mean to do better by you than that. Didn’t I say I don’t care to see him?”

“But now it will be so long before I get to know anything certain, and I am sick of waiting. Martin, do see him, and give him Caroline Helstone’s regards, and say she wished to know how he was, and if anything could be done for his comfort.”

“I won’t.”

She stared at him in the storm, hurt and impatient. “You are changed. You were so friendly last night.”

“Come,” he said, brisk now, “we must not stand in this wood; it is too cold.”

“But before I go, promise me to come again to-morrow with news.”

“No such thing. I am much too delicate to make and keep such appointments in the winter season. If you knew what a pain I had in my chest this morning, and how I went without breakfast, and was knocked down besides, you’d feel the impropriety of bringing me here in the snow. Come, I say.”

“Are you really delicate, Martin?”

“Don’t I look so?”

“You have rosy cheeks.”

“That’s hectic. Will you come—or you won’t?”

“Where?”

“With me. I was a fool not to bring a cloak. I would have made you cosy.”

“You are going home; my nearest road lies in the opposite direction.”

“Put your arm through mine; I’ll take care of you.”

“But the wall—the hedge—it is such hard work climbing, and you are too slender and young to help me without hurting yourself.”

“You shall go through the gate.”

“But—”

“But, but—will you trust me or not?”

Caroline looked into his face. Under the boy’s audacity, she saw earnestness—mischief, yes, but also a strange, determined kindness.

“I think I will,” she said at last. “Anything rather than return as anxious as I came.”

“I can’t answer for that,” he replied. “This, however, I promise you: be ruled by me, and you shall see Moore yourself.”

“See him myself?”

“Yourself.”

“But, dear Martin, does he know?”

“Ah! I’m dear now. No, he doesn’t know.”

“And your mother and the others?”

“All is right.”

Caroline fell into a long silence, walking on with her guide. The snow thickened on her shawl; her breath came in white clouds. They came in sight of Briarmains, its outlines blurred by storm.

“Have you made up your mind?” Martin asked.

She did not answer at first. He pressed, low and peremptory: “Decide; we are just on the spot. I won’t see him—that I tell you—except to announce your arrival.”

“Martin, you are a strange boy, and this is a strange step; but all I feel is and has been, for a long time, strange. I will see him.”

“Having said that, you will neither hesitate nor retract?”

“No.”

“Here we are, then. Do not be afraid of passing the parlour window; no one will see you. My father and Matthew are at the mill, Mark is at school, the servants are in the back kitchen, Miss Moore is at the cottage, my mother in her bed, and Mrs. Horsfall in paradise. Observe—I need not ring. I open the door; the hall is empty, the staircase quiet; so is the gallery. The whole house and all its inhabitants are under a spell, which I will not break till you are gone.”

“Martin, I trust you.”

“You never said a better word. Let me take your shawl. I will shake off the snow and dry it for you. You are cold and wet. Never mind; there is a fire upstairs. Are you ready?”

“Yes.”

“Follow me.”

He left his shoes on the mat and mounted the stairs unshod, turning himself into a silent shadow. Caroline stole after him, step for step, her heart beating louder than the wind. There was a gallery, and then a passage; at the end of that passage Martin paused and tapped at a door.

He had to tap twice—thrice. At last a voice, known to one listener as well as to herself, said, “Come in.”

Martin entered briskly.

“Mr. Moore,” he announced, “a lady called to inquire after you. None of the women were about. It is washing-day, and the maids are over the crown of the head in soap-suds in the back kitchen, so I asked her to step up.”

“Up here, sir?” Moore’s tone—weak, yet affronted—answered.

“Up here, sir; but if you object, she shall go down again.”

“Is this a place—or am I a person—to bring a lady to, you absurd lad?”

“No; so I’ll take her off.”

“Martin, you will stay here. Who is she?”

“Your grandmother from that château on the Scheldt Miss Moore talks about.”

“Martin,” came a soft whisper at the door—Caroline’s voice, trembling, “don’t be foolish.”

“Is she there?” Moore asked hastily. He had caught the sound like a man catching a name in a dream.

“She is there, fit to faint. She is standing on the mat, shocked at your want of filial affection.”

“Martin, you are an evil cross between an imp and a page. What is she like?”

“More like me than you; for she is young and beautiful.”

“You are to show her forward. Do you hear?”

“Come, Miss Caroline.”

“Miss Caroline!” repeated Moore; and when she entered, he met her half-way across the chamber—a tall, thin, wasted figure with hollow eyes, yet a sudden light in them. He took both her hands as if he needed the contact to prove she was real.

“I give you a quarter of an hour,” said Martin, retreating. “No more. Say what you have to say in that time. Till it is past I will wait in the gallery; nothing shall approach; I’ll see you safe away. Should you persist in staying longer, I leave you to your fate.”

He shut the door, and outside stood as elate as a king. Never had he liked an adventure so well; none had made him feel so necessary, so central, so powerful.

“You are come at last,” Moore said, gazing on her as if hunger itself had eyes.

“Did you expect me before?”

“For a month—near two months—we have been very near; and I have been in sad pain, and danger, and misery, Cary.”

“I could not come.”

“Couldn’t you? But the rectory and Briarmains are very near—not two miles apart.”

Pain and pleasure mingled in her face. It was bitter to hear reproach; sweet to have a right to answer it.

“When I say I could not come, I mean I could not see you; for I came with mamma the very day we heard what had happened. Mr. MacTurk then told us it was impossible to admit any stranger.”

“But afterwards—every fine afternoon these many weeks past I have waited and listened. Something here, Cary”—he laid his hand on his breast—“told me it was impossible but that you should think of me. Not that I merit thought; but we are old acquaintance—we are cousins.”

“I came again, Robert; mamma and I came again.”

“Did you? Come, that is worth hearing. Since you came again, we will sit down and talk about it.”

They sat. Caroline drew her chair close, as if nearness itself might strengthen him. Outside, the storm deepened; the wind drove the snow in long, wuthering rushes against the panes. Neither seemed to hear that wild sound-world. Each was conscious only of the other’s breathing—proof of life.

“So mamma and you came again?”

“And Mrs. Yorke did treat us strangely. We asked to see you. ‘No,’ said she, ‘not in my house. I am at present responsible for his life; it shall not be forfeited for half an hour’s idle gossip.’ But I must not tell you all she said; it was very disagreeable. However, we came yet again—mamma, Miss Keeldar, and I. This time we thought we should conquer, as we were three against one, and Shirley was on our side. But Mrs. Yorke opened such a battery.”

Moore smiled faintly. “What did she say?”

“Things that astonished us. Shirley laughed at last; I cried; mamma was seriously annoyed. We were all three driven from the field. Since that time I have only walked once a day past the house, just for the satisfaction of looking up at your window, which I could distinguish by the drawn curtains. I really dared not come in.”

“I have wished for you, Caroline.”

“I did not know that,” she said quickly. “I never dreamt one instant that you thought of me. If I had but most distantly imagined such a possibility—”

“Mrs. Yorke would still have beaten you.”

“She would not. Stratagem should have been tried, if persuasion failed. I would have come to the kitchen door; the servants should have let me in, and I would have walked straight upstairs. In fact, it was far more the fear of intrusion—the fear of yourself—that baffled me than the fear of Mrs. Yorke.”

“Only last night I despaired of ever seeing you again. Weakness has wrought terrible depression in me—terrible depression.”

“And you sit alone?”

“Worse than alone.”

“But you must be getting better, since you can leave your bed?”

“I doubt whether I shall live. I see nothing for it, after such exhaustion, but decline.”

“You—you shall go home to the Hollow.”

“Dreariness would accompany, nothing cheerful come near me.”

“I will alter this. This shall be altered, were there ten Mrs. Yorkes to do battle with.”

“Cary, you make me smile.”

“Do smile; smile again. Shall I tell you what I should like?”

“Tell me anything—only keep talking. I am Saul; but for music I should perish.”

“I should like you to be brought to the rectory, and given to me and mamma.”

“A precious gift! I have not laughed since they shot me till now.”

“Do you suffer pain, Robert?”

“Not so much pain now; but I am hopelessly weak, and the state of my mind is inexpressible—dark, barren, impotent. Do you not read it all in my face? I look a mere ghost.”

“Altered; yet I should have known you anywhere. But I understand your feelings; I experienced something like it. Since we met, I too have been very ill.”

“Very ill?”

“I thought I should die. The tale of my life seemed told. Every night, just at midnight, I used to wake from awful dreams; and the book lay open before me at the last page, where was written ‘Finis.’ I had strange feelings.”

“You speak my experience.”

“I believed I should never see you again; and I grew so thin—as thin as you are now. I could do nothing for myself—neither rise nor lie down; and I could not eat. Yet you see I am better.”

“Comforter—sad as sweet. I am too feeble to say what I feel; but while you speak I do feel.”

“Here I am at your side, where I thought never more to be. Here I speak to you. I see you listen to me willingly—look at me kindly. Did I count on that? I despaired.”

Moore sighed—so deep it was nearly a groan. He covered his eyes with his hand.

“May I be spared to make some atonement,” he murmured; it sounded like a prayer forced up from weakness.

“And for what?”

“We will not touch on it now, Cary; unmanned as I am, I have not the power to cope with such a topic. Was Mrs. Pryor with you during your illness?”

“Yes.” Caroline’s face brightened, tender as a lamp lit in a window. “You know she is mamma?”

“I have heard—Hortense told me; but that tale too I will receive from yourself. Does she add to your happiness?”

“What! mamma? She is dear to me; how dear I cannot say. I was altogether weary, and she held me up.”

“I deserve to hear that in a moment when I can scarce lift my hand to my head. I deserve it.”

“It is no reproach against you.”

“It is a coal of fire heaped on my head; and so is every word you address to me, and every look that lights your sweet face. Come still nearer, Lina; and give me your hand—if my thin fingers do not scare you.”

She took his thin fingers between her two hands, as if warming them by touch alone, and bent her head and kissed them—lightly, as a petal might brush water.

Moore was much moved. A large tear or two coursed down his hollow cheek.

“I’ll keep these things in my heart, Cary; that kiss I will put by, and you shall hear of it again one day.”

“Come out!” cried Martin, abruptly opening the door. “Come away; you have had twenty minutes instead of a quarter of an hour.”

“She will not stir yet, you hempseed,” Moore said, roused to a faint flash of authority.

“I dare not stay longer, Robert,” Caroline answered, rising with reluctance that made her voice shake.

“Can you promise to return?”

“No, she can’t,” responded Martin. “The thing mustn’t become customary. I can’t be troubled. It’s very well for once; I’ll not have it repeated.”

“You’ll not have it repeated—” Moore began, indignant.

“Hush,” Caroline pleaded softly. “Don’t vex him; we could not have met to-day but for him. But I will come again, if it is your wish that I should come.”

“It is my wish—my one wish—almost the only wish I can feel.”

“Come this minute,” Martin urged, suddenly alert with genuine alarm. “My mother has coughed, got up, set her feet on the floor. Let her only catch you on the stairs, Miss Caroline. You’re not to bid him good-bye”—he stepped between her and Moore—“you are to march.”

“My shawl, Martin.”

“I have it. I’ll put it on for you when you are in the hall.”

He made them part. He would suffer no farewell but what could be said in looks—hers, full of promise and pain; his, hungry with gratitude and longing. Then he half carried Caroline down the stairs, swift as a conspirator.

In the hall he wrapped her shawl round her, shook the last snow from its folds, and drew it close as if he could fend off the cold with his own hands. But his mother’s tread creaked above in the gallery, and Martin—held back by a sudden, strange diffidence, the proper natural impulse of a boy’s heart—swallowed the demand that rose to his lips: Now, for all this, give me one kiss.

Before he could speak, she was gone—across the snowy road, rather skimming than wading the drifts, the storm taking her like white water taking a leaf.

“She is my debtor,” he said to himself, watching her vanish into the swirl, “and I will be paid.”

He flattered himself it was opportunity, not audacity, that had failed him. He misjudged his own nature, and held it for something lower than it was.

Martin Yorke, having once tasted the sharp relish of consequence, craved it again as a boy craves the first illicit spoonful from a cooling pan. Authority had sat on his tongue like a warm spice, and now he could not endure the thin, insipid bread-and-milk of common days. Miss Helstone—she whom he had always, with schoolboy cruelty and family candour, set down as ugly—had crossed, for one electric moment, into the circle of his notice; and since then her face haunted him as persistently as a scent clings to wool: in lamplight, in frost-glare, in the soot-dim of afternoon, always the same remembered outline, always the same provoking life in her eyes.

He was no ordinary schoolboy, though he sat among ordinary schoolboys. At his desk in the grammar school he appeared idle, his slate untouched; but within, he was measuring and mixing, like a cook with a new dish in mind, trying this pinch of boldness and that pinch of revenge, seeking the right proportion to draw her again within reach. Many a life-romance, he did not yet know, is set going with a grand first course, and then—through want of fire, or want of courage—never reaches the second.

On the Saturday half-holiday he went to the wood with a book of fairy legends in his pocket, and another book—unwritten, inexhaustible—opening and shutting in his head. The branches above him clacked and whispered; the brook made its thin, clear music under ice; a jay scolded; distant axes bit wood with a steady, muffled thud. Those sounds, clean and various, were meat and drink to him. The town’s mill-clank and loom-roar, heard faintly when the wind set that way, had always seemed an offence—noise that did not belong to place or season, noise that drove thought like smoke drives bees.

He harboured, too, a frank and almost irreligious reluctance for Sunday. His father and mother, while protesting that the Establishment was no better than any other contrivance for saving souls, never failed, once on the sacred day, to fill their large pew in Briarfield Church with the whole blooming family. Mr. Yorke in theory set all sects level as ploughed land; Mrs. Yorke awarded her palm to Moravians and Quakers for their plainness, as though humility were a homely broth best served without garnish. Yet neither had ever been known to set foot in a conventicle.

Martin disliked Sunday because the morning service was long and the sermon commonly to his taste as thin ale is to a boy who has smelt stronger drink. Yet this Saturday, out among the stripped trees, his musings found a new charm in the coming day; and the charm had a name.

Sunday came in deep snow—snow that made the world look baked in white, as though a patient hand had sifted flour over every hedge and furrow. At breakfast Mrs. Yorke, surveying the drifted lanes through the window, announced her conviction that the children, boys and girls, would be better at home; and her decision that, instead of going to church, they should sit silent for two hours in the back parlour, while Rose and Martin alternately read a succession of sermons—John Wesley’s. Wesley, being a reformer and agitator, had a place in both their parents’ favour; his words, like mustard seed, had bite.

“Rose will do as she pleases,” said Martin, not lifting his eyes from the book he was studying over his bread and milk. He broke the bread slowly, as if he were cutting measured portions for a recipe; he sipped the milk with the same deliberate calm. Even as a boy he ate like one who listened to food—its temperature, its texture—more than to talk.

“Rose will do as she is told, and Martin too,” observed the mother.

“I am going to church.”

The quietness with which he said it was the quietness of a true Yorke: a will set like yeast to work, unseen, until the dough must rise. If pushed to the wall he would let himself be crushed, if there were no gap; but he would not capitulate while a single crevice remained.

“It is not fit weather,” said the father.

No answer. Martin read; he broke and sipped; the fire popped; outside, the wind worried the windows.

“Martin hates to go to church, but he hates still more to obey,” said Mrs. Yorke.

“I suppose I am influenced by pure perverseness?”

“Yes, you are.”

“Mother, I am not.”

“By what, then, are you influenced?”

“By a complication of motives, the intricacies of which I should as soon think of explaining to you as I should of turning myself inside out to exhibit the internal machinery of my frame.”

“Hear Martin! hear him!” cried Mr. Yorke. “I must see and have this lad of mine brought up to the bar. Nature meant him to live by his tongue. Hesther, your third son must certainly be a lawyer; he has the stock-in-trade—brass, self-conceit, and words—words—words.”

“Some bread, Rose, if you please,” requested Martin, with intense gravity. His voice was naturally low, plaintive; in his dour moods it sank to a lady’s whisper, and the more stubborn the humour, the softer, sadder the tone—as though he would not waste breath on opposition, but would hoard it, like a miser hoards spice, for some future feast.

He rang the bell and gently asked for his walking-shoes.

“But, Martin,” urged his sire, “there is drift all the way; a man could hardly wade through it. However, lad,” he continued, seeing the boy rise as the church bell began to toll, “this is a case wherein I would by no means balk the obdurate chap of his will. Go to church by all means. There is a pitiless wind, and a sharp, frozen sleet, besides the depth under foot. Go out into it, since thou prefers it to a warm fireside.”

Martin quietly assumed cloak, comforter, and cap, and deliberately went out.

“My father has more sense than my mother,” he pronounced to himself, as he shut the door on the warmth and the smell of toast. “How women miss it! They drive the nail into the flesh, thinking they are hammering away at insensate stone.”

The lane was half buried; the snow had drifted in waves; sleet, like grit, stung his cheeks. Yet even that harsh weather had its own voice—wind keening round gables, the hollow hush of fields muffled deep, his own footfall crunching and complaining. Better this, he thought, than the ceaseless mechanical din of the mill: that other kind of sound which did not change with season or mercy, but went on with the obstinacy of money.

He reached church early.

“Now, if the weather frightens her,” he said, looking up at the leaden sky, “or if that Mrs. Pryor objects to her going out, and I should miss her after all, it will vex me; but tempest or tornado, hail or ice, she ought to come; and if she has a mind worthy of her eyes and features she will come. She will be here for the chance of seeing me, as I am here for the chance of seeing her. She will want to get a word respecting her confounded sweetheart, as I want to get another flavour of what I think the essence of life—a taste of existence, with the spirit preserved in it, and not evaporated. Adventure is to stagnation what champagne is to flat porter.”

The church was cold, silent, empty but for one old woman, who sat hunched in the free sittings like a sparrow enduring winter. As the chimes subsided and the single bell tolled slowly, another and another elderly parishioner came dropping in—grey heads, bent shoulders, hands reddened with washing and frost. It is always the frailest, the oldest, and the poorest who brave the worst weather, to prove and maintain their constancy to dear old mother church. Not one affluent family attended; not one carriage party appeared; all the lined and cushioned pews stood bare. Only on the hard oaken seats sat ranged the elders and feeble paupers, their coughs small in the vast chill, their shawls smelling faintly of peat smoke and damp wool.

“I’ll scorn her if she doesn’t come,” muttered Martin, and his breath made a brief cloud.

The rector’s shovel-hat passed the porch. Mr. Helstone and his clerk were in the vestry. The bells ceased; the reading-desk was filled; the doors were closed; the service commenced.

Void stood the rectory pew. She was not there.

Martin scorned her—scorned her as only a boy can, with the extravagance of a first disappointment.

“Worthless thing! vapid thing! commonplace humbug! Like all other girls—weakly, selfish, shallow!” Such was Martin’s liturgy. “She is not like our picture. Her eyes are not large and expressive; her nose is not straight, delicate, Hellenic; her mouth has not that charm I thought it had, which I imagined could beguile me of sullenness in my worst moods. What is she? A thread-paper, a doll, a toy, a girl, in short.”

So absorbed was the young cynic that he forgot to rise from his knees at the proper place, and remained in an exemplary attitude of devotion when, the litany over, the first hymn was given out. To be caught thus did not soothe him; he started up red, for he was as sensitive to ridicule as any girl.

To make the matter worse, the church door reopened and the aisles began to fill: patter, patter, patter—many little feet trotting in, quick as mice. The Sunday scholars. According to Briarfield winter custom, these children had been kept till now where there was a warm stove, then led into church just before the communion and sermon. Their small bodies brought with them the warm scent of damp mittens, the faint sweetness of cheap treacle, the steam of breath caught in woollen scarves. They were settled first, and at last—when the boys and younger girls were all arranged—when the organ was swelling high and the congregation rose to lift a spiritual song—a tall class of young women came quietly in, closing the procession.

Their teacher, having seen them seated, passed into the rectory pew.

The French-grey cloak and small beaver bonnet were known to Martin; it was the very costume his eyes had ached to catch. Miss Helstone had not suffered the storm to prove an impediment. After all, she was come to church.

Martin probably whispered his satisfaction to his hymn-book; at any rate, he hid his face in it for two minutes, as if the paper could cover the sudden heat that rose through him like wine.

Satisfied or not, he had time to get very angry with her again before the sermon was done. She had never once looked his way; at least he had not been so lucky as to encounter a glance.

“If,” he said—“if she takes no notice of me, if she shows I am not in her thoughts, I shall have a worse, a meaner opinion of her than ever. Most despicable would it be to come for the sake of those sheep-faced Sunday scholars, and not for my sake—or for that long skeleton Moore’s.”

The sermon ended. The benediction was pronounced. The congregation dispersed, sounds waking again in the nave: boots scraping, a child’s suppressed yelp, the creak of a pew-door, a woman’s whisper like paper rubbed together.

She had not been near him.

Now, indeed, as Martin set his face homeward, he felt that the sleet was sharp and the east wind cold. His nearest way lay through some fields—a dangerous way, because untrodden; the drifts might hide a ditch, the crust might break underfoot. He did not care. He would take it.

Near the second stile rose a clump of trees. Was that an umbrella waiting there? Yes—an umbrella, held with evident difficulty against the blast; behind it fluttered a French-grey cloak.

Martin grinned as he toiled up the steep, encumbered field, difficult to the foot as a slope on Etna. When he gained the stile he seated himself coolly upon it, as though it were a chair set out for him at a table, and thus opened a conference which, for his own part, he was willing to prolong indefinitely.

“I think you had better strike a bargain. Exchange me for Mrs. Pryor.”

“I was not sure whether you would come this way, Martin, but I thought I would run the chance. There is no such thing as getting a quiet word spoken in the church or churchyard.”

“Will you agree?—make over Mrs. Pryor to my mother, and put me in her skirts?”

“As if I could understand you! What puts Mrs. Pryor into your head?”

“You call her ‘mamma,’ don’t you?”

“She is my mamma.”

“Not possible—or so inefficient, so careless a mamma; I should make a five times better one. You may laugh. I have no objection to see you laugh. Your teeth—I hate ugly teeth; but yours are as pretty as a pearl necklace, and a necklace of which the pearls are very fair, even, and well matched too.”

“Martin, what now? I thought the Yorkes never paid compliments?”

“They have not done till this generation; but I feel as if it were my vocation to turn out a new variety of the Yorke species. I am rather tired of my own ancestors. We have traditions going back for four ages—tales of Hiram, which was the son of Hiram, which was the son of Samuel, which was the son of John, which was the son of Zerubbabel Yorke. All, from Zerubbabel down to the last Hiram, were such as you see my father. Before that there was a Godfrey. We have his picture; it hangs in Moore’s bedroom; it is like me. Of his character we know nothing; but I am sure it was different to his descendants. He has long, curling dark hair; he is carefully and cavalierly dressed. Having said that he is like me, I need not add that he is handsome.”

“You are not handsome, Martin.”

“No; but wait awhile—just let me take my time. I mean to begin from this day to cultivate, to polish, and we shall see.”

“You are a very strange, a very unaccountable boy, Martin. But don’t imagine you ever will be handsome; you cannot.”

“I mean to try. But we were talking about Mrs. Pryor. She must be the most unnatural mamma in existence, coolly to let her daughter come out in this weather. Mine was in such a rage because I would go to church; she was fit to fling the kitchen brush after me.”

“Mamma was very much concerned about me; but I am afraid I was obstinate. I would go.”

“To see me?”

“Exactly; I thought of nothing else. I greatly feared the snow would hinder you from coming. You don’t know how pleased I was to see you all by yourself in the pew.”

“I came to fulfil my duty, and set the parish a good example.”

And, though he spoke with that habitual, provoking gravity, he felt the words she had given him settle warmly inside—like a spoonful of something rich swallowed in secret.

“And so you were obstinate, were you? I should like to see you obstinate, I should. Wouldn’t I have you in good discipline if I owned you? Let me take the umbrella.”

“I can’t stay two minutes; our dinner will be ready.”

“And so will ours; and we have always a hot dinner on Sundays. Roast goose to-day, with apple-pie and rice-pudding. I always contrive to know the bill of fare. Well, I like these things uncommonly; but I’ll make the sacrifice, if you will.”

“We have a cold dinner. My uncle will allow no unnecessary cooking on the Sabbath. But I must return; the house would be in commotion if I failed to appear.”

“So will Briarmains, bless you! I think I hear my father sending out the overlooker and five of the dyers, to look in six directions for the body of his prodigal son in the snow; and my mother repenting her of her many misdeeds towards me, now I am gone.”

“Martin, how is Mr. Moore?”

That name—spoken with such guarded hunger—was the true seasoning of her errand. The wind, for a moment, seemed to hush to listen; then it came again, driving the sleet in fine needles across the field.

“That is what you came for, just to say that word.”

“Come, tell me quickly.”

“Hang him! he is no worse; but as ill-used as ever—mewed up, kept in solitary confinement. They mean to make either an idiot or a maniac of him, and take out a commission of lunacy. Horsfall starves him; you saw how thin he was.”

“You were very good the other day, Martin.”

“What day? I am always good—a model.”

“When will you be so good again?”

“I see what you are after; but you’ll not wheedle me—I am no cat’s-paw.”

“But it must be done. It is quite a right thing, and a necessary thing.”

“How you encroach! Remember, I managed the matter of my own free will before.”

“And you will again.”

“I won’t. The business gave me far too much trouble. I like my ease.”

“Mr. Moore wishes to see me, Martin, and I wish to see him.”

“I dare say,” he returned coolly, as though her wish were a common thing like wanting salt.

“It is too bad of your mother to exclude his friends.”

“Tell her so.”

“His own relations.”

“Come and blow her up.”

“You know that would advance nothing. Well, I shall stick to my point. See him I will. If you won’t help me, I’ll manage without help.”

“Do; there is nothing like self-reliance, self-dependence.”

“I have no time to reason with you now; but I consider you provoking. Good-morning.”

Away she went, the umbrella shut, for she could not carry it against the wind; she held her cloak close, and the snow took the edge of her outline as she moved, turning her into something half-real, half-legendary—a figure that might have stepped from the fairy tales in his pocket and yet would be expected, quite practically, at a cold Sunday table.

“She is not vapid; she is not shallow,” said Martin, watching her. “I shall like to watch, and mark how she will work her way without help. If the storm were not of snow, but of fire—such as came refreshingly down on the cities of the plain—she would go through it to procure five minutes’ speech of that Moore.

“Now, I consider I have had a pleasant morning. The disappointments got time on; the fears and fits of anger only made that short discourse pleasanter, when it came at last. She expected to coax me at once. She’ll not manage that in one effort. She shall come again, again, and yet again. It would please me to put her in a passion—to make her cry. I want to discover how far she will go—what she will do and dare—to get her will. It seems strange and new to find one human being thinking so much about another as she thinks about Moore.”

He turned at last towards home; and now his appetite, practical as a clock, reminded him of the hour. He could already imagine the kitchen warmth, the smell of goose fat crisping skin, the tart breath of apples under pastry, the thick comfort of rice-pudding—simple fare, but honest; the sort of Sunday abundance that held a family together more surely than any sermon read aloud in a back parlour.

“It is time to go home,” he concluded. “Won’t I walk into that goose? and we’ll try whether Matthew or I shall get the largest cut of the apple-pie to-day.”

Martin had counted on his plan as a careful man counts his eggs before the hen has warmed them; but plans—even those whipped smooth and shining as butter under a practiced hand—will sometimes be overturned by a common, stubborn appetite in another. Fate, in this house, did not arrive with thunder: she came like a housewife with a broom, brisk and irresistible, and the broom was made of Robert Moore’s own recovered will.

Each morning he startled Mrs. Horsfall with some fresh act of disobedience. First he dismissed her from the business of his toilette; he would dress himself, though his fingers still shook a little as if remembering pain. Then he would not take the coffee she carried up—coffee kept, by her rule, as black and scalding as a reprimand—but insisted on going down to breakfast with the family. At last he forbade her the chamber altogether. And the same day—amid the outcries of all the women, who clucked and fluttered like a disturbed poultry-yard—he put his head out of doors into the bite of the frost.

The next morning he followed Mr. Yorke to the counting-house, the sharp scrape of boots on the flagstones sounding, to his own ear, like a challenge thrown in the teeth of weakness. He asked for an envoy to the Red House Inn for a chaise. He was resolved, he said, to return to the Hollow that very afternoon. Mr. Yorke, instead of opposing, aided him in his blunt fashion; the chaise was sent for, though Mrs. Yorke declared the step would be his death. It came. Moore, not inclined for speeches, made his purse do duty for his tongue. Gratitude rang out in coin—clear, comprehensible music. The servants understood it, and Mrs. Horsfall, most perfectly of all; it made amends for every previous contumacy. She and her patient parted, at last, the best friends in the world.

When the kitchen had been visited and soothed—peace patched up there as one smooths a wrinkled cloth with the flat of a warm hand—Moore went to the parlour. Mrs. Yorke sat plunged in sullen dudgeon, the gloomiest speculations on the depths of man’s ingratitude absorbing her thoughts. He drew near and bent over her; she was obliged to look up, if only to bid him “avaunt.” There was still beauty in his pale, wasted features; and there was earnestness and a sort of sweetness—for he was smiling—in his hollow eyes. But that smile, like a flame in a draught, glittered and threatened to go out.

“Good-bye!” he said.

“And what are you going to leave us for?” she demanded. “We will keep you, and do anything in the world for you, if you will only stay till you are stronger.”

“Good-bye!” he repeated; then, softer, “You have been a mother to me; give your wilful son one embrace.”

Foreign-born, foreign-bred in certain outward ways, he offered her first one cheek, then the other. She kissed him.

“What a trouble—what a burden I have been to you!” he muttered, as if the words were forced up from the very soreness in his chest.

“You are the worst trouble now, headstrong youth!” was her answer. “I wonder who is to nurse you at Hollow’s Cottage? Your sister Hortense knows no more about such matters than a child.”

“Thank God! for I have had nursing enough to last me my life.”

The little girls came in—Jessie crying, Rose quiet but grave. Moore took them out into the hall to soothe and pet and kiss them. He knew Mrs. Yorke could not bear to see anything caressed but herself; had he fondled a kitten in her presence, she would have been affronted.

The boys were standing about the chaise when he entered it; but to them he offered no farewell. To Mr. Yorke he only said, “You have a good riddance of me. That was an unlucky shot for you, Yorke; it turned Briarmains into an hospital. Come and see me at the cottage soon.”

He drew up the glass; the chaise rolled away.

In half an hour he alighted at his own garden wicket. Having paid the driver and dismissed the vehicle, he leaned on the gate an instant, at once to rest and to muse. The air was dry and bitter; the black frost lay on everything like a hard glaze. No wind stirred. The stillness was so complete that he could hear his own breathing, a hushed, uneven sound, and, far off, some faint creak in the frozen world—a tree complaining, perhaps, or the hidden shift of ice on the mill-dam.

“Six months ago I passed out at this gate,” said he, “a proud, angry, disappointed man. I come back sadder and wiser; weakly enough, but not worried. A cold, gray, yet quiet world lies round—a world where, if I hope little, I fear nothing. All slavish terrors of embarrassment have left me. Let the worst come, I can work, as Joe Scott does, for an honourable living; in such doom I yet see some hardship but no degradation. Formerly, pecuniary ruin was equivalent in my eyes to personal dishonour. It is not so now; I know the difference. Ruin is an evil, but one for which I am prepared; the day of whose coming I know, for I have calculated. I can yet put it off six months—not an hour longer. If things by that time alter, which is not probable; if fetters, which now seem indissoluble, should be loosened from our trade (of all things the most unlikely to happen), I might conquer in this long struggle yet—I might—good God! what might I not do? But the thought is a brief madness; let me see things with sane eyes. Ruin will come, lay her axe to my fortune’s roots, and hew them down. I shall snatch a sapling, I shall cross the sea, and plant it in American woods. Louis will go with me. Will none but Louis go? I cannot tell—I have no right to ask.”

He entered the house.

It was afternoon, twilight even out of doors—starless and moonless twilight; for though it froze keenly, heaven wore a mask of cloud, congealed and locked. The mill-dam too was frozen; the Hollow lay in that peculiar silence which frost breeds, when every sound is sharpened and every pause is immense. Indoors it was already dark. Sarah had lit a good fire in the parlour; the smell of coal and kindling mingled with the faint, homely promise of tea being made in the kitchen—bread ready for toast, perhaps, the kettle beginning its first shy murmur.

“Hortense,” said Moore, as his sister bustled up to help him off with his cloak, “I am pleased to come home.”

Hortense did not perceive the peculiar novelty of the phrase, for he had never before called the cottage his home; its narrow limits had always seemed to him restrictive rather than protective. Still, whatever contributed to his happiness pleased her, and she said so.

He sat down, but soon rose again. He went to the window, where the dark pane held only his own gaunt reflection; he came back to the fire, drawn by the warmth as by a human presence.

“Hortense!”

“Mon frère?”

“This little parlour looks very clean and pleasant—unusually bright, somehow.”

“It is true, brother; I have had the whole house thoroughly and scrupulously cleaned in your absence.”

“Sister, I think on this first day of your return home you ought to have a friend or so to tea, if it were only to see how fresh and spruce you have made the little place.”

“True, brother. If it were not late I might send for Miss Mann.”

“So you might; but it really is too late to disturb that good lady, and the evening is much too cold for her to come out.”

“How thoughtful in you, dear Gérard! We must put it off till another day.”

“I want some one to-day, dear sister—some quiet guest, who would tire neither of us.”

“Miss Ainley?”

“An excellent person, they say; but she lives too far off. Tell Harry Scott to step up to the rectory with a request from you that Caroline Helstone should come and spend the evening with you.”

“Would it not be better to-morrow, dear brother?”

“I should like her to see the place as it is just now; its brilliant cleanliness and perfect neatness are so much to your credit.”

“It might benefit her in the way of example.”

“It might and must; she ought to come.”

He went into the kitchen. There the life of the house gathered: the discreet clink of crockery, the breath of heat from the range, the kettle’s fuller voice, and the comforting scent of bread catching colour. Domestic peace had its own music, and it was kinder to the nerves than any applause.

“Sarah, delay tea half an hour.”

Then he commissioned her to dispatch Harry Scott to the rectory, giving her a twisted note hastily scribbled in pencil by himself and addressed to “Miss Helstone.”

Scarcely had Sarah time to grow impatient—fearing, above all calamities, damage to toast already prepared—when the messenger returned, and with him the invited guest.

Caroline entered through the kitchen, quietly tripped up Sarah’s stairs to take off her bonnet and furs, and came down as quietly; her beautiful curls were smoothed, her merino dress and delicate collar trim and spotless, her gay little work-bag in her hand. She lingered to exchange a few kind words with Sarah, to glance at the new tortoise-shell kitten basking on the hearthstone like a warm morsel of shadow, and to speak to the canary-bird, which the sudden blaze from the fire had startled on its perch. Then she went into the parlour.

The gentle salutation, the friendly welcome, were interchanged in the tranquil fashion befitting cousins meeting. A sense of pleasure, subtle and quiet as perfume, diffused itself through the room. The lamp, newly kindled, burned up bright; the tray and the singing urn were brought in, the low hiss and simmer of boiling water sounding like content.

“I am pleased to come home,” repeated Mr. Moore.

They sat round the table. Hortense chiefly talked. She congratulated Caroline on the evident improvement in her health. Her colour and her plump cheeks were returning. It was true. There was an obvious change in Miss Helstone. All about her seemed elastic; depression, fear, forlornness, were withdrawn. No longer crushed and saddened and slow and drooping, she looked like one who had tasted the cordial of heart’s ease—something warm and sweet set to the lips at last—and been lifted on the wing of hope.

After tea Hortense went upstairs. She had not rummaged her drawers for a month past, and the impulse to perform that operation was now become resistless. During her absence, the talk passed into Caroline’s hands. She took it up with ease; she fell into her best tone of conversation. A pleasing facility and elegance of language gave fresh charm to familiar topics; a new music in her always soft voice gently surprised and captivated the listener; unwonted shades and lights of expression elevated her young countenance with character, and kindled it with animation.

“Caroline, you look as if you had heard good tidings,” said Moore, after gazing at her for some minutes with the grave attention of a man who has come close to losing what he values.

“Do I?”

“I sent for you this evening that I might be cheered; but you cheer me more than I had calculated.”

“I am glad of that. And I really cheer you?”

“You look brightly, move buoyantly, speak musically.”

“It is pleasant to be here again.”

“Truly it is pleasant; I feel it so. And to see health on your cheek and hope in your eye is pleasant, Cary; but what is this hope, and what is the source of this sunshine I perceive about you?”

“For one thing, I am happy in mamma. I love her so much, and she loves me. Long and tenderly she nursed me. Now, when her care has made me well, I can occupy myself for and with her all the day. I say it is my turn to attend to her; and I do attend to her. I am her waiting-woman as well as her child. I like—you would laugh if you knew what pleasure I have in making dresses and sewing for her. She looks so nice now, Robert; I will not let her be old-fashioned. And then, she is charming to talk to—full of wisdom, ripe in judgment, rich in information, exhaustless in stores her observant faculties have quietly amassed. Every day that I live with her I like her better, I esteem her more highly, I love her more tenderly.”

“That for one thing, then, Cary. You talk in such a way about ‘mamma’ it is enough to make one jealous of the old lady.”

“She is not old, Robert.”

“Of the young lady, then.”

“She does not pretend to be young.”

“Well, of the matron. But you said ‘mamma’s’ affection was one thing that made you happy; now for the other thing.”

“I am glad you are better.”

“What besides?”

“I am glad we are friends.”

“You and I?”

“Yes. I once thought we never should be.”

“Cary, some day I mean to tell you a thing about myself that is not to my credit, and consequently will not please you.”

“Ah, don’t! I cannot bear to think ill of you.”

“And I cannot bear that you should think better of me than I deserve.”

“Well, but I half know your ‘thing;’ indeed, I believe I know all about it.”

“You do not.”

“I believe I do.”

“Whom does it concern besides me?”

She coloured; she hesitated; she was silent, as if the very word she must say had no safe shape.

“Speak, Cary! Whom does it concern?”

She tried to utter a name, and could not.

“Tell me; there is none present but ourselves. Be frank.”

“But if I guess wrong?”

“I will forgive. Whisper, Cary.”

He bent his ear to her lips. Still she would not—or could not—speak clearly to the point. Seeing he waited and was resolved to hear something, she said at last, “Miss Keeldar spent a day at the rectory about a week since. The evening came on very wintry, and we persuaded her to stay all night.”

“And you and she curled your hair together?”

“How do you know that?”

“And then you chattered, and she told you—”

“It was not at curling-hair time, so you are not as wise as you think; and, besides, she didn’t tell me.”

“You slept together afterwards?”

“We occupied the same room and bed. We did not sleep much; we talked the whole night through.”

“I’ll be sworn you did! And then it all came out—tant pis. I would rather you had heard it from myself.”

“You are quite wrong. She did not tell me what you suspect—she is not the person to proclaim such things; but yet I inferred something from parts of her discourse. I gathered more from rumour, and I made out the rest by instinct.”

“But if she did not tell you that I wanted to marry her for the sake of her money, and that she refused me indignantly and scornfully—you need neither start nor blush; nor yet need you prick your trembling fingers with your needle. That is the plain truth, whether you like it or not—if such was not the subject of her august confidences, on what point did they turn? You say you talked the whole night through; what about?”

“About things we never thoroughly discussed before, intimate friends as we have been; but you hardly expect I should tell you?”

“Yes, yes, Cary; you will tell me. You said we were friends, and friends should always confide in each other.”

“But you are sure you won’t repeat it?”

“Quite sure.”

“Not to Louis?”

“Not even to Louis. What does Louis care for young ladies’ secrets?”

“Robert, Shirley is a curious, magnanimous being.”

“I dare say. I can imagine there are both odd points and grand points about her.”

“I have found her chary in showing her feelings; but when they rush out, river-like, and pass full and powerful before you—almost without leave from her—you gaze, wonder; you admire, and—I think—love her.”

“You saw this spectacle?”

“Yes; at dead of night, when all the house was silent, and starlight and the cold reflection from the snow glimmered in our chamber, then I saw Shirley’s heart.”

“Her heart’s core? Do you think she showed you that?”

“Her heart’s core.”

“And how was it?”

“Like a shrine, for it was holy; like snow, for it was pure; like flame, for it was warm; like death, for it was strong.”

“Can she love? tell me that.”

“What think you?”

“She has loved none that have loved her yet.”

“Who are those that have loved her?”

He named a list of gentlemen, closing with Sir Philip Nunnely.

“She has loved none of these.”

“Yet some of them were worthy of a woman’s affection.”

“Of some women’s, but not of Shirley’s.”

“Is she better than others of her sex?”

“She is peculiar, and more dangerous to take as a wife—rashly.”

“I can imagine that.”

“She spoke of you—”

“Oh, she did! I thought you denied it.”

“She did not speak in the way you fancy; but I asked her, and I would make her tell me what she thought of you, or rather how she felt towards you. I wanted to know; I had long wanted to know.”

“So had I; but let us hear. She thinks meanly, she feels contemptuously, doubtless?”

“She thinks of you almost as highly as a woman can think of a man. You know she can be eloquent. I yet feel in fancy the glow of the language in which her opinion was conveyed.”

“But how does she feel?”

“Till you shocked her (she said you had shocked her, but she would not tell me how) she felt as a sister feels towards a brother of whom she is at once fond and proud.”

“I’ll shock her no more, Cary, for the shock rebounded on myself till I staggered again. But that comparison about sister and brother is all nonsense. She is too rich and proud to entertain fraternal sentiments for me.”

“You don’t know her, Robert; and, somehow, I fancy now (I had other ideas formerly) that you cannot know her. You and she are not so constructed as to be able thoroughly to understand each other.”

“It may be so. I esteem her, I admire her; and yet my impressions concerning her are harsh—perhaps uncharitable. I believe, for instance, that she is incapable of love—”

“Shirley incapable of love!”

“That she will never marry. I imagine her jealous of compromising her pride, of relinquishing her power, of sharing her property.”

“Shirley has hurt your amour propre.”

“She did hurt it; though I had not an emotion of tenderness, nor a spark of passion for her.”

“Then, Robert, it was very wicked in you to want to marry her.”

“And very mean, my little pastor, my pretty priestess. I never wanted to kiss Miss Keeldar in my life, though she has fine lips, scarlet and round as ripe cherries; or, if I did wish it, it was the mere desire of the eye.”

“I doubt, now, whether you are speaking the truth. The grapes or the cherries are sour—‘hung too high.’”

“She has a pretty figure, a pretty face, beautiful hair. I acknowledge all her charms and feel none of them, or only feel them in a way she would disdain. I suppose I was truly tempted by the mere gilding of the bait. Caroline, what a noble fellow your Robert is—great, good, disinterested, and then so pure!”

“But not perfect. He made a great blunder once, and we will hear no more about it.”

“And shall we think no more about it, Cary? Shall we not despise him in our heart—gentle but just, compassionate but upright?”

“Never! We will remember that with what measure we mete it shall be measured unto us, and so we will give no scorn, only affection.”

“Which won’t satisfy, I warn you of that. Something besides affection—something far stronger, sweeter, warmer—will be demanded one day. Is it there to give?”

Caroline was moved—much moved. Her fingers, that had been busy with her needle, paused; her breath came quicker, as if the room had grown smaller.

“Be calm, Lina,” said Moore soothingly. “I have no intention, because I have no right, to perturb your mind now, nor for months to come. Don’t look as if you would leave me. We will make no more agitating allusions; we will resume our gossip. Do not tremble; look me in the face. See what a poor, pale, grim phantom I am—more pitiable than formidable.”

She looked shyly. “There is something formidable still, pale as you are,” she said, as her eye fell under his.

“To return to Shirley,” pursued Moore: “is it your opinion that she is ever likely to marry?”

“She loves.”

“Platonically—theoretically—all humbug!”

“She loves what I call sincerely.”

“Did she say so?”

“I cannot affirm that she said so. No such confession as ‘I love this man or that’ passed her lips.”

“I thought not.”

“But the feeling made its way in spite of her, and I saw it. She spoke of one man in a strain not to be misunderstood. Her voice alone was sufficient testimony. Having wrung from her an opinion on your character, I demanded a second opinion of—another person about whom I had my conjectures, though they were the most tangled and puzzled conjectures in the world. I would make her speak. I shook her, I chid her, I pinched her fingers when she tried to put me off with gibes and jests in her queer provoking way, and at last out it came. The voice, I say, was enough; hardly raised above a whisper, and yet such a soft vehemence in its tones. There was no confession, no confidence, in the matter. To these things she cannot condescend; but I am sure that man’s happiness is dear to her as her own life.”

“Who is it?”

“I charged her with the fact. She did not deny, she did not avow, but looked at me. I saw her eyes by the snow-gleam. It was quite enough. I triumphed over her mercilessly.”

“What right had you to triumph? Do you mean to say you are fancy free?”

“Whatever I am, Shirley is a bondswoman. Lioness, she has found her captor. Mistress she may be of all round her, but her own mistress she is not.”

“So you exulted at recognizing a fellow-slave in one so fair and imperial?”

“I did; Robert, you say right, in one so fair and imperial.”

“You confess it—a fellow-slave?”

“I confess nothing; but I say that haughty Shirley is no more free than was Hagar.”

“And who, pray, is the Abraham, the hero of a patriarch who has achieved such a conquest?”

“You still speak scornfully, and cynically, and sorely; but I will make you change your note before I have done with you.”

“We will see that. Can she marry this Cupidon?”

“Cupidon! he is just about as much a Cupidon as you are a Cyclops.”

“Can she marry him?”

“You will see.”

“I want to know his name, Cary.”

“Guess it.”

“Is it any one in this neighbourhood?”

“Yes, in Briarfield parish.”

“Then it is some person unworthy of her. I don’t know a soul in Briarfield parish her equal.”

“Guess.”

“Impossible. I suppose she is under a delusion, and will plunge into some absurdity, after all.”

Caroline smiled.

“Do you approve the choice?” asked Moore.

“Quite, quite.”

“Then I am puzzled; for the head which owns this bounteous fall of hazel curls is an excellent little thinking machine, most accurate in its working. It boasts a correct, steady judgment, inherited from ‘mamma,’ I suppose.”

“And I quite approve, and mamma was charmed.”

“‘Mamma’ charmed—Mrs. Pryor! It can’t be romantic, then?”

“It is romantic, but it is also right.”

“Tell me, Cary—tell me out of pity; I am too weak to be tantalized.”

“You shall be tantalized—it will do you no harm; you are not so weak as you pretend.”

“I have twice this evening had some thoughts of falling on the floor at your feet.”

“You had better not. I shall decline to help you up.”

“And worshipping you downright. My mother was a Roman Catholic. You look like the loveliest of her pictures of the Virgin. I think I will embrace her faith and kneel and adore.”

“Robert, Robert, sit still; don’t be absurd. I will go to Hortense if you commit extravagances.”

“You have stolen my senses. Just now nothing will come into my mind but les litanies de la sainte Vièrge. Rose céleste, reine des anges!”

“Tour d’ivoire, maison d’or—is not that the jargon? Well, sit down quietly, and guess your riddle.”

“But ‘mamma’ charmed—there’s the puzzle.”

“I’ll tell you what mamma said when I told her. ‘Depend upon it, my dear, such a choice will make the happiness of Miss Keeldar’s life.’”

“I’ll guess once, and no more. It is old Helstone. She is going to be your aunt.”

“I’ll tell my uncle; I’ll tell Shirley!” cried Caroline, laughing gleefully. “Guess again, Robert; your blunders are charming.”

“It is the parson—Hall.”

“Indeed, no; he is mine, if you please.”

“Yours! Ay, the whole generation of women in Briarfield seem to have made an idol of that priest. I wonder why; he is bald, sand-blind, gray-haired.”

“Fanny will be here to fetch me before you have solved the riddle, if you don’t make haste.”

“I’ll guess no more—I am tired; and then I don’t care. Miss Keeldar may marry le grand Turc for me.”

“Must I whisper?”

“That you must, and quickly. Here comes Hortense; come near, a little nearer, my own Lina. I care for the whisper more than the words.”

She whispered.

Robert started; a flash shot through his eye; then a brief laugh broke him, sharp as a struck glass. Miss Moore entered, and Sarah followed behind, with word that Fanny was come. The hour of converse was over—finished as cleanly as the tea-service is cleared away, leaving only warmth and a scent of extinguished flame.

Robert found a moment to exchange a few more whispered sentences. He waited at the foot of the staircase as Caroline descended after putting on her shawl.

“Must I call Shirley a noble creature now?” he asked.

“If you wish to speak the truth, certainly.”

“Must I forgive her?”

“Forgive her? Naughty Robert! Was she in the wrong, or were you?”

“Must I at length love her downright, Cary?”

Caroline looked keenly up, and made a movement towards him, something between the loving and the petulant.

“Only give the word, and I’ll try to obey you.”

“Indeed, you must not love her; the bare idea is perverse.”

Louis Moore’s mistrust of Mr. Sympson’s threatened flight from Fieldhead proved sound. The very next day after that grand rupture about Sir Philip Nunnely, uncle and niece contrived a patched-up peace. Shirley—who could no more wear the look of inhospitable mistress than a hearth can refuse its own flame (save in the one case of Mr. Donne)—begged them all to remain yet a little. She begged with a warmth that betrayed an under-current. They took her at her word. Mr. Sympson, besides, could not endure the thought of leaving her unwatched—left loose, as he deemed it, to clasp Robert Moore the moment that gentleman should be able (Heaven avert it, prayed Mr. Sympson) to renew pretensions to her hand. So the whole party stayed.

In his first fury against the house of Moore, Mr. Sympson had borne himself towards Mr. Louis with such coarse insolence that Louis—patient under work or pain, but not under vulgar affront—had at once given in his resignation, and could now be induced to resume his post only on the condition of holding it till the Sympsons quitted Yorkshire. Mrs. Sympson’s entreaties prevailed thus far; his attachment to Henry was a second bond; and perhaps there was a third motive, deeper than either. It would have been hard—harder than he chose to confess—to turn his back on Fieldhead just then.

For a time, outwardly, things smoothed themselves. Shirley’s health came back as quickly as yeast lifts a loaf when the warmth is right: the pale waste of her late alarm filled; the bloom returned; her spirits, once checked as by a sudden hand laid on a singing mouth, flowed again. Moore had found means to ease her of nervous fear; and from the hour she had trusted him, her dread seemed to take wing. She grew lightsome—childlike—trusting responsibility to another, as if danger were a storm heard far off beyond the hills.

He, with William Farren—through whom he made inquiries as to Phœbe—agreed in asserting that the dog was not mad; that ill-usage alone had driven her from home, it being proved her master often chastised her with a violence that might have turned a gentler creature savage. True or not, it served Shirley. The groom and the gamekeeper swore the contrary—both declaring that if Phœbe’s was not clear hydrophobia, then there was no such disease. Louis listened with incredulity. He carried to Shirley only the comforting part, as one might carry a cup of warm broth to a shaken patient and keep back the bitter draught. She believed him; and right or wrong, it is certain the bite, in her case, proved harmless.

November went by; December came. Now the Sympsons were indeed preparing to depart. Christmas called them home. Their trunks and packages were in train; within a few days they would leave. One winter evening, in the last week of their stay, Louis Moore took out his small blank book again and spoke into it—as if the paper were the only listener he could safely trust.

* * * * *

She is lovelier than ever. Since that little cloud was dispersed, all the temporary waste and wanness have vanished. It is marvellous how swiftly youth repairs itself—how the sap rises again, how colour returns, how the eye brightens as if some inner lamp had been trimmed.

After breakfast this morning, having seen her and heard her—and, in some strange manner, felt her, as one feels warmth from a fire without touching it—I passed from her sunny presence into the chill drawing-room. Someone had left a little gilt volume on the table: lyrics. I read a poem or two. Whether the spell lay in the verse or in me I cannot tell; but my heart filled, genial and quick, and my pulse rose. The air was frosty, yet I glowed. I, too, am still young. Though she once said she never counted me young, I am barely thirty. There are instants when life, for no reason beyond the mere fact that one has youth in the veins, takes a tender colour—like wine held to the light.

It was time for the schoolroom. I went. That schoolroom is pleasant in the morning. The low lattice admits a slant of sun; the books are orderly; no litter of papers lies about; the fire is clean and clear, no cinders spilt, no ashes heaped. I found Henry there—and he had brought Miss Keeldar. They were together.

I said she was lovelier than ever. She is. A rose—fine, delicate, not deep—opens on her cheek. Her eye, always dark and clear and speaking, utters now a language I cannot render. It is like speech seen and not heard—the sort of communion angels might hold when there is “silence in heaven.” Her hair has always been dusk as night and fine as silk, her neck always fair and flexible and polished; but both have now a new charm. The tresses are soft as shadow; the shoulders they fall on have a goddess grace. Once I only saw her beauty; now I feel it—felt, like fragrance, like heat.

Henry was repeating his lesson to her before bringing it to me. One of her hands held the book; he held the other. That boy gets more than his share of privilege. He dares caress and be caressed. What indulgence she gives him, what compassion! Too much. If this went on, Henry in a few years—when his soul was formed—would lay it on her altar, as I have laid mine.

I saw her eyelid flutter when I came in, but she did not look up. Now she scarcely ever gives me a glance. She seems to grow silent too; to me she rarely speaks, and when I am present she says little to others. In my dark moments I name it indifference, aversion, anything bitter. In my brighter intervals I translate it otherwise. I say, were I her equal, I could find in this shyness a coyness; and in that coyness, love. As it is—dare I look for it? What could I do with it if found?
